**Purpose:** Relate quaternion ideals to adelic quotients and establish the Hecke correspondences, spectral inputs, and operator bounds used to estimate averages on finite orbits.

## Inversion and adjoints of finite-coset averaging

[Open item](item-lem-finite-coset-inversion-and-adjoints) · level 0


### Statement

Let $G$ be a finite group and $H\le G$ a subgroup. Set $X=H\backslash G=\{Hg:g\in G\}$, the set of distinct right cosets $Hg=\{hg:h\in H\}$. For $Z=G$ or $X$, write $\mathbb C^Z$ for complex functions on $Z$, with pointwise vector operations and the counting inner product
$$\langle F,W\rangle_Z=\sum_{z\in Z}F(z)\overline{W(z)}.$$
Let $U=\{F\in\mathbb C^G:F(hg)=F(g)\ \text{for all }h\in H,\ g\in G\}$ be the subspace of functions invariant under left multiplication by $H$. Functions on $X$ correspond to functions in $U$ by $Jf(g)=f(Hg)$.

A multiset on $G$ assigns a nonnegative integer multiplicity $m(s)$ to each $s\in G$. Define its inverse multiset by $m^\vee(s)=m(s^{-1})$. Whenever a natural number $n$ occurs as a scalar, it means $n\cdot1_{\mathbb R}$ in the embedded real field $\mathbb R\subset\mathbb C$. Define the left translation sum $A_m$ on $\mathbb C^G$ by
$$(A_mF)(g)=\sum_{s\in G}m(s)F(sg).$$
Define right translation on $\mathbb C^X$ by $(R_r f)(Hg)=f(Hgr)$ for $r\in G$.

1. The adjoint of $A_m$ on $\mathbb C^G$ is $A_{m^\vee}$. Here the adjoint of a linear map $T$ is the linear map $T^*$ satisfying $\langle Tu,v\rangle=\langle u,T^*v\rangle$ for all $u,v$. When $A_m$ and $A_{m^\vee}$ both take $U$ into $U$, they induce maps $T_m$ and $T_{m^\vee}$ on $\mathbb C^X$ by $JT_m=A_mJ$ and $JT_{m^\vee}=A_{m^\vee}J$, and $T_m^*=T_{m^\vee}$.
2. If $m(sh)=m(s)$ for every $s\in G$ and $h\in H$, and $m(s^{-1})=m(s)$ for every $s\in G$, then $A_m$ induces $T_m$ on $\mathbb C^X$. This map is self-adjoint, meaning $T_m^*=T_m$, and commutes with right translations, meaning $T_mR_r=R_rT_m$ for every $r\in G$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] A group has associative multiplication, an identity $e$ and two-sided inverses. A subgroup contains $e$ and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group), [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F2] The cosets of $H$ represented by $g$ are the sets $gH=\{gh:h\in H\}$ and $Hg=\{hg:h\in H\}$ ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)).

[F3] Inversion is a bijection of a group onto itself, with $(s^{-1})^{-1}=s$ and $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$ ([math:lem-group-inverse-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-inverse-laws)). The distinct left cosets $gH$ partition $G$ into nonempty disjoint sets ([math:lem-coset-partition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-partition)). For a subgroup of a finite group, there are finitely many left cosets and every coset has size $|H|$, the number of elements of $H$ ([math:lem-finite-coset-partition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-coset-partition)).

[F4] A finite multiset is a multiplicity function with nonnegative integer values and finitely many nonzero values. On a finite group, every such function has finitely many nonzero values ([math:def-multiset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-multiset)).

[F5] Complex numbers form a field containing the real numbers. In particular, complex addition is associative and commutative and multiplication distributes over it ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)).

[F6] A finite sum in a commutative monoid, a set with associative and commutative addition and a zero element, is obtained by adding all terms in any enumeration. The sum is invariant under a change of index by a bijection, splits over disjoint unions and permits exchanging the order of two finite sums ([math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [math:lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini)).

[F7] For $z=a+bi$ with $a,b$ real, $\overline z=a-bi$. Conjugation fixes real scalars, preserves sums and products and is its own inverse ([math:def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus), [math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws)).

[F8] On complex functions on a nonempty finite set $Z$ with pointwise vector operations, the counting form divided by the positive number $|Z|$ is an inner product ([math:lem-standard-hermitian-form-on-functions-on-a-nonempty-finite-set-is-an-inner-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-standard-hermitian-form-on-functions-on-a-nonempty-finite-set-is-an-inner-product)). In the linear-first convention an inner product is linear in its first argument and conjugate symmetric, and $\langle v,v\rangle$ is a nonnegative real number that vanishes exactly at $v=0$. Conjugate symmetry means $\langle u,v\rangle=\overline{\langle v,u\rangle}$ and yields conjugate-linearity in the second argument ([math:def-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inner-product-space)).

[F9] A map is linear if it preserves complex linear combinations. The adjoint is a linear map characterized by the pairing identity in the Statement ([math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map), [math:def-adjoint-of-a-linear-map-between-inner-product-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-adjoint-of-a-linear-map-between-inner-product-spaces)).

[F10] For a group acting on a set by a left action, the identity acts as the identity map and the maps for $r,t$ compose in the order $R_rR_t=R_{rt}$ ([math:def-group-action](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-action)).

[F11] For any set $Z$ and any field $K$, the functions $Z\to K$ form a vector space with pointwise operations ([math:def-function-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-function-space)). A subset of a vector space that contains zero and is closed under addition and scalar multiplication is a linear subspace with the restricted operations ([math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)).

[F12] A map with a two-sided inverse is a bijection ([math:def-injection-surjection-bijection](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-injection-surjection-bijection)). A bijection from a finite set transports finiteness and preserves cardinality, which is a natural number and is zero exactly for the empty set ([math:def-finite-cardinality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-cardinality)). Every subset of a finite set is finite ([math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set)).

[F13] The real numbers form an ordered field ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)). In that field set $\iota(n)=n\cdot1_{\mathbb R}$, so $\iota(0)=0$ and $\iota(n+1)=\iota(n)+1_{\mathbb R}$ ([math:def-canonical-natural](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-canonical-natural)). This scalar is positive for $n\ge 1$ ([math:lem-of-naturals-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-naturals-positive)).

[F14] An assertion about natural numbers holds for all of them if it holds at zero and is preserved by successor ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

### Proof

**Given:** A finite group $G$, a subgroup $H$ and a multiplicity function $m$ as in the Statement.

**Goal:** Identify the adjoint on $G$ and on the coset space under the stated preservation hypotheses, and prove the self-adjointness and commutation assertions.

1.1 The set of inverses of the elements of a left coset is $(gH)^{-1}=\{h^{-1}g^{-1}:h\in H\}=Hg^{-1}$ because inversion is a bijection on $H$. Taking elementwise inverses gives a bijection of the left-coset family onto $X$, since applying inversion again returns the original coset. Inversion also restricts to a bijection on each coset onto its image. Thus inversion carries the finite partition by left cosets to a finite partition by right cosets, each with $|H|$ elements. Both $G$ and $X$ are nonempty since $e\in G$ and $H=He$ belongs to $X$. [F1, F2, F3, F12]

2.1 For $Z=G,X$, the pointwise operations give a complex vector space by [F11]. Both sets are finite and nonempty by step 1.1 and the hypothesis on $G$. Their cardinalities are positive naturals by [F12], and their real images are positive by [F13]. Multiplying the normalized form of [F8] by the positive real scalar $|Z|$ preserves linearity, conjugate symmetry and positive definiteness, so the stated counting form is an inner product. The zero function lies in $U$ and the sum of two functions in $U$ and each scalar multiple of a function in $U$ still satisfy the defining equalities pointwise. Thus $U$ is a linear subspace by [F11], and the counting inner product restricts to it. For $h\in H$ one has $Hhg=Hg$, since multiplication by $h$ on the right is a bijection of $H$ with inverse multiplication by $h^{-1}$. Therefore $Jf$ lies in $U$. For $F\in U$, assigning $f(Hg)=F(g)$ is independent of the representative, since $Hg=Hg'$ implies $g'\in Hg$ and hence $g'=hg$ for some $h\in H$. This rule is a two-sided inverse of $J$, so $J$ is a bijection. Its pointwise formula preserves linear combinations, so $J$ is linear. [F1, F2, F5, F7, F8, F9, F11, F12, F13, step 1.1]

3.1 Split the sum on $G$ into the finitely many right cosets by iterating disjoint-union splitting. Each partial union is finite as a subset of $G$, and induction on the number of blocks starts with the empty sum and adds the contribution of the next disjoint coset. For a fixed complex value $z$, the sum of $n$ copies of $z$ equals $\iota(n)z$ by induction. The empty sum gives the case $n=0$, and the next term changes $\iota(n)z$ to $\iota(n)z+z=(\iota(n)+1_{\mathbb R})z=\iota(n+1)z$. Each coset $C$ contributes $|H|f(C)\overline{k(C)}$ to $\langle Jf,Jk\rangle_G$, since the summand is constant on $C$ and $C$ has $|H|$ elements. Hence $\langle Jf,Jk\rangle_G=|H|\langle f,k\rangle_X$, where the real scalar $|H|$ is positive because $e\in H$. [F1, F5, F6, F12, F13, F14, step 1.1, step 2.1]

3.2 Both $A_m$ and $A_{m^\vee}$ are linear by pointwise expansion of the finite sums using two-term distributivity and induction along a finite enumeration. For $s\in G$, let $L_sF(g)=F(sg)$. Changing from $g$ to $x=sg$, with inverse $g=s^{-1}x$, gives $\langle L_sF,W\rangle_G=\sum_{x\in G}F(x)\overline{W(s^{-1}x)}=\langle F,L_{s^{-1}}W\rangle_G$. Sum this identity with weights $m(s)$. Each weight is the image of $m(s)$ in the real subfield, so conjugation fixes it. First-argument linearity and second-argument conjugate-linearity extend to the finite sums by induction on the number of terms. These laws and reindexing by $t=s^{-1}$ give $\langle A_mF,W\rangle_G=\left\langle F,\sum_{s\in G}m(s)L_{s^{-1}}W\right\rangle_G=\langle F,A_{m^\vee}W\rangle_G$. The adjoint pairing determines at most one map on an inner product space. If $B$ and $D$ satisfy it for the same operator, then $\langle u,(B-D)v\rangle=0$ for every $u,v$, so choosing $u=(B-D)v$ gives $(B-D)v=0$ by positive definiteness. Thus $A_m^*=A_{m^\vee}$. [F1, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8, F9, F12, F13, F14, step 2.1]

4.1 If both sums take $U$ into $U$, define $T_m=J^{-1}A_mJ$ and $T_{m^\vee}=J^{-1}A_{m^\vee}J$. For either corresponding pair $(T,A)$, the identity $JT=AJ$ and the linearity of $J$ and $A$ give $JT(af+bk)=AJ(af+bk)=aAJf+bAJk=J(aTf+bTk)$ for all $f,k\in\mathbb C^X$ and $a,b\in\mathbb C$. Injectivity of $J$ gives $T(af+bk)=aTf+bTk$, so each descended map is linear. The whole-group pairing gives $\langle JT_mf,Jk\rangle_G=\langle Jf,JT_{m^\vee}k\rangle_G$. Applying the scaling identity of step 3.1 to both sides and dividing by the positive real scalar $|H|$ gives $\langle T_mf,k\rangle_X=\langle f,T_{m^\vee}k\rangle_X$. The adjoint characterization and uniqueness give $T_m^*=T_{m^\vee}$. [F5, F9, F12, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 3.2]

5.1 If $m(sh)=m(s)$ for all $s\in G$ and $h\in H$, then for every $F\in\mathbb C^G$ and $h\in H$, $(A_mF)(hg)=\sum_{s\in G}m(s)F(shg)=\sum_{t\in G}m(th^{-1})F(tg)=(A_mF)(g)$. Here $t=sh$ is a bijective change of index with inverse $s=th^{-1}$, and $h^{-1}\in H$. Thus $A_m$ takes $U$ into $U$. If also $m(s^{-1})=m(s)$, then $m^\vee=m$, so the second operator takes $U$ into $U$ and step 4.1 gives $T_m^*=T_m$. [given, F1, F4, F6, F12, step 4.1]

6.1 For $r\in G$, the set equality $Hgr=(Hg)r$ makes right translation on cosets independent of the representative. It yields the indicated action on functions, since $R_e f=f$ and $(R_rR_t f)(Hg)=f(Hgrt)=(R_{rt}f)(Hg)$. On $\mathbb C^G$ put $(\widetilde R_rF)(g)=F(gr)$, so $JR_r=\widetilde R_rJ$. The two expressions $(A_m\widetilde R_rF)(g)$ and $(\widetilde R_rA_mF)(g)$ both equal $\sum_{s\in G}m(s)F(sgr)$ by associativity. When $A_m$ takes $U$ into $U$, the equality descends through the injective map $J$ to $T_mR_r=R_rT_m$. Together with the adjoint and self-adjointness identities above, this proves both assertions. [F1, F2, F6, F10, step 2.1, step 3.2, step 4.1, step 5.1] ∎


## Centered indicator and its Rayleigh quotient bound

[Open item](item-lem-centered-indicator-rayleigh-bound) · level 0


### Statement

Let $Y$ be a nonempty finite set and $O\subseteq Y$ a nonempty subset. Write $|A|$ for the number of elements of a finite set $A$. On $V=\mathbb K^Y$, the space of functions $Y\to\mathbb K$ for $\mathbb K=\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$, use pointwise vector operations and the counting inner product $\langle u,v\rangle=\sum_{y\in Y}u(y)\overline{v(y)}$. The bar fixes real numbers and sends $a+ib$ to $a-ib$ for real $a,b$. Write $\|v\|=\sqrt{\langle v,v\rangle}$. In scalar expressions, a cardinality $n=|A|$ denotes the sum of $n$ copies of $1$ in $\mathbb R$, viewed in $\mathbb C$ by its real field embedding when needed.

Let $f=\mathbf1_O$ be the indicator of $O$, equal to $1$ on $O$ and $0$ elsewhere, and let $\mathbf1$ be the constant function equal to $1$. Put $\mu=|O|/|Y|$ and $h=f-\mu\mathbf1$. Then $h$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf1$, meaning $\langle h,\mathbf1\rangle=0$, and $\|h\|^2=|O|(1-\mu)$.

Suppose $T:V\to V$ is linear, meaning it preserves linear combinations, and self-adjoint, meaning $\langle Tu,v\rangle=\langle u,Tv\rangle$ for all $u,v\in V$. Assume $T\mathbf1=d\mathbf1$ for $d\in\mathbb R$. Suppose $h$ belongs to a linear subspace $W$ (a subset containing zero and closed under linear combinations) that is $T$-invariant, meaning $T(W)\subseteq W$. Assume the restriction $T|_W:w\mapsto Tw$ on $W$ has operator norm at most $L$, where $L\ge0$. This bound says $\|Tw\|\le L$ for every $w\in W$ with $\|w\|=1$, with the operator norm defined to be zero on the zero space. Then the Rayleigh quotient of $f$, the pairing $\langle f,Tf\rangle$ divided by $\langle f,f\rangle=|O|$, satisfies
$$\frac{\langle f,Tf\rangle}{|O|}\le d\mu+L(1-\mu).$$

[math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension)

### Justification

The indicator and constant functions specify a single value at every point of $Y$. Since $O\subseteq Y$ and $Y$ is finite, $O$ is finite by clause 1 of [math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set). Nonemptiness means that the natural cardinalities of $O$ and $Y$ are nonzero ([math:def-finite-cardinality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-cardinality)). Their real images under the canonical natural map ([math:def-canonical-natural](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-canonical-natural)) are positive by [math:lem-of-naturals-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-naturals-positive) applied in the ordered field $\mathbb R$ ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)), so $\mu$ is defined. The real field embedding into $\mathbb C$ ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)) preserves both $1$ and addition, so the embedded count agrees with the sum of the same number of complex units $1$. The scalars form a field in either case ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field), [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)). By [math:def-function-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-function-space), pointwise addition and scalar multiplication make $V$ a vector space over $\mathbb K$, with the constant zero function as zero vector. The sums over $Y$ are independent of enumeration by [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), applied to the additive commutative monoids of $\mathbb K$ and $V$. In any enumeration, distributivity term by term gives linearity of the counting pairing in its first variable. Conjugation is additive, multiplicative and involutive by [math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws) in the complex case, and is the identity in the real case. Consequently conjugating the finite sum for $\langle v,u\rangle$ gives $\langle u,v\rangle$. By [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field) and [math:def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus), each term $v(y)\overline{v(y)}$ on the diagonal is the real number $a^2+b^2$ when $v(y)=a+ib$, with $b=0$ in the real case. In the ordered field $\mathbb R$ ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)), a real square is zero when its base is zero and positive otherwise by [math:lem-of-square-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-square-positive). Thus $a^2+b^2$ is nonnegative and is zero exactly when $a=b=0$, equivalently when $v(y)=0$. Enumerate $Y$ by $y_0,\ldots,y_{n-1}$, with $n=|Y|$ as a natural number. By [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid) and [math:def-sum-over-a-finite-index-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sum-over-a-finite-index-set), the sum of the real diagonal terms over $Y$ is the recursive real sum of this list. In the complex case the diagonal pairing is the image of this real sum because the real field embedding preserves zero and addition. Clause 4 of [math:lem-finite-sum-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-laws) now gives a nonnegative real sum and says that if the sum is zero, every summand is zero. Conversely, a list of all zero terms sums to zero. Therefore the diagonal pairing vanishes exactly when $v$ vanishes at every point. This verifies the inner-product axioms and gives the stated norm by the nonnegative square root.

For each $y\in Y$, let $\delta_y$ equal $1$ at $y$ and $0$ elsewhere. Write $B=\{\delta_y:y\in Y\}$. The map $y\mapsto\delta_y$ is a bijection onto $B$, since distinct points give distinct indicators by evaluation. For each $u\in V$, the equality $u=\sum_{y\in Y}u(y)\delta_y$ holds because the right side evaluates to $u(z)$ at each $z\in Y$. Enumerating $Y$ exhibits the sum as a finite linear combination of elements of $B$ ([math:def-linear-combination-and-span](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-combination-and-span)). It belongs to the span of $B$ by [math:lem-span-is-the-set-of-linear-combinations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-span-is-the-set-of-linear-combinations), so $B$ spans $V$. For any injective finite list $(\delta_{y_j})$ in $B$, evaluation at $y_j$ of a combination $\sum_i c_i\delta_{y_i}=0$ gives $c_j=0$, because the points $y_i$ in the list are distinct. This holds at every index, so $B$ is linearly independent by [math:def-linear-independence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-independence). Thus $B$ is a basis ([math:def-linear-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-basis)). The bijection from $Y$ to $B$ makes $B$ finite ([math:def-finite-cardinality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-cardinality)), so $V$ is finite-dimensional ([math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension)) and clause 1 of [math:thm-dimension-of-a-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-dimension-of-a-linear-subspace) makes $W$ finite-dimensional. The pairing restricts to an inner product on $W$ because its axioms continue to hold on vectors in $W$. Invariance ensures that the value $Tw$ lies in $W$, and the restriction inherits linearity from $T$. The operator norm hypothesis therefore applies to a linear map from a finite-dimensional inner product space to itself. If $W\ne\{0\}$, [math:thm-operator-norm-is-the-largest-singular-value](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-operator-norm-is-the-largest-singular-value) gives attainment of the maximum over the unit vectors of $W$, including when $T|_W=0$. If $W=\{0\}$, [math:def-operator-norm-on-a-finite-dimensional-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-operator-norm-on-a-finite-dimensional-inner-product-space) sets the norm of the restriction to zero. The denominator $\langle f,f\rangle=|O|$ is positive since the diagonal sum contributes one per element of $O$, and $\langle f,Tf\rangle$ is real by self-adjointness and conjugate symmetry. Likewise $\langle T\mathbf1,\mathbf1\rangle=d|Y|$ is real, so the scalar $d$ is necessarily real.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] For a finite set the cardinality is a natural number, nonzero when the set is nonempty ([math:def-finite-cardinality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-cardinality)). As a real scalar it is the corresponding sum of ones ([math:def-canonical-natural](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-canonical-natural)), positive for a nonempty set ([math:lem-of-naturals-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-naturals-positive) in [math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)). Its image in $\mathbb C$ is the corresponding sum of complex ones ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)).

[F2] The counting pairing is linear in its first variable, conjugate-linear in its second, conjugate symmetric and positive definite. The induced norm is the nonnegative square root of the diagonal pairing ([math:def-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inner-product-space), [math:def-inner-product-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inner-product-norm)).

[F3] A linear map preserves linear combinations, and self-adjointness identifies $T$ with its adjoint, whose defining identity gives $\langle Tu,v\rangle=\langle u,Tv\rangle$ ([math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map), [math:def-self-adjoint-and-normal-endomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-self-adjoint-and-normal-endomorphism), [math:def-adjoint-of-a-linear-map-between-inner-product-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-adjoint-of-a-linear-map-between-inner-product-spaces)).

[F4] A linear subspace inherits the vector operations, and invariance $T(W)\subseteq W$ gives the restriction $T|_W:W\to W$ ([math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace), [math:def-invariant-subspace-and-induced-quotient-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invariant-subspace-and-induced-quotient-operator)).

[F5] On a nonzero finite-dimensional inner product space, the operator norm is the maximum of $\|Tw\|$ over vectors of norm one. On the zero space it is $0$ ([math:def-operator-norm-on-a-finite-dimensional-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-operator-norm-on-a-finite-dimensional-inner-product-space)).

[F6] Cauchy–Schwarz gives $|\langle u,v\rangle|\le\|u\|\|v\|$ for vectors in a real or complex inner product space ([math:thm-cauchy-schwarz-for-real-and-complex-inner-product-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-cauchy-schwarz-for-real-and-complex-inner-product-spaces)). The bars denote real absolute value on $\mathbb R$ ([math:def-abs-value](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-abs-value)) and complex modulus on $\mathbb C$ ([math:def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus)).

[F7] Orthogonality of two vectors means their inner product vanishes ([math:def-orthogonal-vectors-sets-and-orthonormal-bases](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-orthogonal-vectors-sets-and-orthonormal-bases)).

[F8] A sum indexed by a finite set in an additive commutative monoid can be evaluated in any enumeration ([math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid)).

[F9] In the ordered real field ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)), every real number $r$ satisfies $r\le |r|_{\mathbb R}$ ([math:lem-of-abs-value](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-abs-value)). When $r$ is viewed in $\mathbb C$, its complex modulus equals $|r|_{\mathbb R}$ ([math:lem-complex-modulus-of-real-embedding-equals-real-absolute-value](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-modulus-of-real-embedding-equals-real-absolute-value)).

### Proof

**Given:** $Y,O,f,\mu,h$ as in the statement and, for the bound, $T,d,W,L$ satisfying its hypotheses.

**Goal:** Show that $h$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf1$ with $\|h\|^2=|O|(1-\mu)$, then establish the quotient bound.

1.1 In the counting sums, $f$ takes value $1$ at the $|O|$ indices in $O$ and $0$ at every other index. Discarding the zero terms leaves sums of ones, which give the scalar counts by [F1]. Thus $\langle f,\mathbf1\rangle=\langle f,f\rangle=|O|$ and $\langle\mathbf1,\mathbf1\rangle=|Y|$. Linearity gives $\langle h,\mathbf1\rangle=|O|-\mu|Y|=0$. Therefore $h$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf1$, and conjugate symmetry gives $\langle\mathbf1,h\rangle=0$. [given, F1, F2, F7, F8]

2.1 In $\langle f,f\rangle=\langle h+\mu\mathbf1,h+\mu\mathbf1\rangle$ both cross terms vanish by step 1.1. Since $\mu$ is real, $\|h\|^2=|O|-\mu^2|Y|=|O|(1-\mu)$. [step 1.1, F2, algebra]

2.2 Self-adjointness and $T\mathbf1=d\mathbf1$ give $\langle\mathbf1,Th\rangle=\langle T\mathbf1,h\rangle=d\langle\mathbf1,h\rangle=0$ and $\langle h,T\mathbf1\rangle=d\langle h,\mathbf1\rangle=0$. Expanding $\langle f,Tf\rangle$ using $f=h+\mu\mathbf1$ now gives $\langle f,Tf\rangle=d\mu^2|Y|+\langle h,Th\rangle=d\mu|O|+\langle h,Th\rangle$. Also $\langle h,Th\rangle=\langle Th,h\rangle=\overline{\langle h,Th\rangle}$, so the centered pairing is real. [given, step 1.1, F2, F3, algebra]

3.1 For real $a\ge0$ and $v\in V$, sesquilinearity gives $\|av\|^2=a^2\|v\|^2$, so $\|av\|=a\|v\|$ by uniqueness of the nonnegative square root. If $h=0$, then $Th=0$ by linearity and $\|Th\|\le L\|h\|$. If $h\ne0$, then $s=\|h\|>0$ by positive definiteness. The vector $w=h/s$ belongs to $W$ and the scaling identity gives $\|w\|=\|h\|/s=1$. Thus $\|Tw\|\le L$, and $Th=sTw$ by linearity. Scaling gives $\|Th\|=s\|Tw\|\le L\|h\|$. In both cases, write $r=\langle h,Th\rangle$, which is real by step 2.2. For $\mathbb K=\mathbb R$, the bars in [F6] give $|r|_{\mathbb R}$. For $\mathbb K=\mathbb C$, they give the modulus of the embedded real $r$, which equals $|r|_{\mathbb R}$ by [F9]. In either case [F9] gives $r\le|r|_{\mathbb R}$. Applying Cauchy–Schwarz to $h,Th\in V$ gives $|r|_{\mathbb R}\le\|h\|\|Th\|\le L\|h\|^2$. [given, step 2.2, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F9]

4.1 Substitute the bound from step 3.1 and the norm identity from step 2.1 into step 2.2. Dividing by the positive number $|O|$ yields $\langle f,Tf\rangle/|O|\le d\mu+L(1-\mu)$. If $O=Y$, then $h=0$ and $\mu=1$, so the identity in step 2.2 gives equality. Together with steps 1.1 and 2.1 this proves all assertions. [F1, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 3.1] ∎


## Local Hamilton algebras and order completions

[Open item](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions) · level 0


### Definition

In the real quaternions, let $B=\mathbb Q1+\mathbb Qi+\mathbb Qj+\mathbb Qk$ be the rational Hamilton algebra. Its basis satisfies $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$ and $ij=k=-ji$, with the other products obtained by cyclic permutation of $i,j,k$. An order $O$ in $B$ is a subset containing $1$ and closed under subtraction and multiplication, with a $\mathbb Z$-basis $b_0,\ldots,b_3$ that is also a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$.

For a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $R$, write $B_R=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}R$ for the algebra with the same basis and multiplication rules over $R$. For $x=x_0+x_1i+x_2j+x_3k$ in $B_R$, write $\bar x=x_0-x_1i-x_2j-x_3k$ and call $N(x)=\sum_{r=0}^3x_r^2$ its reduced norm. A superscript $\times$ denotes the group of elements with a two-sided multiplicative inverse in the indicated ring.

For each prime $\ell$, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the completion of $\mathbb Q$ in the $\ell$-adic metric $d_\ell(a,b)=|a-b|_\ell$. Let $\mathbb Z_\ell$ be its ring of integers, the ball $|a|_\ell\le 1$ for the extended absolute value. The local algebra is $B_\ell=B_{\mathbb Q_\ell}$, and the local order is $O_\ell=O\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Z_\ell=\sum_{r=0}^3\mathbb Z_\ell b_r$ inside $B_\ell$. Give $B_\ell$ the product topology of its four Hamilton coordinates, giving each copy of $\mathbb Q_\ell$ the topology induced by the extended metric. Give $B_\ell^\times$ the induced subspace topology.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The real quaternions have the stated multiplication table and satisfy the unital ring axioms ([math:def-quaternions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quaternions), [math:thm-quaternions-form-a-division-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quaternions-form-a-division-ring), [math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring)). The rationals embed as a subfield of the reals ([math:lem-rat-embeds-dense](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-rat-embeds-dense)). A subset containing the ambient identity and closed under subtraction and multiplication is a unital subring ([math:lem-subring-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-subring-criterion)). A basis gives unique coordinates ([math:def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis)). Over a field, an abelian additive group with a scalar action that distributes over both additions, respects scalar multiplication and has the field identity act as the identity is a vector space ([math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space)).

[F2] The integers form a commutative unital ring and embed in the rationals with their usual arithmetic and identity ([math:thm-int-comm-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-int-comm-ring), [math:lem-int-embeds-rat](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-int-embeds-rat)). A unital algebra over a commutative ring has a central scalar map ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)). Every field is a commutative ring with the same operations and identity, and each nonzero element is a unit ([math:lem-field-is-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-field-is-a-commutative-ring), [math:def-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-commutative-ring)). A ring homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication, and the multiplicative identity ([math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism)). Unital left and right modules have an abelian additive group and a distributive, associative, unital scalar action on the indicated side ([math:def-left-and-right-modules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-and-right-modules), [math:def-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group)). Pure tensors generate the tensor product, add in each variable, and allow base scalars to move between the two factors ([math:def-tensor-product-of-modules-by-generators-and-relations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-tensor-product-of-modules-by-generators-and-relations)). A pairing $q$ from a right and a left module into an abelian group is balanced if it is additive in each variable and satisfies $q(ak,s)=q(a,ks)$ for each base scalar $k$ ([math:def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps)). Such a pairing induces a unique additive map on the tensor product ([math:prop-elementary-tensor-formulas-descend-exactly-when-balanced](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-elementary-tensor-formulas-descend-exactly-when-balanced)). For unital algebras over a commutative base ring, $(a\otimes s)(b\otimes t)=ab\otimes st$ and the unit is $1\otimes1$ ([math:thm-tensor-product-of-algebras-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tensor-product-of-algebras-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F3] For a prime $\ell$, the rational absolute value is $|a|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(a)}$ for $a\ne 0$ and $|0|_\ell=0$ ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime), [math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)). For a nonzero integer $m$, the exponent of $\ell$ in $m$ is the greatest nonnegative integer $e$ for which $\ell^e$ divides $m$ ([math:def-p-adic-valuation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-valuation)). For a nonzero rational $a=u/v$ with nonzero integers $u,v$, the difference of their exponents, taken in $\mathbb Z$, defines $v_\ell(a)$ independently of the chosen representation ([math:lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q)). The integer valuations of $1$ and $-1$ are zero ([math:lem-p-adic-valuation-basic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-basic), assertion 5), and the rational valuation agrees with the integer valuation on nonzero integers ([math:lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q), assertion 2). On $\mathbb Q$, this absolute value is multiplicative and satisfies $|a+b|_\ell\le\max(|a|_\ell,|b|_\ell)$ ([math:thm-p-adic-absolute-value-is-nonarchimedean](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-absolute-value-is-nonarchimedean)). A completion of a metric space is a complete metric space equipped with a dense isometric embedding of the original space ([math:def-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-space), [math:def-metric-completion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-completion)). The metric completion is a field extending $\mathbb Q$, with the extended multiplicative absolute value satisfying $|a+b|_\ell\le\max(|a|_\ell,|b|_\ell)$ ([math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers), [math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)). Addition of Cauchy classes is termwise. For $a=[(a_n)]$ and $b=[(b_n)]$, the completed distance is $\widehat d_\ell(a,b)=\lim_n|a_n-b_n|_\ell$ and the extended absolute value is $|a|_\ell=\lim_n|a_n|_\ell$ ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field), proof step 1.1). The completed metric separates points ([math:def-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-space)). A submodule is an additive subgroup closed under scalars. On the quotient by a submodule $U$ of a $\mathbb Z$-module $M$, the scalar action on cosets is $k(m+U)=km+U$ for $k\in\mathbb Z$ ([math:def-submodule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-submodule), [math:def-quotient-module](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-module), [math:thm-quotient-module-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-module-laws)). Two residue classes modulo a positive integer $q$ are equal exactly when their representatives differ by a multiple of $q$ ([math:def-integers-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integers-modulo-n), [math:def-congruence-modulo-an-integer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-congruence-modulo-an-integer)). The $(\ell)$-adic completion of $\mathbb Z$ is the inverse limit of the residue modules $\mathbb Z/\ell^n\mathbb Z$, consisting of tuples compatible under reduction ([math:def-adic-completion-of-a-module](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-adic-completion-of-a-module), [math:def-inverse-system-and-inverse-limit-of-modules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inverse-system-and-inverse-limit-of-modules)). The residue modules are the additive modules of the commutative quotient rings, where the sum and product of two classes are the classes of the corresponding integer sum and product ([math:prop-integers-modulo-n-as-a-quotient-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-integers-modulo-n-as-a-quotient-ring), [math:def-addition-and-multiplication-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-addition-and-multiplication-modulo-n)). The comparison with its fraction field identifies the completed integer ring with the unit ball in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp), [The valuation ring of the p-adic field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-zp-is-the-valuation-ring-of-qp)). A sequence $(x_j)_j$ in a metric space $(X,d)$ is Cauchy if for each rational $\varepsilon>0$ there is $J$ with $d(x_j,x_k)<\varepsilon$ whenever $j,k\ge J$ ([math:def-cauchy-in-metric](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-cauchy-in-metric)). In the Cauchy-sequence construction the isometric embedding sends $a$ to the class of the constant sequence at $a$, as in assertion 4 of [math:thm-metric-completion-exists](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-completion-exists), for the construction selected by [math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers). The comparison sends an $\ell$-adically Cauchy integer sequence to its eventual residues at each modulus ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp)).

[F4] Units in a unital ring are the elements with a two-sided inverse and form a group under multiplication ([math:def-invertible-element](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-element), [math:lem-ring-units-form-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group)). The subgroup criterion uses the shared identity and closure under the ambient multiplication and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)). Matrices over a commutative ring have entries indexed by row and column, multiply by the row-by-column rule, and have the diagonal identity matrix in square size ([math:def-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring), [math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose)). A square matrix is invertible when it has a two-sided matrix inverse ([math:def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring)). In positive size this holds exactly when its determinant, the Leibniz sum of signed products of entries, is a unit ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix), [math:cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit)). For two ordered bases of a finite-dimensional vector space, the matrix of target coordinates of source basis vectors converts coordinate columns, and the reverse matrix is its inverse ([math:def-change-of-basis-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-change-of-basis-matrix), [math:thm-change-of-basis-matrices-are-inverses](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-change-of-basis-matrices-are-inverses)).

[F5] A set in a metric topology is open if each of its points has a ball contained in the set, where the open ball of radius $r>0$ at $x$ consists of the points at distance strictly less than $r$ from $x$ ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)). Metric open sets form a topology and open balls are open ([math:thm-metric-open-set-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-open-set-algebra)). In any topology finite intersections and arbitrary unions of open sets are open ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)). The product topology has a basis of boxes restricting finitely many coordinates ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)). Every product-open set is a union of these basic boxes ([math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)). The subspace topology takes traces of ambient open sets ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). Continuity is equivalent to taking open sets to open sets under inverse image, equivalently taking closed sets to closed sets under inverse image ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top)). For these equivalences the codomain topology itself serves as a subbasis, since it generates itself ([math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)).

[F6] The principal ideal $(\ell)$ is the smallest two-sided ideal containing $\ell$, where a two-sided ideal is an additive subgroup stable under multiplication from both sides ([math:def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal), [math:def-generated-and-principal-ideals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-generated-and-principal-ideals)). The product of two ideals consists of finite sums of products of their elements, and $IM$ for an ideal $I$ and module $M$ consists of finite sums of scalar products $im$ ([math:def-sum-and-product-of-ideals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sum-and-product-of-ideals), [math:def-product-of-an-ideal-and-a-module](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-of-an-ideal-and-a-module)).

[F7] If a property $P(k)$ of the nonnegative integers holds at $k=0$ and $P(k)$ implies $P(k+1)$ for every $k\ge0$, it holds for every $k\ge0$ ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

### Justification

**Given:** An order $O$ as in the definition, a prime $\ell$, and a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $R$.

1.1 Inside the real quaternions the rational coordinates are closed under subtraction and the Hamilton multiplication formula. The rational span also contains $1$, so it inherits the unital ring structure by the subring criterion. The coordinate formula makes the map $a\mapsto a1$ into $B$ a unital homomorphism with central image, giving the $\mathbb Q$-algebra. The action $(a,x)\mapsto(a1)x$ is coordinatewise multiplication by $a$. Componentwise field arithmetic makes addition abelian, the action distributive over both additions and associative with scalar multiplication, and $1$ acts as the identity. Thus $B$ is a $\mathbb Q$-vector space, with basis $1,i,j,k$ by uniqueness of the four coordinates. The same subring criterion applies to $O$ by its defining conditions. The integer scalar map $m\mapsto m1$ takes values in $O$, and the ring laws give preservation of addition, multiplication and $1$, with central image. This gives its $\mathbb Z$-algebra structure. [F1, F2, given]

1.2 For rational $a,b,c$, the formula in [F3] gives $d_\ell(a,b)\ge0$, with equality exactly when $a=b$. The valuation agreement in [F3] gives rational valuation zero at $1$ and $-1$, so $|1|_\ell=|-1|_\ell=1$. Multiplicativity gives $|b-a|_\ell=|-1|_\ell|a-b|_\ell=|a-b|_\ell$. Since $a-c=(a-b)+(b-c)$, the rational strong triangle inequality bounds $d_\ell(a,c)$ by $\max(d_\ell(a,b),d_\ell(b,c))$, which is at most $d_\ell(a,b)+d_\ell(b,c)$. Thus $(\mathbb Q,d_\ell)$ satisfies the metric hypothesis of the Cauchy-sequence construction in [F3]. Let $I=(\ell)\subseteq\mathbb Z$. The multiples $\ell\mathbb Z$ form an additive subgroup stable under multiplication by integers and contain $\ell$. Every ideal containing $\ell$ contains all its integer multiples, so $I=\ell\mathbb Z$ by [F6]. If $I^n=\ell^n\mathbb Z$, each product of an element of $I^n$ and one of $I$ is a multiple of $\ell^{n+1}$, as is any finite sum of such products. Conversely, $\ell^{n+1}m=(\ell^nm)\ell$ belongs to $I^nI$ for every integer $m$, so $I^{n+1}=I^nI=\ell^{n+1}\mathbb Z$. Apply [F7] to $P(k)$ given by $I^{k+1}=\ell^{k+1}\mathbb Z$. The calculation of $I$ gives $P(0)$ and the product calculation with $n=k+1$ gives $P(k)\Rightarrow P(k+1)$. Hence $I^n=\ell^n\mathbb Z$ for every $n\ge1$. Multiplication of $I^n$ by the module $\mathbb Z$ leaves it unchanged, since sums of integer multiples stay in the ideal and each element is its own product with $1$. The multiples of $\ell^n$ are closed under subtraction and multiplication by integers, so $\ell^n\mathbb Z$ is a $\mathbb Z$-submodule of $\mathbb Z$. The quotients $\mathbb Z/I^n\mathbb Z$ in the adic completion are therefore $\mathbb Z/\ell^n\mathbb Z$. The reduction $\rho_n$ from modulus $\ell^{n+1}$ to $\ell^n$ sends the class of an integer to its class modulo $\ell^n$. Equality of classes at the higher modulus means their representatives differ by a multiple of $\ell^{n+1}$, hence also by a multiple of $\ell^n$, giving equality at the lower modulus. It preserves sums, products, and $1$ since the residue operations are computed on integer representatives and the class of $1$ is the identity. Writing $[a]_m$ for the class of $a$ modulo $m$, for every $k\in\mathbb Z$ the quotient scalar rule gives $\rho_n(k[a]_{\ell^{n+1}})=[ka]_{\ell^n}=k\rho_n([a]_{\ell^{n+1}})$. Thus the reductions are $\mathbb Z$-linear transition maps. Thus the compatible tuples form a commutative unital ring under componentwise operations. For each integer $m$, the rational sequence with every term equal to $m$ is Cauchy because the distance between any two terms is zero. Under the canonical comparison of [F3], the constant Cauchy sequence of any integer $m$ maps to $(m\bmod\ell^n)_{n\ge1}$, since these residues are already constant at each modulus. Consequently the integer scalar map to the completed integer ring agrees, inside $\mathbb Q_\ell$, with the composite of the integer embedding in $\mathbb Q$ and the constant-sequence embedding. [F2, F3, F6, F7, given]

2.1 Negating a rational Cauchy sequence preserves all its termwise distances by step 1.2. Since addition of classes is termwise and a sequence plus its negative is the zero sequence, $a-b=[(a_n-b_n)]$ for the representatives in [F3]. The two limit formulas therefore give $\widehat d_\ell(a,b)=|a-b|_\ell$. In particular $|a|_\ell=\widehat d_\ell(a,0)>0$ for $a\ne0$, by nonnegativity of the absolute value and metric separation. An element of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is a unit there exactly when its absolute value is $1$, since $|1|_\ell=1$ extends from the rationals and multiplicativity gives $|a^{-1}|_\ell=1/|a|_\ell$ for $a\ne0$. An increment of absolute value less than $1$ preserves membership in each of $\{|a|_\ell\le1\}$ and $\{|a|_\ell=1\}$, by applying the ultrametric inequality in both directions. The radius-one open ball about $a$ consists of $a+h$ with $|h|_\ell<1$ and therefore lies on the same side of each membership test as $a$. Both sets and both complements are thus open by the metric criterion, so the sets are open and closed. [F3, F5, step 1.2]

2.2 The fields $\mathbb Q$ and $\mathbb Q_\ell$ are commutative rings by [F2], and the field inclusion $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb Q_\ell$ gives the latter its $\mathbb Q$-algebra structure. Use the pairs $(B,R)$ and $(B,\mathbb Q_\ell)$ over $\mathbb Q$, and $(O,\mathbb Z_\ell)$ over $\mathbb Z$. In the last pair give $\mathbb Z_\ell$ the integer scalar map of step 1.2. For any of these pairs write $(A,S,K)$ for the two factors and base. On each first factor use the right action $ak=a\eta_A(k)$, where $\eta_A$ is its central scalar map. On each second factor use the left action $ks=\eta_S(k)s$ from its scalar map. The ring laws and unital homomorphisms give the left and right module axioms on the additive groups of the rings. Apply the tensor-algebra rule. In each tensor the map $s\mapsto 1\otimes s$ is additive by the tensor relations, and is unital and multiplicative by the pure-tensor multiplication rule. It is central since $S$ is commutative and pure tensors generate. The resulting $S$-scalar action is multiplication on the second factor. Write $(u_r)$ for the basis of $A$ and $c_r:A\to K$ for its coefficient maps. The tensor relations expand $a\otimes s$ as $\sum_r u_r\otimes c_r(a)s$, so the $u_r\otimes1$ span over $S$. Each pairing $(a,s)\mapsto c_r(a)s$ takes values in the abelian additive group of $S$ and is additive in both variables. It is balanced because $c_r(ak)s=c_r(a)(ks)$ for $k\in K$. The descended map sends $\sum_j u_j\otimes s_j$ to $s_r$, proving uniqueness of all four coefficients. The pure-tensor multiplication rule gives the same multiplication constants in each extended basis. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1, step 1.2]

3.1 The pairing $(o,z)\mapsto z(o\otimes1)$ from $O\times\mathbb Z_\ell$ to the abelian additive group of $B_\ell$, using the copy of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ in $\mathbb Q_\ell$, is additive in both variables. It is balanced over $\mathbb Z$ because the two integer actions agree by step 1.2, so $z((om)\otimes1)=(mz)(o\otimes1)$ for every integer $m$. Its descended map is unital and multiplicative on pure tensors by centrality of $z$ and the tensor multiplication rule, hence unital and multiplicative on the entire tensor. Its image is the displayed $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span. Let $P$ be the rational matrix whose columns are the Hamilton coordinates of the $b_r$, and let $T$ be the rational matrix whose columns are the order coordinates of the Hamilton basis vectors. The Hamilton basis and $(b_r)$ are ordered bases of the four-dimensional $\mathbb Q$-vector space $B$, so $PT=TP=I_4$ by [F4]. The embedding $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb Q_\ell$ preserves the sums, products, $0$ and $1$ in these matrix identities, so the same matrices are inverses over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. By step 2.2, the image of a tensor with column of order coefficients $z$ has Hamilton coordinate column $Pz$. Since $T$ inverts $P$, that image vanishes only when the column $z$ is zero, so the map is injective. The image is therefore a unital subring of $B_\ell$. [F1, F2, F3, F4, step 1.2, step 2.2]

3.2 The multiplication table gives $\overline{xy}=\bar y\bar x$ by bilinearity. For basis pairs involving $1$ or two equal imaginary units the two sides agree directly. For distinct imaginary units their product changes sign both under conjugation and under reversal of factors. Expanding $x\bar x$ or $\bar x x$, the cross terms cancel and each square contributes $x_r^2$, giving $x\bar x=\bar x x=N(x)1$. Thus $(xy)\overline{xy}=x(y\bar y)\bar x=N(y)N(x)1$. Comparing the coefficients of $1$ gives $N(xy)=N(x)N(y)$. If $N(x)$ is a unit of $R$, the displayed identity gives the two-sided inverse $\bar x/N(x)$. Conversely, if $x$ is a unit, applying the norm to $xx^{-1}=1$ gives $N(x)N(x^{-1})=1$. Over a field of coefficients, this criterion is $N(x)\ne 0$. [F1, F2, F4, step 2.2]

4.1 For $x\in O_\ell$, let the $j$-th column of $L_x$ be the $b_r$-coordinates of $xb_j$. This is a $4\times4$ matrix over the commutative ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and acts on coordinate columns by left multiplication. The row-by-column rule composes the actions, so associativity gives $L_{xy}=L_xL_y$ and $L_1=I_4$. If $x$ is a unit in $O_\ell$, $L_{x^{-1}}$ thus inverts $L_x$. Conversely, invertibility of $L_x$ gives $y\in O_\ell$ with $xy=1$ by applying the inverse matrix to the coordinate column of $1$. Then $x(yx-1)=0$, so injectivity of $L_x$ gives $yx=1$. Hence $O_\ell^\times$ is specified by $\det L_x\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$. If $a\in\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $a1\in O_\ell$, the integral matrix $L_{a1}=aI_4$ forces $a\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. For a scalar unit of $O_\ell$, applying this to the inverse also gives $a^{-1}\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. Conversely, $a,a^{-1}\in\mathbb Z_\ell$ give $a1,a^{-1}1\in O_\ell$ since it contains $1$ and is a $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module. Thus $O_\ell^\times\cap(\mathbb Q_\ell^\times 1)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times 1$. [F3, F4, step 2.2, step 3.1]

5.1 For continuity of the field operations at $(a,b)$, a pair of increments $h,k$ changes $a+b$ by at most $\max(|h|_\ell,|k|_\ell)$ in absolute value, and changes $ab$ by at most $\max(|a|_\ell|k|_\ell,|b|_\ell|h|_\ell,|h|_\ell|k|_\ell)$. Given an output radius $\varepsilon>0$, set $M=\max(1,|a|_\ell,|b|_\ell)$ and $\delta=\min(1,\varepsilon/M)$. By the completed distance formula in step 2.1, the box $|h|_\ell,|k|_\ell<\delta$ is an open neighbourhood of $(a,b)$ in increment coordinates. It keeps both changes below $M\delta\le\varepsilon$. Thus addition and multiplication are continuous. Coordinate projections are continuous because their open preimages are boxes, and constant functions have either full or empty preimages of open sets. For continuous coordinate functions the preimage of a basic box is a finite intersection of open coordinate preimages. Each open set in the target product is a union of basic boxes by [F5]. Its preimage under the tuple is the union of the corresponding open preimages, so it is open and the tuple is continuous. Compositions are continuous because preimages under a composition are successive preimages. Polynomials are formed by finite compositions of addition and multiplication applied to coordinate projections and constants, so they are continuous. In particular, the linear coordinate maps given by $P$ and $T$ from step 3.1 are continuous in both directions between Hamilton and order coordinates. The order-coordinate map has $O_\ell$ as the preimage of the open box $\mathbb Z_\ell^4$, so $O_\ell$ is open in $B_\ell$. Each entry of $L_x$ is linear in the order coordinates of $x$, by the products of basis elements in step 2.2. The Leibniz formula therefore makes $\det L_x$ a polynomial in those coordinates. The preimage of $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ under this determinant on $O_\ell$ is open and closed in $O_\ell$. [F3, F4, F5, step 2.2, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 2.1]

6.1 The Hamilton product has polynomial coordinates and is continuous. For $z\ne0$ the open ball about $z$ of radius $|z|_\ell>0$ excludes $0$ by step 2.1, so $\mathbb Q_\ell\setminus\{0\}$ is open. The units of $B_\ell$ are its preimage under the continuous norm $N$ by step 3.2, so the unit set is open. Given $z\ne0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, take $\delta=\min(|z|_\ell,\varepsilon|z|_\ell^2)>0$. In the open ball $|w-z|_\ell<\delta$ of the completed metric, the ultrametric inequality in both directions gives $|w|_\ell=|z|_\ell$, so $w\ne0$. Thus $|z^{-1}-w^{-1}|_\ell=|z-w|_\ell/(|z|_\ell|w|_\ell)<\varepsilon$. This proves continuity of reciprocation, and the inverse formula $\bar x/N(x)$ is then continuous in each coordinate on the unit set. Products and inverses take values in that set by the ring-unit lemma. Basic boxes for pairs of units are traces of ambient basic boxes, so multiplication remains continuous on the product of the unit subspaces. For each operation the preimage of an open set in the unit codomain is the preimage of an ambient open whose trace is that set, giving continuity into the unit subspace. Since $O_\ell$ is a unital subring, its units contain the common identity and are closed under ambient products and inverses. They form an open subgroup of $B_\ell^\times$, by the openness of $O_\ell$ and the open determinant preimage in step 5.1. [F3, F4, F5, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 3.2, step 5.1] ∎


## Spherical Hecke algebra and action for a compact open subgroup

[Open item](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra) · level 0


### Definition

Let $G$ be a Hausdorff topological group, a Hausdorff space with a group law and inversion both continuous, and let $K$ be a compact open subgroup. Write $G/K$ for the set of cosets $xK=\{xk:k\in K\}$. The spherical Hecke algebra $\mathcal H(G,K)$ consists of complex functions $f$ invariant on both sides under $K$, meaning $f(kxk')=f(x)$ for $k,k'\in K$, and zero outside a compact subset. It has pointwise vector-space operations over the field $\mathbb C$, unit $\mathbf 1_K$ (one on $K$, zero elsewhere), and multiplication
$$(f*h)(z)=\sum_{xK\in G/K} f(x)h(x^{-1}z).$$
The sum uses only the finitely many cosets where $f$ is nonzero. The scalar structure map is $c\mapsto c\mathbf 1_K$. When a left Haar measure assigns $K$ volume one, this multiplication is convolution for that measure. Here a left Haar measure is a nonzero left-translation-invariant Borel measure, finite on compact sets, outer regular on Borel sets and inner regular on open sets.

A complex representation on a complex vector space $V$ is a group homomorphism $\pi$ from $G$ to the group of invertible complex-linear maps $V\to V$ under composition. The fixed subspace is $V^K=\{w\in V:\pi(k)w=w\text{ for all }k\in K\}$. The Hecke operator of $f$ on this space is
$$T_f w=\sum_{xK\in G/K} f(x)\pi(x)w.$$
Again the sum uses only the cosets where $f$ is nonzero. The assignment $f\mapsto T_f$ gives a unital representation of the algebra on $V^K$. It takes values in $\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb C}(V^K)$, the complex-linear self-maps of $V^K$ with pointwise addition and scalar multiplication, composition as multiplication, and the identity map as unit.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] Left translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms. A homeomorphism and its inverse are continuous. The cosets $xK$ of a subgroup partition the group. Unions of open sets are open, complements of open sets are closed, and a closed set equals its closure ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion), [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps), [math:lem-coset-partition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-partition), [math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space), [math:def-interior-closure-boundary-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-interior-closure-boundary-top)).

[F2] Any cover of a compact subset by ambient open sets admits a finite subcover. A continuous image of a compact subset is compact. A space is locally compact if each point has a compact neighborhood. Continuity at a point follows if every open neighborhood of its image contains the image of an open neighborhood of the point. On $\mathbb C$ use the topology of the metric $d(z,w)=|z-w|$ ([math:lem-compactness-of-a-subspace-is-ambient](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compactness-of-a-subspace-is-ambient), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), [math:def-locally-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space), [math:def-neighbourhood-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-neighbourhood-top), [math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity), [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)).

[F3] The complex numbers form a field, and vector addition in a complex vector space is an abelian group operation. A unital ring has abelian-group addition, associative unital multiplication, and both distributive laws. A complex algebra is a unital ring with a unital structure map from $\mathbb C$ into its center. Finite sums in an additive commutative monoid are invariant under reindexing. Representatives of a finite family of nonempty sets can be picked by enumerating the family and using finite choice ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space), [math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring), [math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring), [math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism), [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)).

[F4] On a locally compact Hausdorff group with left Haar measure $\mu$, convolution of continuous compactly supported functions is $(f*h)(z)=\int_G f(x)h(x^{-1}z)\,d\mu(x)$. Borel sets include open sets, and left invariance gives $\mu(xK)=\mu(K)$ for Borel $K$. The regularity conditions on the Haar measure are the Radon conditions given in the definition. A Borel measure $\mu$ on $G$ means a measure on the Borel sigma-algebra $\mathcal B(G)$, so $(G,\mathcal B(G),\mu)$ is a measure space. ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure), [math:def-measure-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-space), [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space), [math:def-compactly-supported-convolution-on-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compactly-supported-convolution-on-a-group), [math:def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space), [math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra), [math:def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure)).

[F5] The Lebesgue integral of a nonnegative simple function is the sum of its coefficients times the measures of its pairwise disjoint measurable pieces. The integral of a real function is the difference of the integrals of its positive and negative parts when both are finite. The complex integral uses the integrals of the real and imaginary parts, with integrability determined by the integral of the modulus ([math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function), [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral), [math:def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals)).

[F6] A group homomorphism respects products and identity. A linear map respects linear combinations. A subset of a vector space is a linear subspace if it contains zero and is closed under linear combinations. A unital algebra representation on a vector space is a linear map to its linear endomorphisms preserving multiplication and unit ([math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism), [math:lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties), [math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map), [math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace), [math:def-unital-associative-algebra-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-unital-associative-algebra-representation)).

[F7] A group has an associative operation, a two-sided identity and two-sided inverses. In a group, $(ab)^{-1}=b^{-1}a^{-1}$. Invertibility of a linear map means it has a two-sided linear inverse. A subgroup contains the group identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group), [math:lem-group-inverse-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-inverse-laws), [math:def-linear-isomorphism-and-invertible-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-isomorphism-and-invertible-linear-map), [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F8] Finite sums in an additive commutative monoid split over disjoint index sets and can be interchanged on a product of finite index sets ([math:lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini)).

[F9] Every complex vector space is a left $\mathbb C$-module by the vector-space axioms. A map between such spaces is complex-linear exactly when it is additive and commutes with complex scalar multiplication, so the linear self-maps are the module endomorphisms. Linear self-maps form a complex vector space under pointwise operations and a unital ring under pointwise addition and composition, with identity the identity map ([math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space), [math:def-left-and-right-modules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-and-right-modules), [math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map), [math:def-module-homomorphism-kernel-image-and-cokernel](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-module-homomorphism-kernel-image-and-cokernel), [math:def-vector-space-of-linear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space-of-linear-maps), [math:prop-linear-maps-form-a-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-linear-maps-form-a-vector-space), [math:def-endomorphism-ring-of-a-module](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-endomorphism-ring-of-a-module), [math:prop-endomorphisms-form-a-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-endomorphisms-form-a-ring)).

[F10] Measurability means that preimages of measurable sets are measurable, using the Borel sets on the real, extended real, or complex codomain as applicable. A nonnegative simple measurable function is measurable and has finite range in $[0,\infty)$. On $\mathbb R$ use the metric topology of $d_{\mathbb R}(s,t)=|s-t|$. On $\overline{\mathbb R}=\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty,+\infty\}$ use the order topology generated by the open rays of the order extending the real order with $-\infty$ least and $+\infty$ greatest. For a complex value $a+ib$ with $a,b$ real its real part is $a$, imaginary part is $b$, and modulus is $\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$ ([math:def-measurable-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-space), [math:def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces), [math:def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function), [math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra), [math:def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function), [math:def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus), [math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space), clause 1, [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [math:def-extended-reals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-reals), [math:def-order-topology-on-a-linearly-ordered-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-order-topology-on-a-linearly-ordered-set)).

[F11] A finite set is in bijection with a finite initial segment of the natural numbers, so its elements admit a finite list without repetitions. Every subset of a finite set is finite. ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable), [math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set)).

### Justification

**Given:** $G,K$ and $\pi$ as in the definition. For the integral formula assume a left Haar measure $\mu$ with $\mu(K)=1$.

**Goal:** Verify the finite-sum algebra, its action and the normalization.

1.1 Each coset $xK$ is open because it is the preimage of $K$ under the continuous inverse of left translation by $x$. It is compact as a continuous image of $K$ under left translation. It is closed because its complement is a union of other open cosets. The coset $xK$ is a compact neighborhood of $x$, so $G$ is locally compact. By compactness, finitely many cosets cover any given compact subset of $G$. Disjointness implies that every coset meeting the subset belongs to this finite subcover. [F1, F2, given]

1.2 Distributivity gives $(0+0)a=0a+0a$ and $a(0+0)=a0+a0$ for $a\in\mathbb C$. The vector-space distributive laws give $(0+0)v=0v+0v$ and $a(0_V+0_V)=a0_V+a0_V$ for $v\in V$. In each equality the left side equals either summand on the right. Adding the additive inverse of that summand to both sides gives $0a=a0=0$ and $0v=a0_V=0_V$. For any complex-linear map $L:V\to V$, additivity gives $L(0_V)=L(0_V+0_V)=L(0_V)+L(0_V)$, so the same calculation gives $L(0_V)=0_V$. [F3, F6, F7]

2.1 Invertible complex-linear self-maps form a group under composition. The composite is linear and has the composite of the two inverses in reverse order as its inverse. Composition is associative, the identity is linear, and each inverse is linear by invertibility. The subset $V^K$ contains $0_V$ because each $\pi(k)$ is linear and sends $0_V$ to $0_V$ by step 1.2. If $u,v\in V^K$ then $\pi(k)(au+bv)=a\pi(k)u+b\pi(k)v=au+bv$ for all $k\in K$ and $a,b\in\mathbb C$. Thus $V^K$ is a linear subspace. [F6, F7, given, step 1.2]

2.2 Right $K$-invariance makes each input constant on every open coset. The cosets on which an input is nonzero form a subset of the finite set of cosets meeting a compact set outside which the input vanishes. This subset is finite. The coset through a point maps to the value at that point, so it maps into every open neighborhood of that value in $\mathbb C$ with its usual metric topology. This gives continuity. The nonzero set of each input is a finite union of compact closed cosets by step 1.1. A finite union of compact sets is compact because the union of finite subcovers of the individual sets is a finite subcover of the union. The nonzero set is closed, so it equals the support, defined as its closure. Thus the inputs meet the support and continuity requirements of the Haar convolution definition. [F1, F2, F4, F11, step 1.1]

3.1 Write $S_f$ for the finite set of cosets where $f$ is nonzero. For $k\in K$, replacing $x$ by $xk$ leaves $f(x)$ unchanged, sends $x^{-1}z$ to $(xk)^{-1}z=k^{-1}x^{-1}z$ and sends $\pi(x)w$ to $\pi(x)\pi(k)w=\pi(x)w$ for $w\in V^K$. Left $K$-invariance of $h$ thus gives representative independence for convolution, and fixedness of $w$ gives it for the action. In calculations we may choose representatives from the finite sets of contributing cosets. [F3, F6, F7, step 2.2]

4.1 Use the measure space $(G,\mathcal B(G),\mu)$ from [F4] and the measurable spaces $(G,\mathcal B(G))$ and $(Y,\mathcal B(Y))$ for $Y=\mathbb C$, $\mathbb R$, or $\overline{\mathbb R}$ as appropriate. Use the topology from [F2] on $\mathbb C$ and the topologies from [F10] on $\mathbb R$ and $\overline{\mathbb R}$. For fixed $z$, the Haar integrand is $\sum_{xK\in S_f} f(x)h(x^{-1}z)\mathbf 1_{xK}$, with disjoint Borel pieces of measure one. The integrand, its real and imaginary parts, their positive and negative parts, and the modulus are all constant on those pieces and zero on their Borel complement, which is open as a union of the other cosets. A preimage of a Borel set under any of these functions is a union of the pieces whose value belongs to that set, including the complement if the set contains zero. Each such union is open by [F1] and hence Borel. Thus all these functions are measurable. The modulus and the four nonnegative parts are simple with finite integrals because only the finitely many measure-one pieces can contribute. Applying the simple integral formula to the four parts, then subtracting and recombining using the finite sums, gives the complex integral $\sum_{xK\in S_f} f(x)h(x^{-1}z)\mu(xK)=(f*h)(z)$. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F10, step 1.1, step 2.2, step 3.1, step 1.2]

4.2 The zero function belongs to $\mathcal H(G,K)$. A linear combination of two inputs is $K$-invariant on both sides and vanishes outside the union of their two compact supports. This gives a vector space under the pointwise operations, with the vector-space laws holding at each point. In the multiplication formula, replacing $z$ by $zk$ for $k\in K$ leaves each term unchanged, and replacing $z$ by $kz$ reindexes the sum by $xK\mapsto k^{-1}xK$ using left invariance of $f$. For each representative $x$ in $S_f$, the term $f(x)h(x^{-1}z)$ vanishes outside $x\operatorname{supp}h$. The finite union of these translated supports is compact, giving closure under convolution. When combining two first inputs, take the union of their two coset sets as the common index set. Concatenate the two finite lists and discard repeated entries, keeping the first occurrence of each coset. The remaining finite list enumerates the common index set. Terms added to an individual sum have zero coefficient, so the disjoint splitting law preserves its value. Scalar multiplication and addition distribute through the finite sums by starting with the empty sum and using the binary distributive laws when adjoining each term. Applying this to each factor gives bilinearity. [F1, F2, F3, F7, F8, F11, step 2.2, step 3.1, step 1.2]

5.1 For $k\in K$, applying $\pi(k)$ to the term $f(x)\pi(x)w$ gives $f(kx)\pi(kx)w$ by left invariance and the representation law. By linearity applied successively to the finite sum, these transformed terms sum to $\pi(k)T_fw$. The map $xK\mapsto kxK$ permutes $S_f$, so the sum is fixed by $K$. The sum is linear in $w$ because each $\pi(x)$ is linear and scalar multiplication distributes through the sum by the same finite recursion. A linear combination of coefficients also distributes through the sum, using the union of the two finite sets of contributing cosets and extending the sums by zero as in step 4.2. The resulting identities hold at each $w\in V^K$, so $f\mapsto T_f$ is linear for the pointwise vector-space operations on $\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb C}(V^K)$. [F1, F3, F6, F8, F9, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 4.2]

5.2 For a third function $j$, both $(f*h)*j$ and $f*(h*j)$ at $z$ expand to $\sum_{xK\in S_f,\,yK\in S_h} f(x)h(y)j(y^{-1}x^{-1}z)$. In the first product fix representatives $x$ for $S_f$. Take the outer sum over the finite set $U=\bigcup_{xK\in S_f} xS_h$, where $xS_h=\{xyK:yK\in S_h\}$. To see that $U$ is finite, go through the finite list of $xK\in S_f$ and for each $x$ list the cosets $xyK$ for the finite list of $yK\in S_h$. Discard repetitions as in step 4.2 to obtain a finite list of $U$. Every expanded term vanishes outside $U$ because then $h(x^{-1}z')=0$ for each $x$ at the outer coset $z'K$. Expanding the coefficient and interchanging the sums over $U$ and $S_f$ makes $x$ the outer variable. For each fixed $x$, the terms for $z'K\in U\setminus xS_h$ vanish by the same test, so disjoint splitting restricts the inner sum to $xS_h$. Each term $f(x)h(x^{-1}z')j(z'^{-1}z)$ is independent of the representative $z'$ by right invariance of $h$ and left invariance of $j$, using the inverse product law. Thus reindex by $z'K=xyK$ and take $z'=xy$, so $(xy)^{-1}=y^{-1}x^{-1}$. For the second product the expansion already has $x$ as the outer variable. This proves associativity. [F1, F3, F7, F8, step 3.1, step 4.2, step 1.2]

6.1 For $k,k'\in K$, subgroup closure gives $kzk'\in K$ if $z\in K$. Conversely, if $kzk'\in K$, then $z=k^{-1}(kzk')(k')^{-1}\in K$ by closure under inverses and products. Hence $\mathbf 1_K$ is bi-$K$-invariant and it vanishes outside the compact set $K$, so $\mathbf 1_K\in\mathcal H(G,K)$. For $\mathbf 1_K*f$ the only contributing coset is $K$ and the representative $1\in K$ gives $f(z)$. For $f*\mathbf 1_K$, the condition $x^{-1}z\in K$ is equivalent to $z\in xK$. In one direction $k=x^{-1}z\in K$ gives $z=xk$. In the other direction $z=xk$ with $k\in K$ gives $x^{-1}z=k$. Since $z\in zK$ and the cosets partition $G$, the condition holds exactly when $xK=zK$. If $zK\in S_f$, the representative $z$ gives the value $f(z)$ and all other terms vanish. If $zK\notin S_f$, all terms vanish and $f(z)=0$. Pointwise addition is an abelian group law by step 4.2, convolution is associative by step 5.2, and its bilinearity supplies both distributive laws. Together with the two-sided unit these are the unital ring axioms. The unit and bilinearity give $(c\mathbf 1_K)*f=f*(c\mathbf 1_K)=cf$. The map $c\mapsto c\mathbf 1_K$ preserves addition by pointwise operations, preserves products by bilinearity and the unit law, and sends $1$ to the unit. It is therefore the central unital structure map inducing the pointwise scalars. [F1, F3, F7, F8, given, step 1.2, step 3.1, step 4.2, step 5.2]

7.1 In $T_{f*h}w$, sum over the same finite set $U$ as in step 5.2 and expand $f*h$. The additive group of $V$ admits finite-sum interchange, giving the outer sum over $S_f$. For each $x$, terms for cosets in $U\setminus xS_h$ vanish because $h(x^{-1}z')=0$. The expanded term at $z'K$ is independent of $z'$ by right invariance of $h$ and fixedness of $w$. Reindexing by $z'K=xyK$ now gives the terms $f(x)h(y)\pi(xy)w$. Since $T_hw$ is fixed, expanding $T_f(T_hw)$ gives these terms by $\pi(xy)=\pi(x)\pi(y)$, distributing each $\pi(x)$ through the finite linear combination by its linearity on binary combinations and iteration from $\pi(x)0=0$. Thus $T_{f*h}=T_f\circ T_h$ in the endomorphism ring. Also $T_{\mathbf 1_K}w=\pi(1)w=w$, so the unit maps to the identity endomorphism. With step 5.1 this proves the unital algebra action. [F3, F6, F8, F9, step 3.1, step 5.1, step 5.2, step 6.1, step 1.2] ∎


## Weight-two cusp forms with character at level N

[Open item](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form) · level 0


### Definition

Let $N$ be a positive integer and let $\epsilon$ be a Dirichlet character modulo $N$, a homomorphism from the invertible residue classes modulo $N$ to $\mathbb C^\times$ extended by zero on nonunits. Write $\mathfrak H=\{z\in\mathbb C:\operatorname{Im}z>0\}$ and $\Gamma_0(N)=\{\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z):N\mid c_\gamma\}$, where $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ consists of integral matrices of determinant one and $c_\gamma,d_\gamma$ are their lower left and lower right entries. For a real matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ with determinant $\det(A)=ad-bc>0$, use $Az=(az+b)/(cz+d)$ and the weight-two action $(f|_2 A)(z)=\det(A)(cz+d)^{-2}f(Az)$ on $\mathfrak H$.

For the boundary $\mathbb Q\cup\{\infty\}$, write $[r:s]$ for the class of a nonzero rational pair under multiplication by nonzero rational scalars. Identify $[r:s]$ with $r/s$ if $s\ne0$, and $[1:0]$ with $\infty$. For a rational matrix $A$ of positive determinant use $A[r:s]=[ar+bs:cr+ds]$. A cusp of $\Gamma_0(N)$ is an orbit $\{\gamma[r:s]:\gamma\in\Gamma_0(N)\}$ for this action.

The space $S_2(N,\epsilon)$ consists of holomorphic functions $f$ on $\mathfrak H$ satisfying $f|_2\gamma=\epsilon(d_\gamma)f$ for every $\gamma\in\Gamma_0(N)$ and vanishing at all cusps. The vanishing condition is that for every $\sigma\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ and every $\eta>0$, there is a real $Y$ such that $|(f|_2\sigma)(x+iy)|<\eta$ for all real $x$ and $y>Y$.
