At infinity write $q=\exp(2\pi i z)$ and $f(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n q^n$. The $a_n$ are its Fourier coefficients at infinity.

### Justification

Use the order-preserving embeddings $\mathbb Z\hookrightarrow\mathbb Q\hookrightarrow\mathbb R$ ([math:lem-int-embeds-rat](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-int-embeds-rat), [math:lem-rat-embeds-dense](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-rat-embeds-dense)). They preserve $0,1$, sums and products, so integral matrix products and determinants agree with their values in either larger field, and $N$ has positive real image.

For a rational matrix $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ with $\Delta=ad-bc\ne0$, set $J=\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$. Row-by-column multiplication gives $AJ=JA=\begin{pmatrix}ad-bc&0\\0&ad-bc\end{pmatrix}=\Delta I$ ([math:def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix)). Since $\mathbb Q$ is a field, $\Delta^{-1}$ is rational ([math:thm-rat-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-rat-field)). Pulling this scalar through the products gives $A(\Delta^{-1}J)=(\Delta^{-1}J)A=I$ ([math:thm-matrix-multiplication-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-matrix-multiplication-laws)). Thus $A^{-1}=\Delta^{-1}J$. For an integral matrix of determinant one the inverse is the integral matrix $J$.

The identity matrix lies in $\Gamma_0(N)$ since its lower left entry is zero. For $\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma_0(N)$, the lower left entry of $\gamma\delta$ is $c_\gamma a_\delta+d_\gamma c_\delta$ and that of $\gamma^{-1}$ is $-c_\gamma$ by the inverse formula. Both are divisible by $N$ by closure under integer linear combinations and negation ([math:lem-divisibility-basic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-divisibility-basic)). These are the three subgroup conditions for $\Gamma_0(N)\subseteq\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup), [math:def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane)).

Write $[u]_N$ for the class of $u$ modulo $N$. Two classes are equal exactly when $u\equiv v\pmod N$, meaning $N\mid(u-v)$ ([math:def-integers-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integers-modulo-n), [math:def-congruence-modulo-an-integer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-congruence-modulo-an-integer)). Multiplication of classes is $[u]_N[v]_N=[uv]_N$ ([math:def-addition-and-multiplication-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-addition-and-multiplication-modulo-n)). Since $a_\gamma d_\gamma-b_\gamma c_\gamma=1$ and $N\mid c_\gamma$, this gives $[a_\gamma]_N[d_\gamma]_N=[1]_N$. Thus $[d_\gamma]_N$ is a unit with inverse $[a_\gamma]_N$ ([math:def-unit-group-modulo-n-and-euler-totient](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-unit-group-modulo-n-and-euler-totient)). Because $N\ge1$, the class $[u]_N$ is a unit exactly when $\gcd(u,N)=1$ ([math:thm-unit-criterion-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unit-criterion-modulo-n)). The extension by zero on nonunits therefore gives the arithmetic function of [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q). The lower right entry of a product, $c_\gamma b_\delta+d_\gamma d_\delta$, gives $[d_{\gamma\delta}]_N=[d_\gamma]_N[d_\delta]_N$ by the same congruence arithmetic ([math:lem-congruence-respects-integer-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-congruence-respects-integer-arithmetic)). Thus $\gamma\mapsto[d_\gamma]_N$ is a group homomorphism to the units, and composing with $\epsilon$ gives the character $\gamma\mapsto\epsilon(d_\gamma)$ of the subgroup ([math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism), [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q)). In particular $\epsilon(1)=1$ because $\epsilon(1)=\epsilon(1)^2$ and $\epsilon(1)\ne0$.

The denominator $cz+d$ for a real matrix $A$ of positive determinant is nonzero on $\mathfrak H$. If $c\ne0$, its only zero is the real number $-d/c$. If $c=0$, then $ad>0$ gives $d\ne0$. These facts use the field operations and order on $\mathbb R$ ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)). The nonzero denominator therefore defines $(cz+d)^{-2}$ by the complex integer power rule ([math:def-complex-integer-powers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-integer-powers)). For $z=x+iy$, multiplying by the conjugate of the denominator gives $\operatorname{Im}((az+b)\overline{(cz+d)})=(ad-bc)y$. Thus $\operatorname{Im}(Az)=\det(A)\operatorname{Im}z/|cz+d|^2>0$, as in the determinant-one calculation of [math:def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane). The two permutations in the determinant formula give $\det(A)=ad-bc$ ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix)). Write $j(A,z)=cz+d$. Row-by-column multiplication ([math:def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix)) gives $A(Bz)=(AB)z$ and $j(AB,z)=j(A,Bz)j(B,z)$. Determinant multiplicativity gives $\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$ ([math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative)), which is positive by the real field order ([math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field)). Substitution in the slash formula gives $(f|_2 A)|_2 B=f|_2(AB)$, and the identity matrix acts as the identity on functions.

For $z\in\mathfrak H$ set $r=\operatorname{Im}z>0$. The coordinate formula for the complex metric gives $|\operatorname{Im}(w-z)|\le |w-z|=d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)$ ([math:def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity)). Every $w$ in the open ball $B(z,r)$ satisfies $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)<r$ ([math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)), so $\operatorname{Im}w\ge r-|\operatorname{Im}(w-z)|>0$. Thus $B(z,r)\subseteq\mathfrak H$, which is open by the metric definition ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)).

The affine functions $az+b$ and $cz+d$ are holomorphic by the constant, identity and linearity rules. The same derivative rules for quotients and products give holomorphy of $z\mapsto Az$ and $z\mapsto\det(A)(cz+d)^{-2}$ on $\mathfrak H$, where the denominator is nonzero ([math:thm-algebra-of-complex-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-algebra-of-complex-derivatives)). Since $A$ maps the open set $\mathfrak H$ into itself, the chain rule makes $f(Az)$ holomorphic there for holomorphic $f$ ([math:thm-chain-rule-for-complex-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-chain-rule-for-complex-derivatives)). The product rule now gives holomorphy of $f|_2 A$ on $\mathfrak H$.

The homogeneous-coordinate formula agrees with that in [math:def-real-projective-line-and-its-sl2-action](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-projective-line-and-its-sl2-action). For a rational $A$ of positive determinant, the inverse computed above has rational entries. If $A$ sent a nonzero rational column to zero, multiplication by $A^{-1}$ would give that the original column was zero. Rescaling the column rescales its image by the same factor. The positive determinant condition is preserved by products and inverses because $\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$ and $\det(A^{-1})=1/\det(A)$ ([math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative)). The identity fixes every class, and associativity of matrix multiplication gives $A(B[r:s])=(AB)[r:s]$ ([math:thm-matrix-multiplication-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-matrix-multiplication-laws)). This gives a left action on the rational classes ([math:def-group-action](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-action)) and hence the stated cusp orbits on restriction to $\Gamma_0(N)$. For a finite boundary point $x\in\mathbb Q$, choose integers $p,q$ with $q\ne0$ and $x=p/q$ ([math:def-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-rationals)), so $[p:q]$ represents $x$. Since $(p,q)\ne(0,0)$, the gcd $g=\gcd(p,q)$ is positive ([math:def-common-divisor-and-gcd](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-common-divisor-and-gcd)). The pair $(r,s)=(p/g,q/g)$ is integral and coprime by [math:cor-gcd-quotients-coprime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-gcd-quotients-coprime). Rescaling by $1/g$ gives $[r:s]=[p:q]$. Bézout's identity gives integers $u,v$ with $rv-su=1$ ([math:thm-bezout-identity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bezout-identity)), so $\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}r&u\\s&v\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ and $\sigma\infty=[r:s]=x$. The identity represents $\infty$. Thus every boundary point has a representative of the form $\sigma\infty$.

Put $U=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, an element of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$. By the group-power definition, the natural powers are the unique sequence starting at $I$ with each next term obtained by right multiplication by $U$ ([math:def-group-power](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-power)). The matrices $\begin{pmatrix}1&m\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ for $m\ge0$ satisfy this recursion because the first is $I$ and right multiplication by $U$ increases the upper right entry by one ([math:def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrix-product-and-identity-matrix)). For $m<0$, the group-power definition gives $U^m=(U^{-m})^{-1}$, whose upper right entry is $m$ by the determinant-one inverse formula above. Hence $U^m=\begin{pmatrix}1&m\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ for all integers $m$.

If $\sigma'\infty=\gamma\sigma\infty$ with $\gamma\in\Gamma_0(N)$, the integral determinant-one matrix $(\gamma\sigma)^{-1}\sigma'$ fixes $\infty$. Its lower left entry is zero. Its integral diagonal entries $a,d$ satisfy $ad=1$, so each divides $1$. Each is $1$ or $-1$ by [math:lem-units-of-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-units-of-z), and their product forces both to have the same value $s\in\{1,-1\}$. With upper right entry $b$, it equals $sU^{sb}$. It acts by $z\mapsto z+sb$ with weight factor one. The slash composition law and transformation condition give $(f|_2\sigma')(z)=\epsilon(d_\gamma)(f|_2\sigma)(z+sb)$. Multiplication by the fixed nonzero scalar and translation of real $x$ preserve uniform vanishing. This proves independence of the representative of the cusp.

At $m=N$ the power formula gives $U^N=\begin{pmatrix}1&N\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$. The congruence $U^N\equiv I\pmod N$ is entrywise and is preserved under products by congruence arithmetic. Thus $\gamma=\sigma U^N\sigma^{-1}$ is in $\Gamma_0(N)$ with $[d_\gamma]_N=[1]_N$. Since $\sigma U^N=\gamma\sigma$ and $\epsilon(d_\gamma)=1$, the slash composition law yields $(f|_2\sigma)|_2 U^N=f|_2\sigma$. The matrix $U^N$ acts by translation by $N$ with weight factor one.

Consequently $h(z)=(f|_2\sigma)(Nz)$ has period one. It is holomorphic by the chain rule because $z\mapsto Nz$ is holomorphic and maps $\mathfrak H$ into itself for $N>0$. The quantified vanishing condition gives a bound $|h(z)|<1$ on a whole upper half-plane by taking $\eta=1$ and $\operatorname{Im}z>Y/N$. Using all $\eta>0$ gives zero as the uniform limit there as $\operatorname{Im}z\to\infty$. By [math:thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp), the descended holomorphic function of $\exp(2\pi i z)$ extends to zero with value zero. Conversely, an extension with value zero gives uniform vanishing by the limit assertion of that theorem. Undoing the rescaling gives the expansion of $f|_2\sigma$ in $\exp(2\pi i z/N)$ with zero constant coefficient, and gives exponential decay as $\operatorname{Im}z\to\infty$. For $\sigma=I$ we can instead use period one, since $U\in\Gamma_0(N)$ has lower right entry $1$. The theorem then gives the unique expansion in $q$ and the coefficients of the definition. These expansions use complex power series ([math:def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence)), complex integer powers ([math:def-complex-integer-powers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-integer-powers)) and the constant $\pi$ ([math:def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero)).

The zero function satisfies the conditions. For $f,g\in S_2(N,\epsilon)$ and $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb C$, the pointwise combination $\alpha f+\beta g$ is holomorphic by linearity of the derivative. The slash formula is linear in the function, so the transformation law persists. For fixed $\sigma$ and $\eta>0$, take a common tail on which both transforms have modulus below $\eta/(1+|\alpha|+|\beta|)$. The triangle inequality and multiplicativity of modulus ([math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws)) bound the modulus of the transformed combination by $\eta$. Thus $S_2(N,\epsilon)$ is a complex vector space with pointwise sum and scalar multiplication, with the vector space axioms inherited pointwise from the field operations on $\mathbb C$ ([math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space), [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)).


## Fixed vectors for an action on one Hilbert tensor factor

[Open item](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-fixed-space-of-tensor-factor) · level 0


### Statement

Assume Countable Choice. Let $V,W$ be complex Hilbert spaces, complete spaces for the norms from their inner products, and let $\rho$ be a strongly continuous unitary representation of a topological group $L$ on $V$. Thus $\rho$ acts by bijective complex-linear isometries with continuous vector orbit maps. The Hilbert tensor product $V\widehat\otimes W$ is the completion of the finite sums of elementary tensors with pairing $\langle v\otimes w,v'\otimes w'\rangle=\langle v,v'\rangle\langle w,w'\rangle$. Let $L$ act by $\rho(a)\otimes I_W$ for $a\in L$, where $I_W$ is the identity. For any subgroup $J\le L$, let $V^J$ be the vectors fixed by every element of $J$. Then
$$(V\widehat\otimes W)^J=V^J\widehat\otimes W$$
as closed subspaces of $V\widehat\otimes W$. If $W\ne0$, the left side is nonzero exactly when $V^J\ne0$. If a finite linear combination of operators $\rho(a)$ preserves $V^J$ and acts there by a scalar, the same combination of $\rho(a)\otimes I_W$ acts by that scalar on $(V\widehat\otimes W)^J$.

### Statement references

[Countable Choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)

[Hilbert spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space)

[Complex inner products](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-and-complex-inner-product-space)

[Strongly continuous unitary representations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)

[Topological groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)

[Group actions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-action)

[Subgroups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)

[Hilbert tensor construction](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction)

[Linear subspaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)

[Closed sets in the norm metric](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-interior-closure-boundary)

[Finite linear combinations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-combination-and-span)

[Pointwise operations on linear maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space-of-linear-maps)

[Continuity at a point](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top)

[Topologies and open sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)

[Norm topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] Countable Choice supplies a choice function for any sequence of nonempty sets. It is assumed for the tensor construction, bounded extension, the passage from completeness to closedness, and orthogonal decomposition ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice)).

[F1] A Hilbert space is complete for the norm from its inner product. A strongly continuous unitary representation is a group homomorphism into the group of surjective complex-linear isometries with norm-continuous vector orbit maps ([math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space), [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)).

[F2] Under [A1], the Hilbert tensor product is the completion of the algebraic tensor product, which consists of finite sums of elementary tensors. The elementary tensor map is complex-bilinear, the product pairing is positive definite, and the elementary tensor norm is $\|v\|\,\|w\|$. Take all pairings linear in the first argument ([math:lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction)).

[F3] Regard a complex vector space $E$ as a right $\mathbb C$-module by $x\cdot c=cx$. For complex vector spaces $E,F,P$, a complex-bilinear map $b:E\times F\to P$ is additive in each variable and satisfies $b(cx,y)=c\,b(x,y)=b(x,cy)$. These are the additivity and scalar-transfer conditions for balance ([math:def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps)). A balanced map to an abelian group induces a unique additive map on the algebraic tensor product with the prescribed values on elementary tensors ([math:thm-universal-property-of-module-tensor-products](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-universal-property-of-module-tensor-products)).

[F4] Under [A1], a bounded linear map on a dense linear subspace extends uniquely to a bounded linear map on the whole normed space when its target is Banach. The extension has the same operator norm as the original map ([math:thm-extension-of-a-bounded-map-from-a-dense-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-of-a-bounded-map-from-a-dense-subspace)). Any two such maps with a common dense restriction are both bounded extensions of that restriction and are therefore equal.

[F5] A closed subspace of a complete metric space is complete in the inherited metric. Under [A1], a complete subspace of any metric space is closed ([math:thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed)).

[F6] Every closed subspace $M$ in a Hilbert space gives the orthogonal decomposition $V=M\oplus M^\perp$ under [A1], where $M^\perp=\{u:\langle u,m\rangle=0\text{ for all }m\in M\}$ ([math:thm-orthogonal-decomposition-by-a-closed-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-orthogonal-decomposition-by-a-closed-subspace)).

[F7] The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality is $|\langle x,y\rangle|\le\|x\|\|y\|$ ([math:thm-cauchy-schwarz-for-real-and-complex-inner-product-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-cauchy-schwarz-for-real-and-complex-inner-product-spaces)).

[F8] Complex polarization recovers the inner product from the norm and complex scalar operations. For orthogonal vectors $x,y$, $\|x+y\|^2=\|x\|^2+\|y\|^2$ ([math:prop-pythagorean-parallelogram-and-polarisation-identities](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-pythagorean-parallelogram-and-polarisation-identities)).

[F9] The complex inner products are linear in the first argument and satisfy conjugate symmetry $\langle x,y\rangle=\overline{\langle y,x\rangle}$. In particular they are conjugate-linear in the second argument ([math:def-real-and-complex-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-and-complex-inner-product-space)).

[F10] A bounded linear operator between normed spaces is continuous. If $C$ is a bound for $T$, then $\|Tx-Ty\|\le C\|x-y\|$ by linearity ([math:thm-bounded-linear-operator-equivalences](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bounded-linear-operator-equivalences), implication from boundedness to Lipschitz continuity).

[F11] A complex linear subspace contains zero and is closed under addition and complex scalar multiplication ([math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)).

[F12] A finite complex linear combination is a sum of a finite list of scalar multiples, including the empty sum, which is zero ([math:def-linear-combination-and-span](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-combination-and-span)).

[F13] The norm closure of a set consists of the points whose every open norm ball meets that set. A set is closed for the norm metric if each point outside it has an open norm ball disjoint from the set ([math:def-metric-interior-closure-boundary](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-interior-closure-boundary), [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [F22]).

[F14] The inner-product norm satisfies the triangle inequality and absolute homogeneity $\|c x\|=|c|\|x\|$ ([math:cor-inner-product-induces-a-norm](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-inner-product-induces-a-norm)).

[F15] In a metric space a sequence is Cauchy if for every positive rational $\varepsilon$ all pairwise distances on some tail are less than $\varepsilon$ ([math:def-cauchy-in-metric](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-cauchy-in-metric)).

[F16] For a map from a topological space into a Hilbert space, continuity at a point is equivalent to the following condition. For each norm ball about the image, some open set containing the point maps into that ball. The open-set criterion gives this condition because norm balls are open. At a point $y$ with $\|y-c\|<r$, the triangle inequality puts the ball of radius $r-\|y-c\|$ about $y$ in the ball of radius $r$ about $c$. Conversely every open target set containing the image contains such a norm ball ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [F14], [F22]).

[F17] A finite intersection of open sets is open, with the empty intersection taken to be the whole space ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)).

[F18] In the norm metric, a sequence $(x_n)$ converges to $x$ if for every positive rational $\varepsilon$ one has $\|x_n-x\|<\varepsilon$ for all sufficiently large $n$, and convergence implies these same bounds ([math:def-metric-convergence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-convergence)).

[F19] For linear subspaces $A$ and $B$ of the same vector space, their sum is the linear subspace $A+B=\{x+y:x\in A,\ y\in B\}$ ([math:def-sum-of-linear-subspaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sum-of-linear-subspaces)).

[F20] For linear maps between the same two complex vector spaces, addition and scalar multiplication are pointwise ([math:def-vector-space-of-linear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space-of-linear-maps)). In particular, $(\sum_i c_i U_i)(x)=\sum_i c_i U_i(x)$ and $(U-I)(x)=Ux-x$ for an endomorphism $U$. These operations yield linear maps ([math:prop-linear-maps-form-a-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-linear-maps-form-a-vector-space)).

[F21] A group homomorphism preserves products ([math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism)) and carries the identity to the identity ([math:lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties)). For a left action, the identity acts trivially and the product acts by composition in the same order ([math:def-group-action](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-action)).

[F22] On a Hilbert space and its subspaces, the norm gives the metric $d(x,y)=\|x-y\|$ ([math:def-banach-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-space)). Thus the open norm ball centered at $x$ of radius $r>0$ is $B(x,r)=\{y:\|y-x\|<r\}$ by the open-ball definition and symmetry of the metric ([math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)).

### Proof

**Given:** $L,\rho,V,W,J$ and [A1].

**Goal:** Identify the fixed vectors and the induced scalar action.

1.1 For $a\in L$, the prescription $(v,w)\mapsto\rho(a)v\otimes w$ is complex-bilinear by linearity of $\rho(a)$ and bilinearity of the elementary tensor map. By [F3] the prescription is balanced for the right action $v\cdot c=cv$, so it induces an additive map $T_{a,0}$ on $V\odot W$, the algebraic tensor product. For $c\in\mathbb C$, the relation $c(v\otimes w)=(cv)\otimes w$ and bilinearity give $T_{a,0}(c(v\otimes w))=cT_{a,0}(v\otimes w)$, and additivity gives homogeneity on finite sums. Since $\rho(a)$ is a complex-linear norm isometry, complex polarization gives $\langle\rho(a)v,\rho(a)v'\rangle=\langle v,v'\rangle$. Thus $T_{a,0}$ preserves the product pairing on elementary tensors and, by sesquilinearity, on finite sums. It extends by bounded linear extension from the dense algebraic tensor product into the complete space $V\widehat\otimes W$. Write this extension as $T_a=\rho(a)\otimes I_W$. Norm preservation extends by continuity. [A1, F1, F2, F3, F4, F8, F9, F10]

2.1 By [F1] and [F21], $\rho(a)\rho(b)=\rho(ab)$ and $\rho(e)=I_V$ for the identity $e\in L$. Thus $T_aT_b=T_{ab}$ and $T_e=I$ on elementary tensors by step 1.1 and on their span by linearity. In each equality the two maps are bounded linear maps into the completed tensor product with the same restriction to the dense algebraic tensor product. By [F4] the equalities hold on the whole tensor product. These are the left-action laws of [F21], and $T_aT_{a^{-1}}=T_e=T_{a^{-1}}T_a$ makes $T_{a^{-1}}$ the inverse of $T_a$. Fix $b\in L$, $z\in V\widehat\otimes W$, and $\varepsilon>0$. By density choose $z_0=\sum_i v_i\otimes w_i$ with $\|z-z_0\|<\varepsilon/4$ and set $s=\sum_i\|w_i\|$. For each term the orbit continuity in [F1], the criterion in [F16], and the ball formula in [F22] give an open set $U_i$ containing $b$ on which $\|\rho(a)v_i-\rho(b)v_i\|<\varepsilon/(2(1+s))$. The intersection of these finitely many open sets is an open set $U$ containing $b$ by [F17]. For $a\in U$, the norm of $T_a z_0-T_b z_0$ is bounded by $\sum_i\|\rho(a)v_i-\rho(b)v_i\|\,\|w_i\|$, which is at most $\varepsilon s/(2(1+s))<\varepsilon/2$. Since $T_a$ and $T_b$ are isometries, $\|T_a z-T_b z\|\le 2\|z-z_0\|+\|T_a z_0-T_b z_0\|<\varepsilon$. By [F16] the orbit map is continuous at $b$, proving strong continuity. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F14, F16, F17, F21, F22, step 1.1]

2.2 The space $M=V^J$ contains zero since each $\rho(a)$ is linear. If $x,y\in M$ and $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb C$, then for each $a\in J$, $\rho(a)(\alpha x+\beta y)=\alpha\rho(a)x+\beta\rho(a)y=\alpha x+\beta y$. Thus $M$ is a complex linear subspace by [F11]. If $x\notin M$, some $a\in J$ gives $\delta=\|\rho(a)x-x\|>0$. By [F20], the pointwise difference $\rho(a)-I_V$ is linear. It has bound $2$ by the triangle inequality and the isometry property. For $\|y-x\|<\delta/2$, [F10] gives $\|\rho(a)y-y\|\ge\delta-2\|y-x\|>0$. By [F22] the ball of radius $\delta/2$ around $x$ misses $M$, so $M$ is closed by [F13]. It is complete in its inherited norm and hence is a Hilbert space for the restricted pairing. The complex-bilinear inclusion prescription $(m,w)\mapsto m\otimes w$ in $V\odot W$ is balanced by [F3] with the right action $m\cdot c=cm$. Its additive descent to $M\odot W$ is complex-linear by the scalar calculation in step 1.1. The restricted pairing gives equality of norms of all finite sums, so this inclusion extends isometrically to $M\widehat\otimes W$ by bounded extension into $V\widehat\otimes W$. Its image is complete by isometry and closed by [F5]. Denote this image by $A$. For $a\in J$, $T_a$ fixes each elementary tensor from $M\odot W$ and thus their span. The restriction of $T_a$ to $A$ and the inclusion of $A$ into the ambient Hilbert space are bounded linear maps that agree on the dense span of the elementary tensors in $A$. By [F4] they agree throughout $A$. [A1, F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F10, F11, F13, F14, F20, F22, step 1.1]

2.3 Fix $w\in W$. The slice prescription $(v,w')\mapsto\langle w',w\rangle v$ is complex-bilinear since the pairing is linear in $w'$. By [F3] the prescription is balanced for $v\cdot c=cv$. Its additive descent is complex-linear by the scalar calculation in step 1.1, giving $S_{w,0}:V\odot W\to V$. On elementary tensors and then on finite sums $z$, the product pairing gives $\langle S_{w,0}z,u\rangle=\langle z,u\otimes w\rangle$ for $u\in V$. Taking $u=S_{w,0}z$ and using Cauchy–Schwarz gives $\|S_{w,0}z\|^2\le\|z\|\,\|S_{w,0}z\|\,\|w\|$, so $\|S_{w,0}z\|\le\|z\|\,\|w\|$, including when $S_{w,0}z=0$. The domain is dense and $V$ is complete, so bounded linear extension gives $S_w:V\widehat\otimes W\to V$. [A1, F1, F2, F3, F4, F7, F9, step 1.1]

3.1 For fixed $u$, the two sides of $\langle S_wz,u\rangle=\langle z,u\otimes w\rangle$ are linear functionals in $z$. By [F7] their bounds are $\|w\|\|u\|$ and $\|u\otimes w\|$ respectively, using the bound on $S_w$ from step 2.3 and the operator norm equality in [F4]. They agree on the dense algebraic tensor product by step 2.3. For $z_0$ in that subspace, the absolute value of their difference at $z$ is at most $(\|w\|\|u\|+\|u\otimes w\|)\|z-z_0\|$, by linearity, the bounds, and the triangle inequality. Density makes this upper bound arbitrarily small, so the functionals agree at $z$. The maps $S_wT_a$ and $\rho(a)S_w$ are bounded linear maps into $V$, with bound $\|w\|$ since the action operators are isometries. They agree on elementary tensors by the slice prescription and hence on the dense algebraic tensor product. By [F4] they agree everywhere. If $z$ is fixed by $J$, then $\rho(a)S_wz=S_wz$ for every $a\in J$, so $S_wz\in M$. For $u\in M^\perp$, conjugate symmetry gives $\langle S_wz,u\rangle=\overline{\langle u,S_wz\rangle}=0$, so the pairing identity gives $\langle z,u\otimes w\rangle=0$. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F6, F7, F9, F14, step 1.1, step 2.2, step 2.3]

3.2 Let $D$ be the set of finite complex linear combinations of the tensors $u\otimes w$ with $u\in M^\perp$ and $w\in W$, and let $B$ be the norm closure of $D$. The empty sum belongs to $D$, concatenating two finite sums gives their sum in $D$, and multiplying all coefficients by a scalar keeps the result in $D$. Thus $D$ is a linear subspace. Every ball centered at a point of $B$ meets $D$, so $D$ is dense in $B$. If $x\notin B$, there is an open ball of radius $r>0$ about $x$ disjoint from $D$. For every $y$ at distance less than $r/2$ from $x$, the ball of radius $r/2$ about $y$ lies in that ball by [F22] and the triangle inequality and is disjoint from $D$. Hence $y\notin B$, proving that $B$ is closed. Also $0\in D\subseteq B$. For $x,y\in B$, $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb C$, and $\varepsilon>0$, choose $x',y'\in D$ with $\|x-x'\|,\|y-y'\|<\varepsilon/(1+|\alpha|+|\beta|)$. Then $\alpha x'+\beta y'\in D$, and the norm of its difference from $\alpha x+\beta y$ is at most $|\alpha|\|x-x'\|+|\beta|\|y-y'\|<\varepsilon$. By [F13], $\alpha x+\beta y\in B$, so $B$ is a closed linear subspace. [F1, F2, F6, F11, F12, F13, F14, F22, step 2.2]

4.1 The tensor pairing and sesquilinearity make the finite spans of the elementary tensors generating $A$ and $B$ orthogonal. Cauchy–Schwarz extends the orthogonality from these dense spans to $A$ and $B$. Thus $A\cap B=\{0\}$. By [F19] each term of a Cauchy sequence in $A+B$ has a decomposition $x_n+y_n$ with $x_n\in A$ and $y_n\in B$. Two decompositions differ by a vector in $A\cap B$, so these components are unique. Pythagoras applied to the differences gives $\|(x_n+y_n)-(x_m+y_m)\|^2=\|x_n-x_m\|^2+\|y_n-y_m\|^2$. Each component distance is at most $\|(x_n+y_n)-(x_m+y_m)\|$. For any positive rational $\varepsilon$, the tail on which this last distance is less than $\varepsilon$ also works for both components. By [F15] both component sequences are Cauchy. Since $A$ and $B$ are closed in a Hilbert space and hence complete, $x_n$ converges to some $x\in A$ and $y_n$ to some $y\in B$. For each positive rational $\varepsilon$, both component distances to their limits are less than $\varepsilon/2$ on a common tail by [F18]. The triangle inequality gives $\|(x_n+y_n)-(x+y)\|\le\|x_n-x\|+\|y_n-y\|<\varepsilon$ there. Since $x+y\in A+B$ by [F19], the sequence converges in $A+B$ by [F18]. Thus $A+B$ is complete and therefore closed under [A1]. [A1, F1, F2, F5, F7, F8, F9, F14, F15, F18, F19, step 2.2, step 3.2]

5.1 Decomposing the first factor by [F6] puts every elementary tensor in $A+B$. This sum is linear by [F19] and closed by step 4.1, so density gives $V\widehat\otimes W=A+B$. By step 3.1 and sesquilinearity, a fixed vector $z$ is orthogonal to $D$, and Cauchy–Schwarz extends this orthogonality to $B$. Writing it as $x+y$ with $x\in A$ and $y\in B$ gives $\langle z,y\rangle=\|y\|^2=0$, so $z\in A$. Together with step 2.2 this proves the fixed-space identity. [F2, F6, F7, F9, F19, step 2.2, step 3.1, step 3.2, step 4.1]

6.1 If $M=\{0\}$, all its elementary tensors vanish by bilinearity, and $A=\{0\}$. When $M$ and $W$ are both nonzero, $m\otimes w$ for nonzero $m,w$ has nonzero norm, proving the asserted equivalence for $W\ne0$. If $\sum_i c_i\rho(a_i)$ acts on $M$ by a scalar $\lambda$, then by [F20] and tensor bilinearity, $\sum_i c_i T_{a_i}(m\otimes w)=(\sum_i c_i\rho(a_i)m)\otimes w=\lambda(m\otimes w)$. Since each $T_{a_i}$ is an isometry, the norm laws give $\|\sum_i c_iT_{a_i}z\|\le(\sum_i |c_i|)\|z\|$. On $A$ the finite combination and $\lambda$ times the inclusion are bounded linear maps into the ambient Hilbert space. The combination agrees with $\lambda I$ on the dense span of these tensors in $A$, hence everywhere in $A$ by [F4]. [A1, F2, F4, F12, F14, F20, step 1.1, step 2.2, step 5.1] ∎


## Integral determinant-index cosets in degree two

[Open item](item-lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets) · level 1


### Statement

Let $\ell$ be prime, let $F=\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the field of $\ell$-adic numbers, and let $R=\mathbb Z_\ell\subset F$ be the ring of $\ell$-adic integers. Write $v=v_\ell$ for the valuation with $v(\ell)=1$. For a nonnegative integer $n$, let $B_n=\{0,\ldots,\ell^n-1\}\subset R$ via the canonical integer embedding. For a nonnegative integer $e$, set
$$D_e=\{A\in M_2(R):\det A\ne0,\ v(\det A)=e\}.$$
Here $M_2(R)$ is the ring of two-by-two matrices over $R$ with matrix multiplication, and $\det\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-bc$.

Put $H=\mathrm{GL}_2(R)$, the group of two-by-two matrices with two-sided inverses over $R$. Consider the cosets $AH=\{AU:U\in H\}$ for right multiplication on $D_e$.

For $S=R$ or $S=F$, write $S^2$ for the set of columns $(x_1,x_2)^{\mathsf T}$ with $x_1,x_2\in S$, with addition and scalar multiplication in each coordinate. A column lattice in $R^2$ means an $R$-submodule $L$ such that every vector in $F^2$ is a finite $F$-linear combination of elements of $L$. A finite $F$-linear combination of vectors in $L$ has the form $\sum_{i=1}^m c_i u_i$, where $c_i\in F$ and $u_i\in L$, with the empty sum equal to the zero column. As a submodule, $L$ contains zero and is closed under differences and multiplication by scalars in $R$. Its index $[R^2:L]$, when finite, is the number of additive cosets $x+L=\{x+y:y\in L\}$.

The columns of $A$ generate $AR^2=\{Ax:x\in R^2\}$. The map $AH\mapsto AR^2$ is a bijection onto the column lattices of index $\ell^e$. Each coset has exactly one representative
$$T_{r,b}=\begin{pmatrix}\ell^r&b\\0&\ell^{e-r}\end{pmatrix},\qquad 0\le r\le e,\quad b\in B_r.$$
For each $r$ this gives $\ell^r$ representatives, one for each residue modulo $\ell^rR$.

Write $G=\mathrm{PGL}_2(F)$ for the quotient of $\mathrm{GL}_2(F)$ by nonzero scalar matrices, so $[A]=[B]$ means $B=\lambda A$ for some $\lambda\in F^\times$. Let $K$ be the image of $H$ in $G$. The natural map identifies $\mathrm{PGL}_2(R)$, the quotient of $H$ by scalar matrices with invertible scalars in $R$, with $K$. The map $AH\mapsto[A]K$ is a bijection onto the cosets for right multiplication by $K$ in the projective image of $D_e$.

The principal matrix right ideal $AM_2(R)=\{AC:C\in M_2(R)\}$ consists exactly of the matrices with both columns in $AR^2$. A right ideal is an additive subgroup stable under multiplication on the right by the ring. Two matrices in $D_e$ generate the same right ideal exactly when their column lattices agree.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The prime $\ell$ is an integer greater than one whose only positive divisors are one and itself ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime)). The field $F$ is the completion of $\mathbb Q$ for the $\ell$-adic distance ([math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers), [def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)). In $F$, $v(xy)=v(x)+v(y)$ for nonzero factors, $v(\ell)=1$, $R=\{x:v(x)\ge0\}$ with $v(0)=+\infty$, and the units of $R$ are the elements of valuation zero ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)).

[F2] Elements of $R$ are compatible tuples of integer residue classes modulo $\ell^j$ for $j\ge1$, with coordinatewise operations ([math:def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes), [def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)). For positive $m$, write $[a]_m$ for the residue class of $a$ in $\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z$. The equality $[a]_m=[b]_m$ holds exactly when $a-b$ is an integer multiple of $m$ ([math:def-integers-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integers-modulo-n), [math:def-congruence-modulo-an-integer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-congruence-modulo-an-integer)). In the coordinates modulo $\ell^j$, addition and multiplication use $[a]_{\ell^j}+[b]_{\ell^j}=[a+b]_{\ell^j}$ and $[a]_{\ell^j}[b]_{\ell^j}=[ab]_{\ell^j}$ ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)). The canonical integer map sends $a$ to $([a]_{\ell^j})_{j\ge1}$ and is injective ([math:def-canonical-map-from-the-integers-to-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-canonical-map-from-the-integers-to-zp), [math:thm-the-integers-map-injectively-and-densely-into-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-the-integers-map-injectively-and-densely-into-zp)). For positive modulus $m$, integer division gives each residue modulo $m$ a unique representative between $0$ and $m-1$ ([math:thm-division-algorithm-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-division-algorithm-in-z)).

[F3] Matrix products take the indicated linear combinations of columns and are associative and distributive, with identity $I_2$ ([math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose), [math:thm-ring-matrix-arithmetic-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-ring-matrix-arithmetic-laws)). Over the commutative rings $R$ and $F$, the two-by-two determinant expands as $ad-bc$ and satisfies $\det(AB)=\det A\det B$ ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix), [math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative)).

[F4] The columns in $S^2$ form the free $S$-module on the two coordinate positions, with standard columns $(1,0)^{\mathsf T}$ and $(0,1)^{\mathsf T}$ ([math:def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis)). Finite $F$-linear combinations in $F^2$ use the sum of the scalar multiples of a finite list of vectors ([math:def-linear-combination-and-span](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-combination-and-span)). An $R$-submodule of $R^2$ is an additive subgroup closed under multiplication by $R$ ([math:def-submodule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-submodule)). For a subgroup $J$ of a group, the coset represented by $g$ for multiplication on the right by $J$ is $gJ=\{gj:j\in J\}$. In additive notation it is $g+J$ ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)).

[F5] A bijection between sets preserves finite cardinality. The product of two finite sets has cardinality the product of their cardinalities ([math:def-finite-cardinality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-cardinality), [math:thm-product-rule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-product-rule)).

[F6] Over a field or a local ring, the general linear group consists of matrices with two-sided inverses and the projective linear group is its quotient by the unit scalar matrices. Multiplication is $[A][U]=[AU]$, and inclusion of one coefficient ring into another induces the entrywise map on projective groups ([def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)).

[F7] A right ideal is an additive subgroup of the ring closed under right multiplication by every ring element ([math:def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal)).

[F8] A nonempty set of integers with an integer lower bound has a least element ([math:lem-int-bounded-above-has-greatest](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-int-bounded-above-has-greatest)).

[F9] A local ring is a nonzero commutative ring with exactly one maximal ideal ([math:def-local-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-local-ring)). A maximal ideal is a proper ideal with no larger proper ideal containing it ([math:def-prime-and-maximal-ideals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime-and-maximal-ideals)).

[F10] A subgroup contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)). The image of a group homomorphism is a subgroup of its codomain ([math:thm-image-subgroup-and-kernel-normal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-image-subgroup-and-kernel-normal)).

[F11] In a group $G'$ with subgroup $J$, the left cosets $aJ$ and $bJ$ of elements $a,b\in G'$ agree exactly when $b=aj$ for some $j\in J$ ([math:lem-coset-membership-and-equality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-membership-and-equality)).

### Proof

**Given:** A prime $\ell$, the rings $R\subset F$ above, and an integer $e\ge0$.

**Goal:** Classify the cosets of $D_e$ and their projective images, and identify the associated matrix right ideals.

1.1 Let $J$ be a nonzero ideal of $R$. The valuations of its nonzero elements form a nonempty set of integers bounded below by $0$. By [F8] this set has a least element $t$, attained by some $a\in J$ with $a\ne0$. For $x\in J$ with $x\ne0$, $v(x/a)=v(x)-t\ge0$, so $x\in aR$. Conversely $aR\subseteq J$. Since $a/\ell^t$ is a unit, $J=aR=\ell^tR$. These powers are distinct because $t$ is the least valuation of a nonzero element of $\ell^tR$. In particular, $\ell R$ contains exactly the nonunits and is proper since $1$ is a unit. An ideal containing an element outside $\ell R$ contains a unit and hence equals $R$. Every proper ideal is therefore contained in $\ell R$, and the maximal-ideal criterion in [F9] gives that $\ell R$ is the unique maximal ideal. The ring $R$ is commutative and contains the nonzero identity of $F$, so it is a local ring by [F9]. [F1, F7, F8, F9]

1.2 For $n\ge1$, the projection $\pi_n(x)=x_n$ is onto because each residue has an integer lift. Since the operations are coordinatewise, $\pi_n(x-z)=\pi_n(x)-\pi_n(z)$, and $\pi_n(\ell^ny)=0$ for all $y\in R$ by the residue product formula. Conversely, suppose $\pi_n(x)=0$. For each $j\ge1$, every integer representative $a_j$ of $x_{n+j}$ is divisible by $\ell^n$, since it reduces to zero modulo $\ell^n$. Define $y_j=[a_j/\ell^n]_{\ell^j}$. Changing $a_j$ changes the quotient by a multiple of $\ell^j$. Compatibility gives $a_{j+1}-a_j\in\ell^{n+j}\mathbb Z$ for any such representatives, so $y_{j+1}$ reduces to $y_j$ modulo $\ell^j$. The tuple $y\in R$ therefore satisfies $(\ell^ny)_j=[\ell^n(a_j/\ell^n)]_{\ell^j}=[a_j]_{\ell^j}=x_j$ by reduction. Thus two elements have the same projection exactly when their difference lies in $\ell^nR$. Integer division gives one lift in $B_n$ per residue, and the $\ell^n$ integers in the defining interval have distinct images in $R$. For $n=0$, $\ell^0R=R$ and $B_0=\{0\}$, which represents its single additive coset. [F2, F4, F5]

1.3 For $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ with nonzero determinant, both products with $\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$ equal $(\det A)I_2$. Dividing the second matrix by $\det A$ gives the inverse over $F$, and over $R$ when the determinant is a unit. Conversely, an inverse over $R$ has determinant multiplying $\det A$ to $1$ by multiplicativity. Hence $v(\det U)=0$ for $U\in H$, and every product $AU$ for $A\in D_e$ and $U\in H$ again belongs to $D_e$. The identity and matrix product on $H$ are those of $\mathrm{GL}_2(F)$, and an inverse over $R$ is also the inverse over $F$. Thus $H$ is a subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_2(F)$ by [F10]. [F1, F3, F6, F10]

2.1 For $A\in D_e$, $AR^2$ is an $R$-submodule by the matrix laws. Every $w\in F^2$ is $A(A^{-1}w)$, an $F$-linear combination of the two columns of $A$, both of which lie in $AR^2$. For any column lattice $L$, express each of $(1,0)^{\mathsf T}$ and $(0,1)^{\mathsf T}$ as a finite $F$-linear combination of vectors in $L$. Multiplying both expressions by a sufficiently large common power $\ell^N$ makes all coefficients integral by their valuations. The two scaled standard columns lie in $L$ and every column of $\ell^N R^2$ is an $R$-linear combination of them. Thus $\ell^N R^2\subseteq L$. The sets $J_1=\{x\in R:(x,0)^{\mathsf T}\in L\}$ and $J_2=\{y\in R:(x,y)^{\mathsf T}\in L\text{ for some }x\}$ are ideals by closure under differences and $R$-scalars, and each contains $\ell^N$. Write $J_1=\ell^rR$ and $J_2=\ell^sR$ by step 1.1. Take a vector $(b',\ell^s)^{\mathsf T}$ in $L$ and subtract a multiple of $(\ell^r,0)^{\mathsf T}$ to obtain $(b,\ell^s)^{\mathsf T}$ with $b\in B_r$. Every vector in $L$ can now be reduced to the first axis by subtracting an $R$-multiple of $(b,\ell^s)^{\mathsf T}$. Hence $L=TR^2$ for $T=\begin{pmatrix}\ell^r&b\\0&\ell^s\end{pmatrix}$. The two ideals determine $r,s$, and two lifts of $\ell^s$ differ in first coordinate by an element of $\ell^rR$. Thus $b\in B_r$ is determined by $L$. [F1, F3, F4, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 1.3]

2.2 The local ring $R$ embeds in $F$, so [F6] gives the entrywise homomorphism $\mathrm{PGL}_2(R)\to G$. Since every class of $\mathrm{PGL}_2(R)$ has a representative in $H$, the image of this homomorphism is $K$, a subgroup of $G$ by [F10]. If $U,V\in H$ and $U=\lambda V$ for $\lambda\in F^\times$, then $UV^{-1}=\lambda I_2\in H$. The entries of $\lambda I_2$ and its inverse lie in $R$, so $\lambda$ is a unit of $R$. Thus the map identifies $\mathrm{PGL}_2(R)$ with $K$. For $A,B\in D_e$, equality $[A]K=[B]K$ gives $[B]=[AU]$ for some $U\in H$, hence $B=\lambda AU$ for a nonzero $\lambda\in F$. Taking determinant valuations gives $e=2v(\lambda)+e$ because $\det(\lambda AU)=\lambda^2\det A\det U$. Thus $\lambda\in R^\times$ and $B=A(\lambda U)$ with $\lambda U\in H$, so $AH=BH$ by [F11]. Conversely, if $AH=BH$, then $B=AU$ for some $U\in H$ by [F11]. As $[B]=[A][U]$ with $[U]\in K$, [F11] gives $[A]K=[B]K$. The projective image is stable under right multiplication by $K$ because $[A][U]=[AU]$ and $AU\in D_e$ by step 1.3. Each of its cosets has a representative $[A]$ with $A\in D_e$. [F1, F3, F4, F6, F10, F11, step 1.1, step 1.3]

3.1 For $L=TR^2$, regard $L$ as a subgroup of the additive group $R^2$. By [F11] in additive notation, two columns represent the same coset exactly when their difference belongs to $L$. Each additive coset in $R^2/L$ has a representative $(a,z)^{\mathsf T}$ with $(a,z)\in B_r\times B_s$. Subtract a multiple of the second column to put the second coordinate in $B_s$, then a multiple of the first column to put the first coordinate in $B_r$. If two representatives differ by an element of $L$, their second coordinates agree by reduction modulo $\ell^sR$. Their first coordinates then differ by an element of $\ell^rR$, so they agree as well. The index is therefore $\ell^r\ell^s=\ell^{r+s}$. The determinant of $T$ is $\ell^{r+s}$. Conversely any such $T$ with $r,s\ge0$ and $b\in B_r$ has this determinant and gives the same index calculation. [F1, F3, F4, F5, F11, step 1.2, step 1.3, step 2.1]

4.1 If $AH=BH$, then $B=AU$ with $U\in H$ by [F11]. Since $U$ and $U^{-1}$ both send $R^2$ into itself, $UR^2=R^2$, so $BR^2=AR^2$. If $A,B\in D_e$ have $AR^2=BR^2$, writing the columns of each in terms of the columns of the other gives $B=AU$ and $A=BV$ with $U,V\in M_2(R)$. Then $A=AUV$ and $B=BVU$. Multiplying by the respective inverses over $F$ shows $UV=VU=I_2$, so $U\in H$ and [F11] gives $AH=BH$. This same calculation works for any two integral matrices with nonzero determinant and equal column lattices. Applied to $A$ and the $T$ of step 2.1, it gives $T=AU$ with $U\in H$, so $r+s=v(\det T)=e$. Conversely every lattice of index $\ell^e$ yields such a $T$ in $D_e$ by step 3.1. Thus the lattice bijection and the unique representatives $T_{r,b}$ follow. The $\ell^r$ choices of $b$ give the claimed multiplicity. [F1, F3, F4, F5, F6, F11, step 1.2, step 1.3, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 The set $AM_2(R)$ contains the zero matrix and is a right ideal because $AC-AC'=A(C-C')$ and $(AC)C'=A(CC')$. Each column of $AC$ is $A$ times the corresponding column of $C$, so both lie in $AR^2$. Conversely, if the columns of a matrix lie in $AR^2$, take their preimages under $A$ in $R^2$ as the columns of $C$. This gives the required matrix $AC$. The column lattice is recoverable from the ideal by taking first columns of its matrices, since any vector in $AR^2$ occurs as the first column with zero second column. Thus equality of the ideals is equivalent to equality of the lattices, completing the classifications. [F3, F7, step 4.1, step 2.2] ∎


## Finite scalar adeles and ideles

[Open item](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles) · level 1


### Definition

Let $\ell$ range over the primes. Use the $\ell$-adic absolute value on $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and its integer subring $\mathbb Z_\ell=\{t:|t|_\ell\le 1\}$. Give $\mathbb Q_\ell$ the metric topology of $d_\ell(s,t)=|s-t|_\ell$. Give $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ their subspace topologies from $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and give $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ the subspace topology from $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$.

The finite adele ring and its group of units, the finite scalar ideles, are

$$\mathbb A_f=\{(a_\ell)\in\prod_\ell\mathbb Q_\ell:a_\ell\in\mathbb Z_\ell\text{ for all but finitely many }\ell\},$$

$$\mathbb A_f^\times=\{(a_\ell)\in\prod_\ell\mathbb Q_\ell^\times:a_\ell\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times\text{ for all but finitely many }\ell\}.$$

The operations are coordinatewise. Embed $\mathbb Q$ diagonally as constant tuples using the canonical embeddings $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb Q_\ell$.

A restricted product here consists of tuples belonging to the specified integral factors outside a finite set. Its basic opens are boxes $\prod_\ell V_\ell$, with each $V_\ell$ open in the local ambient factor and $V_\ell$ equal to the specified integral factor for all but finitely many $\ell$. The factors for the ring are $(\mathbb Q_\ell,\mathbb Z_\ell)$ and for the group they are $(\mathbb Q_\ell^\times,\mathbb Z_\ell^\times)$, with $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times=\{t:|t|_\ell=1\}$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The prime-indexed local fields and their rings of integers.

[F1] The $\ell$-adic completion is a valued field, $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is its integer subring, and $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ are open in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Local addition and multiplication are continuous for the field metric. The reciprocal is continuous on the nonzero scalars for the restricted metric ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification 2.1 and 5.1–6.1). The rational embedding into each completion is the isometric constant-sequence map, and the local field operations are obtained by termwise addition and multiplication of Cauchy sequences ([math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers), [math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)).

[F2] For nonzero integers $a,b$, the rational valuation satisfies $v_\ell(a/b)=v_\ell(a)-v_\ell(b)$ ([math:lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q)). On a nonzero integer the valuation is the greatest exponent of a dividing power of $\ell$ ([math:def-p-adic-valuation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-valuation)). The absolute value is $|a/b|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(a/b)}$ and $|0|_\ell=0$ ([math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)). An integer $\ell$ divides $a$ when $a=\ell q$ for some integer $q$ ([math:def-divides-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-divides-in-z)). A positive divisor of a nonzero integer has size at most the absolute value of that integer ([math:lem-divisor-bound](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-divisor-bound)).

[F3] A unital ring homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication and the identity ([math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism)). A unital ring with a unital ring homomorphism from $\mathbb Q$ whose image is central is a $\mathbb Q$-algebra ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F4] Basic open sets form a basis when they are open and every open neighborhood contains a basic open neighborhood of its point ([math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)). The basis criterion requires that the proposed family cover the set and refine a binary intersection about each of its points ([math:thm-basis-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-basis-criterion)). In a product topology, basic opens restrict only finitely many factors ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)).

[F5] Metric open sets contain a ball about each of their points ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)). Relatively open sets are traces of ambient open sets ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). A map is continuous at a point if each open neighborhood of its image contains the image of an open neighborhood of the point ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top)). A topological group is a group whose multiplication and inversion are continuous ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)).

[F6] A finite set admits a bijective listing of natural-number length ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)). Every subset of a finite set is finite ([math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set)). For a finite list of nonempty sets one may select one element from each ([math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)). Every prime is an integer greater than $1$ ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime)).

[F7] A ring has an abelian additive group, associative multiplication with identity, and both distributive laws ([math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring)). The two-sided units of a unital ring form a group under its multiplication ([math:lem-ring-units-form-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group)). A subset of a group is a subgroup if it contains the ambient identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F8] For a metric $d$ on $X$, the open ball about $x\in X$ of radius $r>0$ is $B_d(x,r)=\{y\in X:d(x,y)<r\}$ ([math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)). This ball is open in the topology induced by the metric ([math:thm-metric-open-set-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-open-set-algebra), clause 1).

[F9] The whole space is open by axiom (T1), and the intersection of two open sets in a topological space is open by axiom (T3) ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)).

### Justification

**Goal:** Verify the scalar ring and its units, and justify the stated topologies.

1.1 For two finite sets, concatenate their finite listings and keep only the first occurrence of each entry. This lists their union without repetitions, so the union is finite. Outside the union of the exceptional sets of two tuples, their coordinates belong to the local integer subrings. Those subrings are closed under addition, multiplication and negation, so the corresponding coordinatewise operations preserve $\mathbb A_f$. The zero and identity tuples are integral at every prime. The additive group laws, the multiplicative identity and associativity laws, the two distributive laws, and commutativity of multiplication all hold in each coordinate field. Thus $\mathbb A_f$ is a commutative unital ring. Its two-sided units form a group by [F7]. A tuple in $\mathbb A_f$ is invertible precisely when every coordinate is nonzero and the termwise inverse also belongs to $\mathbb A_f$, since multiplication and the identity are coordinatewise. At any prime, a nonzero coordinate and its inverse are both integral exactly when its absolute value is $1$, since the two absolute values are reciprocal. If the tuple is invertible, the union of the tuple's and its inverse's exceptional sets is finite, and every coordinate off that union has absolute value $1$. Conversely, the absolute-value-one condition off a finite set makes both the tuple and its coordinatewise inverse integral there. This proves the displayed description of the unit group. [F1, F6, F7, given]

1.2 Negation satisfies $d_\ell(-s,-t)=d_\ell(t,s)=d_\ell(s,t)$. For an open set containing $-s$, take a ball of radius $r>0$ about $-s$ contained in that set. The ball of radius $r$ about $s$ is open and maps into the target ball by the distance equality. This proves continuity of negation. For $s\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, a ball about $s$ for the restricted metric is the trace of the field ball of the same radius, since both use the same distance inequality. Each such trace is relatively open, and every relatively open neighborhood of $s$ contains one by the ambient metric criterion. The restricted metric therefore induces the stated subspace topology. To use local multiplication on $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, write an open target there as the trace of an open set of $\mathbb Q_\ell$. An ambient input rectangle mapping into that open set restricts to a relatively open rectangle of nonzero inputs, whose products remain nonzero. For inversion, the reciprocal is continuous on the nonzero inputs by [F1] and takes values there, so the same trace test applies to the target. [F1, F4, F5, F8]

2.1 In either restricted product, each tuple belongs to a basic box that uses the full ambient factors at its finitely many exceptional primes and the integral factors elsewhere. The full ambient factors are open by (T1) and $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is open by [F1]. The factor $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ is open in $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ because it is the trace of an open subset of the field. For any two basic boxes, their intersection is the coordinatewise intersection box. Its factors are open by (T3) and equal the integral factors off the union of the two exceptional sets. The covering and intersection properties give the stated basis. [F1, F4, F5, F9, step 1.1]

2.2 The diagonal zero tuple is integral everywhere. For $a/b\ne 0$, choose nonzero integers $a,b$ representing it. If $\ell$ does not divide $b$, no positive power of $\ell$ divides $b$, because any such divisibility would give $b=\ell(\ell^{k-1}q)$ with $k\ge 1$. Thus $v_\ell(b)=0$ and $v_\ell(a)\ge 0$, so $|a/b|_\ell\le 1$. The inverse $b/a$ is integral off the divisors of $a$ by the same calculation. Every prime dividing either integer lies in the finite interval $\{1,\ldots,M\}$ for $M=\max(|a|,|b|)$. This interval has the bijective listing $k\mapsto k+1$ for $0\le k<M$. The two prime-divisor sets and their union are finite as subsets of this interval. Thus the diagonal maps take $\mathbb Q$ into $\mathbb A_f$ and $\mathbb Q^\times$ into $\mathbb A_f^\times$. Each coordinate map preserves addition and multiplication by the termwise operations on constant sequences. It sends $1$ to the constant sequence of ones, which acts as the local identity by termwise multiplication. Therefore the diagonal map is a unital ring homomorphism. It is injective since every coordinate embedding is injective, and its image is central by commutativity of $\mathbb A_f$. This makes $\mathbb A_f$ a commutative $\mathbb Q$-algebra. [F1, F2, F3, F6, step 1.1]

3.1 Fix an input to an operation and a basic box containing its output. Enlarge the finite set of exceptional primes of the target box to include all exceptional primes of the input tuples. The resulting union is finite by applying the binary union argument of step 1.1 at most twice. At each prime in the enlarged set, local continuity gives open input neighborhoods mapping into the target factor, using an open rectangle for a binary operation. Select one such neighborhood or rectangle at each of these finitely many primes. At every other prime use the integral input factors. The integral subrings are closed under the ring operations. In the local multiplicative group $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, which is a group of units by [F7], $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ contains the same identity $1$ because $|1|_\ell=1$. Multiplicativity and $|t^{-1}|_\ell=|t|_\ell^{-1}$ show that products and inverses of elements of absolute value $1$ still have absolute value $1$. Thus $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ is a subgroup by [F7]. This choice produces a restricted-product input box, or a rectangle of two such boxes for a binary operation, mapping into the target box. Thus addition, multiplication and negation are continuous on the adele ring, and multiplication and inversion are continuous on the idele group, making it a topological group. [F1, F4, F5, F6, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 1.2]

4.1 The box of all integral factors is open in each restricted product. Its intersection with a basic box restricts only finitely many coordinates by relatively open conditions, because each condition is the trace of a local ambient open set. Conversely, take a basic product-open set of the integral factors. Each of its finitely many restricted coordinate sets is the trace of a local ambient open set on the integral factor. The integral factor is itself ambient-open, so this trace is open in the ambient factor by (T3). Take these coordinate sets at the restricted primes and the integral factors elsewhere. The open box so obtained has the given product-open set as its intersection with the box of all integral factors. Thus the induced topology on that box is its product topology. [F1, F4, F5, F9, step 2.1] ∎


## Upper-triangular representatives of local matrix cosets

[Open item](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets) · level 1


### Statement

Assume countable choice, meaning that a sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. For a prime $\ell$, put $F=\mathbb Q_\ell$, the completion of $\mathbb Q$ for the $\ell$-adic absolute value, and $O=\mathbb Z_\ell=\{x:|x|_\ell\le1\}$. For $x\in F^\times$, write $\nu(x)=r$ if $|x|_\ell=\ell^{-r}$. Then $\nu$ is integer valued and additive under multiplication. The ring $O$ is compact and open in $F$, its units have valuation zero, and $O/\ell O$ is a field with $\ell$ elements.

Give $F$ its $\ell$-adic metric topology, $F^4$ the product topology, and $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(F)$ and $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(O)$ the subspace topologies on their four entries in $F^4$. Give $G/Z=\mathrm{PGL}_2(F)$ the quotient topology by the scalar subgroup $Z=F^\times I$. The subgroup $K$ and its image $\bar K$ in $G/Z$ are compact and open in their respective Hausdorff topological groups.

Write $B$ for the invertible upper-triangular matrices in $G$. Every coset $gK=\{gk:k\in K\}$ has a representative $b\in B$. The exponents $(\nu(a),\nu(d))$ of the diagonal entries $(a,d)$ of $b$ depend only on $gK$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] A prime is an integer greater than one with positive divisors only one and itself ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime)). The $\ell$-adic field is the metric completion of the rationals with dense rational image and extended multiplicative, nonarchimedean absolute value ([math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers), [math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)). Its absolute value vanishes exactly at zero, is multiplicative, and satisfies $|x+y|_\ell\le\max(|x|_\ell,|y|_\ell)$ for all $x,y\in F$ ([math:def-multiplicative-absolute-value-on-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-multiplicative-absolute-value-on-a-field)). For a nonzero rational $y$, the rational valuation $v_\ell(y)$ is an integer and $|y|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(y)}$. In particular $|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$ ([math:lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q), [math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)).

[F2] Under the canonical embedding of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ into $F$, its image $O$ is the closed unit ball ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp), [The valuation ring of the p-adic field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-zp-is-the-valuation-ring-of-qp)). It is a compact open subring under countable choice ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice), [math:cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected)).

[F3] A quotient ring by an ideal has the additive cosets as elements and uses sums and products of representatives ([math:def-quotient-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-ring)). The unique maximal ideal of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is $\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$, and the quotient is canonically isomorphic to $\mathbb F_\ell=\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z$ ([math:cor-maximal-ideal-and-residue-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-maximal-ideal-and-residue-field-of-zp)). For a natural modulus $n$, the congruence-class ring $\mathbb Z/n$ is the quotient ring $\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z$ ([math:prop-integers-modulo-n-as-a-quotient-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-integers-modulo-n-as-a-quotient-ring)). For a prime $\ell$, the congruence-class ring $\mathbb Z/\ell$ is a field, and its classes have unique representatives in $\{0,\ldots,\ell-1\}$ ([math:thm-z-mod-p-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-z-mod-p-is-a-field), [math:thm-standard-representatives-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-standard-representatives-modulo-n)).

[F4] An ideal contains zero and is closed under differences and multiplication by arbitrary ring elements. A maximal ideal is proper and has no strictly larger proper ideal. A commutative ring with $1\ne0$ and a unique maximal ideal is local. Over a field or local ring, the matrix group consists of matrices with a two-sided inverse over that ring. In the quotient by scalars the product is $[g][h]=[gh]$ ([def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)). A unit of a ring is an element with a two-sided multiplicative inverse in that ring. The class of $g$ consists of the scalar multiples $zg$ with $z\in Z$. The entry formula for a matrix product is $(AB)_{ik}=a_{i1}b_{1k}+a_{i2}b_{2k}$ ([def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)). An upper-triangular two-by-two matrix has zero lower-left entry. Its diagonal entries are those in positions $(1,1)$ and $(2,2)$ ([math:def-triangular-and-diagonal-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-triangular-and-diagonal-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring)). For rows $(a,b)$ and $(c,d)$, the determinant is $ad-bc$ by the two terms in the determinant formula. Invertibility over a commutative ring is equivalent to having unit determinant ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix), [math:cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit)).

[F5] A metric vanishes exactly on the diagonal, is symmetric and satisfies the triangle inequality ([math:def-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-space)). In a metric space with distance $d$, the open ball $B(x,r)$ of radius $r>0$ consists of points $y$ with $d(x,y)<r$ ([math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)). A metric-open set contains a ball about each of its points. Open boxes form a basis in finite products, and the open sets of a subspace are traces of ambient open sets ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology), [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). A Hausdorff space separates distinct points by disjoint open neighborhoods. A compact space has a finite subcover for each open cover, and finite products and continuous images of compact spaces are compact ([math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space), [math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), [math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps)).

[F6] A map is continuous at every point exactly when preimages of open sets are open, equivalently when preimages of closed sets are closed. A topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion. With the quotient topology a set in the quotient is open exactly when its preimage under the projection is open ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), [math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group), [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)). An open map sends every open set in its domain to an open set in its codomain ([math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)). In a topological group, right translation $R_g(x)=xg$ is a homeomorphism, a continuous bijection with continuous inverse ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion), [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)).

[F7] For a subgroup $K$ of $G$, a coset $gK$ consists of the products $gk$ with $k\in K$. In particular it contains $g$ by taking $k=1$ ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)).

[F8] Give $\mathbb R$ its usual topology from the metric $d_{\mathbb R}(s,t)=|s-t|$ ([math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space)).

[F9] The image of a group homomorphism is a subgroup of its target group ([math:thm-image-subgroup-and-kernel-normal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-image-subgroup-and-kernel-normal)).

[F10] For a nonzero real $a$, integer powers satisfy $a^0=1$, $a^1=a$ and $a^{-n}=1/a^n$ for positive integers $n$ ([math:def-integer-power](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integer-power)). They satisfy $a^{m+n}=a^ma^n$ for all integers $m,n$ ([math:lem-power-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-power-laws)). Natural powers of a positive real are positive, and $a^n>1$ when $a>1$ and $n$ is a positive integer ([math:lem-power-monotone](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-power-monotone)). Multiplication by a positive real preserves strict order ([math:lem-of-sign-rules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-sign-rules)).

[F11] In a topology, the empty set and the whole space are open, arbitrary unions of open sets are open, and finite intersections of open sets are open. Closed sets are complements of open sets, so a finite intersection of closed sets is closed by taking the complement of a union of open sets ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)).

### Proof

**Given:** A prime $\ell$ and countable choice.

**Goal:** Establish the local valuation, compact open subgroups and triangular coset coordinates.

1.1 For $x\in F^\times$, choose $y\in\mathbb Q$ with $|x-y|_\ell<|x|_\ell$ by density. The strong triangle inequality in both directions gives $|x|_\ell=|y|_\ell>0$. The rational formula gives $|y|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(y)}$ with $v_\ell(y)\in\mathbb Z$. For integers $r<s$, the difference $s-r$ is a positive integer. Since $\ell>1$, its natural powers are positive by [F10]. Their reciprocals are positive as well, since multiplication by a positive natural power preserves order and takes its reciprocal to $1>0$. Thus $\ell^{-r}=\ell^{-s}\ell^{s-r}>\ell^{-s}$ by [F10]. This strict decrease makes $\nu(x)$ unique. For nonzero $x_1,x_2\in F$, multiplicativity and [F10] give $|x_1x_2|_\ell=\ell^{-\nu(x_1)}\ell^{-\nu(x_2)}=\ell^{-(\nu(x_1)+\nu(x_2))}$, so uniqueness gives $\nu(x_1x_2)=\nu(x_1)+\nu(x_2)$. By the unit definition in [F4], a nonzero $x$ belongs to $O^\times$ exactly when $x$ and $x^{-1}$ both lie in the closed unit ball, which is equivalent to $|x|_\ell=1$ and hence to $\nu(x)=0$. To see that the strict unit ball is $\ell O$, suppose $|z|_\ell<1$. Then either $z=0$ or, because $\ell^0=1$ and the powers $\ell^{-r}$ strictly decrease in $r$, we have $\nu(z)\ge1$ and $|z|_\ell\le\ell^{-1}$. Hence $z/\ell\in O$. Conversely $|\ell u|_\ell\le\ell^{-1}<1$ for $u\in O$. [F1, F2, F4, F5, F10]

1.2 Write $f:\mathbb Z_\ell\to O$ for the isomorphism onto the image in [F2]. For any ideal $J$ of $\mathbb Z_\ell$, its image $f(J)$ contains $0$ and is closed under differences and multiplication by elements of $O$, since $f(a)-f(b)=f(a-b)$ and $uf(a)=f(f^{-1}(u)a)$ for $a,b\in J$ and $u\in O$. The same argument for $f^{-1}$ sends ideals of $O$ to ideals of $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Each map preserves properness and strict inclusion by bijectivity, so by [F4] they carry maximal ideals to maximal ideals. Because $f$ preserves $1$ and multiplication, $f(\ell\mathbb Z_\ell)=\ell O$. Thus $O$, a commutative subring of $F$ containing $1\ne0$, has the unique maximal ideal $\ell O$ by [F3] and is local by [F4]. The map $a+\ell\mathbb Z_\ell\mapsto f(a)+\ell O$ is well defined and bijective because two representatives differ by an element of $\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$ exactly when their images differ by an element of $\ell O$. By the operations in [F3] it preserves sums and products, so $O/\ell O$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb Z_\ell/\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$, which [F3] identifies with the ideal quotient $\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z$. Since $\ell>1$ is an integer, it is a positive natural modulus, so [F3] identifies that quotient with the congruence-class ring $\mathbb Z/\ell$, a field with exactly $\ell$ elements by the unique representatives. Thus the matrix group over $O$ in [F4] applies. Entrywise inclusion $O\subset F$ preserves matrix products and the identity, and takes each two-sided inverse over $O$ to the same inverse over $F$. It embeds $K$ as a subgroup of $G$. [F1, F2, F3, F4]

1.3 If $y\in B(x,r)$, the triangle inequality gives $B(y,r-d(x,y))\subseteq B(x,r)$, so open balls are open in the metric topology. Addition is continuous since $|(x+y)-(a+b)|_\ell\le\max(|x-a|_\ell,|y-b|_\ell)$. If $|x-a|_\ell<1$, then $|x|_\ell\le\max(1,|a|_\ell)$ and $|xy-ab|_\ell\le\max(\max(1,|a|_\ell)|y-b|_\ell,|b|_\ell|x-a|_\ell)$, giving continuity of multiplication at $(a,b)$. Negation preserves distances. For $a\ne0$, if $|x-a|_\ell<|a|_\ell$ then $|x|_\ell=|a|_\ell$ and $|x^{-1}-a^{-1}|_\ell=|x-a|_\ell/|a|_\ell^2$. These estimates give continuity of inversion away from zero. They also show that the absolute value is constant on the ball of radius $|a|_\ell$ about each $a\ne0$. At zero, $d_{\mathbb R}(|x|_\ell,0)=|x|_\ell$ is the distance from $x$ to zero in $F$. Thus the absolute value is continuous into $\mathbb R$ with the topology in [F8]. For distinct points $x,y$ of a metric space, symmetry and the triangle inequality give $0=d(x,x)\le 2d(x,y)$, and separation gives $d(x,y)>0$. The open balls of radius $d(x,y)/2$ about $x$ and $y$ are disjoint. A point $z$ in both would give $d(x,y)\le d(x,z)+d(z,y)<d(x,y)$ by symmetry and the triangle inequality. In particular each singleton is closed, since its complement is the union of open balls avoiding it and hence is open by [F11]. Applying this separation in any differing coordinate makes $F^4$ Hausdorff, as is its subspace $G$. [F1, F5, F6, F8, F11]

2.1 Each projection from a finite product is continuous because the preimage of an open set is an open box. A tuple of continuous functions is continuous into a finite product because the preimage of an open box is a finite intersection of open preimages, and the preimage of a union of such boxes is a union of their preimages. Both are open by [F11]. A map with image in a subspace is continuous as a map to that subspace if its ambient-valued map is continuous, because the preimage of an open trace is the preimage of the ambient open set. For continuous $g:X\to Y$ and $f:Y\to T$ between topological spaces and open $W\subseteq T$, the identity $(f\circ g)^{-1}(W)=g^{-1}(f^{-1}(W))$ makes the preimage open by two applications of [F6]. Thus the composite is continuous by [F6]. The determinant is continuous by its entry formula, so $G=\{\det\ne0\}$ is open in $F^4$. Each entry of the matrix product is a sum of products of the input coordinates, so multiplication is continuous by the tuple and subspace rules. Swapping the diagonal entries, negating the off-diagonal entries and dividing by the determinant gives the two-sided inverse, as multiplying in both orders verifies. The inverse entries are continuous by the operations of the field from step 1.3. The same tuple and subspace rules give continuity of inversion, so $G$ is a topological group. The product topology on $O^4$ is its subspace topology from $F^4$ because open boxes restrict coordinate by coordinate. Hence $O^4$ is compact and open in $F^4$. By the determinant and unit criteria, $K=\{A\in O^4:|\det A|_\ell=1\}$. For every real $s\ne1$, the real metric ball of radius $d_{\mathbb R}(s,1)/2$ about $s$ avoids $1$, so $\{1\}$ is closed in $\mathbb R$.
