Composing the determinant on $O^4$ with the absolute value from step 1.3 gives the continuous function $A\mapsto |\det A|_\ell$. Its preimage of $\{1\}$ is $K$, so $K$ is closed in $O^4$. It is open in $O^4$ because the absolute value is locally constant at each nonzero determinant. It is therefore open in $G$. For any open cover of $K$, take all open subsets of $O^4$ whose traces belong to the cover and adjoin $O^4\setminus K$. A finite subcover of $O^4$ restricts to a finite subcover of $K$, proving compactness. [F2, F4, F5, F6, F8, F11, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 1.3]

2.2 Let $(c,d)$ be the bottom row of $g\in G$. It is nonzero by the determinant criterion. Divide both entries by an entry $t$ of maximal absolute value. Then $c'=c/t$ and $d'=d/t$ belong to $O$, and at least one is a unit. Choose $u,v\in O$ with $c'u+d'v=1$ by taking the inverse of a unit entry for its coefficient and zero for the other. The matrix with columns $(d',-c')^{\mathsf T}$ and $(u,v)^{\mathsf T}$ has determinant one and belongs to $K$. Right multiplication by it changes the bottom row to $(0,t)$, so the resulting invertible matrix in $gK$ is upper triangular. [F1, F2, F4, F7, step 1.1, step 1.2]

3.1 By steps 1.3 and 2.1, both off-diagonal entries and the difference of the diagonal entries are continuous functions on $G$. The scalar subgroup $Z$ is the intersection of their three zero preimages, which are closed by [F6] because $\{0\}$ is closed in $F$. Thus $Z$ is closed by [F11]. Write $q:G\to G/Z$ for the quotient projection. For open $U\subseteq G$, the preimage of $q(U)$ is $UZ=\bigcup_{z\in Z}Uz$. By step 2.1 and [F6], each $Uz$ is open because it is the preimage of $U$ under the inverse of the homeomorphism $R_z$. The union $q^{-1}(q(U))$ is open by [F11], and the quotient topology makes $q(U)$ open. Hence $q$ is an open map, and it is continuous by the quotient topology. Images and preimages of basic open rectangles under $q\times q$ are products of the corresponding images and preimages under $q$, so they are open. Every open set in either product is a union of such rectangles, and images and preimages preserve unions. By [F11] and [F6], $q\times q$ is therefore open and continuous. It is surjective, so if a subset has open preimage under $q\times q$, the subset itself is open as the image of that preimage. Write $i$ and $m$ for inversion and multiplication on $G$, and $\bar i$ and $\bar m$ for the corresponding operations on $G/Z$. For open $W\subseteq G/Z$, the quotient operations give $q^{-1}(\bar i^{-1}(W))=(q\circ i)^{-1}(W)$ and $(q\times q)^{-1}(\bar m^{-1}(W))=(q\circ m)^{-1}(W)$. The sets on the right are open by the composition rule in step 2.1 and [F6]. The quotient criteria give openness of $\bar i^{-1}(W)$ and $\bar m^{-1}(W)$, so [F6] makes the induced operations continuous. [F4, F5, F6, F11, step 1.3, step 2.1]

4.1 The description of scalar cosets in [F4] gives $q(g)=q(h)$ exactly when $g=zh$ for some $z\in Z$, equivalently $gh^{-1}\in Z$. By composition in step 2.1, the second projection followed by inversion is continuous. Together with the first projection it gives the continuous map $(u,v)\mapsto (u,v^{-1})$ by the tuple rule in step 2.1. Composing with multiplication gives continuity of $(u,v)\mapsto uv^{-1}$. For distinct classes $q(g)$ and $q(h)$, the point $(g,h)$ belongs to the open preimage of $G\setminus Z$ under this map. The product topology gives an open rectangle $U\times V$ around $(g,h)$ in that preimage. If $u\in U$ and $v\in V$, then $uv^{-1}\notin Z$, so $q(u)\ne q(v)$. Thus $q(U)$ and $q(V)$ are disjoint open neighborhoods, proving the Hausdorff property. The quotient product in [F4] makes $q$ a homomorphism, so its restriction to the subgroup $K$ in step 1.2 is a homomorphism. By [F9], its image $\bar K=q(K)$ is a subgroup of $G/Z$. It is open and compact by openness and continuity of $q$. [F4, F5, F6, F9, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 If $b,b'\in B$ represent the same coset, then $b'\in b'K=bK$. Thus $b'=bk$ for some $k\in K$. By the entry formula in [F4], the product of two upper-triangular matrices has zero lower-left entry, and each of its diagonal entries is the product of the corresponding diagonal entries of the factors. The inverse formula of step 2.1 preserves a zero lower-left entry. Hence $k=b^{-1}b'$ and its inverse are upper triangular. As $k\in K$, both $k$ and $k^{-1}$ have entries in $O$. The diagonal entry rule applied to $kk^{-1}=I$ gives inverses in $O$ for the diagonal entries of $k$. Applied to $b'=bk$, the same rule gives the diagonal entries of $b'$ as those of $b$ multiplied by the corresponding units of $O$. Their valuations therefore agree. [F4, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 2.2] ∎


## Normalized weight-two cuspidal newforms

[Open item](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform) · level 1


### Definition

Assume countable choice, meaning that a sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. Use the weight-two cusp space $S_2(N,\epsilon)$ with $f|_2\gamma=\epsilon(d_\gamma)f$ on $\Gamma_0(N)$ and $q=\exp(2\pi i z)$ at infinity. For $M$ a proper positive divisor of $N$ and $\epsilon_M$ a Dirichlet character modulo $M$ inducing $\epsilon$ by reduction on units, use the lower-level images $z\mapsto g(tz)$ with $g\in S_2(M,\epsilon_M)$ and $t$ a positive divisor of $N/M$. The old subspace is the complex span of those images. Write $W$ for the old subspace. Use the Petersson inner product
$$\langle f,g\rangle_N=\int_{\Gamma_0(N)\backslash\mathfrak H} f(z)\overline{g(z)}\,dx\,dy,\qquad z=x+iy.$$
Here $dx\,dy$ denotes planar Lebesgue measure $\lambda_2$. The quotient integral is the complex Lebesgue integral on a measurable fundamental region. For such a region $F$, there is a $\Gamma_0(N)$-invariant set $X\subseteq\mathfrak H$ with null complement such that $F\cap X$ meets each orbit of the fractional linear action of $\Gamma_0(N)$ in $X$ exactly once. The new subspace is the orthogonal complement of $W$, consisting of those $f\in S_2(N,\epsilon)$ for which $\langle f,g\rangle_N=0$ for every $g\in W$.

For a positive integer $m$ use the classical Hecke formula in weight two
$$(T_m f)(z)=m\sum_{\substack{ad=m\\a,d>0}}\epsilon(a)d^{-2}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}f\bigl((az+b)/d\bigr).$$
A normalized weight-two cuspidal newform, also called a normalized primitive form, is a cusp form in the new subspace with $a_1=1$ in its expansion $\sum_{n\ge1}a_n q^n$, and with $T_m f$ equal to a scalar multiple of $f$ for every $m\ge1$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The cusp-space definition takes the character to be a group homomorphism on the units. Its justification gives the slash composition rule, holomorphy of slash transforms, the projective action on rational boundary points and determinant-one representatives of all these points. The justification also gives the period-$N$ assertion for every cusp transform at level $N$, uniqueness of the expansion at infinity and the complex vector-space structure of each cusp space with pointwise operations. [def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form)

[F2] Induction of a Dirichlet character agrees with the smaller-level character on units at the larger level. Dirichlet character values at units are roots of unity. [math:def-induced-dirichlet-character](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-induced-dirichlet-character) [math:lem-dirichlet-character-values](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-dirichlet-character-values)

[F3] Determinant is multiplicative on square matrices over a commutative ring. Entrywise integer congruence is preserved by matrix products. [math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative) [math:lem-congruence-respects-integer-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-congruence-respects-integer-arithmetic)

[F4] In a complex vector space the span is the smallest linear subspace containing a given subset. Finite sums in a commutative monoid are independent of the enumeration. [math:def-linear-combination-and-span](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-combination-and-span) [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid)

[F5] The fractional linear action of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ on $\mathfrak H$ descends to the quotient $\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb Z)=\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)/\{\pm I\}$. An orbit consists of the translates of a point, and the stabilizer consists of the elements that fix the point. For the modular group, the open standard region has at most one point per orbit, its closure meets every orbit, and there is no nonidentity stabilizer in its interior in $\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb Z)$. A right coset of a subgroup $\Gamma$ is a set $\Gamma\sigma$. The right cosets $Ha$ and $Hb$ coincide exactly when $ab^{-1}\in H$. The finite index $[G:H]$ equals the number of right cosets of $H$ in $G$. [math:def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-group-action-on-the-upper-half-plane) [math:def-orbit-and-stabilizer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-orbit-and-stabilizer) [math:thm-standard-fundamental-domain-for-the-modular-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-standard-fundamental-domain-for-the-modular-group) [math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset) [math:lem-coset-membership-and-equality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-membership-and-equality) [math:def-index](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-index)

[F6] Under countable choice, planar Lebesgue measure is complete, gives the usual area of half-open rectangles and includes all Borel sets. A null set is a measurable set of measure zero. A countable union of null sets is null, and in a complete space every subset of a null set is measurable and null. The zero set of a nonzero real polynomial in two variables is null. A $C^1$ diffeomorphism between open subsets of the plane preserves both nullity and Lebesgue measurability. [math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice) [math:thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lebesgue-measure-is-a-complete-measure) [math:thm-borel-sets-are-lebesgue-measurable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-borel-sets-are-lebesgue-measurable) [math:def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere) [math:prop-null-sets-form-a-sigma-ideal-in-a-complete-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-null-sets-form-a-sigma-ideal-in-a-complete-space) [math:lem-nonzero-real-polynomial-zero-set-is-lebesgue-null](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nonzero-real-polynomial-zero-set-is-lebesgue-null) [math:lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-null-sets-to-null-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-null-sets-to-null-sets) [math:lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-measurable-sets-to-lebesgue-measurable-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-c-one-diffeomorphisms-map-lebesgue-measurable-sets-to-lebesgue-measurable-sets)

[F7] The quotient rule gives the complex derivative of a fractional linear map on the set where the denominator is nonzero. At each point where two functions are complex differentiable, any constant-coefficient linear combination of them is complex differentiable there. Constant functions have derivative zero. At a complex differentiability point the total real differential is multiplication by the complex derivative. The real determinant of this linear map is the squared modulus of the derivative, by the complex-linear determinant formula in dimension one. A bijection between open planar sets is a $C^1$ diffeomorphism when it and its inverse have continuous real derivatives. The change-of-variables factor is the absolute value of the determinant of the real derivative. Complex conjugation is multiplicative and $u\overline u=|u|^2$. [math:thm-algebra-of-complex-derivatives](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-algebra-of-complex-derivatives) [math:thm-complex-differentiability-real-linearity-wirtinger-and-cauchy-riemann](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-differentiability-real-linearity-wirtinger-and-cauchy-riemann) [math:lem-real-jacobian-determinant-of-a-complex-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-jacobian-determinant-of-a-complex-linear-map) [math:def-ck-euclidean-maps-and-diffeomorphisms](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ck-euclidean-maps-and-diffeomorphisms) [math:def-jacobian-determinant-of-a-c-one-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-jacobian-determinant-of-a-c-one-map) [math:def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus) [math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws)

[F8] Under countable choice the change-of-variables formula applies to nonnegative Lebesgue measurable functions and $C^1$ diffeomorphisms between open sets. The nonnegative integral is monotone by its supremum definition and agrees with the simple integral on nonnegative simple measurable functions. In particular it gives $b\lambda_2(B)$ on a constant $b\ge0$ times the indicator of a measurable set $B$ of finite area. A real function on a measure space is measurable if every strict superlevel set is measurable. A nonnegative measurable function with finite range in $[0,+\infty)$ is simple. For a finite constant $b\ge0$ and a measurable set $B$, the function $b\mathbf{1}_B$ takes values in $\{0,b\}$ and its strict superlevel sets are among the empty set, $B$ and the whole space. Thus it is nonnegative simple measurable. The positive and negative parts of a real measurable function are measurable, as are differences of finite real measurable functions. Multiplying any nonnegative measurable function by an indicator of a measurable set preserves measurability, and its integral over a measurable null set is zero. Indefinite integrals of nonnegative functions are measures, and indefinite integrals of integrable complex functions are countably additive. A complex measurable function is integrable when its absolute integral is finite. Its integral is computed from the positive and negative parts of its real and imaginary parts, and is complex-linear. [math:thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-c-one-change-of-variables-for-nonnegative-lebesgue-measurable-functions) [math:def-nonnegative-lebesgue-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-lebesgue-integral) [math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function) [math:def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function) [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral) [math:def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function) [math:prop-closure-properties-of-measurable-functions-used-by-the-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-closure-properties-of-measurable-functions-used-by-the-integral) [math:cor-integral-over-a-null-set-vanishes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-integral-over-a-null-set-vanishes) [math:thm-indefinite-integral-of-a-nonnegative-function-is-a-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-indefinite-integral-of-a-nonnegative-function-is-a-measure) [math:prop-indefinite-integral-of-an-integrable-function-is-countably-additive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-indefinite-integral-of-an-integrable-function-is-countably-additive) [math:def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals) [math:thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one)

[F9] On an open subset of $\mathbb C$, holomorphy means complex differentiability at every point. [math:def-complex-differentiability-holomorphic-and-entire](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-differentiability-holomorphic-and-entire) A holomorphic function on an open subset of $\mathbb C$ is continuous there by complex differentiability at each point. The period-one expansion theorem gives uniform exponential decay at infinity for a holomorphic function of period one that tends uniformly to zero at infinity. A continuous real function on a nonempty closed bounded subset of the plane is bounded. A geometric series of ratio between zero and one converges. [math:cor-complex-differentiability-implies-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-complex-differentiability-implies-continuity) [math:thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp) [math:thm-euclidean-heine-borel-pseudocompactness-and-extreme-values](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-euclidean-heine-borel-pseudocompactness-and-extreme-values) [math:thm-geometric-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-geometric-series)

[F10] A complex inner product is linear in its first argument, Hermitian and positive definite. The orthogonal complement of a subspace is the set of vectors whose inner product with every element of that subspace vanishes. [math:def-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inner-product-space) [math:def-orthogonal-complement](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-orthogonal-complement)

[F11] A Dirichlet character is primitive if it is not induced from a proper divisor of its modulus. Every Dirichlet character modulo $N$ is induced by a unique primitive character of modulus dividing $N$. That primitive character's modulus is the conductor of the level character. [math:def-primitive-dirichlet-character-and-conductor](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-primitive-dirichlet-character-and-conductor) [math:thm-dirichlet-character-primitive-induction](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-dirichlet-character-primitive-induction) The weight-two Hecke formula in Best et al. assumes that the primitive inducing character takes the value $1$ at $-1$. [Best et al., §6.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2002.04717#page=18)

[F12] The trace of a sigma-algebra on a subset consists of the intersections of its members with that subset and is a sigma-algebra on the subset. A measure on a sigma-algebra is zero on the empty set and countably additive on disjoint measurable sets. A sigma-algebra is closed under finite intersections and set differences. [math:def-trace-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-trace-sigma-algebra) [math:thm-trace-is-a-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-trace-is-a-sigma-algebra) [math:thm-sigma-algebra-closure-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-sigma-algebra-closure-laws) [math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure)

[F13] The subgroup $\Gamma_1(N)$ of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ is defined by $a\equiv d\equiv1$ and $c\equiv0\pmod N$. [Best et al., §4.1, Eq. (4.1.1)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2002.04717#page=6) For two cusp forms on a congruence subgroup $H$, the Petersson product in Diamond and Im integrates $f(z)\overline{g(z)}y^k$ against $dx\,dy/y^2$ over a fundamental region and divides by $[\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb Z):\overline H]$, where $\overline H$ is the projective image of $H$. [Diamond and Im, §3.6, Eq. (3.6.1)](https://wstein.org/edu/Fall2003/252/references/diamond-im/Diamond-Im-Modular_forms_and_modular_curves.pdf)

[F14] A continuous map between metric spaces has open preimages of open sets, by the implication (a) to (b). [math:thm-metric-continuity-characterisations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-continuity-characterisations)

### Justification

**Given:** Countable choice, the level $N$, the character $\epsilon$ and the slash action on the cusp spaces in the definition.

1.1 Put $A=\operatorname{diag}(t,1)$ for a lower-level image. If $\gamma\in\Gamma_0(N)$, the matrix $A\gamma A^{-1}$ has integral entries, determinant one, lower left entry $c_\gamma/t$ divisible by $M$ and lower right entry $d_\gamma$. The latter is a unit modulo $N$ by the determinant-one relation and hence also modulo $M$. Using $A\gamma=(A\gamma A^{-1})A$, the slash law and $g|_2 A=t\,g(tz)$ give the required transformation law with character $\epsilon$. For any $\sigma\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$, the point $A\sigma\infty$ is rational or infinity by the projective boundary action. Use the cusp representative construction to choose $\sigma'$ with $\sigma'\infty=A\sigma\infty$. The positive-determinant matrix $(\sigma')^{-1}A\sigma$ fixes infinity, so it is upper triangular. Its slash factor is constant and its action multiplies imaginary parts by a positive constant. The uniform cusp decay of $g|_2\sigma'$ now implies that of $(z\mapsto g(tz))|_2\sigma$, and $g(tz)$ is holomorphic by [F1]. Thus all lower-level images lie in the complex vector space $S_2(N,\epsilon)$ supplied by [F1]. Their span in this space is a subspace, including the zero subspace if there are no images. [F1, F2, F3, F4]

1.2 For any $\sigma\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ the map $\varphi_\sigma:z\mapsto\sigma z$ is a bijection of the open set $\mathfrak H$ with inverse $\varphi_{\sigma^{-1}}$ by [F5]. Its denominator is nonzero on $\mathfrak H$ and the derivative of $z\mapsto\sigma z$ is $(c_\sigma z+d_\sigma)^{-2}$ by [F7]. Its real differential is multiplication by this complex number. The real matrix of multiplication varies continuously with $z$ because $(c_\sigma z+d_\sigma)^{-2}$ is continuous there. This applies to the inverse matrix as well, so $\varphi_\sigma$ is a $C^1$ diffeomorphism of $\mathfrak H$. In complex dimension one the matrix of multiplication has that number as its sole complex entry, so the real determinant of the differential is its squared modulus. Thus the absolute Jacobian determinant is $J_\sigma(z)=|c_\sigma z+d_\sigma|^{-4}$. [F5, F7]

2.1 Write $\Gamma=\Gamma_0(N)$ and $D=\{z=x+iy\in\mathfrak H:|x|<1/2,\ |z|>1\}$. Its boundary in $\mathfrak H$ is null since it lies in the zero set of $(4x^2-1)(x^2+y^2-1)$. By step 1.2 and [F6] each modular translate of the boundary is null. The modular group is countable because bounding the absolute values of its integer entries leaves only finitely many matrices. The union of all modular translates of the boundary is therefore null by [F6]. The complement is invariant under the full modular group. On it, every full modular orbit meets $D$ once with trivial stabilizer modulo $\{\pm I\}$, since an orbit meeting the closure and avoiding the boundary meets the interior. Two integral determinant-one matrices congruent entrywise modulo $N$ lie in the same right coset of $\Gamma$ by [F5], since their product $\gamma_1\gamma_2^{-1}$ is congruent to the identity and therefore belongs to $\Gamma$. There are at most $N^4$ entrywise residue patterns. Choose a finite list of representatives $\sigma_i$ of the right cosets $\Gamma\sigma_i$. Put $F_0=\bigcup_i\sigma_i D$. Every full modular translate of a point in $D$ lies in the $\Gamma$-orbit of one of its translates by the $\sigma_i$. If $\sigma_i z$ and $\sigma_j w$ with $z,w\in D$ are $\Gamma$-equivalent, then $z=w$ by the full modular orbit condition, and the class of $(\gamma\sigma_j)^{-1}\sigma_i$ fixes $z$ in $\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ for some $\gamma\in\Gamma$. The stabilizer is trivial there, so $\sigma_i=\pm\gamma\sigma_j$. Since $\{\pm I\}\subseteq\Gamma$, this gives $i=j$. In particular $F_0$ is a disjoint union of open sets and is a fundamental region. [F1, F3, F5, F6, step 1.2]

2.2 Fix $f,g\in S_2(N,\epsilon)$ and put $h=f\overline g$. Use the Jacobian from step 1.2. The slash formula gives $h(\sigma z)J_\sigma(z)=(f|_2\sigma)(z)\overline{(g|_2\sigma)(z)}$. In particular $h(\gamma z)J_\gamma(z)=h(z)$ for $\gamma\in\Gamma$, because $\epsilon(d_\gamma)$ has modulus one. The same density identity holds for $|h|$. Since $f$ and $g$ are holomorphic on $\mathfrak H$, they are continuous by [F9]. Complex conjugation preserves distances, and the identity $uv-u'v'=u(v-v')+(u-u')v'$ shows continuity of multiplication since $u$ stays bounded near $u'$. Thus $h=f\overline g$ is continuous. Taking modulus, real part or imaginary part preserves continuity since each of these maps changes by at most the modulus of the difference of its inputs. Take the trace sigma-algebra $\mathcal L(\mathbb R^2)|_{\mathfrak H}$ of the planar Lebesgue sigma-algebra and define $\lambda_{\mathfrak H}(A)=\lambda_2(A)$ for each trace set $A$. Such sets are ambient Lebesgue measurable because $\mathfrak H$ is open and hence Lebesgue measurable by [F6]. The value on the empty set and countable additivity on disjoint trace sets follow directly from those for $\lambda_2$, since their unions are the same in $\mathfrak H$ and in the plane. This gives the measure space used on $\mathfrak H$. For $u=|h|$, $\operatorname{Re}h$ or $\operatorname{Im}h$ and each real $a$, the set $\{z\in\mathfrak H:u(z)>a\}$ is the preimage of the open ray $(a,\infty)$ under $u$, so it is open in $\mathfrak H$ by [F14] and hence open in the plane. It is Borel and belongs to the trace sigma-algebra, so the criterion in [F8] makes each of the three functions measurable on this space. The positive and negative parts of the real and imaginary parts are measurable by [F8]. Consequently $|h|$ and each of those four nonnegative functions remain measurable after multiplication by the indicator of any Lebesgue measurable subset of $\mathfrak H$. Their differences give the measurable real and imaginary parts of the complex restriction as well. [F1, F2, F6, F7, F8, F9, F12, F14, step 1.2]

3.1 Apply change of variables using the diffeomorphisms in step 1.2 and the measurable absolute integrand in step 2.2. Then the absolute integral on $\sigma_i D$ is the integral on $D$ of $|(f|_2\sigma_i)\overline{(g|_2\sigma_i)}|$. The slash transforms are holomorphic by [F1], so the continuity argument of step 2.2 applies to the modulus of their product with one factor conjugated. By [F1], $z\mapsto(f|_2\sigma_i)(Nz)$ and $z\mapsto(g|_2\sigma_i)(Nz)$ are holomorphic and have period one. They tend uniformly to zero as the imaginary part tends to infinity. Apply the period-one expansion theorem to these two rescaled functions and undo the scaling. The product is then bounded by $C e^{-cy}$ for $y\ge Y$, for some $C,c>0$ and $Y\ge2$. The Borel set $K=D\cap\{x+iy:y<Y\}$ lies in a bounded rectangle and has finite area. Its closure is compact and lies in $\mathfrak H$, since $y\ge\sqrt{3}/2$ there. Hence the modulus has a finite bound $A\ge0$ on $K$ by [F9]. The function $A\mathbf{1}_K$ is a nonnegative simple measurable majorant of the modulus restricted to $K$, with range contained in $\{0,A\}$. By [F8] its integral is $A\lambda_2(K)<\infty$. Each strip $B_k=\{x+iy:|x|<1/2,\ Y+k\le y<Y+k+1\}$, for $k\ge0$, has area one because adding the omitted vertical edge gives a half-open unit rectangle and that edge is null. The majorant $Ce^{-c(Y+k)}\mathbf{1}_{B_k}$ is nonnegative simple measurable by [F8], with range contained in $\{0,Ce^{-c(Y+k)}\}$. It bounds the modulus restricted to $D\cap B_k$ and has integral $Ce^{-c(Y+k)}$ by the unit area of $B_k$. Countable additivity bounds the tail by the convergent sum of these bounds. Thus the integral of $|h|$ on the finite union $F_0$ is finite. [F1, F6, F8, F9, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2]

3.2 For the source comparison, take $\Gamma_1(N)$ from [F13], put $P=\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ and let $\overline{\Gamma_1}$ and $\overline{\Gamma_0}$ be the images in $P$ of $\Gamma_1(N)$ and $\Gamma_0(N)$. The defining congruences give $\Gamma_1(N)\subseteq\Gamma_0(N)$. If $\gamma\in\Gamma_1(N)$ then $\epsilon(d_\gamma)=\epsilon(1)=1$ by the character definition in [F1], so the fixed-character forms have trivial slash action on $\Gamma_1(N)$. They still vanish at all cusps since the decay condition in [F1] holds for every determinant-one cusp representative. Two integral determinant-one matrices congruent modulo $N$ are in the same right coset of $\Gamma_1(N)$ by [F5], since their quotient in the order used in step 2.1 is the identity modulo $N$ and therefore belongs to $\Gamma_1(N)$. For either subgroup, projection onto $P$ sends its right cosets onto the right cosets of its projective image. Both projective indices in $P$ are therefore finite by [F5]. The right cosets of $\overline{\Gamma_1}$ in $\overline{\Gamma_0}$ are among its right cosets in $P$. Let $r=[\overline{\Gamma_0}:\overline{\Gamma_1}]$ be their number by [F5] and choose lifts $\tau_j\in\Gamma_0(N)$ of their representatives. Write $X$ for the invariant conull set of step 2.1, on which the projective action is free. The union of $\tau_j(F_0\cap X)$ is a measurable fundamental region for $\Gamma_1(N)$ on $X$. Indeed, each $\overline{\Gamma_0}$-orbit meets $F_0\cap X$ once. For that representative $z$, the points $\tau_j z$ cover the $\overline{\Gamma_1}$-orbits inside its $\overline{\Gamma_0}$-orbit by the coset decomposition, and two such translates can be $\overline{\Gamma_1}$-equivalent only if their right cosets coincide, by freeness at $z$. [F1, F3, F5, F6, F13, step 1.2, step 2.1]

4.1 Let $E$ be another measurable fundamental region and use the trace measure $\lambda_{\mathfrak H}$ from step 2.2 for the integrals. Work on the intersection of the invariant conull sets for $F_0$ and $E$, whose complement in the half-plane is the union of two null sets. By [F6], the discarded sets are measurable and null. By [F8], the integrals of $|h|$ and the four nonnegative parts from step 2.2 over either discarded set are zero. Enumerate the elements $\gamma_i$ of $\Gamma$ by increasing maximum absolute matrix entry and order each finite group of ties lexicographically. Partition the conull part of $F_0$ according to the first $\gamma$ in this enumeration sending a point into $E$. Write $X_*$ for the common conull set. Each set $A_i=F_0\cap X_*\cap\gamma_i^{-1}E$ is measurable by [F12] and [F6], since $F_0$, $X_*$ and $E$ are measurable and the inverse modular map is a diffeomorphism by step 1.2. Remove the finitely many earlier sets $A_j$ from $A_i$ in succession. The first-hit piece $A_i\setminus\bigcup_{j<i}A_j$ is therefore measurable by the set-difference rule in [F12]. Its image under $\gamma_i$ is measurable by [F6]. The images partition the conull part of $E$: they cover it by orbit coverage, and an intersection would give the same representative in $F_0$, which belongs to only one piece. Applying change of variables on $\mathfrak H$ with the indicator of each image and the density identity from step 2.2 equates the absolute integrals on paired pieces. Countable additivity of the nonnegative integral now gives the same finite absolute integral on $E$ and $F_0$. The same change of variables applies to the positive and negative parts of $\operatorname{Re}h$ and $\operatorname{Im}h$, since the Jacobian is positive. The four integrals on each piece are finite, so recombination equates the complex integrals on paired pieces. The absolute integral over $E\cup F_0$ is bounded by the sum of the two absolute integrals since the indefinite integral of $|h|$ is a measure. Thus $h$ restricted to $E\cup F_0$ is integrable. Countable additivity of its integral, including the discarded null pieces with zero contribution, gives equality of the complex integrals on $E$ and $F_0$. [F6, F8, F12, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 3.1]

5.1 Step 2.2 gives $h(\tau_j z)J_{\tau_j}(z)=h(z)$, since every $\tau_j$ belongs to $\Gamma_0(N)$. Applying the same change-of-variables argument as in step 4.1 to these finitely many disjoint translates shows that the integral on the $\Gamma_1(N)$ region equals $r$ times the integral on $F_0$. The source factor $y^k/y^2$ is one at $k=2$. Each right coset of $\overline{\Gamma_0}$ in $P$ splits into $r$ right cosets of $\overline{\Gamma_1}$ by right multiplication of its subgroup decomposition, so [F5] gives $[P:\overline{\Gamma_1}]=r[P:\overline{\Gamma_0}]$. The normalized Petersson product of [F13] on the fixed-character forms is therefore $[P:\overline{\Gamma_0}]^{-1}\langle f,g\rangle_N$. This factor is positive, so the vanishing conditions agree. [F5, F8, F13, step 2.2, step 4.1, step 3.2]

5.2 The complex integral is linear in $f$. Conjugation of the integral conjugates the integrand by the real and imaginary part formula, so the pairing is Hermitian. For $f\ne0$, the nonzero locus is the nonempty preimage of the open set $\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}$ under $f$. It is open by [F14] and the continuity from [F9]. It therefore intersects the invariant conull set for $F_0$. Take a $\Gamma$-translate of such a point in $F_0$. The transformation law keeps $f$ nonzero there. Since $F_0$ is open and $|f|^2$ is continuous by step 2.2, $|f|^2$ is bounded below by some $b>0$ on a small half-open rectangle $B$ of positive area inside $F_0$. By [F8], $b\mathbf{1}_B$ is nonnegative simple measurable with range contained in $\{0,b\}$. Monotonicity and the indicator integral in [F8] give $\int_{F_0}|f|^2\,dx\,dy\ge b\lambda_2(B)>0$. Thus the pairing is a complex inner product. The old span $W$ is a subspace by step 1.1, so [F10] applies to the displayed vanishing condition. For each $g\in W$ that condition is preserved by linear combinations in the first argument, and it holds for $f=0$. Hence the new subspace is a linear subspace. [F1, F6, F7, F8, F9, F10, F14, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 4.1]

6.1 Each summand in the Hecke formula is a constant multiple of the weight-two slash transform by $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\0&d\end{pmatrix}$, whose determinant is $ad>0$. The slash transforms are holomorphic by [F1], so by [F9] they are complex differentiable at every point of $\mathfrak H$. The derivative rules in [F7] give pointwise complex differentiability for scalar multiples, sums of two such functions and the zero function. These functions are holomorphic by [F9]. The zero function is the identity for pointwise addition, which is associative and commutative. Thus the holomorphic functions form the additive commutative monoid required by [F4]. Repeated addition yields a holomorphic finite Hecke sum, and the scalar equality is well defined as equality of functions there. The expansion at infinity in the cusp-space definition makes the coefficient-one condition unambiguous. By [F11], $\epsilon$ is induced by a primitive character $\chi$ whose modulus divides $N$ and equals the conductor of $\epsilon$. The matrix $-I$ lies in $\Gamma_0(N)$ and acts trivially under the weight-two slash formula, since its fractional linear action is the identity and its slash factor is $(-1)^{-2}=1$. For a nonzero $f\in S_2(N,\epsilon)$, the transformation law applied to $-I$ gives $f=\epsilon(-1)f$, hence $\epsilon(-1)=1$. The integer $-1$ is a unit modulo $N$, so $\chi(-1)=\epsilon(-1)=1$ by [F2]. Thus the parity hypothesis in [F11] holds for the source formula. On the terms with $\gcd(a,N)=1$, [F2] gives $\epsilon(a)=\chi(a)$ and $a^2/m=m/d^2$ since $ad=m$. Terms with $\gcd(a,N)>1$ have $\epsilon(a)=0$ by the definition of the level character, so including them gives the formula in the Definition. For $f=0$, the displayed sum is zero for any level character. Whenever the cusp space contains a nonzero form, the displayed operators therefore agree with the source operators at every positive integer $m$. The common eigenform condition uses all those indices. [F1, F2, F4, F7, F9, F11] ∎

### Source notes

Best et al., *Computing classical modular forms*, §4.1 gives the normalized cuspidal eigenform convention. Section 6.1 uses the scaled lower-level forms in a basis and gives the classical Hecke formula. In weight two its prefactor on the positive factorization $ad=m$ is $a^2/m=m/d^2$. Extending the level character by zero on nonunits accounts for the restriction $\gcd(a,N)=1$ in that source. The construction of the pairing and the admissibility of the lower-level images are proved above.

Diamond and Im, *Modular forms and modular curves*, §6.1 gives the fixed-character old space as the span of all admissible proper-level cusp images and the new space as its Petersson orthogonal complement. The conductor restriction agrees with requiring $\epsilon_M$ to induce $\epsilon$. If $\epsilon_M$ induces $\epsilon$, its primitive ancestor also induces $\epsilon$ by composition of reductions on units and zero extension at level $N$. Conversely, if the conductor of $\epsilon$ divides $M$, inducing its primitive ancestor to $M$ gives such an $\epsilon_M$. At weight two the source map is $g(z)\mapsto t\,g(tz)$, so its image agrees with that of the unscaled map.

Steps 3.2 and 5.1 count the translates using the right cosets of the projective images and compare the resulting integral with the normalization in §3.6, Eq. (3.6.1). Diamond and Im, §6.3, use the names newform and primitive cusp form for a normalized eigenform in the new subspace for the full Hecke ring. In weight two, that ring acts through the algebra generated by all $T_m$ by their Proposition 3.5.1. The all-index $T_m$ convention used here is the one in Best et al., §§4.1 and 6.1.


## Projective classes of square-norm quaternions are commutators

[Open item](item-lem-author-eebeda62e446-projective-norm-one-commutator) · level 1


### Statement

Let $F$ be a field with $\mathbb Q$ as a subfield and let $B_F$ be the Hamilton algebra over $F$, with basis $1,i,j,k$, relations $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$ and $ij=k=-ji$ with cyclic rotations. Write $N(x)=\sum_{r=0}^3 x_r^2$ for the reduced norm and $B_F^\times$ for the group of invertible quaternions. Identify $F^\times$ with the normal subgroup of scalar units $\{\lambda1:\lambda\in F^\times\}$ in $B_F^\times$. The projective class $[x]$ is the coset of $x\in B_F^\times$ in the quotient by that subgroup.

If $N(x)=s^2$ for some $s\in F^\times$, then $[x]$ is a commutator in $B_F^\times/F^\times$, meaning a product $aba^{-1}b^{-1}$ of projective classes. In particular every group homomorphism from this projective group to a commutative group sends $[x]$ to the identity.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] In a field each nonzero scalar has a multiplicative inverse, and scalar multiplication is commutative ([math:def-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field)). Both $\mathbb Q$ and $F$ are unital commutative rings with their field operations, zeros and identities ([math:lem-field-is-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-field-is-a-commutative-ring), [math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring), [math:def-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-commutative-ring)). The rational subfield uses the restricted addition and multiplication and shares $0$ and $1$ with $F$ ([math:def-subfield](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subfield)), so the rational $2=1+1$ remains nonzero in $F$. The inclusion $\mathbb Q\hookrightarrow F$ therefore preserves addition, multiplication and the multiplicative identity, making it a unital ring homomorphism ([math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism)). Its image is central since $F$ is commutative, so $F$ is a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F2] In the Hamilton algebra over a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra, conjugation $\bar x$ negates the $i,j,k$ coordinates, $x\bar x=\bar x x=N(x)1$, and the norm is multiplicative. The scalars $\lambda1$ commute with every quaternion. Over $F$ a quaternion is invertible exactly when its norm is nonzero, with inverse $\bar x/N(x)$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)).

[F3] A subgroup contains the ambient identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)). It is normal if conjugation by each ambient element maps it onto itself ([math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup)). The left coset of a subgroup $S$ represented by $g\in G$ is $gS=\{gh:h\in S\}$ ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)). For a normal subgroup $S$ of $G$, the quotient has elements $[g]=gS$ and product $[g][h]=[gh]$ ([math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group)). Its identity is $[1]$ and its inverses satisfy $[g]^{-1}=[g^{-1}]$ ([math:thm-quotient-group-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-group-laws)).

[F4] A group homomorphism preserves products ([math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism)) and therefore preserves inverses ([math:lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties)). Every group law is associative and every element has a two-sided inverse. In a commutative group the group law is also commutative ([math:def-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group)).

### Proof

**Given:** An invertible $x$ with $N(x)=s^2$ for $s\in F^\times$.

**Goal:** Express $[x]$ as a projective commutator and deduce its image under any homomorphism to a commutative group.

1.1 The scalar units $S=\{\lambda1:\lambda\in F^\times\}$ contain $1$. The identities $(\lambda1)(\mu1)=(\lambda\mu)1$ and $(\lambda1)^{-1}=\lambda^{-1}1$ give closure under products and inverses. Since the scalar copy of $F$ is central, $g(\lambda1)g^{-1}=\lambda1$ for every $g\in B_F^\times$. Thus $S$ is a normal subgroup, and the quotient rules in [F3] apply. [F1, F2, F3]

2.1 For $h\in S$ we have $hS\subseteq S$ by closure under products. Each $r\in S$ belongs to $hS$ because $r=h(h^{-1}r)$ and $h^{-1}r\in S$. Thus $hS=S$, so $(gh)S=g(hS)=gS$ for every $g\in B_F^\times$ by associativity. Put $w=x/s$. Its norm is $s^{-2}N(x)=1$ by the coordinate formula. If its scalar coordinate is $-1$, use $-w$, whose norm is also one and whose scalar coordinate is $1\ne-1$ because $2\ne0$. These rescalings multiply by $s^{-1}1$ and, when needed, $-1$, both in $S$, so they preserve the projective class. Denote the resulting representative by $t+z$, where $t\ne-1$ and $z$ has scalar coordinate zero. If $z=0$, then $t^2=1$, so the representative lies in $S$ and its class is $[1]$, the commutator of identity elements. [F1, F2, F3, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 For $z\ne0$ write $z=ai+bj+ck$, using a cyclic rotation of $i,j,k$ to arrange $a\ne0$. The quaternions $y_1=bi-aj$ and $y_2=ci-ak$ satisfy $y_1z+zy_1=-2(ba-ab)=0$ and $y_2z+zy_2=-2(ca-ac)=0$ by the multiplication table. If $b=0$ then $N(y_1)=a^2\ne0$, and if $c=0$ then $N(y_2)=a^2\ne0$. If $bc\ne0$ and both candidate norms vanish, then $N(y_1+y_2)=N(y_1)+N(y_2)+2bc=2bc\ne0$. Thus one of $y_1,y_2,y_1+y_2$ is an invertible $y$ with $yz=-zy$. [F1, F2, step 2.1]

4.1 Write $v=t+z$ and set $u=1+v$. Its norm is $N(u)=1+2t+N(v)=2(1+t)\ne0$, so $u$ and $\bar u$ are invertible. The anticommutation in step 3.1 gives $yuy^{-1}=\bar u$, and $v\bar u=v(1+\bar v)=v+1=u$. Since $yu^{-1}y^{-1}$ inverts $yuy^{-1}=\bar u$, we have $uyu^{-1}y^{-1}=u\bar u^{-1}=v$. Passing to projective classes gives the required commutator for the initial $[x]$. [F1, F2, F3, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 In either case write $[x]=aba^{-1}b^{-1}$. If $f$ is a group homomorphism to a commutative group, then $f([x])=f(a)f(b)f(a)^{-1}f(b)^{-1}$. Commutativity lets the inverse factors cancel their respective factors, giving the target identity. [F4, step 2.1, step 4.1] ∎


## Rational and local values of the Hamilton norm

[Open item](item-lem-author-eebeda62e446-hamilton-norm-values) · level 1


### Statement

Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton algebra and $O$ any order, a subring containing $1$ with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. At a prime $\ell$, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the $\ell$-adic completion of $\mathbb Q$, let $\mathbb Z_\ell$ be its ring of integers, the ball of radius $1$ about $0$ including its boundary, and put $B_\ell=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $O_\ell=O\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Z_\ell$. The superscript $\times$ denotes the multiplicative group of elements invertible in the specified ring. Use the reduced norm $N$, the sum of the squares of the four Hamilton coordinates. Give $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ the metric $d_\ell(a,b)=|a-b|_\ell$ from the $\ell$-adic absolute value.

The rational norm has image $\mathbb Q_{>0}$ on $B^\times$. At every prime $\ell$ the norm sends $B_\ell^\times$ onto $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, and $1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$ is an open neighborhood of $1$ consisting of squares in $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$. At all but finitely many primes, $N(O_\ell^\times)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The Hamilton norm is multiplicative and gives $x^{-1}=\bar x/N(x)$ when $N(x)\ne0$, with conjugation negating the last three Hamilton coordinates. Scalar multiplication by $s$ changes the norm by $s^2$. The local order is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of the order basis ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). The rational matrices converting between the order basis and the Hamilton basis remain inverse over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)).

[F2] The completion is a valued field with multiplicative absolute value $|\cdot|_\ell$, with $|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$ and $|a+b|_\ell\le\max(|a|_\ell,|b|_\ell)$ ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)). Its closed unit ball identifies with the ring of compatible residue classes modulo the powers of $\ell$. The canonical embedding sends a compatible residue tuple to the limit of its standard integer lifts ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp), Justification of [def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)). The local valuation agrees with the rational numerator-minus-denominator valuation, so an integer multiple of $\ell^n$ has absolute value at most $\ell^{-n}$ ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)).

[F3] Every nonnegative integer is the sum of four integer squares ([math:thm-lagrange-four-square-theorem](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-lagrange-four-square-theorem)). In $\mathbb Z_\ell$, a root modulo $\ell$ of a polynomial with coefficients in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ lifts to a root in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ if its formal derivative, computed by differentiating the powers of the variable, is nonzero modulo $\ell$ at that root ([math:cor-p-adic-simple-root-lifting](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-p-adic-simple-root-lifting)).

[F4] Every positive divisor of a positive integer $D$ is at most $D$ ([math:lem-divisor-bound](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-divisor-bound)).

[F5] In the metric $d_\ell$, a subset of $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ is open if each of its points has a positive-radius ball in $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ contained in the subset. An open neighborhood of $1$ is an open set containing $1$ ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)).

### Proof

**Given:** The Hamilton norm, a fixed order $O$, and a prime $\ell$.

**Goal:** Determine the stated rational and local images and exhibit the neighborhood of squares.

1.1 A nonzero rational quaternion has positive norm by the coordinate formula. If $m,n$ are positive integers, choose an integral Hamilton-coordinate quaternion $z$ of norm $mn$ by the four-square theorem. Then $z/n$ has norm $m/n$ and is invertible. This gives the rational image. [F1, F3]

1.2 Let $r\in1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$. For odd $\ell$, the polynomial $T^2-r$ has root $1$ modulo $\ell$ and derivative $2$ there, which is nonzero modulo $\ell$. For $\ell=2$, use $T^2+T-(r-1)/4$, whose constant term is in $2\mathbb Z_2$. Its root $0$ modulo $2$ has derivative $1$. A lifted root $z$ of this polynomial satisfies $(1+2z)^2=r$. Thus in each case $r$ is a square. By the unit-ball description and multiplicativity in [F2], $1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$ is the set of $r$ with $|r-1|_\ell\le\ell^{-3}$. It contains $1$, and every such $r$ has $|r|_\ell=1$ by the ultrametric inequality applied to $r=1+(r-1)$ and $1=r-(r-1)$, since $|r-1|_\ell<1$. For each such $r$, if $t\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ satisfies $d_\ell(t,r)<\ell^{-3}$, then $|t-1|_\ell\le\max(|t-r|_\ell,|r-1|_\ell)\le\ell^{-3}$. The pointwise criterion in [F5] therefore gives an open neighborhood of $1$. [F2, F3, F5]

2.1 Given $a\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, take $k\ge0$ with $b=\ell^{2k}a\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. Choose $n\ge1$ so large that $\ell^{-n}\le\ell^{-3}|b|_\ell$. Take a positive integer $m$ representing the residue of $b$ modulo $\ell^n$, using $\ell^n$ for the zero residue. For every $j\ge n$, the standard lift of the residue of $b$ modulo $\ell^j$ differs from $m$ by a multiple of $\ell^n$, so it is at distance at most $\ell^{-n}$ from $m$. These lifts converge to $b$ by [F2], so $|m-b|_\ell\le\ell^{-n}$ by the ultrametric inequality. Since $\ell^{-n}<|b|_\ell$, $|m|_\ell=|b|_\ell$ by applying the ultrametric inequality in both directions. It follows that $b/m\in1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$. Choose $s\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ with $s^2=b/m$ by step 1.2, where $|s|_\ell=1$ follows from $|s^2|_\ell=1$. An integral Hamilton-coordinate quaternion $z$ of norm $m$ exists by the four-square theorem. The quaternion $\ell^{-k}sz$ has norm $a$ and hence is invertible in $B_\ell$. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.2]

3.1 In the rational change-of-basis matrices between the order basis and the Hamilton basis, clear the denominators of the entries of both matrices by one positive integer $D$. At a prime $\ell$ not dividing $D$, every entry of either matrix has the form $u/D$ with $u\in\mathbb Z$. A nonzero such entry has $v_\ell(u/D)=v_\ell(u)-v_\ell(D)=v_\ell(u)\ge0$ by [F2], and a zero entry is integral as well. The matrix taking order coordinates to Hamilton coordinates therefore sends every column in $\mathbb Z_\ell^4$ to an integral Hamilton coordinate column. Its inverse sends every integral Hamilton coordinate column to an integral order coordinate column. By [F1], the two inclusions show that $O_\ell$ consists of the quaternions with integral Hamilton coordinates. There, if $x\in O_\ell^\times$, both $x$ and its inverse have integral coordinates, and their integral norms multiply to one. Conversely, for $a\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ use $k=0$ in step 2.1. The resulting $x=sz$ and its inverse $\bar x/a$ both have integral coordinates. This gives $N(O_\ell^\times)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ off the prime divisors of $D$. These primes lie in the finite integer interval from $2$ to $D$ by [F4]. [F1, F2, F4, step 2.1] ∎


## Weight-two discrete series at the real place

[Open item](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-real-weight-two-discrete-series) · level 1


### Definition

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Write $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)$ for the group of invertible real two-by-two matrices and $S=\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb R)$ for its subgroup of determinant-one matrices. Give both groups the subspace topology from the Euclidean space of real two-by-two matrices. Write $q:G\to\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb R)$ for the quotient by nonzero scalar matrices and give $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb R)$ the quotient topology induced by $q$.

Let $\rho$ be a strongly continuous unitary representation of $G$ with trivial central character, so every scalar matrix acts as the identity. It has **weight-two discrete-series type** if its restriction to $S$ is unitarily equivalent to the unitary direct sum of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic weight-two discrete-series models. In the holomorphic model the space consists of holomorphic $u$ on the upper half-plane $\mathfrak H$ with $\int_{\mathfrak H}|u(x+iy)|^2\,dx\,dy<\infty$. For $g\in S$ and $g^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ the action sends $u(z)$ to $(cz+d)^{-2}u((az+b)/(cz+d))$. The other model is obtained by complex conjugating functions and action. The unitary direct sum uses the sum of the two squared norms and acts on each summand separately.

A strongly continuous unitary representation $\bar\rho$ of $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb R)$ has weight-two discrete-series type if its pullback $\bar\rho\circ q$ has this type.

### Definition references

[math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)

[def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)

[math:ex-general-and-special-linear-lie-groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/ex-general-and-special-linear-lie-groups)

[math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)

[math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)

[math:def-holomorphic-and-antiholomorphic-discrete-series-models](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-holomorphic-and-antiholomorphic-discrete-series-models)

[math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)

[math:def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations)

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice applies in particular to countable families. [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)

[F1] With the stated Euclidean subspace topologies, $G$ and $S$ are real Lie groups. The scalar subgroup is central, and $q$ is the algebraic quotient homomorphism. [math:ex-general-and-special-linear-lie-groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/ex-general-and-special-linear-lie-groups) [def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group) The subspace topology $S$ inherits from the Euclidean matrix space agrees with the one it inherits from $G$, so the inclusion $S\hookrightarrow G$ is continuous. [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)

[F2] The group quotient by a closed normal subgroup of a finite-dimensional real Lie group is a Lie group with the quotient topology under countable choice. The quotient topology makes the projection continuous. [math:thm-quotient-manifold-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-manifold-by-a-closed-lie-subgroup) [math:thm-quotient-by-a-closed-normal-subgroup-is-a-lie-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-by-a-closed-normal-subgroup-is-a-lie-group) [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)

[F3] Strong continuity means norm-continuity of every orbit map, and unitary equivalence is given by a unitary intertwiner. [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)

[F4] For the rotation subgroup $K=\mathrm{SO}(2)\subset S$, use $k_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$. A vector $v$ is $K$-finite for a model action $\sigma$ if the span of $\{\sigma(k)v:k\in K\}$ is finite-dimensional. The two displayed models at weight two are irreducible strongly continuous unitary representations. On $k_\theta$ the holomorphic characters are $e^{-i(2+2j)\theta}$ and the antiholomorphic characters are $e^{i(2+2j)\theta}$ for integers $j\geq0$. [math:def-k-finite-and-smooth-vectors-for-sl2-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-k-finite-and-smooth-vectors-for-sl2-r) [math:def-holomorphic-and-antiholomorphic-discrete-series-models](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-holomorphic-and-antiholomorphic-discrete-series-models) [math:thm-irreducibility-and-k-types-of-the-discrete-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-irreducibility-and-k-types-of-the-discrete-series)

[F5] Let $dg$ be the fixed left Haar measure on $S$ used in the KAK integration formula. Write $g=n_xa_sk$ with $n_x=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, $a_s=\operatorname{diag}(e^{s/2},e^{-s/2})$, $x,s\in\mathbb R$ and $k\in K$. In these NAK coordinates it is $e^{-s}\,dx\,ds\,dk$, with $dk$ the Haar probability measure on $K$. [math:def-iwasawa-and-minimal-parabolic-data-for-sl2-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-iwasawa-and-minimal-parabolic-data-for-sl2-r) [math:thm-iwasawa-decomposition-for-sl2-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-iwasawa-decomposition-for-sl2-r) [math:lem-kak-integration-formula-for-k-bi-invariant-functions-on-sl2-r](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-kak-integration-formula-for-k-bi-invariant-functions-on-sl2-r)

[F6] The complex Haar space $L^2(S,dg)$ consists of almost-everywhere classes of measurable complex functions with finite squared-modulus integral against $dg$. [Complex Haar $L^p$ spaces and compactly supported functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-haar-lp-spaces-and-compactly-supported-functions) For either model action $\sigma$ at weight two, the continuous matrix coefficient $g\mapsto\langle\sigma(g)v,w\rangle$ on $S$ represents a class in $L^2(S,dg)$ when both $v$ and $w$ are $K$-finite. [math:def-matrix-coefficient-of-a-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrix-coefficient-of-a-unitary-representation) [math:thm-square-integrability-of-sl2-r-discrete-series-matrix-coefficients](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-square-integrability-of-sl2-r-discrete-series-matrix-coefficients)

[F7] Under the Axiom of Choice, the Hilbert direct sum of two strongly continuous unitary representations of a topological group is again strongly continuous and unitary, with componentwise action and the sum of the two squared norms. [math:def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations)

### Justification

**Given:** The groups and topologies above, the Axiom of Choice and a representation of either input kind in the definition.

1.1 The scalar subgroup is closed in $G$ since the off-diagonal entries are zero and the two diagonal entries are equal, all closed conditions on the entries. It is normal by centrality. Thus $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb R)$ is a topological group for the indicated quotient topology, and $q$ is a continuous homomorphism. Every orbit map of $\bar\rho\circ q$ is the composition of $q$ with a norm-continuous orbit map of $\bar\rho$. The pullback is unitary and trivial on scalars because $q$ sends them to the identity. [A1, F1, F2, F3]

1.2 Both weight-two model actions are strongly continuous and unitary by [F4]. With the Axiom of Choice and the topological group $S$, [F7] gives those same properties for their Hilbert direct sum. In the holomorphic formula, $-I$ sends $z$ to $z$ and has multiplier $(-1)^{-2}=1$, so $-I$ acts as the identity in both models. Their rotation weights have opposite signs, and replacing $k_\theta$ by $k_{-\theta}$ reverses both signs while preserving the least absolute weight two. Thus the test specifies the same weight under either angle convention. [A1, F1, F4, F7, algebra]

2.1 Each restricted orbit map is the corresponding orbit map on $G$ composed with the continuous inclusion $S\hookrightarrow G$. Restriction along it preserves strong continuity and unitarity. A unitary intertwiner remains an intertwiner on restriction and on pullback, so the test depends only on the unitary equivalence class of either input. [F1, F3, step 1.1]

3.1 For either model action on $S$, apply [F6] at weight two to the matrix coefficient of two $K$-finite vectors. The resulting continuous function represents a class in $L^2(S,dg)$ for the fixed left Haar measure in [F5]. [F4, F5, F6] ∎


## Spherical Hecke operators under equivalence

[Open item](item-lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-spherical-action-under-equivalence) · level 1


### Statement

Let $H$ be a Hausdorff topological group with compact open subgroup $J$. Let $V$ and $W$ be complex representations of $H$ and let $I:V\to W$ be a bijective linear map with $I\pi_V(h)=\pi_W(h)I$ for every $h\in H$. Then $I$ identifies the $J$-fixed spaces, meaning the vectors unchanged by every $j\in J$, and it intertwines their spherical Hecke operators. A spherical Hecke function is a compactly supported complex function invariant under $J$ on both sides. Its operator on fixed vectors is the sum of contributions $f(x)\pi(x)$ over the cosets of the form $xJ$, with weight one per coset. In particular, if the fixed spaces are lines, their Hecke eigencharacters, the scalar actions of all these functions, are equal.

### Statement references

[math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)

[def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)

[math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map)

[math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space)

[math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)

[math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)

[math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)

[math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)

[math:def-injection-surjection-bijection](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-injection-surjection-bijection)

[math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension)

[math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space)

[math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] In a Hausdorff topological group $H$, the subgroup $J$ is assumed open in $H$ and compact in the subspace topology inherited from $H$. A complex representation acts by a homomorphism to the invertible linear operators. The $J$-fixed vectors form complex linear subspaces with the inherited operations. For a spherical Hecke function $f$, the operator on fixed vectors is the finite sum $\sum_{xJ} f(x)\pi(x)$ over the cosets where $f$ is nonzero. The sum is independent of the choice of representatives and defines a linear endomorphism of the fixed space. [math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group) [math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space) [math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space) [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup) [math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space) [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top) [def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)

[F2] The spaces $V$ and $W$ are vector spaces over the field $\mathbb C$. Vector addition is associative and commutative with the zero vector as identity, and scalar multiplication satisfies $1v=v$. Linearity gives preservation of addition and scalar multiplication, and the zero-preservation lemma gives $I(0_V)=0_W$. [math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space) [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field) [math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map) [math:lem-linear-map-elementary-properties](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-linear-map-elementary-properties)

[F3] Bijectivity gives a preimage for every vector in the codomain and makes that preimage unique. [math:def-injection-surjection-bijection](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-injection-surjection-bijection)

[F4] A line is a vector space of dimension one and therefore has a singleton basis. A basis is linearly independent, and an injective finite list with image a basis is an ordered basis. In an ordered basis each vector has a unique list of scalar coordinates. [math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension) [math:def-linear-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-basis) [math:def-linear-independence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-independence) [math:thm-unique-coordinates-with-respect-to-an-ordered-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-unique-coordinates-with-respect-to-an-ordered-basis)

[F5] A finite sum can be evaluated in any enumeration of its index set. Evaluation starts at zero and adjoins each next term by addition. Induction on the length of a finite list uses the empty prefix and the step of adjoining the next term. [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid) [math:def-monoid-finite-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-monoid-finite-product) [math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)

### Proof

**Given:** $H$, $J$, the actions and the bijective intertwining map $I$.

**Goal:** Identify the fixed spaces and their Hecke actions.

1.1 If $v$ is fixed by $J$, then $\pi_W(j)Iv=I\pi_V(j)v=Iv$ for each $j\in J$. Conversely, for a fixed vector $w$ in $W$ choose its preimage $v$ under $I$. For each $j\in J$, $I\pi_V(j)v=\pi_W(j)w=w=Iv$, so injectivity gives $\pi_V(j)v=v$. Thus $I$ restricts to a bijection on the fixed spaces and remains linear for their inherited operations. [given, F1, F2, F3]

1.2 Fix a spherical Hecke function $f$ and $v\in V^J$. Enumerate the finitely many contributing cosets. Let $P_k$ be the sum of the first $k$ contributions $f(x)\pi_V(x)v$, and let $Q_k$ be the sum of their images under $I$. For the empty prefix, $I(P_0)=I(0_V)=0_W=Q_0$. If $I(P_k)=Q_k$ and the next contribution is $u$, then $I(P_{k+1})=I(P_k+u)=I(P_k)+I(u)=Q_k+I(u)=Q_{k+1}$. Induction gives that $I$ sends the full sum to the sum of the images. [given, F1, F2, F5]

1.3 If the fixed spaces are lines, take a singleton basis $\{b\}$ of $V^J$. Listing it as $(b)$ gives an ordered basis because the list is injective and has image $\{b\}$. If $b=0_V$, the coefficient $1$ would yield a zero linear combination, contrary to independence. The ordered-basis coordinate theorem gives a unique expression $ab$ for each vector of $V^J$, since the coordinate sum has one term. [F1, F2, F4, F5]

2.1 For each contributing coset, $I(f(x)\pi_V(x)v)=f(x)I\pi_V(x)v=f(x)\pi_W(x)Iv$. Since $Iv$ is fixed, the spherical operator on $W^J$ uses the same cosets and the same weights. Hence $IT_f^Vv=T_f^W Iv$, where the superscripts specify the representation. [given, F1, F2, step 1.1, step 1.2]

3.1 Under the line hypothesis, for any $f$, write $T_f^V b=\lambda b$ using step 1.3. Since $I(0_V)=0_W$ and $I$ is injective, $Ib\ne 0_W$. The fixed-space bijection and linearity express every vector of $W^J$ as $I(ab)=aIb$. By step 2.1, $T_f^W Ib=IT_f^V b=\lambda Ib$, and linearity gives $T_f^W(aIb)=\lambda(aIb)$. If another scalar $\mu$ gave that action, then $I(\mu b)=\mu Ib=\lambda Ib=I(\lambda b)$. Injectivity and uniqueness of the coordinate in $V^J$ give $\mu=\lambda$. This proves equality of the scalar actions for every $f$. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 1.3] ∎


## Determinant-index spherical Hecke correspondence

[Open item](item-def-determinant-index-spherical-hecke-correspondence) · level 2


### Definition

Assume countable choice, meaning that every sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. Let $\ell$ be a prime, an integer greater than one with positive divisors only $1$ and itself. Let $F=\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the field obtained by completing $\mathbb Q$ for the $\ell$-adic distance and extending its field operations. Its valuation $v_\ell$ has $v_\ell(\ell)=1$, satisfies $|a|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(a)}$ for $a\ne0$, and has $v_\ell(0)=+\infty$. Put $O=\mathbb Z_\ell=\{a\in F:v_\ell(a)\ge0\}$.

Write $G=\mathrm{PGL}_2(F)$ for the group of invertible two-by-two matrices over $F$ modulo nonzero scalar matrices. The projection $q$ sends a matrix $A$ to its class $[A]$, and $[A][B]=[AB]$. Give $F$ its $\ell$-adic metric topology and $F^4$ the product topology. Give the invertible matrices the subspace topology under their identification with a subset of $F^4$ by their entries. Give $G$ the quotient topology, in which a subset is open exactly when its inverse image under $q$ is open. Let $H=\mathrm{GL}_2(O)$ be the group of matrices with inverses over $O$, and set $K=q(H)$. The group $G$ is Hausdorff, and $K$ is compact and open. Here Hausdorff means distinct points have disjoint open neighborhoods, a topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion, and a compact set admits a finite subcover from each open cover.

For a nonnegative integer $e$, let
$$D_{\ell,e}=\{A\in M_2(O):\det A\ne0,\ v_\ell(\det A)=e\},\qquad S_{\ell,e}=q(D_{\ell,e}).$$
Here $M_2(O)$ is the set of two-by-two matrices over $O$ and $\det\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\ c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-bc$. Thus a projective class lies in $S_{\ell,e}$ when it has an integral matrix representative with determinant of valuation $e$. The **determinant-index spherical Hecke correspondence of index $\ell^e$** is the spherical operator of the indicator $\mathbf 1_{S_{\ell,e}}$, which is one on $S_{\ell,e}$ and zero elsewhere.

More generally a spherical Hecke operator here is the right convolution action of a complex weight $\phi$ that vanishes outside a compact subset and is invariant under $K$ on both sides, meaning $\phi(khk')=\phi(h)$ for $k,k'\in K$. A right action of $G$ on a set $X$ is a map $(x,h)\mapsto xh$ satisfying $x1=x$ and $(xh)j=x(hj)$. Use the space $\mathbb C^X$ of functions $X\to\mathbb C$ with addition $(f+g)(x)=f(x)+g(x)$, scalar multiplication $(af)(x)=af(x)$ and zero the constant zero function. Right translation is the complex-linear map on $\mathbb C^X$ given by $(R_h f)(x)=f(xh)$. A complex-linear map preserves addition and complex scalar multiplication. On the subspace of functions satisfying $f(xk)=f(x)$ for every $k\in K$, the action is
$$(T_\phi f)(x)=\sum_{hK\in G/K}\phi(h)f(xh).$$
Here $hK=\{hk:k\in K\}$ is the coset formed by multiplying on the right by $K$, and $G/K$ denotes the set of these cosets. The cosets partition $G$, and the sum ranges over the finitely many cosets where $\phi$ is nonzero. In particular
$$(T_{\ell,e}f)(x)=\sum_{hK\in S_{\ell,e}/K} f(xh),$$
where $S_{\ell,e}/K$ denotes the cosets contained in $S_{\ell,e}$.

In the measure formulation use a left Haar measure $\mu$, a nonzero regular Borel measure invariant under left translation and finite on compact sets. Here Borel means defined on the smallest collection containing all open sets and closed under complements and countable unions, and regularity means outer approximation of Borel sets by open sets and inner approximation of open sets by compact sets. Normalize by $\mu(K)=1$. Such a measure is available under the additional assumption of the axiom of choice, meaning that any family of nonempty sets has a choice function. For this integral the measurable domain is $(G,\mathcal B(G))$ and the measure space is $(G,\mathcal B(G),\mu)$, where $\mathcal B(G)$ is the Borel sigma-algebra of $G$. Equip $\mathbb C$ with the Borel sigma-algebra generated by the topology of the Euclidean metric $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)=|z-w|$. Equip the real and imaginary components' codomain $\mathbb R$ with the Borel sigma-algebra of its usual metric $d_{\mathbb R}(a,b)=|a-b|$. View the nonnegative functions used in the simple integral as taking values in $\overline{\mathbb R}$ with the Borel sigma-algebra of its order topology. With this normalization the convolution action is the complex Lebesgue integral of $h\mapsto\phi(h)f(xh)$ over $G$ against $\mu$, for each fixed $x\in X$.

For a finite list of distinct primes $\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_n$ and nonnegative integer exponents $e_i$, use the product group $P=\prod_{i=1}^n G_i$ of the corresponding groups $G_i$. Its elements are tuples and products are taken coordinate by coordinate. Let $\iota_i(h)$ be the tuple with $h$ in coordinate $i$ and identities elsewhere. On any set $X$ with a right action of $P$, apply the local operators along $\iota_i$ to functions invariant on the right under $\prod_i K_i$. These operators commute, and their product is
$$\left(\prod_{i=1}^n T_{\ell_i,e_i}f\right)(x)=\sum_{(h_iK_i)_i\in\prod_i(S_{\ell_i,e_i}/K_i)} f\bigl(x(h_1,\ldots,h_n)\bigr).$$
The Cartesian product in the sum consists of tuples with one coset from each local set. For the empty list the product operator is the identity.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] Countable choice and the prime, local field and valuation have the meanings specified above. The completion has field operations extending those of $\mathbb Q$, and every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse. The valuation is additive on products of nonzero elements, and the units of $O$ are precisely the elements with valuation zero ([countable choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice), [prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime), [adic completion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers), [field structure of the completion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field), [normalized valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)).

[F2] Give $F$ the metric topology of the $\ell$-adic distance. The projective quotient map is a group homomorphism. The entry topology is the subspace topology from the product topology on $F^4$. With these topologies on the matrix group and its quotient, $G$ is a Hausdorff topological group and $K=q(H)$ is compact open, by the local compact-open result under countable choice. A Hausdorff space is locally compact when each point has a compact neighborhood ([projective group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group), [metric topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [product topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology), [subspace topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top), [quotient topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology), [topological group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group), [compact-open integral subgroups](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets), [locally compact space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space)).

[F3] Over a commutative ring the two-by-two determinant is $ad-bc$, the determinant is multiplicative, and a square matrix is invertible exactly when its determinant is a unit ([determinant](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix), [multiplicativity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative), [invertibility criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit)).

[F4] The fixed-exponent map $AH\mapsto[A]K$ is a bijection from the cosets in $D_{\ell,e}$ onto those in $S_{\ell,e}$. The former have one triangular representative with diagonal $(\ell^r,\ell^{e-r})$ and upper entry $b$ for every $0\le r\le e$ and $0\le b<\ell^r$. In particular the projective support is a disjoint union of finitely many cosets of the form $hK$ ([coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset), [fixed-index classification](item-lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets)).

[F5] For a Hausdorff topological group with compact open $K$, the spherical convolution algebra consists of compactly supported complex weights invariant on both sides by $K$. A representation, meaning an action by invertible complex-linear maps that respects products, carries the operator of such a weight on its $K$-fixed vectors by summing the weighted actions of the coset representatives ([spherical convolution and action](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F6] A left Haar measure is invariant under left translation. Under the axiom of choice it exists on every locally compact Hausdorff group. Its mass on a nonempty compact open subgroup is finite and strictly positive ([left Haar measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure), [axiom of choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [Haar existence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures), [positivity and finiteness](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-haar-measure-is-positive-on-nonempty-open-sets-and-finite-on-compact-sets)).

[F7] Finite sums are invariant under bijective reindexing. An iterated sum over finite sets equals the sum over their Cartesian product. The group laws are associativity and the identity and inverse laws. Finite sums of complex numbers use associative, commutative addition and are independent of enumeration. A finite Cartesian product of finite sets is finite, including the singleton product of the empty list ([group laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group), [finite sums](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [finite sum reindexing and product sums](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini), [finite products of sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-product-rule)).

[F8] The Euclidean metric $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)=|z-w|$ on $\mathbb C$ induces its usual topology. The Borel sigma-algebra on $\mathbb C$ is generated by the open sets of this topology. Open sets are Borel, and the Borel sets form a sigma-algebra. A measurable space is a set with a sigma-algebra, and a measure space is a measurable space equipped with a measure. For the real and imaginary components use the Borel sigma-algebra of the usual metric on $\mathbb R$. For nonnegative simple functions use the Borel sigma-algebra of the order topology on $\overline{\mathbb R}$, the topology generated by all its strict lower and upper rays. A function is measurable when preimages of measurable sets are measurable. On a measure space, a measurable function with finite range in $[0,+\infty)$ viewed in $\overline{\mathbb R}$ is nonnegative simple. The integral of a nonnegative function constant on finitely many disjoint measurable sets and zero elsewhere is the sum of its constant values multiplied by the respective measures. On nonnegative simple measurable functions the Lebesgue integral agrees with this formula. A complex measurable function is integrable when its modulus has finite integral. Its integral is the integral of its real part plus $i$ times the integral of its imaginary part. Each real integral is the difference of the integrals of the positive and negative parts when both are finite.
