([Euclidean metric on the complex plane](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity), [metric topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [metric open sets form a topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-open-set-algebra), [Borel sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra), [measurable spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-space), [measure spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-space), [usual metric on the real line](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space), [order topology generated by open rays](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-order-topology-on-a-linearly-ordered-set), [order-Borel structure and measurable maps into the extended reals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function), [measurable maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces), [nonnegative simple functions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function), [simple-function integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function), [agreement with the Lebesgue integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral), [real and complex Lebesgue integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals), [real part, imaginary part and modulus](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-conjugate-real-imaginary-part-and-modulus)).

[F9] The field $\mathbb C$ supplies the scalars for complex vector spaces. A subset of a vector space is a linear subspace if it contains zero and is closed under addition and scalar multiplication. A linear map satisfies $T(af+bg)=aT(f)+bT(g)$ for complex scalars $a,b$ ([complex field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [vector spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space), [linear subspaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace), [linear map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map)).

[F10] The cosets of a subgroup partition the ambient group ([coset partition](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-partition)).

[F11] Left translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms. Inverse images of open sets under continuous maps are open. Every cover of a compact subset by ambient open sets has a finite subcover. Continuous maps take compact subsets to compact subsets ([translations in a topological group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion), [open preimages under continuous maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), [compact subsets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), [ambient open covers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compactness-of-a-subspace-is-ambient), [continuous images of compact sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps)).

[F12] For a subgroup $L$ of a group and elements $a,b$, the equality $aL=bL$ holds exactly when $a^{-1}b\in L$. ([coset equality criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-membership-and-equality)).

[F13] A property of natural numbers holds for every natural number if it holds at zero and passes from each number to its successor ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

[F14] A union of open sets in a topological space is open. ([topology axioms](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space))

[F15] Every subset of a finite set is finite. ([subsets of finite sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set))

[F16] A Borel measure takes values in $[0,+\infty]$, assigns zero to the empty set and is countably additive on pairwise disjoint sequences of Borel sets. Regularity here means outer regularity on Borel sets and inner regularity on open sets. The measure of a Borel set is the infimum of the measures of its open supersets, and the measure of an open set is the supremum of the measures of its compact subsets. ([countable additivity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure), [Radon regularity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space))

[F17] A nonnegative extended series is the supremum of its finite partial sums. Adding a nonnegative extended real to $+\infty$ gives $+\infty$, and multiplying $+\infty$ by a positive finite real gives $+\infty$. ([nonnegative extended sums](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-extended-series), [extended real order and arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-reals))

[F18] In the real ordered field a positive real has a positive reciprocal, and multiplication by a positive real preserves order and distributes over addition. ([real ordered field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field))

[F19] A monoid has an associative binary operation and a two-sided identity. A finite list in a monoid has product given by starting at the identity and appending each new factor on the right. ([monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-semigroup-and-monoid), [finite monoid product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-monoid-finite-product)).

### Justification

**Given:** The countable choice assumption, prime, local groups, exponents and right actions in the definition. For the existence of the measure in the measure formulation, also assume the axiom of choice.

**Goal:** Verify the spherical support, the well-defined operator and unit weights, and the product correspondence.

1.1 A matrix in $D_{\ell,e}$ is invertible over $F$ by the determinant criterion because its nonzero determinant is a unit in the field $F$. For $U,V\in H$ the determinants have valuation zero. The product $UAV$ is integral and its determinant still has valuation $e$, by multiplicativity and additivity of the valuation. The same property for $U^{-1},V^{-1}\in H$ gives equality of the translated supports $kS_{\ell,e}k'=S_{\ell,e}$ for $k,k'\in K$. This construction includes every integral matrix of determinant valuation $e$, including those whose entries are all divisible by $\ell$ when they occur. [F1, F2, F3]

2.1 By the fixed-exponent bijection the support is a finite union of distinct cosets $hK$. Left translation by $h$ is continuous by [F11], and $hK$ is open as the preimage of $K$ under the continuous left translation by $h^{-1}$. Each coset is compact as a continuous image of $K$. By the union axiom of the quotient topology, $S_{\ell,e}$ is open. For compactness, fix an ambient open cover of the union and induct on the number of cosets, using [F13]. The empty list of cover members covers the union of zero cosets. If finitely many cover members cover the first cosets, apply [F11] to obtain a finite subcover of the next compact coset by members of the same ambient cover. Concatenating the two finite lists gives a finite cover of the enlarged union. Thus the full union has a finite subcover and is compact by the ambient criterion. Thus the indicator is a compactly supported weight invariant on both sides by $K$. The same bijection allows the coset sum for this indicator to be taken over $AH$ in $D_{\ell,e}$, with each triangular representative contributing exactly once through its projective class. [F2, F4, F5, F11, F13, F14, step 1.1]

3.1 The operations on $\mathbb C^X$ satisfy the vector space axioms by evaluation at each $x\in X$, with additive inverse $x\mapsto-f(x)$. For complex scalars $a,b$, right translation satisfies $R_h(af+bg)(x)=af(xh)+bg(xh)$, so it is complex-linear. The action laws give $R_1f=f$ and $R_hR_jf(x)=f((xh)j)=f(x(hj))$, so $R_h$ has inverse $R_{h^{-1}}$ and these operators constitute a representation. The $K$-fixed functions contain zero, and for fixed $f,g$ and every $k\in K$, linearity gives $R_k(af+bg)=af+bg$. Thus the fixed functions form the stated right $K$-invariant subspace. The spherical action formula applies by [F5]. The open cosets partition $G$ and therefore cover the compact set outside which $\phi$ vanishes. Let $\mathcal C$ be the finite subcover supplied by the ambient open-cover criterion in [F11]. If $\phi(h)\ne0$, then $h$ belongs to the compact set and therefore lies in a coset in $\mathcal C$. That coset is $hK$ by the coset partition, so the contributing cosets form a subset of $\mathcal C$ and are finite. Replacing $h$ by $hk$ leaves both $\phi(h)$ and $f(xh)$ unchanged. To evaluate at $xk_0$ with $k_0\in K$, reindex by $hK\mapsto k_0hK$, a bijection with inverse multiplication by $k_0^{-1}$. The coefficient is unchanged by left invariance, so the output is right $K$-invariant. The finite weighted sum is linear by distributivity and hence defines a linear operator on the invariant subspace. Taking $\phi=\mathbf 1_{S_{\ell,e}}$ gives the displayed counting formula. [F4, F5, F7, F9, F10, F11, F15, given, step 2.1]

4.1 The translates of $K$ give compact neighborhoods of all points, so $G$ is locally compact and Hausdorff. Under the additional choice assumption [F6] gives a left Haar measure $\mu_0$ with $0<\mu_0(K)<\infty$. Set $c=1/\mu_0(K)$ and $\mu(E)=c\mu_0(E)$ for every Borel set $E$. By [F18], $c$ is positive and finite, and multiplication by $c$ and by its reciprocal $1/c$ preserves order on the finite nonnegative reals. Both multiplications fix $+\infty$ by [F17], so multiplication by $c$ is an order isomorphism of $[0,+\infty]$ and carries greatest lower bounds and least upper bounds to those of the scaled sets. Multiplication by $c$ distributes over addition of nonnegative extended reals by real distributivity for finite terms and the rules for $+\infty$ otherwise. By induction it scales each finite partial sum by $c$, and taking the supremum gives the same scaling for the series. Thus $\mu(\varnothing)=0$, and countable additivity of $\mu_0$ gives countable additivity of $\mu$. Applying the same scaling to the infimum of the masses of open supersets of a Borel set and the supremum of the masses of compact subsets of an open set gives the two regularity identities for $\mu$. The scaled measure is left invariant and finite on compact sets because $\mu_0$ has those properties and $c$ is positive and finite. Since $\mu(K)=c\mu_0(K)=1$, it is nonzero and is a normalized left Haar measure. For every such normalization $\mu(hK)=1$ by left invariance. The integrand is constant on each of the finitely many contributing cosets and zero elsewhere by step 3.1. These cosets are disjoint and open, hence Borel, and the complement of their union is also Borel. For every set $B$ in the Euclidean Borel sigma-algebra on $\mathbb C$, the preimage of $B$ is a union of pieces from this finite measurable partition, so the integrand is measurable. The real and imaginary components themselves, the modulus, and the positive and negative parts of both components are constant on the same pieces. For the real Borel codomain of the components and the extended-real Borel codomain of the nonnegative functions, the preimage of any Borel set is again a union of these measurable pieces. Thus the components are measurable, and the modulus and the four parts are nonnegative simple measurable functions by [F8]. Each of the five nonnegative functions is zero outside the contributing cosets, each of which has measure one. The simple formula and its agreement with the nonnegative Lebesgue integral in [F8] therefore give finite integrals. Taking the positive-minus-negative differences and recombining into the complex integral yields the finite coset sum with mass one per coset. [F2, F5, F6, F8, F10, F13, F16, F17, F18, step 2.1, step 3.1]

4.2 The product group laws hold in each coordinate, with coordinatewise identities and inverses. For distinct coordinates the embedded elements commute, since at every coordinate at least one element is the identity. Each local operator preserves invariance under its own $K_i$ by step 3.1. It preserves invariance under every other $K_j$ because a right shift by $\iota_j(k)$ can be moved past $\iota_i(h)$ inside each summand and then dropped by invariance of $f$. Since the embedded $K_j$ generate $\prod_j K_j$ by coordinatewise factorization, the common invariant space is preserved. Write $V$ for the functions in $\mathbb C^X$ invariant under the right action of $\prod_i K_i$. They form a subspace because zero and every linear combination of invariant functions are invariant at each point. For $1\le i\le n$, let $T_i$ denote $T_{\ell_i,e_i}$ acting along $\iota_i$, restricted to $V$. Step 3.1 gives linearity, and the invariance just proved makes each $T_i$ a linear self-map of $V$. The identity map is linear. The composition of two linear self-maps is linear by applying their linearity laws in succession. Composition is associative because either grouping of $A,B,C$ sends $f$ to $A(B(Cf))$, and composing with $\mathrm{id}_V$ on either side leaves a map unchanged. Thus these maps form a monoid under composition. For $0\le j\le n$, let $B_j$ be the product of the first $j$ maps from the list $T_1,\ldots,T_n$. By [F19], $B_0=\mathrm{id}_V$ and $B_{j+1}=B_j\circ T_{j+1}$ for $0\le j<n$. The operator product in the Definition is $B_n$. [F7, F9, F19, given, step 3.1]

5.1 Let $C_i=S_{\ell_i,e_i}/K_i$. For a tuple of cosets in the first $j$ positions write $u_j=(h_1,\ldots,h_j,1,\ldots,1)$ using their representatives in $P$. For every $f\in V$ and $x\in X$ we show by induction that $(B_jf)(x)$ is the sum of $f(xu_j)$ over the tuples in the first $j$ positions. For $j=0$ there is one empty tuple, and $u_0$ is the identity of $P$. The sum is then $f(x)$, which equals $(B_0f)(x)$. If the formula holds at $j<n$, apply it to $T_{j+1}f$, which belongs to $V$ by step 4.2. By the prefix rule this evaluates $B_{j+1}f$. At $xu_j$ the local formula for $T_{j+1}$ sums $f(xu_j\iota_{j+1}(h_{j+1}))$ over $C_{j+1}$. The group action law gives this evaluation, and the coordinate group law gives $u_j\iota_{j+1}(h_{j+1})=u_{j+1}$. Finite Fubini identifies the sum over a prefix tuple followed by the next coset with the sum over the tuples of length $j+1$. Induction gives the formula for $B_n$. [F4, F7, F13, F19, given, step 3.1, step 4.2]

6.1 For distinct $i,k$, expand both $T_i\circ T_k$ and $T_k\circ T_i$ on $V$ using the two local coset sums as in step 5.1. In the first order each term is $f(x\iota_i(h_i)\iota_k(h_k))$, and in the second the two embedded factors occur in the opposite order. They commute by step 4.2, and interchanging the two coset coordinates is a bijection of the indexing sets. Finite reindexing gives equality of the two compositions. [F7, step 3.1, step 4.2, step 5.1]

7.1 For representatives $h=(h_i)_i$ and $g=(g_i)_i$, the coset-equality criterion gives $h(\prod_i K_i)=g(\prod_i K_i)$ exactly when $h^{-1}g\in\prod_i K_i$. In the coordinatewise group law this means $h_i^{-1}g_i\in K_i$ for every $i$, which by the same criterion says $h_iK_i=g_iK_i$ for every $i$. The finite product of local coset sets therefore indexes the product sum with coefficient one for each tuple. This proves the product formula and commutation in the stated normalization. [F4, F7, F12, step 5.1, step 6.1] ∎


## Finite quaternion ideles

[Open item](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles) · level 2


### Definition

Let $O$ be an order in the rational Hamilton algebra $B$. Write $(B_\ell,O_\ell)$ for its local algebra and local order at each prime $\ell$. Define the finite quaternion idele group and the subgroup of integral units by
$$\widehat B^\times=\{(x_\ell)\in\prod_\ell B_\ell^\times:x_\ell\in O_\ell^\times\text{ for all but finitely many }\ell\},\qquad \widehat O^\times=\prod_\ell O_\ell^\times.$$
Multiplication and inversion are coordinatewise. The restricted product topology has basic opens $\prod_\ell V_\ell$, where each $V_\ell$ is open in $B_\ell^\times$ and $V_\ell=O_\ell^\times$ outside a finite set. Write $B_{\mathbb A_f}=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb A_f$ for the algebra obtained by extending scalars to the finite adele ring.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** A rational Hamilton algebra $B$, an order $O$ with a fixed basis $b_0,\ldots,b_3$ and the finite adele ring $\mathbb A_f$.

[F1] Local multiplication and inversion on $B_\ell^\times$ are continuous, and $O_\ell^\times$ is an open subgroup. The order-coordinate map is a homeomorphism $B_\ell\to\mathbb Q_\ell^4$ and takes $O_\ell$ to $\mathbb Z_\ell^4$. For $x\in O_\ell$, membership in $O_\ell^\times$ is equivalent to the determinant of left multiplication in the order basis being a unit of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). For each commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $R$, the order basis extends to an $R$-basis of $B_R$ with the same multiplication constants ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification 2.2).

[F2] Finite scalar adeles form a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra via the diagonal map, with coordinatewise operations and integral coordinates outside a finite set ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Definition and Justification 1.1 and 2.2). A $\mathbb Q$-algebra homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication, the identity and the structural map from $\mathbb Q$ ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)). A binary union of finite sets has a finite listing obtained by concatenating their listings and retaining the first occurrence of each entry ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification 1.1). The group axioms and subgroup conditions are those of [math:def-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group) and [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup).

[F3] The whole space is open by (T1), and the intersection of two open sets is open by (T3) ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)). The basis criterion applies to a covering family whose pairwise intersections are unions of its members ([math:thm-basis-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-basis-criterion)). Product topology has a basis of boxes restricting finitely many factors ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)), and subspace opens are traces of ambient opens ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). A topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)). Compactness means that every open cover has a finite subcover ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). A map is continuous if at every point each open neighborhood of its image contains the image of an open neighborhood of the point ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top)).

[F4] The canonical comparison identifies $\mathbb Z_\ell$ with the compatible residue tuples modulo $\ell^n$, with coordinatewise ring operations, and identifies the resulting ring with the unit ball in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([math:def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes), [math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp)). Residue addition and multiplication are computed on integer representatives, and the reduction maps are unital ring homomorphisms ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification 1.2). Give each finite additive group $\mathbb Z/\ell^n\mathbb Z$ the discrete topology by declaring every subset open. The traces of product cylinders on the inverse limit of these groups form a basis ([math:def-inverse-limit-topology-for-finite-discrete-groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inverse-limit-topology-for-finite-discrete-groups)). For a prime $\ell$ and an integer $a$ with $\ell\nmid a$, the greatest common divisor $\gcd(\ell,a)$ is $1$ ([math:lem-prime-not-dividing-is-coprime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-prime-not-dividing-is-coprime)). Bézout's identity expresses $\gcd(\ell,a)$ as $\ell c+ab$ for integers $c,b$ whenever $\ell$ and $a$ are integers and at least one is nonzero ([math:thm-bezout-identity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bezout-identity)). The determinant over a commutative ring is the Leibniz sum of signed products of entries ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix)).

[F5] The extended absolute value is multiplicative and ultrametric ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)) and satisfies $|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$ ([math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)). Open balls form a neighborhood basis for the metric topology ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)). Since $\ell$ is prime it is greater than $1$ ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime)), and a real geometric sequence with ratio $r$ satisfying $|r|<1$ tends to zero ([math:lem-geometric-sequence-null](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-geometric-sequence-null), claim 1).

[F6] Every finite list of primes omits a prime ([math:thm-euclid-infinitude-of-primes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-euclid-infinitude-of-primes)). The natural embedding $\iota:\mathbb N\to\mathbb Z$ preserves and reflects order and has image the nonnegative integers ([math:lem-nat-embeds-int](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-embeds-int)). Every nonempty subset of $\mathbb N$ has a least element ([math:thm-well-ordering-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-well-ordering-principle)). Natural-number recursion iterates a function from a set to itself starting at a given member of that set ([math:thm-recursion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-recursion)). For every natural number $m$, there is no injection from $\{0,\ldots,m\}$ into the initial segment of naturals strictly below $m$ ([math:lem-pigeonhole](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-pigeonhole)).

[F7] For every positive integer $M$, two integers represent the same residue modulo $M$ exactly when they are congruent modulo $M$ ([math:def-integers-modulo-n](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integers-modulo-n)). Congruence means that their difference is an integer multiple of $M$ ([math:def-congruence-modulo-an-integer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-congruence-modulo-an-integer)). Division with remainder by $M$ gives a unique representative $r$ with $0\le r<M$ for every residue class ([math:thm-division-algorithm-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-division-algorithm-in-z)).

[F8] A finite set admits a bijective listing indexed by the naturals less than some $N\in\mathbb N$ ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)). Every subset of a finite set is finite ([math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set)). For any list of nonempty sets indexed by the natural numbers less than a fixed $N\in\mathbb N$, there is a choice function on the family of its values ([math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)). Evaluating it at each list entry selects a member of that entry.

### Justification

**Goal:** Verify the group and topology, the identification of its elements with adelic quaternion units, and the compactness of the integral subgroup.

1.1 Pointwise multiplication is associative and the identity tuple is integral at every prime. Products and inverses stay integral units outside the union of the inputs' exceptional sets, so $\widehat B^\times$ is a group. The all-integral tuples contain the same identity and are closed under products and inverses at every prime, so they form a subgroup. Apply the scalar-extension calculation in [F1] to $R=\mathbb A_f$ and $R=\mathbb Q_\ell$, using the diagonal algebra structure in [F2]. Define $\Phi:B_{\mathbb A_f}\to\prod_\ell B_\ell$ by sending $\sum_r b_r\otimes a_r$ at prime $\ell$ to $\sum_r b_r\otimes a_{r,\ell}$, where $a_r=(a_{r,\ell})_\ell$. Write $b_rb_s=\sum_j m_{rsj}b_j$ and $1=\sum_j u_jb_j$ over $\mathbb Z$. By [F1], the product coefficients over $\mathbb A_f$ are $\sum_{r,s}m_{rsj}a_r a'_s$, and the identity has coefficients $u_j$. By [F2], evaluating these coefficients at $\ell$ gives the corresponding product and identity coefficients over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Evaluation also preserves addition coordinatewise and sends the coefficient tuple of a rational scalar times $1$ to that of the same scalar times $1$ in each $B_\ell$. Thus $\Phi$ is a $\mathbb Q$-algebra homomorphism. It is injective because zero image forces every $a_{r,\ell}$ to vanish by uniqueness of the local order coordinates. The four coefficient sequences of an element are integral outside the union of their exceptional sets, which is finite by three applications of the binary union argument in [F2]. Conversely, a tuple integral in $O_\ell$ outside one finite set has all four coefficient sequences in $\mathbb A_f$, so it lifts under $\Phi$. For a unit of $B_{\mathbb A_f}$, both the tuple and its inverse satisfy this condition, so its image belongs to $\widehat B^\times$. Conversely, for a tuple in $\widehat B^\times$, both it and its coordinatewise inverse lie in the image. Their preimages multiply in either order to $1$ by injectivity. This identifies $B_{\mathbb A_f}^\times$ with $\widehat B^\times$. [F1, F2, given]

1.2 An element of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is a unit exactly when its first residue is nonzero. Suppose its first residue is nonzero. For any $n\ge1$, take an integer representative $a$ of the level-$n$ residue. By compatibility the first residue is the class of $a$ modulo $\ell$. This class differs from the class of $0$, so [F7] gives $\ell\nmid a$. Since $\ell$ is prime, [F4] gives $\gcd(\ell,a)=1$. As $\ell>1$ by [F5], the identity in [F4] gives integers $c,b$ with $\ell c+ab=1$, so $1-ab=\ell c$. Then $b\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(1-ab)^k$ inverts $a$ modulo $\ell^n$ because multiplying by $a$ gives $1-(1-ab)^n$. Uniqueness of each inverse makes the inverses compatible, and reduction sends units to units. For $x=\sum_r a_r b_r\in O_\ell$, the $j$-th column of the left multiplication matrix is the coefficient column of $xb_j$. Each entry is a linear form in the $a_r$ with integral coefficients because the products $b_rb_j$ belong to $O$. Reduction to $\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z$ preserves each sum and product in the determinant formula, so the determinant residue, and hence the unit test, depends only on the four coefficient residues modulo $\ell$. [F1, F4, F5, F7]

1.3 For each $n\ge1$, the representatives in $0\le r<\ell^n$ from [F7] are indexed by the naturals less than $\iota^{-1}(\ell^n)$ by [F6], so the residue factor is finite by [F8]. The singleton consisting of a specified residue is open in its discrete factor by [F4], so agreement with a tuple at one residue level is an open cylinder trace. Any cylinder neighborhood of the tuple can be refined by fixing each of the finitely many restricted residues to the corresponding value of that tuple. Fixing agreement at the largest restricted level forces agreement at each smaller level by compatibility. Agreement at one level thus gives a neighborhood basis, using any level when the cylinder has no restrictions. The kernel of reduction modulo $\ell^n$ in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is $\ell^n\mathbb Z_\ell$. If $z=(z_m)_{m\ge1}$ has $z_n=0$, an integer representative of $z_{m+n}$ is divisible by $\ell^n$. By [F7], if two representatives $a$ and $a'$ give the same residue at level $m+n$, then $a-a'=\ell^{m+n}q$ for an integer $q$. After division by $\ell^n$, the difference is $\ell^m q$, so the quotients represent the same residue modulo $\ell^m$. Thus the quotient modulo $\ell^m$ is independent of the representative. These quotients are compatible, since congruence modulo $\ell^{m+n}$ becomes congruence modulo $\ell^m$ after division by $\ell^n$. They give a tuple $w$ with $\ell^n w=z$, since residue multiplication at level $m$ with the integer scalar $\ell^n$ gives $z_{m+n}\bmod\ell^m=z_m$. Conversely, every such multiple has zero residue at level $n$. In the local field, $\ell^n\mathbb Z_\ell=\{z:|z|_\ell\le\ell^{-n}\}$, by multiplicativity and the unit ball description. Thus an inverse-limit basic neighborhood of a given residue tuple is the translated ball of radius $\ell^{-n}$ with the non-strict inequality. For radius $r=\ell^{-n}$, any point of the ball has its strict open ball of radius $r$ inside it by the ultrametric inequality, so each residue neighborhood is open. By [F5] with ratio $1/\ell$, for every $\varepsilon>0$ some $n\ge1$ has $\ell^{-n}<\varepsilon$, so the residue neighborhood of that level is contained in the strict ball of radius $\varepsilon$ at the same point. These neighborhoods therefore give exactly the topology inherited from $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Taking four coordinate conditions at a common level in the order basis and tracing to $O_\ell^\times$ gives a neighborhood basis there. [F1, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8]

2.1 A tuple lies in the box using the full factor $B_\ell^\times$, which is open by (T1), at its finitely many exceptional primes and the open unit subgroup $O_\ell^\times$ elsewhere. The intersection of two boxes has the factorwise intersections, open by (T3) and equal to $O_\ell^\times$ off the finite union of their exceptional sets. They therefore form a basis. For either operation, fix a target box containing the output. Starting from a finite listing of each exceptional set by [F8], apply the binary union construction in [F2] at most twice to obtain a finite listing without repetitions of the exceptional primes of the target box and of the one or two inputs. At each listed prime the family of admissible open rectangles for multiplication is nonempty by local continuity and the product basis. For inversion the family of admissible open neighborhoods is nonempty by local continuity. Here admissibility means containing the local input or input pair and mapping into the target factor. In each case apply [F8] to the list of these families to select a rectangle or a neighborhood at every listed prime. At every other prime use the full integral unit group for each input, since it is closed under products and inverses. The resulting input boxes, taken as a rectangle for multiplication, map into the prescribed target box. Since target boxes form a basis, the pointwise criterion yields continuity of both operations. This gives a topological group. The all-integral box is open, and each restricted-product box has trace on it with factors $V_\ell\cap O_\ell^\times$, equal to the full integral unit group except at finitely many primes. With each $O_\ell^\times$ given its subspace topology from $B_\ell^\times$, these traces are product basic opens. Conversely, each open factor of a product basic open is the intersection of an ambient open set with the open subgroup $O_\ell^\times$, so (T3) makes it open in $B_\ell^\times$. The product basic open itself is then a restricted-product box with the prescribed trace. [F1, F2, F3, F8, step 1.1]

3.1 Use recursion on finite lists of distinct primes, starting with the empty list. The primes missing from the current list form a nonempty set by [F6]. Apply well-ordering to their natural preimages under $\iota$ and append the image of the least preimage. This produces a strictly increasing sequence. If a prime $p=\iota(m)$ never occurred, every appended prime would be smaller than $p$. The first $m+1$ appended primes would then have distinct natural preimages strictly below $m$, giving an injection forbidden by [F6]. Hence every prime occurs. At stage $n\ge1$, partition $\widehat O^\times$ by the four coefficient residues modulo $\ell^n$ at the first $n$ primes. At each selected prime the four residues are recorded by their unique representatives in $0\le r<\ell^n$ by [F7]. In the fixed prime and basis order, start with the one-entry list of the empty prefix. At each coordinate replace every listed prefix, in order, by the block of its extensions by one representative, taken in increasing order. Each block has the length of the new coordinate range, and concatenating the blocks gives a finite listing of all extended prefixes without repetition, since each extension determines its prefix and last representative. Iterating this rule by [F6] through the finitely many coordinates gives a finite lexicographic listing of all labels. The partitions refine each other. By the local residue basis in step 1.3 and the product topology in step 2.1, any neighborhood of a tuple contains its cell at a stage large enough to include all specified primes and residue levels. If an open cover has no finite subcover, start with the whole product. Consider any current cell with no finite subcover by members of that cover. The indices of labels of nonempty child cells form a subset of the finite index segment for the next stage, so this subset is finite by [F8]. Scan that segment in order and retain exactly these labels. Each retained label determines one child, giving a finite listing of the nonempty children in lexicographic order. If every child had a finite subcover, the family of finite lists of cover members covering that child would be nonempty for each listed child. By [F8] select one such list for every child. Use the binary concatenation underlying [F2] on the selected cover lists. For lists of lengths $a$ and $b$, take their entries at indices $i<a$ from the first list and at $a\le i<a+b$ from index $i-a$ of the second. Starting with the empty list, append each selected list in child order by recursion [F6] on the pair of the index and the accumulated list, fixing the pair once all children have been processed. The result is a finite list covering the current cell because the children partition it. The indices of the children with no finite subcover therefore form a nonempty subset of the naturals. Take its least member by well-ordering in [F6]. Pair the selected child with the next stage. This gives a uniquely specified successor on the set of pairs consisting of a stage and a cell with no finite subcover. Recursion from stage zero therefore gives a sequence of nested cells. The nested cells prescribe compatible residues in every coordinate, at every prime, giving a tuple by [F4]. Its first-level residues pass the unit tests because the cells with those labels are nonempty. Hence the tuple lies in every selected cell. A cover member containing it contains a sufficiently fine selected cell by the basis property, a contradiction. Thus $\widehat O^\times$ is compact, with the open subgroup structure and product topology established above. [F2, F3, F4, F6, F7, F8, step 1.2, step 1.3, step 2.1] ∎


## Normalized degree-two Satake transform by cosets

[Open item](item-def-author-d45da46285be-satake-transform) · level 2


### Definition

Assume countable choice, meaning that a sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. For a prime $\ell$, put $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ and $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$, where $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is the $\ell$-adic field and $\mathbb Z_\ell$ its valuation ring. For either coefficient ring $R$, $\mathrm{GL}_2(R)$ consists of two-by-two matrices over $R$ with a two-sided inverse over $R$. Give $G$ and $K$ the subspace topologies on their four matrix entries in $\mathbb Q_\ell^4$ with its product of $\ell$-adic metric topologies. Write $G/K$ for the set of cosets $xK=\{xk:k\in K\}$. For each $xK$, take an upper-triangular representative $b$, meaning $b_{21}=0$, and set the integer pair $(r(x),s(x))$ to $(\nu(b_{11}),\nu(b_{22}))$. The valuation $\nu(a)$ of a nonzero field element is specified by $|a|_\ell=\ell^{-\nu(a)}$.

For $f$ in the spherical Hecke algebra $\mathcal H(G,K)$ of complex functions on $G$ that vanish outside a compact subset of $G$ and are invariant under $K$ on both sides, define its normalized Satake transform by
$$S(f)=\sum_{xK\in G/K} f(x)\ell^{(s(x)-r(x))/2}X^{r(x)}Y^{s(x)}.$$
The sum runs over the finitely many cosets on which $f$ is nonzero. The factor $\ell^{(s(x)-r(x))/2}$ is the positive real rational power of $\ell$, viewed in $\mathbb C$. It equals the $(s(x)-r(x))$th integer power of the positive square root of $\ell$.

The target is the complex Laurent polynomial algebra $\mathbb C[X^{\pm1},Y^{\pm1}]$, the group algebra of $\mathbb Z^2$ with coordinatewise addition. Its elements are finitely supported coefficient functions on $\mathbb Z^2$, with equality, addition and scalar multiplication defined coefficientwise. Write $X^rY^s$ for the basis monomial of $(r,s)$. The unit is $X^0Y^0$, and the product is the complex-bilinear extension of $(X^rY^s)(X^aY^b)=X^{r+a}Y^{s+b}$.

For $u,v\in\mathbb C^\times$, define evaluation of a Laurent polynomial $P$ with coefficients $a_{r,s}$ by
$$\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(P)=\sum_{(r,s)\in\operatorname{supp}(P)}a_{r,s}u^rv^s.$$
Here $\operatorname{supp}(P)$ is the set of pairs with nonzero coefficient. The integer powers are taken in the multiplicative group $\mathbb C^\times$ of nonzero complex numbers.

### Definition references

[Metric topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)

[Product topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)

[Subspace topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)

[Compactness of a subset with its subspace topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)

[Invertible matrices over a coefficient ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring)

[The cosets of a subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)

[Extended absolute value on the completed field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)

[The additive group of integer pairs](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-external-direct-product-is-a-group)

[Finitely supported coefficient families and their basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis)

[Bilinear multiplication](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps)

[The group of units under multiplication](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] Under countable choice, with these topologies $G$ is a Hausdorff topological group and $K$ is a compact open subgroup. Each coset has an upper-triangular representative, and the integer valuations of its diagonal entries are independent of the representative ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice), [lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets)). On the completed field $\mathbb Q_\ell$, $|\cdot|_\ell$ is the extension of the rational $\ell$-adic absolute value ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)).

[F2] A spherical Hecke function is constant on each coset $xK$ and nonzero on only finitely many cosets ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F3] Finite sums in a commutative additive monoid are independent of enumeration and are computed by starting at zero and adding the terms one by one. They split over a union of two disjoint finite sets and may be iterated over finite products ([math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [math:lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini)).

[F4] The prime $\ell>1$ is read in the real power using the composite of $n\mapsto n/1$ and the ordered inclusion of the rationals into the reals. This sends $1$ to $1$ and preserves strict order, so the real base is positive ([math:lem-int-embeds-rat](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-int-embeds-rat), [math:lem-rat-embeds-dense](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-rat-embeds-dense)). For a positive real base, a rational power with denominator two uses the positive square root and the integer power given by its numerator. The complex numbers form a commutative field containing the embedded reals ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime), [math:def-rational-power](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-rational-power), [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)).

[F5] The integers form an additive group. Its direct product with itself is the group of integer pairs with coordinatewise addition, identity $(0,0)$ and inverse $(r,s)\mapsto(-r,-s)$ ([math:thm-int-comm-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-int-comm-ring), [math:thm-external-direct-product-is-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-external-direct-product-is-a-group)). For a commutative coefficient ring and a group, the group ring has the group elements as a basis, with coefficientwise linear operations, a bilinear product extending the group law, and the basis element of the group identity as its algebra unit ([math:def-group-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-ring), [math:def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-free-module-on-a-set-and-standard-basis), [math:thm-group-ring-is-a-unital-algebra-with-basis-g](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-group-ring-is-a-unital-algebra-with-basis-g)).

[F6] The units of a unital ring form a group under multiplication ([math:lem-ring-units-form-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group)). In a group, the zeroth power is the identity, negative integer powers use inverses, and $z^{a+b}=z^az^b$ for integer exponents $a,b$ ([math:def-group-power](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-power), [math:lem-group-power-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-power-laws)).

[F7] A complex-linear map preserves linear combinations, and a complex-bilinear map is linear in each variable ([math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map), [math:def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-balanced-and-bilinear-maps)). A unital ring homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication and the unit ([math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism)). A complex-algebra homomorphism is a unital ring homomorphism respecting the complex scalar structure maps ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F8] A finite set admits an enumeration without repetitions by a finite initial segment of the naturals, and every subset of a finite set is finite ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable), [math:thm-subset-of-a-finite-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-subset-of-a-finite-set)). Induction on the number of terms proves a property once it holds at zero and passes from each number to its successor ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

[F9] A Cartesian product of two finite sets is finite ([math:thm-product-rule](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-product-rule)).

### Justification

**Given:** Countable choice, a prime $\ell$, $f\in\mathcal H(G,K)$ and nonzero complex numbers $u,v$.

**Goal:** Verify that the normalized coset sum defines a Laurent polynomial and that evaluation is a unital complex-algebra homomorphism.

1.1 Let $T$ be the finite set of cosets on which $f$ is nonzero. The value of $f$ and the two exponents are constant on each coset. The base is positive in the reals by [F4], and $(s-r)/2$ is rational, so the normalization defines a complex scalar. With $M_{(r,s)}=X^rY^s$, each summand is a complex scalar multiple of a basis monomial in the group algebra. Finite addition in that algebra therefore defines $S(f)$ as a Laurent polynomial. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5]

1.2 Since $\mathbb C$ is a field with $1\ne 0$, its units are exactly its nonzero elements. Thus $\mathbb C^\times$ is a group under multiplication and all the integer powers in the evaluation sum are defined. For $\alpha=(r,s)$ put $w_\alpha=u^rv^s$. The integer power law and commutativity of complex multiplication give $w_{\alpha+\beta}=w_\alpha w_\beta$ and $w_{(0,0)}=1$. Evaluation depends only on the finitely supported coefficient function, and $\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(M_\alpha)=w_\alpha$ since $M_\alpha$ has the sole nonzero coefficient $1$ at $\alpha$. [F3, F4, F5, F6]

2.1 Fix $(r,s)$ and let $T_{r,s}$ be the subset of $T$ with that exponent pair. Both $T_{r,s}$ and its complement in $T$ are finite. By coefficientwise addition, taking the $(r,s)$-coefficient commutes with the finite sum: the zero polynomial has coefficient zero, and adding another summand adds its coefficient. The resulting scalar sum has zero terms on $T\setminus T_{r,s}$, whose sum is zero by the finite sum recursion. Splitting across these two sets gives the coefficient $\sum_{xK\in T_{r,s}}f(x)\ell^{(s-r)/2}$. Thus a nonzero coefficient can occur only at an exponent pair realized in $T$. To enumerate the realized pairs, read an enumeration of $T$ and append each pair only on its first occurrence. This gives a finite list without repetitions, including the empty list if $T$ is empty. [F3, F5, F8, step 1.1]

2.2 Let $I,J$ be the finite supports of Laurent polynomials $P,Q$, with coefficients $a_\alpha,b_\alpha$, respectively. The set $I\cup J$ is finite by concatenating an enumeration of $I$ with one of $J\setminus I$, which is a subset of $J$. For $c,d\in\mathbb C$, the coefficient of $cP+dQ$ at $\alpha$ is $ca_\alpha+db_\alpha$, so its support also lies in $I\cup J$. For each of $P,Q,cP+dQ$, the evaluation sum can run over $I\cup J$ by splitting off the finite set of additional indices, whose terms are zero. For each index the field laws give $(ca_\alpha+db_\alpha)w_\alpha=c(a_\alpha w_\alpha)+d(b_\alpha w_\alpha)$. Summing this equality gives $\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(cP+dQ)=c\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(P)+d\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(Q)$: the formula holds for the empty sum, and adding the next term preserves it by distributivity and commutativity of addition. This is the linear map criterion. [F3, F4, F5, F7, F8, step 1.2]

3.1 Since $I$ and $J$ are finite supports, $I\times J$ is finite by [F9]. Expand $P$ and $Q$ in the monomial basis over $I$ and $J$. Multiplication is linear in each variable, so applying linearity to each finite expansion gives $PQ=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in I\times J}a_\alpha b_\beta M_{\alpha+\beta}.$ Each application to a finite sum follows by starting at zero and adding one term at a time. By linearity of evaluation and the definition on a monomial, evaluating this expansion gives the sum of $a_\alpha b_\beta w_{\alpha+\beta}=(a_\alpha w_\alpha)(b_\beta w_\beta)$. The finite product index rule and distributivity in $\mathbb C$ now give $\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(PQ)=\left(\sum_{\alpha\in I}a_\alpha w_\alpha\right)\left(\sum_{\beta\in J}b_\beta w_\beta\right)=\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(P)\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(Q).$ Here multiplication by a fixed complex scalar distributes through each finite sum by iterating binary distributivity, including the empty sum since the scalar times zero is zero. [F3, F4, F5, F7, F8, F9, step 1.2, step 2.2]

4.1 The unit $M_{(0,0)}$ evaluates to $1$. Together with additivity and multiplicativity, this gives a unital ring homomorphism. Complex linearity gives $\operatorname{ev}_{u,v}(cM_{(0,0)})=c$, so evaluation respects the scalar structure map $c\mapsto cM_{(0,0)}$ of the Laurent algebra and the identity structure map of $\mathbb C$. Thus evaluation is a unital complex-algebra homomorphism on the target of the well-defined transform. [F5, F7, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 3.1] ∎


## Normalized upper-triangular spherical model

[Open item](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-model) · level 2


### Definition

Assume countable choice, meaning that a sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. For a prime $\ell$, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the canonical metric completion of $\mathbb Q$ for $d_\ell(x,y)=|x-y|_\ell$, where $|\cdot|_\ell$ on $\mathbb Q$ is the $\ell$-adic absolute value. Its points are classes of rational $d_\ell$-Cauchy sequences, with two sequences equivalent when their termwise distance tends to zero. Write $\mathbb Z_\ell=\{x\in\mathbb Q_\ell:|x|_\ell\le 1\}$ for its ring of integers, using the extended absolute value. Let $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ and $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. Identify each matrix with its four entries and give $G$ the subspace topology from the product topology on $\mathbb Q_\ell^4$, with each factor carrying the metric topology of the extended $\ell$-adic metric.

Let $B$ be the upper-triangular subgroup of $G$, consisting of matrices $b=(b_{ij})$ with $b_{21}=0$. For an ordered pair $u,v\in\mathbb C^\times$ and $b\in B$ whose diagonal entries $b_{11},b_{22}$ have $\ell$-adic valuations $r,s$, set
$$c_{u,v}(b)=\ell^{(s-r)/2}u^rv^s.$$
Here $\ell^{(s-r)/2}$ means $q^{s-r}$ for the positive real square root $q=\sqrt{\ell}$. Regard $q$ as a complex number and take all three integer powers in $\mathbb C^\times$, using inverses for negative exponents.

Let $I(u,v)$ be the vector space of locally constant complex functions $F$ on $G$ with $F(bg)=c_{u,v}(b)F(g)$ for every $b\in B$ and $g\in G$, using the pointwise operations inherited from $\mathbb C^G$. A function is locally constant when, for each $g\in G$, it is constant on some open set $U\subseteq G$ containing $g$. The group acts by right translation, $(R(h)F)(g)=F(gh)$.

The normalized upper-triangular spherical vector $\Phi_{u,v}$ is the $K$-fixed vector in $I(u,v)$ taking the value one at the identity. Being $K$-fixed here means being invariant under right translation by every $k\in K$. Its value on a triangular representative $b$ is $c_{u,v}(b)$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] For the prime $\ell$, the rational metric $d_\ell(x,y)=|x-y|_\ell$ uses the rational $\ell$-adic absolute value ([math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)). The canonical Cauchy-sequence quotient for this metric gives the metric space $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([math:def-field-of-p-adic-numbers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-of-p-adic-numbers)). This quotient is a complete field with the extended nonarchimedean absolute value ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)). Its ring of integers $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is identified with the subring $\{x\in\mathbb Q_\ell:|x|_\ell\le 1\}$ under the canonical embedding, and its unique maximal ideal is $\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$ ([Valuation ring of the local field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-zp-is-the-valuation-ring-of-qp), [math:cor-maximal-ideal-and-residue-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-maximal-ideal-and-residue-field-of-zp)). The matrix-entry topology on $G$ is the subspace topology from the product of four copies of $\mathbb Q_\ell$, each with its $\ell$-adic metric topology ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology), [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology), [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). Under countable choice, $G$ is a topological group and $K$ is a compact open subgroup in the matrix-entry topology. Each coset $gK$ has an upper-triangular representative with diagonal valuations determined by the coset. The valuation on nonzero field elements is integer valued and additive under multiplication ([math:def-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable-choice), [lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets)).

[F2] A group representation on a complex vector space acts by invertible linear maps, with product sent to composition. Its $K$-fixed vectors are those unchanged by all the actions of $k\in K$ ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F3] A local ring is a commutative ring with $1\ne 0$ and exactly one maximal ideal. Over a field or local ring, $\mathrm{GL}_2$ consists of two-by-two matrices with two-sided inverses. The matrix product is given by $(AB)_{ik}=a_{i1}b_{1k}+a_{i2}b_{2k}$ ([def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)). A subset of a group is a subgroup if it contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F4] The reals are a complete ordered field. Since the prime $\ell$ is an integer greater than one, it is the image of a unique natural $m\ge1$ under the natural-to-integer embedding ([math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime), [math:lem-nat-embeds-int](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-embeds-int)). In the real square root, $\ell$ means the canonical real numeral $m\cdot1_{\mathbb R}$, which is positive. Every nonnegative element of a complete ordered field has a unique nonnegative square root ([math:cor-cauchy-reals-lub-complete](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-cauchy-reals-lub-complete), [math:lem-of-naturals-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-naturals-positive), [math:lem-nonnegative-square-root-exists-and-unique](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nonnegative-square-root-exists-and-unique)).

[F5] The field $\mathbb C$ contains an embedded copy of $\mathbb R$, and its nonzero elements form an abelian group under multiplication. In a group, integer powers have $z^0=1$, use inverses for negative exponents, and satisfy $z^{m+n}=z^mz^n$ ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [math:def-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field), [math:def-group-power](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-power), [math:lem-group-power-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-power-laws)).

[F6] All functions from a set into a field form a vector space under pointwise operations. A subset of a vector space that contains zero and is closed under addition and scalar multiplication is a vector space under the restricted operations ([math:def-function-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-function-space), [math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)).

[F7] The whole space is open, and an intersection of two open sets is open. Left and right translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space), [math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion)). A homeomorphism sends open sets to open sets and has open preimages of open sets ([math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)).

### Justification

**Given:** A prime $\ell$, countable choice and nonzero $u,v\in\mathbb C$.

**Goal:** Establish the right-translation representation and the existence and uniqueness of its normalized $K$-fixed vector.

1.1 The ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is a unital subring of the field $\mathbb Q_\ell$, so it is commutative and has $1\ne 0$. Its unique maximal ideal from [F1] makes it a local ring by [F3]. The degree-two matrix group construction thus applies to both $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $\mathbb Z_\ell$. An invertible upper-triangular matrix has nonzero diagonal entries, since a zero diagonal entry would give a zero first column or second row. The product rule makes the lower-left entry of a triangular product zero and multiplies the diagonal entries componentwise. If $b$ is invertible and upper triangular, the lower-left entry of $bb^{-1}=1$ is $b_{22}(b^{-1})_{21}=0$, so the inverse is upper triangular as well. The identity has zero lower-left entry. The identity, product and inverse tests in [F3] therefore show that $B$ is a subgroup with the restricted matrix multiplication. [F1, F3]

2.1 Since $\ell$ is positive, its nonnegative square root is positive and gives a nonzero element $q$ of $\mathbb C$. The bases $q,u,v$ therefore admit all integer powers. If $b,b'\in B$ have valuation pairs $(r,s)$ and $(r',s')$, the product has valuations $(r+r',s+s')$ by the componentwise diagonal product and additivity of the valuation. The exponent addition law and commutativity of $\mathbb C^\times$ then give $c_{u,v}(bb')=c_{u,v}(b)c_{u,v}(b')$. The identity has valuation pair $(0,0)$ by additivity, so $c_{u,v}(1)=1$. [F1, F4, F5, step 1.1]

3.1 With the scalar multiplier now defined, the zero function is constant on the open set $G$ and satisfies the covariance condition. For $F,H\in I(u,v)$ and $a,d\in\mathbb C$, at any $g\in G$ take open sets $U,V$ containing $g$ on which $F,H$ respectively are constant. Then $U\cap V$ is open, contains $g$, and $aF+dH$ is constant there. At $bg$ both terms acquire the same scalar multiplier by covariance, so $(aF+dH)(bg)=c_{u,v}(b)(aF+dH)(g)$. Hence $I(u,v)$ is a vector subspace of $\mathbb C^G$. [F5, F6, F7, step 2.1]

4.1 For $h\in G$ and $F\in I(u,v)$, take an open set $U$ containing $gh$ on which $F$ is constant. Its preimage under right translation by $h$ is open by [F7], contains $g$, and $R(h)F$ is constant there. Also $(R(h)F)(bg)=F(b(gh))=c_{u,v}(b)(R(h)F)(g)$, so $R(h)$ maps $I(u,v)$ into itself. Evaluation at $gh$ commutes with pointwise linear combinations, which makes $R(h)$ linear. The formulas $(R(h)R(j)F)(g)=F(ghj)$ and $R(1)F=F$ give the representation law and the inverse $R(h^{-1})$ of $R(h)$. [F1, F2, F6, F7, step 3.1]

5.1 Define $\Phi_{u,v}(bk)=c_{u,v}(b)$ for $b\in B$ and $k\in K$. Every element has this form and its triangular exponents are uniquely determined by the coset, so the value is well defined. Each coset $gK$ is open as a left translate of the open subgroup $K$. Constancy on each coset gives local constancy and right $K$-invariance. For $b_0\in B$, the product $b_0b$ is triangular and $\Phi_{u,v}(b_0bk)=c_{u,v}(b_0b)=c_{u,v}(b_0)\Phi_{u,v}(bk)$, so $\Phi_{u,v}$ lies in $I(u,v)$. Its value at the identity is $c_{u,v}(1)=1$. [F1, F2, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 4.1]

6.1 If $F\in I(u,v)^K$, then for every $b\in B$ and $k\in K$ we have $F(bk)=F(b)=c_{u,v}(b)F(1)=F(1)\Phi_{u,v}(bk)$. Every element of $G$ is of this form, so $F=F(1)\Phi_{u,v}$. [F1, F2, F5, F6, step 5.1]

7.1 Conversely, for $a\in\mathbb C$, the function $a\Phi_{u,v}$ lies in $I(u,v)$ and $R(k)(a\Phi_{u,v})=aR(k)\Phi_{u,v}=a\Phi_{u,v}$ for every $k\in K$. Hence $I(u,v)^K=\{a\Phi_{u,v}:a\in\mathbb C\}$. Evaluating at the identity gives $(a\Phi_{u,v})(1)=a$, so distinct scalars give distinct fixed vectors and $\Phi_{u,v}$ is the unique fixed vector of value one there. [F2, F5, F6, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 5.1, step 6.1] ∎


## Reconstructing a quaternion lattice from its finite completions

[Open item](item-lem-author-f5ba73d33e6e-reconstructing-quaternion-lattices) · level 2


### Statement

In the rational Hamilton algebra $B=B(\mathbb Q)$, fix a full integral lattice $L$, the integer span of a rational basis of $B$. At each prime $\ell$, use the local Hamilton space $B_\ell=B(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ and embed $B$ into it by including each rational coefficient in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. The completion $L_\ell$ is the span of $L$ over the $\ell$-adic integers $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Let $J_\ell\subseteq B_\ell$ be the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of a $\mathbb Q_\ell$-basis, meaning four vectors in which each element of $B_\ell$ has a unique expression with coefficients in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Assume $J_\ell=L_\ell$ for all but finitely many $\ell$.

The set $M=\{b\in B:b\in J_\ell\text{ for every prime }\ell\}$ is the unique full integral lattice with $M_\ell=J_\ell$ for every $\ell$. Every full integral lattice $A$ satisfies $A=\{b\in B:b\in A_\ell\text{ for every prime }\ell\}$ and $A_\ell=L_\ell$ for all but finitely many $\ell$.

### Statement references

[def-author-24fda87362bc-hamilton-algebra-and-reduced-norm](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-hamilton-algebra-and-reduced-norm)

[def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions)

[def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] A rational lattice basis remains a basis over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Its $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span is the completion and is the closure of the rational lattice for convergence in those basis coordinates ([def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions)).

[F2] The normalized valuation $v_\ell$ on $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is additive on products of nonzero scalars. A scalar belongs to $\mathbb Z_\ell$ if its valuation is nonnegative, with zero included, and is a unit exactly at valuation zero. Here $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is the completion of $\mathbb Q$ for the absolute value $\ell^{-v_\ell(\cdot)}$ on nonzero rationals, with absolute value zero at zero. For nonzero rationals the valuation is the numerator exponent minus the denominator exponent ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)).

[F3] Positive integers factor uniquely into primes, including the empty product for $1$ ([math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic)).

[F4] Pull a nonempty set of positive integers back along the canonical embedding of the natural numbers into the integers. The embedding is one-to-one, has image the nonnegative integers, and preserves and reflects order. Well-ordering gives a least natural number in the inverse image, and its integer image is the least member of the original set. ([math:lem-nat-embeds-int](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-embeds-int), [math:thm-well-ordering-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-well-ordering-principle)).

[F5] Division of an integer by a positive integer has an integer remainder at least zero and less than the divisor ([math:thm-division-algorithm-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-division-algorithm-in-z)).

### Proof

**Given:** The reference lattice $L$ and the family of local spans $J_\ell$ in the statement.

**Goal:** Reconstruct the unique full integral lattice and establish the intersection and finite-exception properties.

1.1 A rational number belonging to every $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is an integer. For a nonzero rational number $r$, take a representation $r=a/b$ with $a\in\mathbb Z$ and the least positive integer denominator $b$, which exists by [F4]. If $b>1$, choose a prime $\ell$ dividing $b$ by [F3]. It cannot divide $a$, since cancelling it would give the smaller positive denominator $b/\ell$. Then $v_\ell(a)=0$ and $v_\ell(b)>0$, so $r\notin\mathbb Z_\ell$ by [F2]. Thus $b=1$ if $r$ belongs to every $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Zero and all other integers belong to every $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Test the coordinates of $b\in B$ in a basis of an arbitrary full integral lattice $A$. Membership in all $A_\ell$ means each of these rational coordinates belongs to every $\mathbb Z_\ell$, which gives the stated recovery of $A$. [F1, F2, F3, F4]

1.2 For an arbitrary full integral lattice $A$, clear the denominators in both changes of basis between $A$ and $L$ using one positive integer $d$. Then $dL\subseteq A$ and $dA\subseteq L$. At primes not dividing $d$, multiplication by $d$ preserves each $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span because $d$ is a scalar unit. The inclusions give $A_\ell=L_\ell$ there. [F1, F2, F3]

2.1 For each prime $\ell$ with $J_\ell\ne L_\ell$, express each basis vector of $L_\ell$ in the chosen local basis of $J_\ell$ and each basis vector of $J_\ell$ in the basis of $L_\ell$. Choose a positive integer $n_\ell$ so that multiplying these coefficients by $\ell^{n_\ell}$ puts them in $\mathbb Z_\ell$. This is possible because the nonzero entries have integer valuations and there are finitely many entries. The scaled basis vectors give $\ell^{n_\ell}L_\ell\subseteq J_\ell$ and $\ell^{n_\ell}J_\ell\subseteq L_\ell$. Put $D=\prod_{\ell:J_\ell\ne L_\ell}\ell^{n_\ell}$. At each exceptional prime $D$ is $\ell^{n_\ell}$ times a local scalar unit, and elsewhere $D$ is a local scalar unit. Thus $D L_\ell\subseteq J_\ell\subseteq D^{-1}L_\ell$ at every prime. Applying step 1.1 to $L$ gives $DL\subseteq M\subseteq D^{-1}L$. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1]

3.1 Each $J_\ell$ contains zero and is closed under subtraction, since subtracting its linear combinations subtracts their coefficients in $\mathbb Z_\ell$. These properties hold for $M$ by the intersection condition and for $K=DM$ by scalar multiplication. By step 2.1, $D^2 L\subseteq K\subseteq L$. Also, a rational vector $w$ belongs to $K$ exactly when $w\in D J_\ell$ for every $\ell$, by dividing by $D$. In the basis of $L$, for each $j=1,\ldots,4$ consider the $j$th coordinates of elements of $K$ whose first $j-1$ coordinates vanish. The resulting coordinate set $S_j\subseteq\mathbb Z$ contains zero and is closed under subtraction, since a difference of two vectors in $K$ with those vanishing coordinates still has those coordinates zero. It thus contains every integer multiple of any of its elements, using additive inverses and repeated addition. It contains $D^2$ by taking $D^2$ times the $j$th basis vector of $L$. Let $c_j$ be its least positive integer by [F4]. For any $t\in S_j$, division by $c_j$ gives $t=qc_j+r$ with $0\le r<c_j$ by [F5]. The difference $r=t-qc_j$ lies in $S_j$, so minimality gives $r=0$. Hence $S_j=c_j\mathbb Z$. Take a vector $k_j$ of this form with $j$th coordinate $c_j$. Successively subtracting integer multiples of $k_1,\ldots,k_4$ clears every coordinate of any element of $K$. The vectors are rationally independent because the first nonzero coefficient in a rational relation would contribute a nonzero coordinate at its index. Their rational span contains $D^2L$, which spans $B$. Hence $K$ is a full integral lattice, and dividing its basis by $D$ gives a basis for $M$. [F1, F2, F4, F5, step 2.1]

4.1 Taking local spans gives $D^2L_\ell\subseteq K_\ell\subseteq D J_\ell\subseteq L_\ell$. These sets are equal at primes not dividing $D$. Fix a prime $\ell$ dividing $D$ and let $a=v_\ell(D)$ and $E=D^2/\ell^{2a}$. For $z\in D J_\ell\subseteq L_\ell$, the scalar $E$ is a unit at $\ell$, so $E^{-1}z\in L_\ell$. By the closure property in [F1], choose $y\in L$ with $y-E^{-1}z\in\ell^{2a} L_\ell$, by approximating each coordinate to distance at most $\ell^{-2a}$. Hence $Ey-z\in D^2L_\ell$. At every other prime $q$, the scalar $\ell^{2a}$ is a unit, so $Ey\in E L_q=D^2L_q\subseteq D J_q$. At $\ell$, the containment $Ey-z\in D^2L_\ell\subseteq D J_\ell$ gives $Ey\in D J_\ell$ as well. By the description of $K$ in step 3.1, $Ey\in K$. Then $z\in K_\ell$ because $Ey\in K_\ell$ and $Ey-z\in D^2L_\ell\subseteq K_\ell$. This proves $K_\ell=D J_\ell$ for every prime, so $M_\ell=J_\ell$. Uniqueness follows from step 1.1, and step 1.2 gives the asserted finite-exception property. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1] ∎


## The first Hecke coefficient of a weight-two cusp form

[Open item](item-lem-author-e2fc0f4318ab-classical-hecke-first-coefficient) · level 2


### Statement

Assume countable choice and take a normalized weight-two cuspidal newform $f(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n q^n$ of level $N$ and character $\epsilon$, with $q=\exp(2\pi i z)$ and $a_1=1$. Let $\ell$ be a prime not dividing $N$. Then the scalar of the classical Hecke operator $T_\ell$ on $f$ is $a_\ell$.

### Statement references

[Weight-two cusp forms and their Fourier coefficients](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form)

[Newforms and the classical Hecke convention](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform)

[Dirichlet characters modulo the level](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q)

[Prime integers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime)

[Integer divisibility](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-divides-in-z)

[Complex exponential](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-exponential)

[The constant $\pi$](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero)

[Complex powers with natural exponents](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-integer-powers)

[Complex power series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The residue class of $1$ is the identity in $(\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z)^\times$ ([math:def-unit-group-modulo-n-and-euler-totient](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-unit-group-modulo-n-and-euler-totient)). The character homomorphism sends it to the identity of $\mathbb C^\times$, which gives $\epsilon(1)=1$ ([math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q), [math:lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-group-homomorphism-basic-properties)). In weight two, the two positive factorizations of the prime $\ell$ are $(1,\ell)$ and $(\ell,1)$. Substituting them in the classical formula and using $\epsilon(1)=1$ gives $(T_\ell f)(z)=\ell^{-1}\sum_{b=0}^{\ell-1}f((z+b)/\ell)+\ell\epsilon(\ell)f(\ell z)$. A normalized newform is an eigenfunction for this operator ([def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform), [math:def-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime), [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q)).

[F2] A cusp form is holomorphic, has period one at infinity and tends uniformly to zero as $\operatorname{Im}z\to\infty$. Indeed the translation matrix $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_0(N)$ has lower right entry $1$, so the transformation law and [F1] give $f(z+1)=f(z)$. The vanishing condition for the identity matrix gives the uniform limit. The cusp expansion theorem then gives $f(z)=F(q(z))$ with $F(w)=\sum_{n\ge1}a_n w^n$ convergent for $|w|<1$. Its cusp coordinate $q(z)=\exp(2\pi i z)$ maps $\mathfrak H$ onto the punctured unit disc ([def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form), [math:thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-q-expansion-principle-at-the-cusp), [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q), [math:def-complex-exponential](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-exponential), [math:def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero), [math:def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence)).

[F3] The exponential is multiplicative under addition in the exponent ([math:thm-complex-exponential-addition-and-real-extension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-exponential-addition-and-real-extension)). The natural-to-integer embedding $\iota$ identifies $\mathbb N$ with the nonnegative integers in order and preserves addition and multiplication ([math:lem-nat-embeds-int](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-embeds-int)). For the prime $\ell$, take the unique natural $L$ with $\iota(L)=\ell$. In real and complex field expressions $\ell$ denotes the canonical real numeral of $L$, using the embedded real field in $\mathbb C$. This numeral is positive in $\mathbb R$ and nonzero in $\mathbb C$ ([math:def-canonical-natural](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-canonical-natural), [math:thm-reals-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-ordered-field), [math:lem-of-naturals-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-naturals-positive), [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)). Set $\zeta=\exp(2\pi i/\ell)$. The $L$th roots of unity are the distinct numbers $\exp(2\pi i k/\ell)$ for natural $0\le k<L$, with $k$ read as its canonical real numeral in the exponent ([math:def-complex-exponential](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-exponential), [math:def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-pi-via-first-positive-cosine-zero), [math:thm-complex-nth-roots-and-roots-of-unity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-nth-roots-and-roots-of-unity)). A nonnegative integer used as a complex power exponent acts by its unique natural preimage ([math:def-complex-integer-powers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-integer-powers)).

[F4] Integer divisibility $\ell\mid n$ means $n=\ell j$ for some integer $j$. Since $\ell>0$, the division algorithm gives $n=\ell j+r$ with $0\le r<\ell$, and $r=0$ exactly when $\ell\mid n$. Integer inequalities are compatible with addition and multiplication by a positive integer ([math:def-divides-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-divides-in-z), [math:thm-division-algorithm-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-division-algorithm-in-z), [math:thm-int-ordered-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-int-ordered-ring)).

[F5] A linear combination of two complex power series about the same centre is represented coefficientwise on their common open disc of convergence ([math:prop-sums-and-scalar-multiples-of-complex-power-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-sums-and-scalar-multiples-of-complex-power-series)).

[F6] Two convergent power series that represent the same function on a neighbourhood of their common centre have equal coefficients ([math:cor-uniqueness-of-complex-power-series-coefficients](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-uniqueness-of-complex-power-series-coefficients)).

[F7] In the complex field the natural power laws are $x^{j+k}=x^j x^k$, $(x^j)^k=x^{jk}$ and $(xy)^k=x^k y^k$. The field power laws apply with the complex power convention because both natural powers use the same recursion $x^0=1$ and $x^{k+1}=x^kx$ ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [math:lem-power-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-power-laws), [math:def-complex-integer-powers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-integer-powers)).

[F8] Finite sums in the additive commutative monoid of $\mathbb C$ are invariant under bijections of their index sets. A sum over a disjoint union splits into the sums over its parts, and a sum of terms that are all zero is zero by the finite-sum definition ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [math:lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini)).

[F9] Convergence of a complex power series means convergence of its ordinary partial sums in $d_{\mathbb C}(x,y)=|x-y|$. Given a positive rational tolerance, all partial sums with sufficiently large cutoff are within that tolerance of the limit ([math:def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-series-power-series-and-absolute-convergence), [math:def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity), [math:def-metric-convergence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-convergence)).

[F10] A complex power series diverges outside its radius of convergence, so convergence throughout the open unit disc implies radius at least $1$ ([math:thm-cauchy-hadamard-for-complex-power-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-cauchy-hadamard-for-complex-power-series)).

[F11] The finite initial segment $I_L=\{b\in\mathbb N:b<L\}$ has cardinality $L$ ([math:lem-cardinality-of-finite-natural-initial-segment](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-cardinality-of-finite-natural-initial-segment)). In its natural enumeration the finite complex sum uses the recursion $P_0=0$ and $P_{j+1}=P_j+x_j$ for the list $(x_b)_{b\in I_L}$ ([math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), [math:def-monoid-finite-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-monoid-finite-product)).

[F12] For real $a>0$ and $c\in\mathbb R$, the map $v\mapsto av+c$ takes $\mathfrak H$ into itself. It is the fractional linear map of $\begin{pmatrix}a&c\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, whose determinant is $a>0$, so the upper half-plane calculation in the cusp-form definition applies ([def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form)).

[F13] If a property of natural numbers holds at zero and passes from each natural number to its successor, it holds for every natural number ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

### Proof

**Given:** Countable choice, the normalized newform $f$ and the good prime $\ell$.

**Goal:** Identify its classical Hecke eigenvalue with its Fourier coefficient $a_\ell$.

1.1 At $k=0$ the root formula in [F3] gives $\exp(0)^L=1$. Since $L>0$, the natural power recursion in [F7] gives $0^L=0$, so $\exp(0)\ne0$. The addition law gives $\exp(0)=\exp(0)^2$, and cancellation gives $\exp(0)=1$. Write $\nu(r)$ for the canonical real numeral of a natural $r$ embedded in $\mathbb C$. Fix $w\in\mathbb C$. To show $\exp(\nu(r)w)=\exp(w)^r$ by induction on $r$, first note that $\nu(0)=0$, so both sides are $1$ at $r=0$. For the successor, $\nu(r+1)=\nu(r)+1$ by the canonical-natural recursion. Thus $\exp(\nu(r+1)w)=\exp(\nu(r)w)\exp(w)=\exp(w)^r\exp(w)=\exp(w)^{r+1}$ by the addition law, the induction hypothesis and the power recursion. By [F13] the identity holds for every natural $r$. With $w=2\pi i/\ell$ it gives $\exp(2\pi i k/\ell)=\zeta^k$, so the powers $\zeta^k$ for $0\le k<L$ are the distinct $L$th roots from [F3]. [F3, F7, F13]

1.2 Fix a complex scalar $c$ and a complex list $(x_b)_{b\in I_L}$. If $P_j$ and $Q_j$ are the partial sums of $x_b$ and $cx_b$, then $cP_0=Q_0=0$ and $cP_{j+1}=cP_j+cx_j$ by distributivity. The uniqueness of the finite-sum recursion therefore gives $Q_j=cP_j$ for every $0\le j\le L$. [F7, F11]

2.1 For natural $0\le b<L$, read $b$ as its canonical real numeral in the affine maps. The maps $v\mapsto v/\ell$, $v\mapsto (v+b)/\ell$ and $v\mapsto v+b/\ell$ all preserve $\mathfrak H$ by [F12] since $1/\ell>0$ by [F3]. For $z\in\mathfrak H$ put $t=q(z/\ell)$. By step 1.1 and the exponential addition law, $q((z+b)/\ell)=\exp(2\pi i b/\ell)t=\zeta^b t$ for natural $0\le b<L$. Applying step 1.1 with $r=L$ and $w=2\pi i z/\ell$ gives $t^L=q(z)$. The power laws give $(\zeta^b t)^n=\zeta^{bn}t^n$. Each shifted function is thus represented by the power series $\sum_{n\ge1}a_n\zeta^{bn}t^n$ in the same variable $t$. These series converge for $|t|<1$. If $0<|t|<1$, write $t=q(w)$ for some $w\in\mathfrak H$ by [F2]. Then $\zeta^b t=q(w+b/\ell)$ lies in the punctured unit disc since $w+b/\ell\in\mathfrak H$ by the same affine-map check. At $t=0$ each series is zero. [F2, F3, F7, F12, step 1.1]
