2.2 Divide the integer $\iota(n)$ as $\iota(n)=\ell j+r$ with $0\le r<\ell$. If $j\le-1$, then $\iota(n)\le-\ell+r<0$, which contradicts $n\ge1$. Taking natural preimages gives $n=Lh+k$ with $h\ge0$ and $0\le k<L$. Since $\zeta^L=1$, the power laws give $\zeta^n=(\zeta^L)^h\zeta^k=\zeta^k$. Distinctness of the first $L$ powers then gives $\zeta^n=1$ exactly when $k=0$, equivalently $\ell\mid\iota(n)$. [F3, F4, F7, step 1.1]

3.1 For $b\in I_L$ denote the series from step 2.1 by $H_b(t)=\sum_{n\ge0}x_{b,n}t^n$, where $x_{b,0}=0$ and $x_{b,n}=a_n\zeta^{bn}$ for $n\ge1$. Each has radius at least $1$ by [F10]. For $0\le j\le L$ put $U_j(t)=\sum_{0\le b<j}H_b(t)$ and $d_{j,n}=\sum_{0\le b<j}x_{b,n}$. Induct using [F13] on the assertion that whenever $j\le L$, the series $\sum_{n\ge0}d_{j,n}t^n$ has radius at least $1$ and represents $U_j(t)$ throughout $|t|<1$. At $j=0$ both sums over $b$ are empty, so the zero series represents $U_0=0$ and has radius at least $1$ by [F10]. If $j+1\le L$, the finite-sum recursion gives $U_{j+1}=U_j+H_j$ and $d_{j+1,n}=d_{j,n}+x_{j,n}$. The induction hypothesis and the radius bound for $H_j$ give a common disc containing $|t|<1$. Applying [F5] to these two series gives the required representation with coefficient $d_{j+1,n}$. Convergence throughout $|t|<1$ gives radius at least $1$ by [F10]. At $j=L$ and $n\ge1$ the coefficient is $\sum_{b\in I_L}a_n\zeta^{bn}$. By step 1.2 this is $a_n\sum_{b\in I_L}\zeta^{bn}$. Scaling the series by $\ell^{-1}$ as in [F1] gives the averaged coefficient $a_n S_n$, where $S_n=\ell^{-1}\sum_{b\in I_L}\zeta^{bn}$. [F1, F5, F8, F10, F11, F13, step 2.1, step 1.2]

3.2 The dilation $z\mapsto\ell z$ preserves $\mathfrak H$ by [F12] since $\ell>0$ by [F3]. Applying step 1.1 with $r=L$ and $w=2\pi i z$ gives $q(\ell z)=q(z)^L$. For each $q$ in the punctured disc, take a preimage $z\in\mathfrak H$. Then $\ell z\in\mathfrak H$, so $|q^L|<1$, which also holds at $q=0$. Define $c_{Ln}=a_n$ for natural $n\ge1$ and $c_m=0$ at every other degree $m$. For each natural $M$ write $M=LJ+k$ with $0\le k<L$ by [F4] and the natural embedding as in step 2.2. The remainder bound gives $LJ\le M<L(J+1)$. Since multiplication by $L>0$ preserves strict order, $n\mapsto Ln$ is a bijection from $\{1,\ldots,J\}$ onto the positive multiples of $L$ at most $M$. Splitting off the zero terms and reindexing by this bijection using [F8] gives $\sum_{m=0}^M c_mq^m=\sum_{n=1}^J a_n(q^L)^n$. For any positive rational tolerance, the right-hand partial sum satisfies the bound relative to $F(q^L)$ whenever $J\ge K$ for some $K$. The bound therefore holds on the left whenever $M\ge LK$, since $J<K$ would give $M=LJ+k<LK$. By [F9], $B(q)=\sum_{m\ge0}c_mq^m$ converges to $F(q^L)$ on the unit disc. Its degree-one coefficient is zero because $L>1$. [F2, F3, F4, F7, F8, F9, F12, step 1.1, step 2.2]

4.1 Put $u=\zeta^n$. If $u=1$, the sum defining $S_n$ adds $L$ copies of $1$ by [F11]. The resulting sum is $\ell$ by the canonical-natural recursion in the real field and its embedding in $\mathbb C$, so $S_n=\ell^{-1}\ell=1$. Otherwise $u^L=(\zeta^L)^n=1$ and $\zeta^{bn}=u^b$. Multiplying by $u$ replaces $u^b$ by $u^{b+1}$. The map sending $b$ to $b+1$ for $0\le b<L-1$ and sending $L-1$ to $0$ is a bijection of $\{0,\ldots,L-1\}$. Each $1\le b<L$ has preimage $b-1$, and $0$ has preimage $L-1$. At the wrap we have $u^L=u^0=1$. By step 1.2 and commutativity in the complex field, $uS_n=\ell^{-1}\sum_{b\in I_L}u^{b+1}$. Reindexing by this permutation using [F8] gives $uS_n=S_n$. Since $1-u\ne0$ in the field, $(1-u)S_n=0$ gives $S_n=0$. [F3, F7, F8, F11, step 1.2, step 3.1]

5.1 The surviving positive indices are $n=Lj$ with natural $j\ge1$. For every $q$ with $0<|q|<1$, choose $z\in\mathfrak H$ with $q(z)=q$ by [F2]. Since $z\mapsto z/\ell$ preserves $\mathfrak H$ by step 2.1, put $t=q(z/\ell)$. Let $P_M(t)$ be the partial sum through degree $M$ of the averaged series and let $s$ be its sum at $t$. For each natural $J$, the map $j\mapsto Lj$ from $\{1,\ldots,J\}$ is a bijection onto the surviving positive indices up to $LJ$, since $L>0$ and $Lj\le LJ$ exactly when $j\le J$. Splitting off the zero terms and reindexing by this bijection using [F8], then using $t^L=q$, gives $P_{LJ}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^J a_{Lj}q^j$. For each positive rational $\eta$, choose $K$ so that $|P_M(t)-s|<\eta$ for all $M\ge K$. Since $LJ\ge J$, this bound holds at $M=LJ$ for every $J\ge K$. Thus $A(q)=\sum_{j\ge1}a_{Lj}q^j$ converges and represents the first term of [F1] on the punctured disc. It converges to zero at $q=0$. [F1, F2, F4, F7, F8, F9, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 2.2, step 4.1]

6.1 The second term in [F1] is $\ell\epsilon(\ell)B(q)$ at $q=q(z)$. $A$, $B$ and $F$ converge throughout the unit disc and thus have radius at least $1$. By [F5] the power series for $A(q)+\ell\epsilon(\ell)B(q)$ has degree-one coefficient $a_L=a_\ell$, where $\ell$ has natural representative $L$. Write $T_\ell f=\lambda f$ by [F1]. Surjectivity of the cusp coordinate gives equality of this sum with $\lambda F(q)$ on the punctured unit disc, and both sides have value zero at $q=0$. By [F5], the series for $\lambda F(q)$ has coefficient $\lambda a_1$ at degree one. They represent the same function on the unit disc, so [F6] gives $a_\ell=\lambda a_1=\lambda$. [F1, F2, F3, F5, F6, F10, step 5.1, step 3.2, given] ∎


## Split adelic matrix groups

[Open item](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups) · level 2


### Definition

Write $\mathbb A=\mathbb R\times\mathbb A_f$ for the rational adele ring, with coordinatewise operations. Its finite tuples have entries in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes $\ell$. Its group of units $\mathbb A^\times=\mathbb R^\times\times\mathbb A_f^\times$ has the restricted-product topology using $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ at the finite places. Give $\mathbb R$ its usual topology, induced by $d(x,y)=|x-y|$, and give $\mathbb R^\times$ the subspace topology from $\mathbb R$. Each $\mathbb Q_\ell$ has its $\ell$-adic topology.

The group $L=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$ consists of two-by-two matrices over $\mathbb A$ with a two-sided inverse over $\mathbb A$. Give each local field matrix group the subspace topology from the fourfold product of its field. Equip $L$ with the restricted-product topology of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)$ and the $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, using the subgroups $K_\ell=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ at the finite places. In a restricted product the tuple lies in the specified subgroup at all but finitely many places, and the basic open boxes are products of local open sets equal to the specified subgroups at all but finitely many places.

The normal scalar subgroup is $Z=\{aI:a\in\mathbb A^\times\}$, where $I$ is the identity matrix. The projective adelic matrix group is $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb A)=L/Z$ with the quotient topology. A representation has trivial central character here when each $aI$ acts as the identity.

### Definition references

[math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring)

[math:def-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-commutative-ring)

[math:def-product-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-ring)

[def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)

[math:def-complete-ordered-real-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complete-ordered-real-field)

[math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)

[math:def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring)

[math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)

[math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)

[math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)

[math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)

[math:thm-basis-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-basis-criterion)

[math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)

[math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)

[math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group)

[math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)

[math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)

[math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space)

[math:lem-ring-units-form-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group)

[math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup)

[math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose)

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The real and $\ell$-adic fields with their indicated topologies, and the scalar adele ring and its units.

[F1] The finite adeles form a commutative ring under coordinatewise operations. Its units are the tuples of nonzero local scalars that belong to $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many primes. Each $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is a valued field and both $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ are open in it. [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)

[F2] The real field is an ordered commutative field with its usual absolute value. On $\mathbb R$ the usual topology is the metric topology of $d(x,y)=|x-y|$. For either local field $F$, use the metric $d(x,y)=|x-y|$ from its multiplicative absolute value with the triangle inequality. In the metric topology an open set contains a ball about each of its points. For a positive radius $r$, the open ball about $x$ is $B(x,r)=\{y:d(x,y)<r\}$. [math:def-complete-ordered-real-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complete-ordered-real-field) [math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space) [math:lem-of-abs-value](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-abs-value) [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles) [math:def-multiplicative-absolute-value-on-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-multiplicative-absolute-value-on-a-field) [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology) [math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)

[F3] Over a commutative ring the general linear group consists of matrices with two-sided inverses. Matrix multiplication and the identity are given by the usual entry formulas. [math:def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring) [math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose)

[F4] An element of a ring with identity is a unit exactly when it has a two-sided multiplicative inverse. The units form a group under multiplication. For a commutative ring $R$ and $n\geq 1$, an $n$-by-$n$ matrix over $R$ is invertible exactly when its determinant is a unit in $R$. For a two-by-two matrix with entries $a,b,c,d$ in row order its determinant is $ad-bc$. [math:lem-ring-units-form-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-ring-units-form-a-group) [math:cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit) [math:ex-two-by-two-determinant-formula](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/ex-two-by-two-determinant-formula)

[F5] A covering family of subsets that is closed under binary intersections is a basis for a topology. Finite products have a basis of open rectangles, and subspace opens are traces of ambient opens. Continuity at a point can be tested by finding an input neighborhood mapped into each open neighborhood of the image. A topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion. [math:thm-basis-criterion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-basis-criterion) [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology) [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top) [math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top) [math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)

[F6] A subgroup is normal when conjugation by each group element maps it onto itself. For a subgroup $Z$ of $L$, the left coset of $g$ is $gZ=\{gz:z\in Z\}$. For normal $Z$, the quotient consists of these cosets and has multiplication $(gZ)(hZ)=(gh)Z$. A subset of the target of a quotient map is open exactly when its inverse image is open. A map out of the quotient is continuous exactly when its pullback along the quotient map is continuous. [math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup) [math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset) [math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group) [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology) [math:thm-quotient-universal-property](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-universal-property)

[F7] Use the metric $d_H(v,w)=\|v-w\|$ on a complex Hilbert space $H$, with the norm induced by its inner product. A strongly continuous unitary representation is a homomorphism to the unitary operators on a complex Hilbert space whose orbit map is norm-continuous for each vector. A group homomorphism $f$ satisfies $f(gh)=f(g)f(h)$ for every $g,h$ in its domain. [math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space) [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology) [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation) [math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism)

[F8] The whole space, binary intersections of open sets, and arbitrary unions of open sets are open. A set is closed when its complement is open. [math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)

[F9] A subset of a group is a subgroup when it contains the ambient identity and is closed under products and inverses. [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)

[F10] A finite set has a bijective listing indexed by a natural number ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)). For a list of nonempty sets indexed by a natural number, the family of its values has a choice function ([math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)). Evaluating that function on each entry selects one member of each listed set.

[F11] A ring has an abelian additive group, associative multiplication with identity, and both distributive laws. A commutative ring also has commutative multiplication. [math:def-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring) [math:def-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-commutative-ring) The product of two commutative rings is a commutative ring with coordinatewise operations. [math:def-product-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-ring)

[F12] The union of two finite sets is finite, and adjoining one element to a finite set gives a finite set. [Finite-set fact (ii)](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-standard-topologies)

[F13] For any element $z$ of a topological group, right translation by $z$ is a homeomorphism. [math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion) A homeomorphism has a continuous inverse. An open map sends open sets to open sets. [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)

[F14] The composite of two continuous maps between topological spaces is continuous. [Claim 1](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-continuity-is-local-and-pastes)

[F15] A map between topological spaces is continuous exactly when the preimage of every open set is open. For parts (a) and (b), take the target topology itself as the subbasis. [math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top) [math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)

### Justification

**Goal:** Verify the restricted-product realization, the group topologies, and descent of strongly continuous unitary representations with trivial scalar action.

1.1 The product of the real field and the finite adele ring uses coordinatewise operations. The additive and multiplicative identities of $\mathbb A$ are the pairs of identities in the two factors, and additive inverses are taken in each coordinate. The addition and multiplication laws, both distributive laws, and commutativity of multiplication hold in each coordinate. Thus it is a commutative unital ring. An element of it is a unit exactly when each of its two components is a unit. A two-sided inverse projects to inverses in both factors, and a pair of factor inverses gives a two-sided inverse in the product. Thus the indicated idele group and the general-ring matrix group apply. If $g\in L$, its coordinates and those of $g^{-1}$ are local inverses. The union of the eight finite entry exception sets of $g$ and $g^{-1}$ is finite by successive binary unions. Outside this union both matrices have integral entries and are inverses over $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Conversely, a tuple in the restricted product has a unique inverse at each place. At primes where the tuple belongs to $K_\ell$, that inverse is integral. The given matrices and the inverse matrices therefore assemble into two adelic matrices, and both matrix products are the identity coordinatewise. This identifies $L$ with the restricted product, with coordinatewise multiplication. [given, F1, F2, F3, F4, F11, F12]

1.2 In the absolute-value metric on either local field, increments $h,k$ at $(x,y)$ change the sum and product by at most $|h|+|k|$ and $|x||k|+|y||h|+|h||k|$, respectively. Balls are open: if $v\in B(u,r)$, the triangle inequality puts $B(v,r-d(u,v))$ inside $B(u,r)$. Given an output radius $\varepsilon>0$ at the sum or product, set $\delta=\min(1,\varepsilon/(2+|x|+|y|))$ and use the open rectangle $B(x,\delta)\times B(y,\delta)$. There the sum changes by less than $2\delta\le\varepsilon$, and the product changes by at most $(|x|+|y|+1)\delta<\varepsilon$. Negation preserves distances, so it sends $B(x,\varepsilon)$ into $B(-x,\varepsilon)$. For reciprocal at $z\ne0$ and output radius $\varepsilon>0$, use the input ball $B(z,\eta)$ with $\eta=\min(|z|/2,\varepsilon|z|^2/2)$. For $w$ in this ball, $|w|>|z|/2$ by the triangle inequality, so the ball lies in the nonzero-scalar subspace and $|w^{-1}-z^{-1}|=|w-z|/(|w||z|)<\varepsilon$. Every open neighborhood of an output contains a ball about it, or the trace of a ball for reciprocal. These input neighborhoods prove continuity by [F5]. Entry projections on the matrix input spaces are continuous by the product and subspace topologies. By composition, the entry polynomials defining matrix products and the determinant $ad-bc$ are continuous. For $g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ invertible over the local field, $ad-bc\ne0$. Multiplying $g$ on either side by $\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\-c&a\end{pmatrix}$ gives $(ad-bc)I$, since the diagonal entries are $ad-bc$ and the off-diagonal entries cancel. Dividing by $ad-bc$ gives the inverse. The determinant is continuous into the nonzero scalars by the subspace topology. Composing it with scalar inversion and multiplying by the adjugate entries gives continuous inverse entries. Finite rectangles test continuity of the entry tuples, and open traces test continuity into the invertible-matrix subspace. The local field matrix groups are therefore topological groups. [F2, F3, F4, F5, F14]

2.1 Each $K_\ell$ contains the ambient identity matrix. Its products and inverses are still invertible over $\mathbb Z_\ell$, so it is a subgroup of the local field matrix group. The determinant criterion over $\mathbb Z_\ell$ cuts out $K_\ell$ by requiring all entries in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and the determinant in $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$. Both scalar sets are open in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Their preimages under the entry and determinant maps are open by step 1.2 and [F15], and the intersection of these five preimages is $K_\ell$. [F1, F3, F4, F8, F9, F15, step 1.2]

3.1 The restricted boxes cover $L$ by taking the full factors at the exceptional places of a tuple and the integral factors at other places. The full factors are open. Binary intersections are formed by intersecting the local open sets and still equal the integral factors off the union of the two finite exceptional sets. The union is finite by the binary union fact. The restricted boxes therefore form a basis. For a box about a product, take the real place and the finitely many primes where the target box differs from $K_\ell$ or either input lies outside $K_\ell$. The union of these three sets of primes is finite by two binary unions, and adjoining the real place gives a finite set of places. Take a bijective listing of this set by [F10]. At each listed place, the set of open rectangles containing the local input pair and having products in the target factor is nonempty by local continuity and the product basis. Apply [F10] to the list of these nonempty sets and use the selected rectangles as the finitely many local factors of the two input boxes. Elsewhere use $K_\ell$ for both inputs, since it is a subgroup. This gives an open rectangle in the product topology on $L\times L$ mapped into the target box. For inversion, the primes where the target box differs from $K_\ell$ or the input lies outside $K_\ell$ form a finite set by one binary union. Adjoin the real place and take a bijective listing by [F10]. At each listed place, local continuity gives a nonempty set of open neighborhoods of the local input whose inverses lie in the target factor. Apply [F10] to their list to select the finitely many local factors of an input box. At every other prime use $K_\ell$, which is preserved by inversion. Multiplication and inversion are continuous, so $L$ is a topological group. [F5, F8, F10, F12, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1]

4.1 At a tuple in the preimage of an open local set, restrict the chosen coordinate to that set, allow the full factors at the other exceptional primes and at the real place when it is not the chosen place, and use $K_\ell$ at the remaining primes. This gives a basic box contained in the preimage, since at most the chosen place is added to the tuple's finite set of exceptional places. The preimage is a union of such boxes and hence open, so each place projection is continuous. The scalar conditions at a place are the vanishing of both off-diagonal entries and of the difference of the diagonal entries. The entry functions are continuous by composing the place projection with the local entry projections from step 1.2. The pair of diagonal entry functions is continuous by the rectangle basis. Composing with addition and negation gives a continuous difference of the diagonal entries. For $x\ne0$ in a local field, the ball $B(x,|x|/2)$ lies in the complement of zero because $d(x,0)=|x|>|x|/2>0$. The metric-topology criterion therefore makes the complement open, so zero is closed. At each point where one of the scalar condition functions is nonzero, continuity gives an open neighborhood where that function stays nonzero. The union of these neighborhoods is the complement of the simultaneous vanishing locus, so the locus is closed in $L$. A matrix in this locus has the form $aI$, and if $B$ is its adelic inverse, the first diagonal entry of $(aI)B=I$ gives $ab_{11}=1$, so $a$ is a unit by commutativity. Every scalar unit gives such a matrix, so the locus is $Z$. The map $a\mapsto aI$ sends $1$ to $I$, and $(aI)(bI)=(ab)I$ and $(aI)^{-1}=a^{-1}I$ for units $a,b$. Its image contains the ambient identity and is closed under products and inverses, so $Z$ is a subgroup. The entry formulas give $g(aI)=(aI)g$, hence $g(aI)g^{-1}=aI$ for every $g\in L$ and every $aI\in Z$. Thus conjugation by each $g\in L$ maps $Z$ onto itself, and $Z$ is central and normal. [F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F8, F9, F12, F14, F15, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 3.1]

5.1 Set $P=L/Z$ and let $q:L\to P$ be the quotient projection $q(g)=gZ$. For $g,h\in L$, if $q(h)=q(g)$ then $h\in hZ=gZ$ since $1\in Z$. Conversely, if $h=gz$ for $z\in Z$, then $hZ=gZ$ since $zZ=Z$ by closure under products and multiplication by $z^{-1}$. Thus the fiber of $q$ over $q(g)$ is $gZ$. For open $U\subseteq L$, the set $q^{-1}(q(U))$ is the union of the right translates $Uz$ over $z\in Z$. By step 3.1, $L$ is a topological group. Right translation by $z\in Z$ is a homeomorphism by [F13]. Its inverse is right translation by $z^{-1}$, so $Uz$ is the preimage of $U$ under that continuous inverse and is open. The quotient topology makes $q(U)$ open and ensures that $q$ is continuous. Every coset has a representative, so $q$ is an open continuous surjection. The map $q\times q$ is surjective. The preimage of an open rectangle is a product of open preimages under $q$, and the image of an open rectangle is a product of open images under $q$. By the rectangle bases and preservation of unions by images and preimages, $q\times q$ sends open sets to open sets and has open preimages of open sets. Thus $q\times q$ is open and continuous. It is a quotient map since any subset with open preimage is the image of that open preimage. Inversion on $P$ pulls back along $q$ to $g\mapsto q(g^{-1})$, and coset multiplication on $P$ pulls back along $q\times q$ to $(g,h)\mapsto q(gh)$. Each pullback is continuous by composition of a continuous group operation on $L$ from step 3.1 with the continuous map $q$. The characteristic property of the respective quotient maps proves continuity of both operations on $P$. [F5, F6, F8, F9, F13, F14, F15, step 3.1, step 4.1]

6.1 Let $\pi$ be a strongly continuous unitary representation of $L$ on a complex Hilbert space $H$ acting trivially on $Z$. If $q(h)=q(g)$, then $h=gz$ for some $z\in Z$ by step 5.1. Since $\pi(h)=\pi(g)\pi(z)=\pi(g)$, the rule $\bar\pi(q(g))=\pi(g)$ is well defined with values in $U(H)$. For $g,h\in L$, the coset product gives $\bar\pi(q(g)q(h))=\bar\pi(q(gh))=\pi(gh)=\pi(g)\pi(h)=\bar\pi(q(g))\bar\pi(q(h))$. Surjectivity of $q$ makes this the homomorphism law on $P$. For each $v\in H$, the descended orbit map $p\mapsto\bar\pi(p)v$ pulls back along $q$ to the norm-continuous map $g\mapsto\pi(g)v$. The quotient-map property gives norm continuity of the descended orbit map, so the factored unitary representation is strongly continuous. [F6, F7, step 5.1] ∎


## Rational and finite adelic projective quaternion units

[Open item](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units) · level 3


### Definition

Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton algebra and fix an order $O$ in $B$. In projective three-space over $\mathbb Q$, let $G=PB^\times$ be the open set $D_+(N)$ defined by the homogeneous reduced norm $N(x)=\sum_{r=0}^3x_r^2$. In a standard chart, a chosen coordinate of a representative is $1$, the other three coordinates are its ratios to that coordinate, and the condition on the chart is that the norm of the normalized vector be invertible. The open is given the algebraic group structure over $\mathbb Q$ with the identity, multiplication and inverse described below. For any field extension $F/\mathbb Q$, the elements of $G(F)$ are classes $[x_0:x_1:x_2:x_3]$ with $N(x)\ne0$, where tuples are identified under common scaling by $F^\times$. Multiplication is induced by the Hamilton product, so $G(F)=B_F^\times/F^\times$ with scalars acting through $a\mapsto a1$. In particular $G(\mathbb Q)=B^\times/\mathbb Q^\times$.

Embed the finite scalar ideles in the finite quaternion ideles by $(a_\ell)\mapsto(a_\ell1)$. The finite adelic projective group is
$$G(\mathbb A_f)=\widehat B^\times/\mathbb A_f^\times.$$
Each quotient identifies the cosets of its scalar subgroup and multiplies them by multiplying representatives. The topology on $G(\mathbb A_f)$ is the quotient topology: a subset is open exactly when its preimage in $\widehat B^\times$ is open. Use the analogous quotient topology on $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ from $B_\ell^\times$, and write $K_\ell$ for the image of $O_\ell^\times$ in $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The Hamilton algebra $B$, a full order $O$ with a fixed basis and the finite adele ring $\mathbb A_f$.

[F1] The local Hamilton construction gives $B_R^\times=\{x:N(x)\in R^\times\}$ for a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $R$, with multiplicative norm and inverse $\bar x/N(x)$. The local absolute value is multiplicative and satisfies the ultrametric inequality. The local integer ring is the unit ball, and its units have absolute value $1$. Integral units are detected by the determinant of left multiplication in the order basis, and $O_\ell^\times\cap(\mathbb Q_\ell^\times1)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times1$. The operations of multiplication and inversion on $B_\ell^\times$ are continuous, and $O_\ell^\times$ is an open subgroup of $B_\ell^\times$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). A scalar tuple is a finite idele exactly when all coordinates are nonzero and all but finitely many are integral units ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)). Units of $B_{\mathbb A_f}$ are the finite quaternion ideles, with open compact integral unit product $\widehat O^\times$. Multiplication and inversion on $\widehat B^\times$ are continuous for the restricted product topology, whose basic boxes have open factors in $B_\ell^\times$ and use $O_\ell^\times$ outside a finite set ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles)).

[F2] A scheme over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$ has a structure morphism to that base, and a morphism over the base commutes with the structure morphisms ([math:def-scheme-over-base](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-scheme-over-base)). The standard affine charts of projective space use coordinate ratios and overlap by renormalizing where the new denominator is a unit ([math:def-relative-projective-space-standard-charts](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-relative-projective-space-standard-charts)). A group scheme of finite type over $\mathbb Q$ has multiplication, identity and inverse given by morphisms over $\mathbb Q$ satisfying the group laws. Its $R$-points are $\mathbb Q$-morphisms $\operatorname{Spec}R\to G$ ([math:def-group-scheme-over-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-scheme-over-a-field)). Here $\operatorname{Spec}R$ is the affine scheme of the ring $R$. An affine scheme defined by polynomial equations has $R$-points given by their solutions over $R$, through the $\mathbb Q$-algebra maps from its coordinate ring to $R$. The affine scheme correspondence is contravariant, and preservation of the $\mathbb Q$-algebra structures makes the corresponding morphisms commute with the maps to $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$ ([math:thm-affine-scheme-ring-anti-equivalence](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-affine-scheme-ring-anti-equivalence)). Restricting an affine chart to the open where a function is invertible corresponds to adjoining the inverse of that function ([math:def-affine-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-affine-scheme), [math:lem-spectrum-localization-open-immersion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-spectrum-localization-open-immersion)). An affine open subscheme has the structure sheaf restricted to the open set ([math:def-affine-open-subscheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-affine-open-subscheme)).

[F3] A subgroup contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)). A normal subgroup is invariant under conjugation ([math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup)). The left cosets of a normal subgroup form a group under multiplication of representatives ([math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group), [math:thm-quotient-group-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-group-laws)). For a subgroup $H$ of a group, $gH=g' H$ holds exactly when $(g')^{-1}g\in H$ ([math:lem-coset-membership-and-equality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-membership-and-equality)). The quotient topology of a surjection is given by preimages of open sets ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)). The basic opens in a product topology restrict finitely many coordinates ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)), and a topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)). Left and right translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion)).

[F4] A product of two schemes over $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$ is covered by the products of the affine opens in covers of the two factors. The coordinate ring of such a product is the tensor product over $\mathbb Q$ ([math:thm-fibre-products-of-schemes-exist](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fibre-products-of-schemes-exist)). The points of an affine spectrum are proper prime ideals, and the principal open $D(f)$ consists of those that omit $f$ ([math:def-prime-spectrum-and-vanishing-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime-spectrum-and-vanishing-sets), [math:def-prime-and-maximal-ideals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime-and-maximal-ideals), [math:def-principal-distinguished-subset-of-spectrum](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-principal-distinguished-subset-of-spectrum)). Compatible scheme morphisms on a source open cover glue uniquely, and equality of morphisms can be checked on a source open cover ([math:lem-morphism-schemes-local-on-source-target](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-morphism-schemes-local-on-source-target)).

[F5] A scheme over $\mathbb Q$ is locally of finite type if it has affine charts whose coordinate rings are finitely generated $\mathbb Q$-algebras. Finite type also requires the structure morphism to be quasi-compact, meaning that its preimages of quasi-compact opens have the finite-subcover property ([math:def-finite-type-and-module-finite-algebras](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-type-and-module-finite-algebras), [math:def-locally-finite-type-and-finite-type-morphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-finite-type-and-finite-type-morphism), [math:def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-morphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-morphism), [math:def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-scheme)). Every open neighborhood of a point of an affine spectrum contains a principal open neighborhood of that point ([math:lem-distinguished-open-refinement-at-a-point](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-distinguished-open-refinement-at-a-point)). Maximal ideals are prime ([math:cor-maximal-ideals-are-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-maximal-ideals-are-prime)), and an ideal generated by a set in a commutative ring consists of finite linear combinations of its elements ([math:thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring)).

[F6] A map is continuous exactly when the preimage of every open set is open ([math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top)). Compactness means that each open cover has a finite subcover, with the subspace topology used for a subset ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)).

[F7] The rationals admit a bijection with $\mathbb N$ ([math:thm-rationals-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-rationals-countable)). An initial state and a self-map of a set determine a sequence by natural-number recursion ([math:thm-recursion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-recursion)). In a commutative ring, an ideal is an additive subgroup closed under multiplication by arbitrary ring elements ([math:def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-right-and-two-sided-ideal)).

[F8] A monomial is a finitely supported family of nonnegative exponents indexed by the variables ([math:def-monomials-on-an-index-set](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-monomials-on-an-index-set)). A polynomial has only finitely many nonzero monomial coefficients ([math:def-polynomial-ring-on-a-family-of-indeterminates](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-polynomial-ring-on-a-family-of-indeterminates)). Over a commutative base ring, assigning values to the variables of a polynomial ring in a commutative target algebra gives a unique algebra homomorphism ([math:thm-universal-property-of-a-polynomial-ring-on-a-family](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-universal-property-of-a-polynomial-ring-on-a-family)). The quotient by an ideal is the ring of additive cosets with multiplication induced by representatives ([math:def-quotient-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-ring)). If that evaluation kills the generators of an ideal, it kills the whole ideal and factors uniquely through the quotient ([math:thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring), [math:thm-quotient-ring-universal-property](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-ring-universal-property)). A homomorphism of commutative unital rings that sends all elements of the set being inverted to units extends uniquely across localization ([math:thm-universal-property-of-localisation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-universal-property-of-localisation)).

[F9] The determinant of a positive-size square matrix over a commutative ring is the finite sum of signed products of entries indexed by permutations ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix)).

[F10] A finite set admits a bijection from $\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$ for some natural number $n$ ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)). For a list of nonempty sets indexed by a natural number, one may select a member of each set in the list ([math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)).

[F11] A field extension $F/\mathbb Q$ is a field $F$ with a specified embedding $\mathbb Q\to F$ ([math:def-field-extension-generated-subfields-and-simple-extension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field-extension-generated-subfields-and-simple-extension)). A field has $0\ne1$ and every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse ([math:def-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field)).

[F12] A morphism of ringed spaces gives section homomorphisms on target opens compatible with restriction ([math:def-morphism-ringed-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-morphism-ringed-spaces)). For an open subspace $U$ of $Y$, the restricted sheaf on an open $W\subseteq U$ has the same sections as the ambient sheaf on $W$ ([math:def-restriction-sheaf-open-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-restriction-sheaf-open-subspace)). A scheme morphism is a morphism of the underlying locally ringed spaces, so its homomorphisms on stalks are local ([math:def-morphism-of-schemes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-morphism-of-schemes)). A stalk at a point consists of germs of sections on open neighborhoods of the point. Two sections define the same germ exactly when they agree on a smaller open neighborhood ([math:def-stalk-of-presheaf](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-stalk-of-presheaf)).

[F13] An arbitrary union of open subsets of a topological space is open ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)).

### Justification

**Given:** The objects and conventions in the definition above.

**Goal:** Verify the coordinate law, the interpretation as adelic points, and the quotient descriptions and topology.

1.1 Since $N(a x)=a^2N(x)$ for a scalar $a$, invertibility of the norm on normalized projective charts is independent of the chosen chart. Each norm-open chart is affine by localization. Products of these charts cover $G\times_{\mathbb Q}G$, with coordinate rings given by the corresponding tensor products over $\mathbb Q$. In each such ring form the coordinates of the Hamilton product of the two chart representatives. Their norm is a unit by multiplicativity. No proper prime ideal contains all four product coordinates, since their squares sum to that unit. Thus the principal opens where one product coordinate is invertible cover this product chart. Dividing by that coordinate gives regular output chart coordinates, with unit norm after normalization. Substitution defines a map from the target chart's polynomial ring into the localized source ring. Since the substituted norm is a unit, this map extends uniquely to the norm-localized target chart ring. Scalar changes of input chart representatives cancel in these ratios, and different choices of the invertible output coordinate are related by the target chart transitions. The compatible local maps glue to multiplication. Each chart ring map preserves the $\mathbb Q$-algebra structure, so the glued map commutes with the structure morphisms to $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$. Conjugation acts by sign changes in the homogeneous coordinates and preserves the norm, so it gives a morphism in these same charts and is the projective inverse since $x\bar x=N(x)1$. The identity is $[1:0:0:0]$, obtained by evaluating the three variable coordinates of the first chart at zero, where the norm is $1$. Both this evaluation and the sign changes for conjugation preserve the $\mathbb Q$-algebra structures, giving morphisms over $\mathbb Q$. The group identities hold on the corresponding chart covers by associativity and the inverse formula in the chart rings. Equality on the covers gives the group identities as morphisms. [F1, F2, F4, F8]

1.2 Over each field $F/\mathbb Q$, the scalar map sends $a\in F^\times$ to $a1$, with inverse element $a^{-1}1$. It is injective by the Hamilton coordinates and preserves products. The Hamilton scalar action satisfies $(a1)x=ax=x(a1)$. For the finite ideles the same map acts coordinatewise. At every prime where $a_\ell\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$, both $a_\ell1$ and $a_\ell^{-1}1$ lie in $O_\ell$ since it contains $1$ and is a $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module. These primes include all but finitely many. Thus the scalar idele map takes values in $\widehat B^\times$ and is injective and multiplicative by the coordinate formulas. The image in each case contains the identity, is closed under products and inverses, and lies in the center. Conjugation by any quaternion unit thus fixes each element of the scalar subgroup, so the scalar subgroup is normal and the coset product gives a quotient group. [F1, F3, F11]

2.1 Each of the four charts has a coordinate ring generated over $\mathbb Q$ by its three coordinate functions and the inverse of the norm polynomial, giving local finite type. On the four-variable index set, an exponent family from [F8] is specified by a quadruple of nonnegative integers, and every such quadruple has finite support. Fix a bijective listing of $\mathbb Q$ as in [F7]. At stage $n\ge0$, list the monomials in four variables whose four exponents are at most $n$, comparing exponent tuples by the smaller exponent at the first position where they differ. List all coefficient assignments for these monomials using $0$ and the first $n+1$ rationals. Compare assignments at the first monomial where they differ, using the indices of their coefficients in the fixed rational listing. Evaluate each resulting polynomial at the four chart generators. Each stage gives a finite nonempty list. The finitely many monomials with nonzero coefficients have a common bound on their exponents and coefficient indices. Thus every polynomial appears at some stage, and concatenating the lists gives a sequence $(a_n)_{n\ge0}$ containing every element of the chart ring $A$. To show its spectrum is quasi-compact, refine an open cover by all the principal opens $D(f)$ contained in its members. Let $I$ be the ideal generated by the corresponding $f$. If $I$ is proper, start at $(0,I)$ in the set of pairs consisting of a natural number and a proper ideal of $A$. From $(n,J)$ pass to $(n+1,J')$, where $J'$ is the ideal generated by $J$ and $a_n$ if that ideal is proper, and is $J$ otherwise. This self-map gives an increasing sequence of proper ideals by [F7]. The union is an ideal because any two of its elements belong to one stage and the ideal closure laws hold there. It is proper because membership of $1$ would already occur at a finite stage. It is maximal because each element outside it was rejected at a stage where adjoining that element produced $1$. This maximal ideal is prime and contains every $f$ of the refinement, contradicting that the refinement covers. Hence $1$ is a finite linear combination of the $f$, and their principal opens already cover because no prime can contain all those finitely many $f$. Index these principal opens by their terms in the finite linear combination. Choosing one original cover member containing each principal open by [F10] gives the desired finite subcover. The four charts then give quasi-compactness of $G$ by choosing a finite subcover for each chart, indexed by $0,1,2,3$, and joining them. In a field, any ideal containing a nonzero $a$ contains $a^{-1}a=1$ by [F7] and [F11], so it is the whole field. The zero ideal is proper since $0\ne1$. If $ab=0$ with $a\ne0$, multiplying by $a^{-1}$ gives $b=0$, so the zero ideal is prime by [F4]. Thus a field spectrum has exactly one point. The only nonempty open of $\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb Q$ is therefore the whole base. Therefore the structure morphism is quasi-compact and $G$ is of finite type. Here the structure morphism is the restriction of the one on projective three-space, given on each norm-open chart by the inclusion of the rational constants into its coordinate ring. With step 1.1 this makes $G$ a group scheme of finite type over $\mathbb Q$. An $F$-point is a morphism $\operatorname{Spec}F\to G$ over $\mathbb Q$, where the structure map of $\operatorname{Spec}F$ is induced by the specified embedding $\mathbb Q\to F$. The source has one point by the field-spectrum calculation, so its image lies in a chart. The point map is continuous into that chart because each open subset of the open chart is open in $G$. Write $f:\operatorname{Spec}F\to G$ for the original point and $U$ for the norm-open chart. For each open $W\subseteq U$, the chart's sections on $W$ are the ambient sections on $W$ by [F12]. Use the original section map on $W$ because the preimage of $W$ under the factored point map is $f^{-1}(W)$. These section maps remain compatible with restriction and give the sheaf map into the direct image for the factorization. Every germ at the image point in $G$ has a representative in the chart, obtained by intersecting its open neighborhood with the chart and restricting the section. Two such representatives agree on a smaller open neighborhood in $G$ exactly when they agree on one in the chart, since one can intersect any witnessing neighborhood with the chart. Thus restriction identifies the two stalk rings, and under this identification the stalk homomorphism is the original local one. The factorization is therefore a scheme morphism. It is over the base because composing with the chart inclusion recovers the original $F$-point. On that chart it is specified by a $\mathbb Q$-algebra map to $F$, hence by a tuple with the chosen coordinate $1$ and with nonzero norm. Conversely such a tuple gives a point by evaluation. Two chart representatives specify the same point exactly when they agree under the chart transitions, which renormalize by a nonzero scalar. Thus these points are precisely the classes of quaternion units modulo $F^\times$ by the norm test, and step 1.1 gives the coset multiplication. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8, F10, F11, F12, step 1.1, step 1.2]

2.2 To see explicitly what an $\mathbb A_f$-point means, use the affine model consisting of symmetric $4\times4$ matrices $T=(t_{rs})$ with trace $1$ and all $2\times2$ minors zero. Take the spectrum of the polynomial ring over $\mathbb Q$ on the entries $t_{rs}$ with $0\le r\le s\le 3$, modulo the ideal generated by $\sum_i t_{ii}-1$ and the minors using the quotient construction in [F8], writing $t_{sr}=t_{rs}$. For rows $a,b$ with $a\ne b$ and columns $c,d$ with $c\ne d$, the corresponding minor is $t_{ac}t_{bd}-t_{ad}t_{bc}$ by [F9], with all four indices in $\{0,1,2,3\}$. These are the minor generators of the equation ideal. On each norm-open chart, the substitutions $t_{rs}\mapsto x_rx_s/N(x)$ are regular and unchanged under scaling. The trace is $1$ and the two products in each minor coincide, so evaluation descends through the equation ideal and the local morphisms to the matrix scheme glue. Likewise, the matrix model's points over a commutative unital $\mathbb Q$-algebra $R$ are exactly matrices over $R$ solving the equations, since their evaluations factor through the same quotient ring. The principal opens where $t_{ii}$ is invertible cover the matrix scheme because $\sum_i t_{ii}=1$ and no proper prime ideal contains all the diagonal entries. On such an open define $v_r=t_{ri}/t_{ii}$, so $v_i=1$. For $r\ne i$ and $s\ne i$ the minor from rows $r,i$ and columns $s,i$ gives $t_{rs}t_{ii}=t_{ri}t_{is}$. If $r=i$ or $s=i$, the two products coincide directly. Symmetry gives $t_{rs}=t_{ii}v_rv_s$, and the trace equation gives $N(v)=1/t_{ii}$. Thus substituting the $v_r$ extends to the norm-localized chart ring because $N(v)$ is a unit. Conversely, on the normalized projective chart $x_i=1$ the forward formula gives $t_{ii}=1/N(x)$ and $t_{ri}/t_{ii}=x_r$. These formulas give inverse chart morphisms. On overlaps the chosen columns are proportional by a unit and the resulting projective classes agree by the coordinate transition, since $t_{jj}=t_{ii}v_j^2$ forces $v_j$ to be invertible there. The inverse chart morphisms therefore glue, and the inverse identities on charts give an isomorphism. [F1, F2, F4, F8, F9, step 1.1]

3.1 Each $x\in B_{\mathbb A_f}^\times$ defines a matrix point by the entries $x_rx_s/N(x)$, which satisfy the equations by step 2.2. Given a matrix point over $\mathbb A_f$, at each prime $\ell$ choose the first $i=i(\ell)$ in the order $0,1,2,3$ for which $|t_{ii,\ell}|_\ell$ is largest. It is at least $1$ by the trace equation and the ultrametric inequality. At all but finitely many primes every matrix entry is integral. At those primes $|t_{ii,\ell}|_\ell=1$, so the chosen entry is a unit in $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Put $v_{r,\ell}=t_{ri,\ell}/t_{ii,\ell}$ at each prime. These are adelic coefficients, and the resulting $v$ has $N(v)_\ell=1/t_{ii,\ell}$ at every prime by step 2.2. Thus $N(v)\in\mathbb A_f^\times$ and $v\in B_{\mathbb A_f}^\times$. At every prime the minor identities give $t_{rs,\ell}=v_{r,\ell}v_{s,\ell}/N(v)_\ell$. Equality of all local coordinates is equality in $\mathbb A_f$, so $v$ represents the given matrix point. If two quaternion ideles give the same point, their coordinates give the same projective class at each prime by the affine model. The coset criterion in [F3] shows that they differ by a local scalar at each prime. At all but finitely many primes both are in $O_\ell^\times$, so the local scalars there lie in $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$. Thus the scalars form a finite idele and are central because each coordinate is scalar. Conversely a scalar finite idele preserves the matrix point. Evaluation at each prime through $\mathbb A_f\to\mathbb Q_\ell$ respects the group law by precomposition of scheme points. The map from quaternion units respects multiplication at each prime by the field description, and the matrix entries are determined by their prime coordinates. Send the coset $x\mathbb A_f^\times$ to the matrix point represented by $x$. The scalar invariance makes the map well-defined, and the calculation of fibers proves injectivity. The lift $v$ proves surjectivity. The product calculation and the coset law of step 1.2 make this a group isomorphism. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2]

4.1 The diagonal map $B^\times\to\widehat B^\times$ is defined since the rational coefficients of an element and its inverse in the $O$ basis have only finitely many denominator primes. This follows by applying the scalar diagonal integrality argument to each of the finitely many coefficients. Send $b\mathbb Q^\times$ to $(b)_\ell\mathbb A_f^\times$. Changing $b$ by a rational scalar changes its diagonal tuple by a scalar idele, so this map is well-defined. It preserves products by coordinatewise multiplication and the coset law. If the images of $b$ and $c$ coincide, the coset criterion in [F3] makes the diagonal tuple of $c^{-1}b$ a scalar idele, so $c^{-1}b$ is scalar at every prime. Its imaginary coordinates vanish already over $\mathbb Q$ by injectivity of $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb Q_\ell$ at any one prime. Thus the rational classes coincide, proving injectivity. The coordinate images of $\widehat B^\times$ in $\prod_\ell G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ belong to $K_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes. Conversely, given such a family of classes, at each prime normalize using the first coefficient of largest absolute value in the $O$ basis. This rule is independent of the input representative. If a class admits an integral unit representative, that representative has some unit coefficient. Indeed, the left multiplication matrix has entries that are linear combinations of the coefficients of the representative with integer weights, since the products of order basis vectors belong to the order. If all coefficients have absolute value less than $1$, the ultrametric inequality and multiplicativity give the same bound for each matrix entry. Each signed product in the determinant expansion has absolute value less than $1$, so the finite sum does as well. The determinant would then fail to be a unit, contrary to the integral unit criterion. The normalizing scalar is therefore a unit in that case. The normalized representatives form a quaternion idele, proving surjectivity to the group of families that lie in $K_\ell$ at almost all primes. Send an adelic scalar coset to the family of its local cosets. The family is independent of its representative because a scalar idele acts by a scalar at each prime, and the map preserves products by the local and adelic coset laws. If two families coincide, the coset criterion in [F3] makes the two idele representatives differ by a local scalar at every prime. Outside their finite exceptional sets these scalars are integral units by the scalar intersection in [F1], so the two ideles differ by a scalar idele and represent the same adelic coset. [F1, F3, F9, step 1.2, step 3.1]

5.1 The local and finite adelic quotient maps are continuous by their quotient topologies. The local and adelic unit groups are topological groups by continuity of multiplication and inversion in [F1] and the definition in [F3]. By the coset criterion in [F3], the preimage of the image of an open set is the union of its translates by scalars. Each translate is open by the translation homeomorphism in [F3]. This union is therefore open, so the quotient maps are open. For either quotient map $q$, the map $q\times q$ is surjective. The image of each basic open rectangle is open since each factor map is open. Every open set in the source product is a union of basic open rectangles, so its image is a union of open rectangles and is open. For an open set $W$ in the quotient, the preimage under $q\times q$ of the multiplication preimage of $W$ is the preimage of $q^{-1}(W)$ under multiplication upstairs, which is open by continuity there. Its image under $q\times q$ is the multiplication preimage of $W$ downstairs. Using $q$ instead of $q\times q$ gives the same open-preimage calculation for inversion. Both operations are therefore continuous. Under the coordinate description in step 4.1, the image of a basic box is the box of its local images. To lift a point of that image box, index the finite set of varied primes by a bijection from $\{0,\ldots,n-1\}$ as in [F10]. At each index the lifts in the prescribed factor form a nonempty set by the definition of its local image. Apply [F10] to this list of sets and use the chosen lift at the corresponding prime. Use the normalized representatives of step 4.1 at every other prime. Conversely, any basic box with open factors and with $K_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes is the image of a box obtained by taking their local preimages at the finitely many varied factors and using $O_\ell^\times$ elsewhere. This proves equality of the quotient and restricted product topologies. Each $K_\ell$ is open by the local quotient map. The image of $\widehat O^\times$ is the open subgroup $\prod_\ell K_\ell$ with its product topology. Restricting a basic box to this subgroup restricts only finitely many factors in their subspace topologies. Conversely each relatively open restriction is open in $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ since $K_\ell$ is open there, so it can be used directly as the corresponding factor of a basic box. An open cover of that image pulls back to an open cover of $\widehat O^\times$ by continuity of the quotient map on the subspaces. A finite subcover of the pullback gives a finite subcover of the image. Each $K_\ell$ is compact by the same open-cover argument applied to the coordinate projection from $\prod_\ell K_\ell$, continuous in the product topology and surjective by using identity elements in the other factors. [F1, F3, F6, F10, F13, step 3.1, step 4.1] ∎


## Normalized spherical character in degree two

[Open item](item-thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character) · level 3


### Statement

Assume countable choice, meaning that a sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. Let $\ell$ be prime, $F=\mathbb Q_\ell$ the $\ell$-adic field, $O=\mathbb Z_\ell$ its ring of integers, $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(F)$ and $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(O)$. Give $G$ and $K$ the subspace topologies from $F^4$ with its product of $\ell$-adic metric topologies. Let $\pi$ be the restriction of a strongly continuous unitary representation of $G$ to its space $V$ of smooth vectors, those fixed by some open subgroup. A strongly continuous unitary representation acts by complex-linear isometries on a complex Hilbert space with continuous orbit maps. Suppose $V$ is irreducible, meaning it is nonzero and has no nonzero proper invariant linear subspace, and admissible, meaning its fixed space under every compact open subgroup is finite dimensional. Suppose $V^K\ne0$.

Then $V^K$ is one dimensional. There is a unique unordered pair of nonzero complex numbers $u,v$, counted with multiplicity, such that for every spherical Hecke function $f$, its operator acts on $V^K$ by the scalar $S(f)(u,v)$. The spherical Hecke algebra consists of compactly supported complex functions invariant on both sides by $K$. Here $S$ is the normalized Satake transform by triangular cosets. Haar measure is normalized by $\operatorname{vol}(K)=1$, so each contributing coset $xK=\{xk:k\in K\}$ has weight one.

For either ordering, the same Hecke character, meaning the same unital complex-algebra map to $\mathbb C$, acts on the normalized upper-triangular spherical vector $\Phi_{u,v}$. This is the right $K$-invariant vector of value one at the identity in the function model where left multiplication by a triangular matrix of diagonal valuations $(r,s)$ multiplies the value by $\ell^{(s-r)/2}u^rv^s$. Its value at an invertible upper-triangular matrix with diagonal $(\ell^r,\ell^s)$ is $\ell^{(s-r)/2}u^rv^s$.

If the central character is trivial, meaning all scalar matrices act as the identity, then $uv=1$. The representation and its fixed-space action descend to $G/(F^\times I)=\mathrm{PGL}_2(F)$ with the quotient topology and compact subgroup the image of $K$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The field has normalized absolute value $|a|_\ell=\ell^{-\nu(a)}$ on nonzero elements, where the valuation is additive on products. Its ring of integers is compact open with residue field of size $\ell$. Both $K$ and its projective image are compact open in their respective Hausdorff topological groups, and triangular representatives of cosets $xK$ have well-defined diagonal valuations ([lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets), [def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group)). The completion theorem makes $F$ a complete nonarchimedean valued field and the extension agrees with the rational absolute value on $\mathbb Q$ ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field)). On the rationals the normalization gives $|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$ ([math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals)).

[F2] A unitary representation acts by complex-linear norm-preserving bijections with continuous orbit maps. The smooth vectors form a stable subspace: sums are fixed by intersections of their fixing subgroups, and translating a vector conjugates its fixing subgroup ([math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)).

[F3] The spherical Hecke algebra acts on any $K$-fixed space by the operators given by finite coset summation and its convolution product uses the same finite-coset convention with each $xK$ of weight one ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F4] The transform weights each coset with diagonal valuations $(r,s)$ by $\ell^{(s-r)/2}X^rY^s$. In the function model, the line of $K$-fixed vectors has normalized generator $\Phi_{u,v}$ with value $\ell^{(s-r)/2}u^rv^s$ on this coset ([def-author-d45da46285be-satake-transform](item-def-author-d45da46285be-satake-transform), [def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-model](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-model)).

[F5] Every symmetric polynomial in two variables over $\mathbb C$ is a polynomial in $X+Y$ and $XY$. A nonconstant complex polynomial has a root ([math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-symmetric-polynomials](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-symmetric-polynomials), [math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-algebra-minimum-modulus-proof](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-algebra-minimum-modulus-proof)).

[F6] Use the normalized Satake isomorphism and its defining integral in Gross, §3, and the dimension bound for irreducible smooth representations in §6, with hypotheses recorded below. Use also Gross’s integral spherical Hecke ring from §2 and the description of the Weyl group in §1. Evaluation at the chosen uniformizer identifies the cocharacter lattice with the diagonal torus quotient by its integral points in §2.

[F7] A continuous image of a compact space is compact ([math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps)). In a quotient topology a subset is open exactly when its preimage is open ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)). Left translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion)).

[F8] Use the complex plane with its usual norm $|z|$, for which it is complete ([math:thm-complex-plane-is-complete](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-plane-is-complete)). The scalar case of the Banach-valued simple integral is a sum over the disjoint nonzero level sets. When each such set has finite measure, its contribution is the level value times its measure ([math:def-banach-valued-simple-function-and-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-banach-valued-simple-function-and-integral)). A measure is finitely additive on disjoint measurable sets, by countable additivity with empty sets padding the finite family ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure)).

[F9] For a finite-type group scheme over $F$, the Lie algebra at the identity identifies with the kernel of reduction from its $F[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^2)$-points to its $F$-points ([math:def-lie-algebra-of-a-group-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-lie-algebra-of-a-group-scheme)). Over $F$, the group schemes $U_2$ and $D_2$ are the upper unitriangular and diagonal subgroups of the general linear group scheme. Their points over a commutative $F$-algebra are the matrices with those forms ([math:def-upper-unitriangular-group-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-upper-unitriangular-group-scheme)). A morphism of finite-type group schemes induces a linear map on Lie algebras, computed on the reduction kernels by applying the morphism to dual-number points. ([math:lem-lie-algebra-tangent-space-and-functoriality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-lie-algebra-tangent-space-and-functoriality))

[F10] A left Haar measure is a left-translation-invariant Borel measure. ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)) The Borel sigma-algebra is generated by the open sets, so it contains every open set. ([math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra))

[F11] The product of two-by-two matrices over a field or local ring has entries $(AB)_{ij}=\sum_{h=1}^2 A_{ih}B_{hj}$. ([def-degree-two-projective-linear-group](item-def-degree-two-projective-linear-group))

[F12] A scheme morphism is of finite type if it is locally of finite type and quasi-compact. On an affine chart over an affine base, a finitely generated coordinate algebra gives the local finite-type condition ([math:def-locally-finite-type-and-finite-type-morphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-finite-type-and-finite-type-morphism), [math:def-finite-type-and-module-finite-algebras](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-type-and-module-finite-algebras)). A morphism is quasi-compact when preimages of quasi-compact open subsets are quasi-compact ([math:def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-morphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quasi-compact-and-quasi-separated-morphism)).

[F13] The distinguished opens $D(f)$, consisting of primes omitting $f$, form a basis of an affine spectrum ([math:lem-distinguished-opens-cover-every-open](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-distinguished-opens-cover-every-open)). A one-variable polynomial ring over a field is Euclidean, and every Euclidean domain is a principal ideal domain ([math:cor-polynomial-ring-over-a-field-is-euclidean](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-polynomial-ring-over-a-field-is-euclidean), [math:thm-euclidean-domain-is-a-pid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-euclidean-domain-is-a-pid)).

[F14] A ring map induces a morphism of affine spectra in the opposite direction. Its maps on stalks are local, so it is a scheme morphism ([math:def-morphism-affine-schemes-from-ring-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-morphism-affine-schemes-from-ring-map), [math:lem-spectrum-map-stalk-homomorphisms-local](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-spectrum-map-stalk-homomorphisms-local)).

[F15] The determinant of a linear operator in positive dimension is the determinant of its matrix in an ordered basis ([math:def-determinant-of-a-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-linear-operator)). The determinant of a one-by-one matrix is its sole entry, since only the identity permutation contributes ([math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix)).

[F16] Matrix products over any commutative algebra use the row-by-column formula ([math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose)). Use the algebraic normalizer criterion and the permutation representatives for the split general linear group recorded below.

[F17] Extension of scalars along a map of commutative rings $R\to A$ sends an $R$-module $M$ to $A\otimes_R M$ and sends inverse maps to inverse maps ([math:def-restriction-and-extension-of-scalars](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-restriction-and-extension-of-scalars)). Tensor products commute with direct sums. Thus each basis summand $R$ becomes a basis summand $A$ under scalar extension ([math:thm-tensor-products-commute-with-arbitrary-direct-sums](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tensor-products-commute-with-arbitrary-direct-sums)).

[F18] A space is quasi-compact when every open cover has a finite subcover ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), where this property is called compactness). In a commutative ring, a maximal ideal is a proper ideal with no strictly larger proper ideal, and a prime ideal is a proper ideal containing a factor of every product it contains ([math:def-prime-and-maximal-ideals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-prime-and-maximal-ideals)). Every maximal ideal in a commutative ring is prime ([math:cor-maximal-ideals-are-prime](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-maximal-ideals-are-prime)). The ideal generated by a set consists of finite sums of ring multiples of elements of that set ([math:thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-generated-ideal-description-in-a-commutative-ring)).

[F19] The points of an affine spectrum are the prime ideals of its coordinate ring ([math:def-affine-scheme-spectrum](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-affine-scheme-spectrum)). For an affine scheme over $F$, its points over a commutative $F$-algebra are the $F$-algebra homomorphisms from its coordinate ring to that algebra, by the affine functor of points ([math:def-functor-of-points-affine-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-functor-of-points-affine-scheme)). A morphism of finite-type group schemes over $F$ is an $F$-scheme morphism commuting with multiplication, identity and inverse ([math:def-morphism-and-closed-subgroup-scheme](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-morphism-and-closed-subgroup-scheme)).

[F20] Use the determinant-invertible general linear group scheme over the valuation ring and the fiberwise criteria for reductivity, maximal tori, Borel subgroups and unipotent radicals recorded below. Use the field-level general linear group and triangular Borel structure from Milne.

[F21] A polynomial algebra in finitely many variables and each of its principal localizations give smooth affine schemes over the coefficient ring, by the standard smooth criterion recorded below.

### External input

Gross’s integral spherical Hecke ring $\mathcal H_{\mathbb Z}$ consists of the locally constant, compactly supported, integer-valued bi-$K$-invariant functions. The double-coset indicators form its integral basis. Its product uses Haar convolution with $K$ of volume one, and the indicator of $K$ is its unit. [On the Satake isomorphism, §2](https://people.math.harvard.edu/~gross/preprints/sat.pdf#page=3)

For the standard integral compact subgroup and upper-triangular choice, Gross's formula (3.4) gives the value at every diagonal matrix $t$ as $S_{\mathrm{Gross}}(f)(t)=\delta(t)^{1/2}\int_N f(tn)\,dn$. Here $N=\mathbf N(F)$ for the upper unitriangular group scheme $\mathbf N=U_2$. For $t=\operatorname{diag}(\ell^r,\ell^s)$, formula (3.3) gives the half modulus as the positive square root of the absolute determinant of conjugation on $\operatorname{Lie}(\mathbf N)$. The subgroup $N\cap K$ has measure one. By Proposition 3.6 and (3.5), after extending scalars to $\mathbb C$ this transform is an algebra isomorphism onto the Weyl-invariant group algebra of the torus quotient. [On the Satake isomorphism, §3](https://people.math.harvard.edu/~gross/preprints/sat.pdf#page=6)

Gross's §1 defines the Weyl group of the algebraic diagonal torus $\mathbf T$ as its algebraic normalizer modulo $\mathbf T$. [On the Satake isomorphism, §1](https://people.math.harvard.edu/~gross/preprints/sat.pdf#page=2)

The separate fixed-space bound applies to an irreducible smooth complex representation of this group. [On the Satake isomorphism, §6](https://people.math.harvard.edu/~gross/preprints/sat.pdf#page=11)

By Milne’s normalizer criterion, a matrix over a field extension $E/F$ lies in the algebraic normalizer of $\mathbf T$ when its conjugation preserves the base-changed torus, equivalently when it preserves the diagonal subgroup over every commutative $E$-algebra. For the diagonal torus of split $\mathrm{GL}_n$ the Weyl group is constant, with its classes represented by the permutation matrices. [Milne, Algebraic Groups, Proposition 1.59](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=33) and [Corollary 22.60 and Example 22.62](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=400)

For the split torus in Gross’s setting, a cocharacter is a group-scheme homomorphism from $\mathbf G_m$ to the torus. Quotienting its field points by its integral points identifies the quotient with the cocharacter lattice. The inverse sends a cocharacter to the class of its value at the uniformizer, and the torus Hecke basis element for that class corresponds to the cocharacter basis element of the group algebra. [On the Satake isomorphism, §1 and (2.11)–(2.13)](https://people.math.harvard.edu/~gross/preprints/sat.pdf#page=5)

The general linear group scheme over a base ring is given by the polynomial ring in the entries with the determinant inverted. Its points over an algebra of the base ring are the invertible matrices of the given size over that algebra. [The Stacks Project, Example 39.5.4](https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/022W)

Over a field the general linear group is connected reductive and its diagonal torus is split maximal. Over an algebraically closed field, the upper-triangular subgroup is a Borel. In the split general linear group, the positive root groups for this Borel generate its unipotent radical. In degree two the only positive root group is the upper unitriangular subgroup. [Milne, general linear groups and maximal tori](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=384) and [Borels](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=346), with [Example 22.76](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=404) and [Theorem 22.85](https://www.jmilne.org/math/CourseNotes/iAG200.pdf#page=407)

A standard smooth algebra gives a smooth affine structure morphism. This applies to a polynomial algebra in finitely many variables and to its principal localizations. [The Stacks Project, §10.137](https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/00T1) and [Definition 29.35.1](https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/tag/01V5)

A smooth affine group scheme over a base is reductive if all geometric fibers are connected reductive. Maximality of a torus is tested on geometric fibers. A smooth affine subgroup with Borel geometric fibers in a reductive group scheme is a Borel over the base, using the parabolic criterion on those fibers. Its unipotent radical is the unique smooth closed normal subgroup having the geometric fiber radicals. [Conrad, Reductive Group Schemes, Definitions 3.1.1, 3.2.1, 5.2.1 and 5.2.10 and Corollary 5.2.5](https://math.stanford.edu/~conrad/papers/luminysga3smf.pdf)

### Proof

**Given:** $\ell,\pi,V,K$ and the hypotheses in the statement.

**Goal:** Identify the Hecke character, its normalized function model and its projective descent.

1.1 By [F1], the absolute value on $F$ extends that on $\mathbb Q$, so $|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$ and $\nu(\ell)=1$. The integer values of $\nu$ therefore range over $\mathbb Z$, and the nonzero elements of positive valuation are divisible by $\ell$ in $O$. Thus $F$ is complete discretely valued, with uniformizer $\ell$ and residue field of size $\ell$. Take the affine group scheme $\mathbf G_O=\operatorname{Spec} O[x_{11},x_{12},x_{21},x_{22},d^{-1}]$ with $d=x_{11}x_{22}-x_{12}x_{21}$, as in [F20]. Its $O$-points are the invertible matrices $K$, and its generic and special fibers are the corresponding general linear groups over $F$ and $O/\ell O$. The determinant localization is smooth over $O$ by [F21]. The geometric fibers are connected reductive by the field result in [F20], so the integral model is reductive. The compact open subgroup $K$ is the one in [F1]. [F1, F20, F21]

2.1 Inside $\mathbf G_O$ take the diagonal subgroup $\mathbf T_O$ and the upper-triangular subgroup $\mathbf B_O$, defined by setting the off-diagonal entries and the lower-left entry to zero, respectively. These equations are stable under matrix multiplication and inversion over every $O$-algebra. For an upper-triangular matrix with diagonal $(a,d)$ and upper-right entry $b$, the product $ad$ is a unit, and its inverse has diagonal $(a^{-1},d^{-1})$ and upper-right entry $-a^{-1}bd^{-1}$. The diagonal subgroup is $\mathbf G_m^2$ and is maximal on all geometric fibers by [F20]. In particular the generic fiber of $\mathbf G_O$ is split. The upper-triangular subgroup is smooth and affine by [F21], since its coordinate ring is the polynomial ring on the two diagonal entries and the upper-right entry with the product of the diagonal entries inverted. It has Borel geometric fibers by [F20] and hence is a Borel over $O$. The upper unitriangular subgroup $\mathbf N_O$ is the closed normal kernel of the diagonal projection of $\mathbf B_O$ onto $\mathbf T_O$. It is smooth with coordinate ring $O[Z]$, and on each geometric fiber it is the unipotent radical by [F20]. The relative radical criterion identifies it with the unipotent radical over $O$. These data meet the field and group hypotheses in Gross’s local setting. [F1, F6, F20, F21, step 1.1]

3.1 The smooth complex representation on $V$ is irreducible by hypothesis, so the fixed-space bound from [F6] and the nonzero hypothesis yield $\dim_{\mathbb C}V^K=1$. Write $\lambda(f)$ for the scalar of $T_f$ on this line. The unital action in [F3] makes $\lambda$ a unital complex-algebra homomorphism. [F2, F3, F6, given, step 2.1]

3.2 Let $\mathbf T=D_2$ and $\mathbf N=U_2$ be the diagonal and upper unitriangular group schemes over $F$, and put $T=\mathbf T(F)$ and $N=\mathbf N(F)$. These group schemes are the generic fibers of $\mathbf T_O$ and $\mathbf N_O$ from step 2.1. Write $n(z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&z\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ for an upper unitriangular matrix of size two. By [F9] the group scheme $\mathbf N$ has the polynomial coordinate ring $F[Z]$, generated over $F$ by $Z$. Thus the structure morphism is locally of finite type by [F12].
