To check quasi-compactness, refine an open cover of $\operatorname{Spec} F[Z]$ by the distinguished opens $D(f)$ contained in its members. If their polynomials generated a proper ideal, it would be principal by [F13]. The zero ideal is prime by [F18] because the polynomial ring is a domain. For a proper nonzero principal ideal $(p)$, choose a divisor $h$ of $p$ of least positive degree. Such a divisor exists since $p$ is nonconstant. It is irreducible, since a factorization into two nonconstant factors would give a divisor of $p$ of smaller positive degree. A generator of any larger principal ideal divides $h$. If the larger ideal is proper, its generator is therefore a unit multiple of $h$, so the ideal is $(h)$. Thus $(h)$ is maximal, and it is prime by [F18]. In either case a prime containing all the covering polynomials would be in none of the distinguished opens. Thus the polynomials generate the unit ideal. By [F18] write $1$ as a finite sum of multiples of these polynomials. No proper prime ideal contains all polynomials in that expression, so their distinguished opens cover the spectrum. For each of those finitely many opens, choose a member of the original cover containing it, using countable choice with singleton sets padding the finite list of nonempty choices. The chosen members form a finite subcover, so the spectrum is quasi-compact by [F18]. By [F19], $\operatorname{Spec} F$ has just the point $(0)$ because $F$ is a field. The quasi-compactness of the source therefore gives quasi-compactness of the structure morphism, since the only opens of the target are the empty set and the singleton. This proves $\mathbf N$ is of finite type. [F9, F12, F13, F18, F19, given, step 2.1]

4.1 Reduction from the dual numbers sends $n(w+\varepsilon z)$ to $n(w)$. By [F9] the tangent vectors at $n(0)$ are parametrized by $n(\varepsilon z)$ with $z\in F$. The coefficient of $\varepsilon$ in the coordinate $Z$ identifies the tangent space with the one-dimensional vector space $F$. For $a,d\in F^\times$, substitution $Z\mapsto(a/d)Z$ in $F[Z]$ is a ring automorphism with inverse $Z\mapsto(d/a)Z$. It induces an automorphism of the affine scheme by [F14]. By [F19], a point $n(z)$ over a commutative $F$-algebra corresponds to the map sending $Z$ to $z$. Precomposing this map with the substitution sends $n(z)$ to $n((a/d)z)$. This equals conjugation by $\operatorname{diag}(a,d)$ by entrywise multiplication. The multiplication map pulls $Z$ back to $Z_1+Z_2$, the identity sends it to zero, and inversion sends it to $-Z$. Writing $c=a/d$, the two compositions for compatibility with multiplication pull $Z$ back to $c(Z_1+Z_2)$ and $cZ_1+cZ_2$, which agree. Both identity compositions send $Z$ to zero, and both inverse compositions send $Z$ to $-cZ$. These equalities on the generator verify the group-scheme morphism conditions in [F19]. The same identities with $c^{-1}$ verify them for the inverse, so this is a group-scheme automorphism. On the dual-number points this sends $n(\varepsilon z)$ to $n(\varepsilon(a/d)z)$. By functoriality in [F9], the induced map on $\operatorname{Lie}(\mathbf N)$ is this map on the reduction kernel. On the tangent line this is multiplication by $a/d$. Its matrix in the basis corresponding to $n(\varepsilon)$ has the single entry $a/d$, so [F15] gives determinant $a/d$. By Gross's formula (3.3), the positive half modulus at $t=\operatorname{diag}(\ell^r,\ell^s)$ is $|\ell^{r-s}|_\ell^{1/2}=\ell^{(s-r)/2}$. The subgroup $T\cap K$ consists of diagonal matrices with unit entries. Each nonzero diagonal entry $a$ is $\ell^{\nu(a)}$ times a unit, so the quotient $T/(T\cap K)$ has coordinates $(r,s)\in\mathbb Z^2$ and group-algebra monomial $X^rY^s$. By the uniformizer identification in [F6], the cocharacter $x\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(x^r,x^s)$ maps to this quotient class. This is a group-scheme homomorphism since the powers are Laurent monomials and preserve multiplication. Its value at $\ell$ is the diagonal representative with valuations $(r,s)$, because $\nu(\ell)=1$. Evaluation is bijective by Gross’s identification, so these cocharacters exhaust the lattice and have the same monomials in the group algebra. [F1, F4, F6, F9, F14, F15, F16, F19, step 1.1, step 3.2]

5.1 Fix any integer pair $(r,s)$ and its diagonal representative $t$ from step 4.1. Each $tn$ has diagonal valuations $(r,s)$, so a coset with a different pair has empty preimage under $n\mapsto tn$. If the pair of $xK$ is $(r,s)$, choose an upper-triangular representative of $xK$ with diagonal $(\ell^r\epsilon_1,\ell^s\epsilon_2)$, where $\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2$ are units in $O$. Right multiplication by $\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_1^{-1},\epsilon_2^{-1})\in K$ yields a representative with diagonal $(\ell^r,\ell^s)$. It has the form $tn_0$ with $n_0\in N$, since left multiplication by $t^{-1}$ gives an upper unitriangular matrix. Thus $tn\in xK$ exactly when $n_0^{-1}n\in N\cap K$, making its preimage $n_0(N\cap K)$. The preimage is open in $N$ because the map $n\mapsto tn$ is continuous in the inherited topology and $xK$ is open in $G$ by the translation property in [F7]. By [F10] the open preimage is Borel and therefore measurable for the Haar measure on $N$. Its measure is one by left invariance and the normalization in [F6]. [F1, F6, F7, F10, step 4.1]

6.1 The function $n\mapsto f(tn)$ is constant with value $f(x)$ on the preimage of each $xK$. These preimages are disjoint, and only finitely many from cosets where $f(x)\ne0$ can contribute. A nonzero level set for $f(tn)$ is the disjoint union of the contributing preimages with its level value. Each preimage is measurable and has measure one by step 5.1. By [F8] each nonzero level set has finite measure, equal to the number of its contributing preimages. The zero level set is the complement of the union of the nonzero level sets and is measurable as well. Thus the integrand is an integrable complex simple function. Its integral is therefore the sum of $f(x)$ over the contributing cosets with pair $(r,s)$. Multiplication by $\ell^{(s-r)/2}$ gives the coefficient of $X^rY^s$ in [F4]. This agrees with Gross’s coefficient formula on each integral input and at every pair. [F3, F4, F6, F8, step 4.1, step 5.1]

7.1 Each $f\in\mathcal H(G,K)$ is locally constant because it is constant on the open cosets $xK$. It is constant on double cosets and is nonzero on only finitely many of them because only finitely many cosets $xK$ contribute. The indicators of double cosets therefore span the complex algebra and are linearly independent by disjointness. By [F17], sending each integral basis indicator to the same function identifies $\mathbb C\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathcal H_{\mathbb Z}$ with $\mathcal H(G,K)$ as complex vector spaces. For convolution at $z\in G$, the integrand $f(x)h(x^{-1}z)$ is constant on each $xK$ by right invariance of $f$ and left invariance of $h$. Every such coset has Haar measure one, so Gross’s convolution on integral inputs gives exactly the coset-sum product in [F3]. Extending by complex bilinearity matches multiplication, and the indicator of $K$ is the unit of both algebras. With this identification, the coefficient agreement in step 6.1 extends from the integral basis to all complex inputs. [F1, F3, F6, F7, F17, step 6.1]

8.1 Use the normalizer quotient of [F6] and the criterion in [F16]. For any field extension $E/F$, including $F$, write $T_E=\mathbf T(E)$. An $E$-point $g=(g_{ij})$ of the algebraic normalizer preserves the base-changed torus over every commutative $E$-algebra, so in particular $gT_Eg^{-1}=T_E$. Then $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,\ell)$ lies in $T_E$ and its conjugate $gDg^{-1}$ is diagonal, say $\operatorname{diag}(b_1,b_2)$. The equation $gD=(gDg^{-1})g$ gives $(\lambda_j-b_i)g_{ij}=0$ by [F11], where $\lambda_1=1$ and $\lambda_2=\ell$. Because $1\ne\ell$ in $E$, each row has at most one nonzero entry. Invertibility forces exactly one nonzero entry per row, with the two entries in distinct columns. Thus $g$ is diagonal or a diagonal matrix times $w=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. Each of these two matrix forms normalizes the diagonal torus over every commutative $E$-algebra, since diagonal matrices commute and $w$ interchanges the two entries. The two cosets modulo $T_E$ are distinct. Milne’s identification of the split Weyl quotient with the permutation matrices gives exactly these two geometric classes, both represented over $F$. Conjugation by $w$ swaps the diagonal entries and acts by $(r,s)\mapsto(s,r)$ on the torus quotient. Under Gross’s uniformizer identification in step 4.1, conjugation by $w$ sends the cocharacter $x\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(x^r,x^s)$ to $x\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(x^s,x^r)$. Hence the same swap acts on Gross’s cocharacter group algebra. Its invariants consist of Laurent polynomials with coefficients constant on the swap orbits. The orbit sums are a basis over the integral coefficient ring and over $\mathbb C$. Extending the coefficient ring $\mathbb Z[\sqrt{\ell},1/\sqrt{\ell}]$ to $\mathbb C$ therefore gives the symmetric Laurent algebra by [F17]. Scalar extension preserves Gross’s isomorphism and its inverse, and step 7.1 identifies the resulting transform with $S$. [F1, F4, F6, F9, F11, F16, F17, step 4.1, step 7.1]

9.1 Transfer $\lambda$ through $S$ to the unital character $\eta=\lambda\circ S^{-1}$ of the symmetric Laurent polynomials. This algebra is generated by $X+Y$, $XY$ and $(XY)^{-1}$, because multiplying any symmetric Laurent polynomial by a sufficiently high power of $XY$ gives a symmetric polynomial, to which [F5] applies. Set $a=\eta(X+Y)$ and $b=\eta(XY)$. The value $b$ is nonzero because $XY$ is invertible. The polynomial $t^2-at+b$ has a complex root $u$, which is nonzero since $b\ne0$. Put $v=b/u$. The root equation gives $u+v=a$, so $t^2-at+b=(t-u)(t-v)$. Evaluation at $(u,v)$ agrees with $\eta$ on the three generators and hence everywhere. Any pair giving the scalars for every $f$ must give $a$ and $b$ upon evaluation of $X+Y$ and $XY$, since $S$ is surjective onto the symmetric Laurent algebra. Its entries are therefore the roots of $t^2-at+b$ counted with multiplicity. [F5, step 3.1, step 8.1]

10.1 In the function model, the Hecke operator preserves the fixed line. Its scalar is its value on $\Phi_{u,v}$ evaluated at the identity, since $\Phi_{u,v}(1)=1$. Right translation and the coset action give $\sum_{xK}f(x)\Phi_{u,v}(x)$ for that scalar. Substituting the triangular values gives precisely $S(f)(u,v)$. This also gives the stated triangular value and identifies the same Hecke character for either ordering. [F3, F4, step 9.1]

10.2 Put $z=\ell I$. Centrality gives $k_1zk k_2=z k_1k k_2$ for $k,k_1,k_2\in K$, so the indicator of $zK$ is invariant on both sides by $K$. The set $zK$ is compact as the image of the compact subgroup $K$ by the continuous translation $k\mapsto zk$. The indicator is zero outside that compact coset, so it belongs to the spherical Hecke algebra. Its transform is $XY$ because there is one coset, of diagonal valuations $(1,1)$, and its action on $V^K$ is $\pi(z)$ by the coset sum. If the central character is trivial this operator is the identity, giving $uv=1$. [F1, F3, F4, F7, step 9.1]

11.1 Under this triviality, the lifts of a projective element act by the same operator on $V$, so $\bar\pi([g])=\pi(g)$ is a well-defined representation with $\bar K$-fixed space $V^K$. Write $q(g)=[g]$ and $\bar K=q(K)$. Each orbit map of $\bar\pi$ on $V$ is continuous for the induced norm topology since its composite with $q$ is the continuous orbit map of $\pi$ and $q$ has the quotient topology. For an open subgroup $U$ fixing a vector, $q(U)$ fixes it, and $q^{-1}(q(U))$ is the union of the scalar translates of $U$. Each translate is open by the homeomorphism assertion in [F7], so the quotient-topology criterion in [F7] makes $q(U)$ open. Thus the descended representation is smooth. [F1, F2, F7, step 10.2]

12.1 For any projective spherical Hecke function, each coset of $\bar K$ acts in its finite sum via any one lift, with the same operator on $V^K$ for all lifts and representatives because scalar matrices act trivially and $K$ fixes those vectors. For $f\in\mathcal H(G,K)$, sum the finitely many nonzero values $f(x)$ in each fiber of $xK\mapsto q(x)\bar K$ to define a function $\bar f$ on the projective cosets. Left multiplication by $K$ permutes the corresponding fibers and preserves $f$, so $\bar f$ is invariant on both sides by $\bar K$. Take a compact set $C\subseteq G$ outside which $f$ is zero. If $\bar f(\bar g)\ne0$, some coset $xK$ with $q(x)\bar K=\bar g\bar K$ has $f(x)\ne0$. There is then $k\in K$ with $q(xk)=\bar g$. Right $K$-invariance gives $f(xk)=f(x)\ne0$, so $xk\in C$ and $\bar g\in q(C)$. Thus $\bar f$ is zero outside the compact set $q(C)$, which is compact by continuity of the quotient map and [F7]. Grouping the formula for $T_f$ by these fibers gives $T_f=T_{\bar f}$ on the identified fixed spaces. This gives the descended action with the same volume-one convention. [F1, F3, F7, step 11.1] ∎


## Good-prime Hecke scalars in the weight-two adelic model

[Open item](item-lem-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-adelic-hecke-scalars) · level 3


### Statement

Assume countable choice and let $f$ be a normalized weight-two cuspidal newform of level $N$ and character $\epsilon$, with Fourier coefficients $a_n$ at infinity. Fix a prime $\ell\nmid N$. Write $\mathbb A=\mathbb R\times\mathbb A_f$, where the finite adele ring $\mathbb A_f$ consists of tuples of $p$-adic numbers integral for all but finitely many primes $p$. Thus $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$ consists of a real invertible matrix and invertible $p$-adic matrices lying in $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_p)$ at all but finitely many $p$.

Let $K_0(N)$ be the group of tuples $k=(k_p)_p$ with $k_p\in\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_p)$ and lower left entry in $N\mathbb Z_p$ at every $p$, embedded with real component the identity. For each $p\mid N$ let $e_p$ be its exponent in the prime factorization of $N$. Identify the unit ball $\mathbb Z_p$ in $\mathbb Q_p$ with the ring of compatible residues and reduce the lower right entry of $k_p$ modulo $p^{e_p}$. Combine these residues for the distinct maximal prime powers $p^{e_p}$ by the Chinese remainder theorem to obtain the unit class $d(k)$ modulo $N$. For $N=1$ the list is empty and $d(k)$ is the sole class modulo $1$.

Let $\Phi:\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)\to\mathbb C$ be invariant under left multiplication by $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$ embedded by using the same rational matrix at every place. Suppose
$$\Phi(gk)=\epsilon(d(k))^{-1}\Phi(g),\qquad \Phi(h_\infty)=(f|_2 h)(i)$$
for all $g\in\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$, $k\in K_0(N)$ and $h\in\mathrm{GL}_2^+(\mathbb R)$, the real matrices of positive determinant. Here $h_\infty$ denotes $h$ at the real place with identity at every prime.

On the complex vector space of functions from $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$ to $\mathbb C$ with pointwise operations, use the right-translation representation $(\pi(x)F)(g)=F(gx_\ell)$, where $x_\ell$ denotes insertion of $x\in\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ at $\ell$ with identity elsewhere. Then $\Phi$ is fixed by $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. Let $\mathcal T_\ell$ and $\mathcal R_\ell$ be the Hecke operators of the indicators of $K\operatorname{diag}(\ell,1)K$ and $(\ell I)K$, respectively, summing one term per coset $xK$. If these two operators act on $\Phi$ by scalars, the scalars are respectively $a_\ell$ and $\epsilon(\ell)$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The finite adele ring uses coordinatewise operations and the rational embeddings give $|a/b|_p\le1$ for integers $a,b$ with $p\nmid b$, with equality if $p\nmid a$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), justification of the diagonal rational embedding). The distinct maximal prime powers $p^{e_p}$ are pairwise coprime and have product $N$. The Chinese remainder map for this list is a unital ring isomorphism, also for the empty list at $N=1$ ([math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic), [math:thm-chinese-remainder-theorem](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-chinese-remainder-theorem)). The canonical identification of the unit ball with the compatible-residue ring gives unital ring maps to $\mathbb Z/p^{e_p}\mathbb Z$. The construction by eventual residues gives usual reduction on an embedded integer, using the constant integer sequence ([math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp), [math:def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes)). The character is a group homomorphism from units modulo $N$ to $\mathbb C^\times$ ([math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q)).

[F2] The weight-two action has $(f|_2 A)|_2 B=f|_2(AB)$ for real matrices of positive determinant. The transformation for $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma_0(N)$ gives $f(z+1)=f(z)$ since its lower right entry is $1$ and $\epsilon(1)=1$ ([def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form), [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q)). At the good prime the classical formula is $(T_\ell f)(z)=\ell^{-1}\sum_{b=0}^{\ell-1}f((z+b)/\ell)+\ell\epsilon(\ell)f(\ell z)$ and $T_\ell f=a_\ell f$ ([def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform), [lem-author-e2fc0f4318ab-classical-hecke-first-coefficient](item-lem-author-e2fc0f4318ab-classical-hecke-first-coefficient)).

[F3] Under countable choice the local group is Hausdorff, $K$ is compact and open, and the valuation is additive and zero on local scalar units ([lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets)). Determinant is multiplicative over the local field and the local integer ring ([math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative)). At exponent one the index-coset formula for integral matrices of determinant valuation one gives exactly the representatives $M_b=\begin{pmatrix}\ell&b\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ for $0\le b<\ell$ and $M_*=\operatorname{diag}(1,\ell)$ ([lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets](item-lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets)).

[F4] The complex numbers form a field ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)). For any set, the functions from that set to a field form a vector space under pointwise operations ([math:def-function-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-function-space)). For a complex representation of a Hausdorff group with a compact open subgroup, the spherical action on fixed vectors sums one term per coset $xK$ for the indicator of a compact double coset ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

### Proof

**Given:** The normalized newform, the good prime and the invariance, covariance and real restriction of $\Phi$ stated above.

**Goal:** Establish $K$-fixedness and identify the scalars when the two operators act by scalars on $\Phi$.

1.1 The lower left condition defining $K_0(N)$ is preserved by multiplication since the lower left entry of a product is a sum of multiples of the two lower left entries. Inversion preserves it since the lower left entry of an inverse is the negative of the original entry divided by the determinant, which is a unit by determinant multiplicativity applied to the integral inverse. For $p\mid N$, reduction modulo $p^{e_p}$ sends $N$ to zero and $k_p$ to an invertible upper triangular matrix, with inverse given by the reduction of $k_p^{-1}$. Its diagonal product is a unit, so each diagonal entry is a unit. The lower right entries multiply under products of these triangular matrices. The Chinese remainder isomorphism therefore gives a well-defined unit class $d(k)$ and a group homomorphism $d$, including for $N=1$. [F1, F3, given]

2.1 For $x\in K$, $x_\ell$ lies in $K_0(N)$ since $N$ is a unit of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ by $\ell\nmid N$, and the other finite components are identities. Hence $d(x_\ell)=1$. Covariance and $\epsilon(1)=1$ give $\pi(x)\Phi=\Phi$. On the function space in [F4], evaluating at $gx_\ell$ preserves pointwise linear combinations. For each function $F$, $(\pi(x)\pi(y)F)(g)=F(gx_\ell y_\ell)=F(g(xy)_\ell)$ because insertion at $\ell$ preserves multiplication. Insertion of the identity gives the identity operator, so $\pi(x^{-1})$ is the inverse of $\pi(x)$. In particular $\pi(yk)\Phi=\pi(y)\Phi$ for $k\in K$, which makes each term in a coset sum independent of its representative. [F1, F3, F4, given, step 1.1]

3.1 Each matrix in $K\operatorname{diag}(\ell,1)K$ is integral and has determinant valuation one since the determinants of the two $K$ factors are units. Conversely, $M_b=\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\0&1\end{pmatrix}\operatorname{diag}(\ell,1)$ and $M_*$ is the conjugate of $\operatorname{diag}(\ell,1)$ by the coordinate swap in $K$. Thus all the representatives in [F3] belong to the named double coset, which consequently equals the integral determinant-valuation-one locus. The scalar matrix $M'_0=\ell I$ is central, so its double coset is the single coset $(\ell I)K$. Both supports are bi-invariant under $K$ and compact as finite unions of translates of $K$. Hence their indicators act by the finite coset sums in [F4] on the $K$-fixed vector from step 2.1. [F3, F4, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 For each rational representative $M$ from step 3.1, take $k(M)$ to have $M^{-1}$ at finite primes other than $\ell$ and identity at $\ell$ and the real place. The entries of $M^{-1}$ have denominators that are powers of $\ell$ and $M$ is integral, so both matrices are integral away from $\ell$. The inverse is upper triangular, giving $k(M)\in K_0(N)$. The lower right entry of $M^{-1}$ is the inverse of the embedded integer $d_M$, the lower right entry of $M$. The unital reduction map sends it to the inverse of the usual residue of $d_M$, so $d(k(M))=[d_M]_N^{-1}$ by the Chinese remainder map. The factorization $h_\infty M_\ell=M\,(M^{-1}h)_\infty\,k(M)$ holds coordinate by coordinate. The assumed invariance and covariance give $\Phi(h_\infty M_\ell)=\epsilon(d_M)((f|_2 M^{-1})|_2 h)(i)$. [F1, F2, given, step 1.1, step 3.1]

5.1 The weight-two formula gives $(f|_2 M_b^{-1})(z)=\ell^{-1}f((z-b)/\ell)$ with $d_{M_b}=1$ and $(f|_2 M_*^{-1})(z)=\ell f(\ell z)$ with $d_{M_*}=\ell$. By period one of $f$, the sum with $-b$ equals the sum with $b$, since negation permutes the residues modulo $\ell$. Thus the sum in $\mathcal T_\ell\Phi$ at $h_\infty$ is $((T_\ell f)|_2 h)(i)=a_\ell\Phi(h_\infty)$. The matrix $(M'_0)^{-1}$ is a positive scalar matrix whose weight-two action is the identity, and $d_{M'_0}=\ell$. Hence $\mathcal R_\ell\Phi(h_\infty)=\epsilon(\ell)\Phi(h_\infty)$. Since $a_1=1$, $f$ is nonzero at some $x+iy$ with $y>0$, and $h=\begin{pmatrix}y&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ sends $i$ there and has nonzero weight factor. Equality at this $h_\infty$ identifies both assumed scalars. [F2, F4, given, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 4.1] ∎


## Cuspidal representations of the split adelic group

[Open item](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-cuspidal-spectrum) · level 3


### Definition

Assume the Axiom of Choice, which provides a choice function for any family of nonempty sets. For $L=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$ and its scalar subgroup $Z$, identify $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$ with its image in $L$ under the entrywise diagonal embedding, which uses the same rational entries at every place. Put $X_L=(\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)Z)\backslash L$, the space of orbits for left multiplication by the subgroup $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)Z$ with the quotient topology.

Give the additive group $\mathbb A=\mathbb R\times\mathbb A_f$ the product of the usual real topology and the restricted-product topology on the finite adeles. In the additive quotient $\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A$, the rational subgroup is diagonal and its cosets carry the quotient topology.

Use a nonzero invariant Radon measure on $X_L$ for right translation by $L$, and translation-invariant Haar probability on the additive quotient $\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A$.

For a continuous complex square-integrable function $\phi$ on $X_L$, viewed on $L$ by composition with the coset projection, define its constant term by the complex Lebesgue integral against Haar probability.
$$C\phi(g)=\int_{\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A}\phi\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix}g\right)\,dx.$$
Write $L^2_{\mathrm{cusp}}(X_L)$ for the closure in the square-integral norm of these continuous functions for which $C\phi(g)=0$ for every $g$. An irreducible cuspidal representation with trivial central character is a strongly continuous unitary representation occurring on a nonzero closed invariant subspace of this space with no proper nonzero closed invariant subspace. Its action is right translation, $R(h)\phi(g)=\phi(gh)$. Its projective realization uses the same action through $L/Z$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice is assumed. It implies dependent choice and countable choice. [math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice) [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)

[F1] The scalar adeles under addition and the split adelic matrix group have coordinatewise operations with the restricted-product topologies. The split adelic matrix group $L$ is a topological group. The scalar subgroup $Z$ is closed and central in $L$, and $L/Z$ is a topological group with open quotient projection. The finite diagonal map is an injective unital ring homomorphism $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb A_f$. Together with the usual inclusion into $\mathbb R$ it gives the injective unital ring homomorphism $\mathbb Q\to\mathbb A$. [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles) [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups) [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)

[F2] The local field $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is locally compact Hausdorff and its integer ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is compact and open. An integral square matrix is invertible over that ring exactly when its determinant is a unit. Compact spaces have compact products under AC, and real closed bounded boxes are compact. [math:cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected) [math:thm-zp-is-compact-hausdorff-totally-disconnected-and-complete](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-zp-is-compact-hausdorff-totally-disconnected-and-complete) [math:cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit) [math:thm-tychonoff](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tychonoff) [math:thm-heine-borel-rn](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-heine-borel-rn)

[F3] The integer ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is the compatible-residue inverse limit of the rings $\mathbb Z/\ell^k\mathbb Z$. The Chinese remainder theorem realizes any prescribed residues for a finite list of pairwise coprime positive moduli by one integer. [math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp) [math:thm-chinese-remainder-theorem](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-chinese-remainder-theorem)

[F4] A closed-subgroup quotient of a locally compact Hausdorff group is locally compact Hausdorff and its projection is open under AC. For an abelian group the quotient is a topological group. Continuous images of compact sets are compact, and continuous real functions on compact sets are bounded. Compact Hausdorff groups admit Haar probability under AC. [Compact lifts and averaging on a homogeneous space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-closed-subgroup-quotient-averaging-and-compact-lifts) [math:lem-quotient-of-an-lca-group-by-a-closed-subgroup-is-lca](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-quotient-of-an-lca-group-by-a-closed-subgroup-is-lca) [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps) [math:cor-normalized-haar-probability-on-a-compact-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-normalized-haar-probability-on-a-compact-group)

[F5] The modular function is a continuous homomorphism to the positive reals under multiplication. Discrete groups have modular function one. For a locally compact Hausdorff group $G$ and closed subgroup $H$, with AC and left Haar measures, agreement of the modular functions on $H$ supplies a nonzero left $G$-invariant Radon measure on $G/H$. [math:thm-the-modular-function-is-a-continuous-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-the-modular-function-is-a-continuous-homomorphism) [math:prop-compact-discrete-and-abelian-groups-are-unimodular](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-compact-discrete-and-abelian-groups-are-unimodular) [math:prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree)

[F6] Complex $L^2$ uses measurable functions modulo equality almost everywhere and is Hilbert for the integral pairing under countable choice. A measurable self-map $T$ preserves $\mu$ if $\mu(T^{-1}[E])=\mu(E)$ for every measurable $E$. Composing a nonnegative measurable function with a measure-preserving map preserves its integral. On a locally compact Hausdorff space with a Radon measure, continuous functions with compact support are dense in real $L^2$ under dependent choice. Here the support is the closure of the nonzero set. [Complex Lebesgue spaces and Euclidean test-function conventions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions) [math:lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-l-two-with-the-integral-pairing-is-a-hilbert-space) [math:def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system) [math:thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps) [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space) [math:thm-c-c-is-dense-in-l-p-for-radon-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-c-c-is-dense-in-l-p-for-radon-measures) [math:def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space)

[F7] The left-multiplication orbits are the cosets of the form $Hg$ and the coset projection has the quotient topology. A unitary representation is a group homomorphism to the bijective complex-linear isometries, with norm-continuous orbit maps. [math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset) [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology) [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)

[F8] Basic opens in the product topology restrict finitely many coordinates. A map from a quotient is continuous if its composition with the quotient projection is continuous. A continuous map that is constant on the fibers of a quotient map factors through it as a continuous map. [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology) [math:thm-quotient-universal-property](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-universal-property)

[F9] Locally compact Hausdorff groups have left Haar measures under AC. The modular function compares the left Haar integral with its right translates. A closed subspace of a complete metric space is complete for the restricted metric. [math:cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures) [math:def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group) [math:thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed)

[F10] On a nonzero integer $m$, $v_\ell(m)$ is the greatest $k\ge 0$ with $\ell^k\mid m$. For a nonzero rational $a/b$ the valuation is $v_\ell(a)-v_\ell(b)$ and the associated absolute value is $\ell^{-v_\ell(a/b)}$. The absolute value on the completed field is multiplicative and extends this value on the rationals. [math:thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-is-a-field) [math:def-p-adic-valuation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-valuation) [math:lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-p-adic-valuation-on-q) [math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals) [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)

[F11] The canonical inclusion of the natural numbers into the integers is one-to-one, has image the nonnegative integers, and preserves and reflects order. The inverse image of a nonempty set of positive integers therefore has a least element by natural-number well-ordering. Its image is the least member of the given set. Every integer greater than one has a prime divisor by prime factorization. Prime factorization is unique up to order. [math:lem-nat-embeds-int](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-embeds-int) [math:thm-well-ordering-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-well-ordering-principle) [math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic)

[F12] In a locally compact metric space, sufficiently small closed balls form a base of compact neighborhoods at each point. A compact subset of a Hausdorff space is closed, and a closed subset of a compact Hausdorff space is compact. [math:lem-compact-closed-balls-in-a-locally-compact-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compact-closed-balls-in-a-locally-compact-metric-space) [math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed)

[F13] For each real $t$ there is an integer $m$ with $m\le t<m+1$. [math:lem-integer-part](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-integer-part)

[F14] Continuous maps are measurable for the Borel sigma-algebras. A measurable complex function is integrable if the integral of its modulus is finite. The nonnegative integral is monotone on nonnegative measurable functions. The integral on complex $L^1$ is complex-linear. [math:thm-continuous-preimages-of-borel-sets-are-borel](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuous-preimages-of-borel-sets-are-borel) [math:def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals) [math:prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral) [math:thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one)

[F15] For a measurable set $E$ of finite measure and a finite $c\ge 0$, the integral of the scaled indicator $c\mathbf{1}_E$ is $c\mu(E)$ by agreement of the nonnegative integral with the simple integral and the formula for that simple integral. [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral) [math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function)

[F16] The complex modulus is multiplicative and satisfies the triangle inequality $|z+w|\le |z|+|w|$. [math:lem-real-and-complex-modulus-rules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-and-complex-modulus-rules)

[F17] For real $r$, powers $r^j$ tend to zero if $|r|<1$, and powers $|r|^j$ tend to $+\infty$ if $|r|>1$. [math:lem-geometric-sequence-null](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-geometric-sequence-null)

[F18] An open map sends open sets to open sets. A homeomorphism is a continuous bijection with continuous inverse. [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)

[F19] In a metric space, a point is in the closure of a subset exactly when every open ball about it meets that subset. [math:def-metric-interior-closure-boundary](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-interior-closure-boundary)

[F20] For measurable sets $A\subseteq B$ one has $\mu(A)\le\mu(B)$. The measure of a finite union of measurable sets is at most the sum of their measures. [math:prop-measure-monotonicity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-measure-monotonicity) [math:thm-finite-and-countable-subadditivity-of-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-and-countable-subadditivity-of-measures)

### Justification

**Given:** The adele ring, matrix group and quotients in the definition, and the Axiom of Choice.

1.1 The additive adeles are a locally compact Hausdorff abelian group. Indeed $\mathbb R\times\prod_\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$ is an open subgroup with its product topology. Tracing restricted-product boxes onto this block gives the basis that restricts only finitely many coordinates, and every such basic product open is a trace. Thus $[-1,1]\times\prod_\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$ is a compact identity neighborhood. The open coordinate conditions separate distinct adeles. To test the diagonal rational subgroup, use the box $(-1/2,1/2)\times\prod_\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$. For a nonzero rational, take a fraction $a/b$ with the least positive denominator $b$ by [F11], using a positive denominator by moving its sign to the numerator if needed. If $b>1$, let $\ell$ be a prime divisor of $b$. It cannot divide $a$, since cancellation would give a smaller positive denominator. The integer valuation criterion in [F10] gives $v_\ell(a)=0$ and $v_\ell(b)\ge 1$. The rational absolute value formula in [F10] then gives $|a/b|_\ell>1$. Thus a rational integral at every prime is an integer, and the only such integer in the indicated real interval is zero. In a Hausdorff group, a subgroup with an isolated identity is discrete and closed. To see closedness, choose an identity neighborhood $V$ with $V^{-1}V$ meeting the subgroup only in the identity. Each translate $xV$ contains at most one subgroup point, so for $x$ outside the subgroup one can remove that point, if present, to obtain an open neighborhood disjoint from the subgroup. Apply the diagonal ring map of [F1] entrywise to a rational invertible matrix $M$ and its rational inverse $M^{-1}$. The entrywise map preserves matrix multiplication and the identity matrix, so the two images are inverses in $L$. It is injective because the scalar map is injective. The image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$ is therefore a subgroup of $L$. Its product with $Z$ is a subgroup because centrality lets the scalar factors commute past the rational matrices in products and inverses. [A1, F1, F2, F8, F10, F11]

1.2 For an adele $a$, for each of the finitely many primes where $a_\ell$ is nonintegral, choose an exponent $j_\ell\ge 1$ with $\ell^{-j_\ell}|a_\ell|_\ell\le 1$, using [F17] with $r=1/\ell$. Let $D$ be the product of the powers $\ell^{j_\ell}$ over these primes, with the empty product equal to one. At each of those primes all other factors of $D$ have $\ell$-adic absolute value one by [F10]. Multiplicativity gives $|Da_\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-j_\ell}|a_\ell|_\ell\le 1$, so $Da_\ell\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. At a prime dividing $D$, put $k=v_\ell(D)$, the exponent of $\ell$ in $D$. Projection from the compatible-residue inverse limit to its $k$th coordinate has kernel $\ell^k\mathbb Z_\ell$. Indeed if the $k$th residue is zero, divide the residue modulo $\ell^{j+k}$ by $\ell^k$ modulo $\ell^j$ for each $j\ge 1$. The quotients are compatible and yield a preimage under multiplication by $\ell^k$. The reverse inclusion follows by reduction. The moduli $\ell^k$ for the distinct primes dividing $D$ are pairwise coprime. A common divisor greater than one would have a prime divisor by [F11], and uniqueness of prime factorization would force that prime to be both of the distinct primes. The Chinese remainder theorem gives an integer $b$ agreeing with $Da_\ell$ in all these residue coordinates. If $D=1$, take $b=0$. At a prime dividing $D$, maximality of $k=v_\ell(D)$ gives $v_\ell(D/\ell^k)=0$. Thus $D/\ell^k$ is a unit of $\mathbb Z_\ell$, and dividing $Da_\ell-b\in\ell^k\mathbb Z_\ell$ by $D$ gives $a_\ell-b/D\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. At all other primes $v_\ell(D)=0$, so $D$ is a unit and both $a_\ell$ and $b/D$ are integral. [F1, F3, F10, F11, F17, algebra]

2.1 Use the maximum of the four entry distances on each matrix space, with the usual real distance or the local adic distance. Its open balls are products of entry balls of the same radius and refine each open rectangle about their center, so the metric induces the product topology. Finite products of compact entry neighborhoods give local compactness of the matrix space. The determinant is continuous by the local field operations. Its nonzero locus is open and, by [F12], each point of that locus has a compact closed ball neighborhood contained in it. At a finite prime $K_\ell=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ is an open subgroup by the split matrix group construction in [F1]. By the determinant criterion in [F2], it is given in the compact Hausdorff space $\mathbb Z_\ell^4$ by the condition $|\det|_\ell=1$. This condition is closed by continuity of determinant and absolute value, so $K_\ell$ is compact by [F12]. The open subgroup $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)\times\prod_\ell K_\ell$ of $L$ has its product topology by the same trace-of-boxes argument as in step 1.1. Taking a compact identity neighborhood in the real factor and the compact product of all finite integral factors gives a compact identity neighborhood in $L$. Coordinate separation makes $L$ Hausdorff. Since $Z$ is closed, $G=L/Z$ is a locally compact Hausdorff group by the closed-subgroup quotient result. Denote the projection by $q$. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F8, F12, step 1.1]

2.2 Apply the integer-part lemma to the real component $t$ of $a-b/D$. Subtract its integer part diagonally so that the real component lies in $[0,1)$ and the finite components remain integral. Thus the compact set $[0,1]\times\prod_\ell\mathbb Z_\ell$ maps onto the additive quotient under the continuous projection. The closedness in step 1.1 and the abelian quotient result give a Hausdorff topological group. Compactness of the image gives the compact Hausdorff group required for Haar probability. The map $x\mapsto n(x)$ with $n(x)=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ is continuous by the local entry topologies and $n(\mathbb Z_\ell)\subseteq \mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. In a basic open box one need only impose finitely many local conditions together with integrality off a finite set containing the nonintegral coordinates of $x$. For fixed $g\in L$, replacing $x$ by $x+r$ with $r\in\mathbb Q$ left-multiplies $n(x)g$ by $n(r)\in\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$. Thus the continuous integrand descends by the quotient topology to a function $u_g$ on the additive quotient $Y=\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A$. By multiplicativity, $|w-z|=|z-w|$ since $|-1|=1$. Applying the triangle inequality in [F16] to $z=(z-w)+w$ and to $w=(w-z)+z$ gives $\bigl||z|-|w|\bigr|\le |z-w|$. Thus the modulus is continuous. The function $u_g$ is Borel measurable by [F14], and its modulus is bounded by a finite $M\ge 0$ by compactness. If $\lambda$ is Haar probability on $Y$, then $\int |u_g|\,d\lambda\le\int M\mathbf{1}_Y\,d\lambda=M$ by [F14] and [F15]. Hence the complex integral exists. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F7, F8, F13, F14, F15, F16, step 1.1, step 1.2]

3.1 Using the entrywise embedding from step 1.1, put $\Gamma=q(\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q))$. To isolate its identity, let $U_\infty$ be the open neighborhood of the identity in $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)$ where the real linear operator $T\mapsto uTu^{-1}$ on two-by-two matrices has all coefficients within $1/2$ of the identity operator in the basis $E_{ij}$ for $i,j\in\{1,2\}$, where $E_{ij}$ has entry $1$ in row $i$, column $j$, and zeros elsewhere. The open neighborhood $q(U_\infty\times\prod_\ell K_\ell)$ meets $\Gamma$ only in the identity. In fact a rational matrix $M$ representing an element of this intersection is a scalar multiple at each place of a representative in the box. Conjugation by $M$ therefore preserves the integral matrix ring at every finite prime, since each matrix in $K_\ell$ and its inverse have integral entries. Its rational conjugation coefficients are integral at every prime and hence are integers by step 1.1. The real bound forces these integers to be the identity operator's coefficients. Writing $M=(m_{ij})$, the equality $ME_{11}=E_{11}M$ gives $m_{12}=m_{21}=0$ by comparing the off-diagonal entries. The $(1,2)$ entry of $ME_{12}=E_{12}M$ then gives $m_{11}=m_{22}$, so $M$ is scalar. By the subgroup argument in step 1.1, $\Gamma$ is closed and discrete in $G$. [F1, F2, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 Both $G$ and its closed discrete subgroup $\Gamma$ are locally compact Hausdorff groups, so take left Haar measures by [F9]. The modular function of $G$ is trivial on $q(\{I\}\times\prod_\ell K_\ell)$. Its image is a compact subgroup of the positive reals and is bounded as a subset of $\mathbb R$. If that image contained $t\ne 1$, it would contain all positive powers of $r=\max(t,t^{-1})>1$, which are unbounded by [F17]. At a single place, insert a matrix in $L$ by taking identity coordinates elsewhere and then apply $q$. For either an upper or a lower elementary unipotent matrix, conjugating by the diagonal with entry $2$ in the corresponding row and $1$ in the other doubles the off-diagonal entry. Doubling also squares the unipotent, so its modular value equals its square and is one. The single-place diagonal with entries $t,1$ is conjugate to the diagonal with entries $1,t$. Their product is a scalar in $L$ and maps to the identity in $G$, so their equal positive modular values are both one. For an invertible local matrix, first make its upper left entry nonzero if necessary. If that entry is zero, the lower left entry is nonzero by invertibility, and left multiplication by $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$ exchanges the rows with a sign on the new second row. Multiplying the upper, lower and upper unipotents with entries $1,-1,1$ in that order gives $J$. Write the resulting matrix as $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}$ with $a\ne 0$. Left multiplication by the lower unipotent with entry $-c/a$ gives $\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\0&e\end{pmatrix}$ with $e=d-cb/a\ne 0$, since the product is invertible. Left multiplication by the upper unipotent with entry $-b/e$ then gives $\operatorname{diag}(a,e)$. This diagonal is the product of $\operatorname{diag}(a,1)$ and $\operatorname{diag}(1,e)$. Reversing the multiplications expresses the original matrix using unipotent and diagonal matrices, whose modular values are one. Each element of $L$ is a product of a real factor, finitely many exceptional single-place finite factors, and an element of $\{I\}\times\prod_\ell K_\ell$. It follows that $\Delta_G=1$, which agrees on $\Gamma$ with $\Delta_\Gamma=1$ by discreteness. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F5, F9, F17, step 2.1, step 3.1, algebra]

4.2 Inversion induces a bijection $I:G/\Gamma\to\Gamma\backslash G$ with $I(a\Gamma)=\Gamma a^{-1}$. Inversion is continuous and is its own inverse on $G$, so both induced maps are continuous by the quotient topology. The map $\Phi:X_L\to\Gamma\backslash G$ induced by $q$ is bijective since $q^{-1}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)Z$. In both $L$ and $G$, left multiplication by a fixed element $a$ is continuous by continuity of multiplication, and its inverse is left multiplication by $a^{-1}$, which is continuous for the same reason. For either left-orbit projection, the saturation of an open set is the union of its left translates by the subgroup. Each translate is open by the homeomorphism just proved, so the saturation is open and the quotient topology makes the projection open. The map $\Phi$ is continuous by the quotient property. If $U\subseteq X_L$ is open, its preimage in $L$ is open. Applying $q$ and then the projection onto $\Gamma\backslash G$ to this preimage gives $\Phi(U)$, which is open since both maps are open. Thus $I$ and $\Phi$ are homeomorphisms, and $X_L$ is locally compact Hausdorff by the closed-subgroup quotient result for $G/\Gamma$. Write $H=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)Z$ and let $p:L\to X_L$ be $p(g)=Hg$. Set $(Hg)h=H(gh)$. If $g^{\prime}=ag$ with $a\in H$, then $g^{\prime}h=a(gh)$, so $H(g^{\prime}h)=H(gh)$. The identity and associativity in $L$ give the right action laws, and any two cosets $Hg,Hk$ are related by translating by $g^{-1}k$. The formula for $\Phi$ gives $\Phi(xh)=\Phi(x)q(h)$ for $x\in X_L$ and $h\in L$. The map $p\times\mathrm{id}_L$ is a quotient map because it is a continuous surjection and is open by the product rectangle basis. The continuous map $(g,h)\mapsto p(gh)$ is constant on its fibers by the coset check, so the induced right action is jointly continuous by [F8]. [F1, F4, F7, F8, F18, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 By [F5] and step 4.1, take a nonzero left $G$-invariant Radon measure $\nu$ on $G/\Gamma$. Let $T=\Phi^{-1}\circ I$ and put $\mu(E)=\nu(T^{-1}[E])$ for Borel $E\subseteq X_L$. Continuous preimages of Borel sets are Borel, and preimages preserve disjoint unions, so this defines a Borel measure. It is nonzero because $T$ is onto. The preimage of a compact set is compact by continuity of the inverse homeomorphism, so $\mu$ is finite on compact sets. The preimages of open supersets of $E$ are exactly the open supersets of $T^{-1}[E]$, so outer regularity of $\nu$ gives outer regularity of $\mu$ on Borel sets. Preimages of compact subsets of an open $U$ are exactly the compact subsets of $T^{-1}[U]$, so inner regularity of $\nu$ gives inner regularity of $\mu$ on open sets. For $a\in G$ and $h\in L$, inversion of cosets and the right equivariance of $\Phi$ give $T(q(h)^{-1}a\Gamma)=T(a\Gamma)h$. Right translation by $h$ is a homeomorphism with inverse right translation by $h^{-1}$, so $Eh$ is Borel. Thus $T^{-1}[Eh]=q(h)^{-1}T^{-1}[E]$ and left invariance of $\nu$ gives $\mu(Eh)=\mu(E)$. [A1, F1, F4, F5, F6, F14, F18, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 4.2]

6.1 Fix any nonzero right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X_L$. With the integral pairing $\langle f_1,f_2\rangle=\int f_1\overline{f_2}\,d\mu$, complex $L^2(X_L)$ is a Hilbert space under the countable choice supplied by AC. The invariant measure is positive on every nonempty open set. Otherwise right translates of a nonempty null open set would cover $X_L$ by transitivity, and finitely many such translates would cover any compact set. For a compact set $K$ covered by the finitely many translates $Uh_1,\dots,Uh_m$ of such an open set $U$, all the sets are Borel, since $K$ is closed and the translates are open. By [F20] and right invariance, $0\le\mu(K)\le\mu(\bigcup_{j=1}^m Uh_j)\le\sum_{j=1}^m\mu(Uh_j)=0$. Inner regularity on the open space $X_L$ would then give $\mu(X_L)=0$, contradicting that $\mu$ is nonzero. Consequently continuous square-integrable functions have unique representatives among continuous functions in their almost-everywhere classes. If two continuous representatives differed at a point, their difference would be nonzero on a nonempty open set of positive measure. The continuous square-integrable functions with zero constant term at all $g$ form a linear subspace because the integrands in step 2.2 are integrable and [F14] gives complex linearity of their integrals. Explicitly, $C(\alpha\phi+\beta\psi)(g)=\alpha C\phi(g)+\beta C\psi(g)$ for all complex $\alpha,\beta$. These functions define classes in $L^2(X_L)$. [A1, F6, F7, F12, F14, F20, step 2.2, step 4.2, step 5.1]

7.1 Let $\tau_h(x)=xh$. This homeomorphism is Borel measurable by [F14]. For each Borel $E\subseteq X_L$, one has $\tau_h^{-1}[E]=Eh^{-1}$, so $\mu(\tau_h^{-1}[E])=\mu(E)$ by step 5.1. In particular, the preimage of the null set where two representatives differ is null, so $R(h)$ is well defined on almost-everywhere classes. Applying [F6] to the nonnegative measurable function $|f|^2$ gives $\int |f\circ\tau_h|^2\,d\mu=\int |f|^2\,d\mu=\|f\|_2^2$. Composition with $\tau_h$ is complex-linear, and the right action laws give $R(h)R(k)=R(hk)$ and inverse $R(h^{-1})$. Strong continuity at the identity follows first for a continuous compactly supported function $f$. Such an $f$ lies in $L^2$: its modulus is bounded on its compact support by [F4] and modulus continuity from step 2.2, and the square of this bound times the support indicator has finite integral by [F15] and the Radon property. If $K$ is its support and $V$ is a compact identity neighborhood in $L$, the inverse set $V^{-1}$ is compact because inversion is continuous. The product $K\times V^{-1}$ is compact by [F2], so its image $B=KV^{-1}$ under the joint action is compact by [F4]. For $h\in V$, $R(h)f$ has support $Kh^{-1}$ because right translation is a homeomorphism. Both $K$ and $Kh^{-1}$ lie in $B$ since $1,h\in V$. The continuous function $(x,h)\mapsto f(xh)-f(x)$ vanishes at $(x,1)$ for each $x\in B$. Given $\varepsilon>0$, continuity gives a rectangle at each such point on which its modulus is less than $\varepsilon$. A finite cover of $B$ by the first factors and intersection of the corresponding identity neighborhoods give this bound uniformly on $B$ for $h$ sufficiently near $1$ in $V$. The set $B$ is closed since it is compact in the Hausdorff space $X_L$ and hence is Borel. Also $\mu(B)<\infty$ by the Radon property. The squared difference is bounded by $\varepsilon^2\mathbf{1}_B$, so integrating by [F14] and [F15] gives $\|R(h)f-f\|_2^2\le\varepsilon^2\mu(B)$. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F6, F7, F8, F12, F14, F15, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 4.2, step 5.1, step 6.1]

8.1 Density for real functions applies since $X_L$ is locally compact Hausdorff, $\mu$ is Radon and AC supplies dependent choice. Apply it to real and imaginary parts for density in complex $L^2$. For any $v\in L^2(X_L)$ and compactly supported continuous approximation $f$, the isometries give $\|R(h)v-v\|_2\le 2\|v-f\|_2+\|R(h)f-f\|_2$. This yields continuity at the identity, and the group law gives continuity at all $h$. [A1, F6, step 4.2, step 6.1, step 7.1]

8.2 Let $S$ be the linear space from step 6.1. By [F19], a point is in its norm closure exactly when every norm ball about it meets $S$. If a ball of radius $r>0$ about $v$ misses $S$, then each point within $r/2$ of $v$ also has a ball of radius $r/2$ missing $S$, by the triangle inequality. Hence the closure is closed. For $u,v$ in the closure, scalars $\alpha,\beta$ and $\varepsilon>0$, choose $s,t\in S$ within $\varepsilon/(1+|\alpha|+|\beta|)$ of $u,v$, respectively. By the norm triangle inequality and homogeneity, $\|\alpha u+\beta v-(\alpha s+\beta t)\|_2\le |\alpha|\|u-s\|_2+|\beta|\|v-t\|_2<\varepsilon$. Since $\alpha s+\beta t\in S$, the closure is a linear subspace. On continuous functions $C(R(h)\phi)(g)=C\phi(gh)$, so translations map $S$ into itself. The isometries send approximating elements of $S$ to approximating elements of $S$, so they preserve the closure. Applying the same argument to $h^{-1}$ gives invariance with equality. [F6, F19, step 2.2, step 6.1, step 7.1]

9.1 The closed linear subspace of the Hilbert space in step 6.1 is complete for the restricted norm by [F9], so the restricted action is a strongly continuous unitary representation. A scalar acts as the identity since it is central and lies in the subgroup by which we quotient on the left. The orbit maps descend through the quotient topology of $L/Z$ and hence are norm-continuous there. This gives the claimed projective realization on the same space. [F1, F6, F7, F8, F9, step 6.1, step 7.1, step 8.1, step 8.2] ∎


## Finite ideles and locally principal right ideals

[Open item](item-thm-adelic-right-ideal-dictionary) · level 4


### Statement

Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra, with reduced norm $N$, and let $O$ be an order, a unital subring that is a full integral lattice in $B$. At a prime $\ell$ write $B_\ell$ for the quaternion algebra with coefficients in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $O_\ell$ for the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of $O$. The superscript $\times$ denotes the elements with two-sided inverses in the indicated ring. A finite quaternion idele $x=(x_\ell)_\ell$ has $x_\ell\in B_\ell^\times$ everywhere and $x_\ell\in O_\ell^\times$ for all but finitely many primes. Write $\widehat B^\times$ for the group of these tuples and $\widehat O^\times=\prod_\ell O_\ell^\times$.

The assignment
$$x\widehat O^\times\longmapsto I_x=\{b\in B:b\in x_\ell O_\ell\text{ for every prime }\ell\}$$
is a bijection from the cosets for right multiplication by $\widehat O^\times$ to the locally principal right fractional $O$-ideals. Such an ideal is a full integral lattice stable under right multiplication by $O$ whose completed span at each prime is generated by one invertible local quaternion on the left, meaning a generator in $B_\ell^\times$. The completion satisfies $(I_x)_\ell=x_\ell O_\ell$. Moreover, $I_x\subseteq O$ exactly when $x_\ell O_\ell\subseteq O_\ell$ for every $\ell$. If $d$ is the positive generator of the integer span of the norms $N(b)$ for $b\in I_x$, then
$$v_\ell(d)=v_\ell(N(x_\ell))\quad\text{for every prime }\ell,\qquad d=\prod_\ell\ell^{v_\ell(N(x_\ell))}.$$

For a rational unit $a\in B^\times$ and a locally principal right fractional $O$-ideal $I$, write $aI=\{ab:b\in I\}$. This is again a locally principal right fractional $O$-ideal, and $I_{ax}=aI_x$. Call two such ideals $I,J$ equivalent if $J=aI$ for some $a\in B^\times$. The double cosets $B^\times\backslash\widehat B^\times/\widehat O^\times$ classify these equivalence classes. A double coset consists of the representatives $a x k$ with $a\in B^\times$ and $k\in\widehat O^\times$.

Write $G(\mathbb Q)=B^\times/\mathbb Q^\times$, $G(\mathbb A_f)=\widehat B^\times/\mathbb A_f^\times$, and $K$ for the image of $\widehat O^\times$ in $G(\mathbb A_f)$. Here $\mathbb A_f^\times$ is the group of finite scalar ideles, the tuples in $\prod_\ell\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ that belong to $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many primes, embedded as scalar quaternions. Projection induces a bijection from the unprojectivized double cosets to $G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A_f)/K$.

[def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The Hamilton products over $\mathbb Q$ and $\mathbb Q_\ell$ are associative and bilinear, with the same rational multiplication law under coefficient extension and with scalars central ([def-author-24fda87362bc-hamilton-algebra-and-reduced-norm](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-hamilton-algebra-and-reduced-norm)). An order and each of its completed spans are unital subrings. A rational basis of the order that generates it over $\mathbb Z$ remains a basis over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and spans $O_\ell$ over $\mathbb Z_\ell$ ([def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions)).

[F2] The quaternion idele group and its integral unit subgroup use coordinatewise multiplication ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles)). For a subgroup $U$ of a group, $xU=\{xu:u\in U\}$ and $yU=xU$ exactly when $x^{-1}y\in U$, since $y\in yU$ and multiplication by an element of $U$ permutes $U$ ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)).

[F3] A family of local $\mathbb Z_\ell$-spans of $\mathbb Q_\ell$-bases equal to $O_\ell$ outside finitely many primes comes from one full integral lattice by rational intersection. Every rational full lattice is recovered by that intersection and has the same completion as $O$ outside finitely many primes ([lem-author-f5ba73d33e6e-reconstructing-quaternion-lattices](item-lem-author-f5ba73d33e6e-reconstructing-quaternion-lattices)).

[F4] A locally principal right fractional ideal has a positive rational norm generator $d$ over $\mathbb Z$. Its completion at $\ell$ has local norm ideal $d\mathbb Z_\ell$, where the local norm ideal is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of the norms of elements, and a local lattice generated by $x_\ell\in B_\ell^\times$ has local norm ideal $N(x_\ell)\mathbb Z_\ell$ ([def-quaternion-right-ideal-and-reduced-norm](item-def-quaternion-right-ideal-and-reduced-norm), [lem-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-lattice-norms-and-local-generators](item-lem-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-lattice-norms-and-local-generators)). Here $v_\ell$ is the normalized valuation with $v_\ell(\ell)=1$, additive under multiplication of nonzero scalars and zero exactly on units of $\mathbb Z_\ell$. On nonzero rationals it is the exponent in the numerator minus the exponent in the denominator ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)). Here $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is the completion of $\mathbb Q$ for the absolute value $\ell^{-v_\ell(\cdot)}$ on nonzero inputs, with absolute value zero at zero. The ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ consists of the elements of nonnegative valuation, including zero. Positive integers have unique prime factorizations ([math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic)).

[F5] The two projective unit groups are the quotient groups by the indicated rational and finite scalar subgroups, and the rational projective group embeds by the diagonal map ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)). A finite scalar idele is a nonzero scalar in each coordinate and a unit of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ in all but finitely many coordinates ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)).

[F6] For subgroups $L,R$ of a group $H$, the double coset of $h$ is $LhR=\{u h v:u\in L,\ v\in R\}$ ([math:def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups)). The double cosets of $h$ and $h'$ are equal exactly when $h'=u h v$ for such $u,v$. Indeed, $h'$ belongs to its own double coset, and if $h'=u h v$ then $Lh'R=LhR$ because $Lu=L$ and $vR=R$.

[F7] A finite set admits a bijection from some natural number $n$ viewed as the set of its predecessors ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)). If $F$ is a function with domain $n$ whose values are all nonempty sets, its family of values has a function $g$ satisfying $g(S)\in S$ for every set $S$ in that family ([math:lem-finite-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-choice)).

### Proof

**Given:** An order $O$ in $B$ and the local orders, unit groups and norm conventions above.

**Goal:** Establish the ideal bijection, the local tests, and the two equivalent descriptions of the ideal classes.

1.1 For $x\in\widehat B^\times$, set $J_\ell=x_\ell O_\ell$. Take an order basis $b_1,\ldots,b_4$ as in [F1]. Given $w\in B_\ell$, expand $x_\ell^{-1}w$ in that basis over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and multiply the expansion by $x_\ell$ on the left. Bilinearity and associativity express $w$ in the vectors $x_\ell b_1,\ldots,x_\ell b_4$. Applying $x_\ell^{-1}$ on the left to a linear relation among these vectors gives the same relation among the $b_i$, so all coefficients vanish. The same bilinearity shows that $J_\ell$ is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of this basis. If $x_\ell\in O_\ell^\times$, then $x_\ell O_\ell=O_\ell$ by closure under multiplication by $x_\ell$ and by $x_\ell^{-1}$. Thus [F3] gives a full integral lattice $I_x$ with these completions. Each $J_\ell$ is stable on the right under $O_\ell$ because $O_\ell$ is a ring. For $b\in I_x$ and $o\in O$, this gives $bo\in J_\ell$ at every prime and hence $bo\in I_x$. So $I_x$ is a locally principal right fractional ideal. [F1, F2, F3, given]

2.1 For two ideles $x,y$, the equality $J_\ell=x_\ell O_\ell=y_\ell O_\ell$ holds exactly when $h=x_\ell^{-1}y_\ell$ satisfies $hO_\ell=O_\ell$. If the latter equality holds then $h=h\cdot1\in O_\ell$ and $1=hu$ for some $u\in O_\ell$, so $h^{-1}=u\in O_\ell$. Conversely an element of $O_\ell^\times$ acts bijectively on $O_\ell$. It follows from step 1.1 that $I_x=I_y$ exactly when all these local spans agree, using recovery by intersection for the reverse implication. This is equivalent to $x^{-1}y\in\widehat O^\times$, which is exactly equality of the cosets. [F1, F2, F3, step 1.1]

2.2 If $I_x\subseteq O$, taking completed spans gives $x_\ell O_\ell\subseteq O_\ell$ for every $\ell$. Conversely these inclusions put every element of $I_x$ in all $O_\ell$, whose rational intersection is $O$. For the norm claim [F4] gives $d\mathbb Z_\ell=N(x_\ell)\mathbb Z_\ell$. The ratio of the generators and its reciprocal are both in $\mathbb Z_\ell$, so the ratio is a unit and the generators have equal valuations. Factoring the positive numerator and denominator of $d$ gives $d=\prod_\ell \ell^{v_\ell(d)}$. This is a finite product of its nontrivial factors because the numerator and denominator are integers. [F3, F4, step 1.1]

2.3 For $a\in B^\times$, express $a$ and $a^{-1}$ in an order basis. The denominators of their finitely many rational coefficients have only finitely many prime divisors. At every other prime, each nonzero coefficient has nonnegative valuation because its denominator has valuation zero, so all the coefficients of both elements lie in $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Thus $a$ and its inverse lie in $O_\ell$ at those primes. The diagonal map preserves products and is injective by the coordinate inclusion $\mathbb Q\subseteq\mathbb Q_\ell$, so it identifies $B^\times$ with a subgroup of $\widehat B^\times$. For any $b\in B$, the membership $b\in a x_\ell O_\ell$ is equivalent to $a^{-1}b\in x_\ell O_\ell$. Intersecting the conditions over all primes yields $I_{ax}=aI_x$. [F1, F2, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 Let $I$ be a locally principal right fractional $O$-ideal. The set $E=\{\ell:I_\ell\ne O_\ell\}$ of exceptional primes is finite by [F3], so take a bijection $e:n\to E$ by [F7]. For each $i\in n$ put $S_i=\{u\in B_{e(i)}^\times:uO_{e(i)}=I_{e(i)}\}$. Each $S_i$ is nonempty by local principality. Apply [F7] to the function $i\mapsto S_i$ on $n$ to obtain a choice function $g$ on its family of values. Set $x_{e(i)}=g(S_i)$ for $i\in n$ and $x_\ell=1$ for $\ell\notin E$. Thus $x_\ell O_\ell=I_\ell$ at every prime, and $x$ is a finite quaternion idele since $1\in O_\ell^\times$ outside $E$. The intersection of these spans is $I$ by [F3]. This proves surjectivity and completes the bijection. [given, F2, F3, F7, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 By step 3.1, every locally principal right fractional ideal has the form $I_x$, and $aI_x=I_{ax}$ is again such an ideal by steps 2.3 and 1.1. For any such ideal $I$ and rational units $a,b$, the identity gives $1I=I$ and associativity gives $b(aI)=(ba)I$. Thus $I$ is equivalent to itself, $J=aI$ gives $I=a^{-1}J$, and $J=aI$ and $L=bJ$ give $L=(ba)I$. For ideles $x,y$, the ideals $I_x,I_y$ are equivalent exactly when $I_y=I_{ax}$ for some $a\in B^\times$, which by step 2.1 holds exactly when $y=axk$ for some $k\in\widehat O^\times$. By [F6] this is equality of double cosets. [F1, F2, F6, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 2.3]

5.1 Let $\pi:\widehat B^\times\to G(\mathbb A_f)$ be the quotient map. The rational projective group embeds in $G(\mathbb A_f)$ by [F5]. The image $K=\pi(\widehat O^\times)$ is a subgroup since $\pi$ preserves products, identities and inverses. If $y=a x k$ with $a\in B^\times$ and $k\in\widehat O^\times$, then $\pi(y)=\pi(a)\pi(x)\pi(k)$. By [F6] the projective double coset thus depends only on the original double coset. Every projective representative lifts to a quaternion idele by surjectivity of $\pi$, giving surjectivity on double cosets. [F2, F5, F6, step 2.3, step 4.1]

6.1 If $\pi(x)$ and $\pi(y)$ belong to the same projective double coset, [F6] gives $\pi(y)=\bar a\pi(x)\bar k$ with $\bar a\in G(\mathbb Q)$ and $\bar k\in K$. Choose lifts $a\in B^\times$ and $k\in\widehat O^\times$ of these multipliers. The quotient descriptions give $y=c a x k$ for a finite scalar idele $c=(c_\ell)_\ell$. Set $r=\prod_\ell\ell^{v_\ell(c_\ell)}\in\mathbb Q^\times$, which uses only finitely many nonzero exponents by [F5]. At a fixed prime $\ell$, any distinct prime $q$ is a rational integer with $v_\ell(q)=0$. Additivity on products and the rule $v_\ell(t^{-1})=-v_\ell(t)$ give $v_\ell(r)=v_\ell(c_\ell)$, using $v_\ell(\ell)=1$ for the factor at $\ell$. Hence $e_\ell=c_\ell/r\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$. Both $e_\ell\cdot 1$ and its inverse belong to $O_\ell$ because that ring contains the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of $1$. So $e=(e_\ell\cdot 1)_\ell\in\widehat O^\times$. Centrality gives $y=(ra)x(ek)$, which puts $x,y$ in the same unprojectivized double coset by [F6]. This proves the projective bijection. [F1, F2, F4, F5, F6, step 2.3, step 5.1] ∎

### Source notes

Voight gives the idele and ideal-class correspondences in Lemma 27.6.8 and equation (27.6.9). The proof here uses the lattice reconstruction and norm results in the stated facts and derives the projective correspondence from the scalar quotient. [Adelic Framework](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-030-56694-4_27)


## Projective integral stabilizer over the rationals

[Open item](item-lem-projective-integral-stabilizer-over-rationals) · level 4


### Statement

Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra, with basis $1,i,j,k$, identity $1$, and products determined by $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$, $ij=k=-ji$, $jk=i=-kj$, and $ki=j=-ik$. Let $O$ be an order in $B$, meaning a subring containing $1$ that is the integer span of a rational basis of $B$. For each prime $\ell$, a positive integer greater than $1$ whose only positive divisors are $1$ and itself, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the completion of $\mathbb Q$ for the $\ell$-adic absolute value $|\cdot|_\ell$, which assigns $\ell^{-m}$ to a nonzero rational with net exponent $m$ of $\ell$ in its prime factorization. Let $\mathbb Z_\ell$ be the subring of elements $t\in\mathbb Q_\ell$ with $|t|_\ell\le 1$. Write $B_\ell$ for the same quaternion algebra with coefficients in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $O_\ell$ for the span of $O$ over $\mathbb Z_\ell$ inside $B_\ell$. The superscript $\times$ denotes elements with a two-sided multiplicative inverse in the indicated ring.

Write $G(\mathbb Q)=B^\times/\mathbb Q^\times$ and $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$. These are projective groups: two units represent the same class if one is obtained from the other by multiplication by a nonzero scalar in the indicated field, and classes multiply by multiplying representatives. Let $K_\ell$ be the image of $O_\ell^\times$ in $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$. The finite adelic projective group $G(\mathbb A_f)$ is identified with the families in $\prod_\ell G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ whose coordinates lie in $K_\ell$ for all but finitely many $\ell$, with componentwise multiplication. View $G(\mathbb Q)$ inside this group by sending each rational class to its local classes.

If $[x]\in G(\mathbb Q)$ has an integral-unit representative at every prime, meaning its local class belongs to $K_\ell$ for every $\ell$, then there is a positive rational $r$ such that $rx\in O^\times$. Equivalently, inside $G(\mathbb A_f)$,
$$G(\mathbb Q)\cap\prod_\ell K_\ell=\operatorname{im}\bigl(O^\times\to G(\mathbb Q)\bigr).$$

### Statement references

[def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)

[def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)

[def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)

[math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic)

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] In an order basis, $O_\ell$ consists of the vectors with all coefficients in $\mathbb Z_\ell$. A scalar quaternion $a1$ with $a\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ belongs to $O_\ell^\times$ exactly when $a\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)).

[F2] The rational projective group embeds in the finite adelic projective group by its family of local classes, and the image of the product of integral unit groups is $\prod_\ell K_\ell$ ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)).

[F3] The normalized valuation $v_\ell(t)$ of a nonzero $t\in\mathbb Q_\ell$ is the integer determined by $|t|_\ell=\ell^{-v_\ell(t)}$. It satisfies $v_\ell(st)=v_\ell(s)+v_\ell(t)$ for nonzero factors, equals the net prime exponent on nonzero rationals, and vanishes exactly on $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ ([def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation)).

[F4] Every positive integer factors into finitely many primes, with the factorization unique up to order ([math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic)).

[F5] For any full integral lattice $L$ in $B$, meaning the integer span of a rational basis, its completed spans $L_\ell$ determine it by $L=\{y\in B:y\in L_\ell\text{ for every prime }\ell\}$ ([lem-author-f5ba73d33e6e-reconstructing-quaternion-lattices](item-lem-author-f5ba73d33e6e-reconstructing-quaternion-lattices)).

### Proof

**Given:** $O$ as above and $x\in B^\times$ whose local class is in $K_\ell$ for every prime $\ell$.

**Goal:** Construct a positive rational $r$ with $rx\in O^\times$ and prove the intersection formula.

1.1 At any prime $\ell$, local class membership gives a scalar $a\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ with $ax\in O_\ell^\times$. If $b\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ is another such scalar, then $(ax)(bx)^{-1}=(a/b)1$ lies in $O_\ell^\times$. Thus $a/b\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ and $v_\ell(a)=v_\ell(b)$. Write $n_\ell$ for this common integer. [given, F1, F2, F3]

2.1 Write $x$ and $x^{-1}$ in the rational basis of $B$ whose integer span is $O$. The positive denominators of these finitely many rational coefficients have only finitely many prime divisors. At any prime $\ell$ outside this finite set, each nonzero coefficient has nonnegative valuation because its denominator has exponent zero, and every zero coefficient lies in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ as well. Therefore $x$ and $x^{-1}$ both belong to $O_\ell$, so $x\in O_\ell^\times$. The scalar $1$ is then allowed in step 1.1 and gives $n_\ell=0$. [F1, F3, F4, step 1.1]

3.1 Let $S=\{\ell:n_\ell\ne0\}$ and put $r=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell^{n_\ell}$, with empty product $1$ and negative exponents giving reciprocal powers. This is a positive rational by finiteness of $S$. For every prime $\ell$ its net exponent satisfies $v_\ell(r)=n_\ell$, since a prime power at any other prime contributes zero. At a fixed $\ell$ take any scalar $a$ allowed in step 1.1. Then $v_\ell(r/a)=0$, so $(r/a)1\in O_\ell^\times$. Consequently $rx=(r/a)(ax)\in O_\ell^\times$. This holds for every prime. [F1, F3, F4, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 Since $rx$ is a unit in each $O_\ell$, both $rx$ and its rational inverse belong to every $O_\ell$. Apply the lattice intersection formula with $L=O$ to each of these rational elements to obtain $rx,(rx)^{-1}\in O$. Thus $rx\in O^\times$ and has rational projective class $[x]$. Conversely, a unit of $O$ and its inverse map into every $O_\ell$, since integer coefficients are also $\ell$-adic integers. Its local classes therefore lie in all $K_\ell$, and its rational class belongs to the intersection. This proves the formula. [F1, F2, F5, step 3.1] ∎


## Two spherical Hecke scalars determine the Satake pair

[Open item](item-lem-author-e2fc0f4318ab-spherical-trace-and-product) · level 4


### Statement

Assume countable choice, so every sequence of nonempty sets has a choice function. For a prime $\ell$, let $G=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ and $K=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$, the groups of invertible matrices over the $\ell$-adic field and its integer ring respectively. Give $G$ and $K$ the subspace topologies induced by their four entries in $\mathbb Q_\ell^4$ with its product of $\ell$-adic metric topologies. Take a unitary representation of $G$ acting by complex-linear isometries of a complex Hilbert space with norm-continuous orbits. Let $V$ be its space of smooth vectors, those fixed by an open subgroup. Suppose $V$ is irreducible and admissible, meaning it is nonzero with no proper nonzero invariant linear subspace and the vectors fixed by any compact open subgroup form a finite-dimensional space. Assume $V^K\ne0$.

Use the normalized Satake pair $\{u,v\}$, with multiplicity, characterized by the scalar $S(H)(u,v)$ on $V^K$ for each compactly supported, left and right $K$-invariant Hecke function $H$. The transform $S$ gives a coset $xK=\{xk:k\in K\}$ with triangular diagonal valuations $(r,s)$ weight $\ell^{(s-r)/2}X^rY^s$. Normalize the Hecke action by summing one term per coset $xK$. For $g\in G$, write $KgK=\{k_1gk_2:k_1,k_2\in K\}$. Let $I$ be the two-by-two identity matrix with ones on the diagonal and zeros off the diagonal. Write $\operatorname{diag}(a,d)=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\0&d\end{pmatrix}$ and set $D=\operatorname{diag}(\ell,1)$. For a subset $A\subseteq G$, its indicator $\mathbf 1_A:G\to\mathbb C$ has value $1$ on $A$ and $0$ elsewhere. Let $\mathcal T_\ell$ and $\mathcal R_\ell$ be the Hecke operators of $\mathbf 1_{KDK}$ and $\mathbf 1_{(\ell I)K}$, respectively. Then their respective scalars are $\sqrt\ell(u+v)$ and $uv$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The spherical-character theorem supplies the one-dimensional $K$-fixed space and its normalized character under these hypotheses. The triangular coset coordinates, the transform and the coset action use the conventions in the statement ([thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character](item-thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character), [lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets), [def-author-d45da46285be-satake-transform](item-def-author-d45da46285be-satake-transform), [def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F2] The integral matrices of determinant valuation one have one triangular coset representative $\operatorname{diag}(1,\ell)$ and $\ell$ representatives $\begin{pmatrix}\ell&b\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ with $0\le b<\ell$. This is the $e=1$ case of the index-coset lemma ([lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets](item-lem-degree-two-spherical-index-cosets)).
