Choose such an isomorphism. It identifies $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ with $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)/N_\ell$, the quotient by the central subgroup $N_\ell=\{aI_2:a\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times\}$. Here $\mathrm{GL}_2(R)$ denotes the matrices with a two-sided inverse over the commutative ring $R$, and the projective quotient identifies matrices differing by a nonzero scalar. Let $J_\ell\subseteq G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ correspond to the image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$, where $\mathbb Z_\ell=\{a\in\mathbb Q_\ell:|a|_\ell\le1\}$.

Give $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ the product topology of its four entries. Give $\mathbb Z_\ell$ the subspace topology from $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and give $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ and both general linear groups the subspace topologies from $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$. Give $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ the quotient topology of the scalar projection.

A strongly continuous unitary representation $\pi_\ell$ of the topological group $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ on a complex Hilbert space $H_\ell$ is unramified if there is a nonzero vector $v$ with $\pi_\ell(j)v=v$ for every $j\in J_\ell$. The same test applies to a strongly continuous unitary representation $\pi$ of the full adelic topological group $G(\mathbb A)$ on a complex Hilbert space $H$, by restricting to the copy of $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ supported at $\ell$, with all other components equal to the identity.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The rational Hamilton algebra $B$, a split prime $\ell$, a chosen matrix isomorphism, and a strongly continuous unitary representation of either $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ or $G(\mathbb A)$ on a complex Hilbert space.

[A1] The Axiom of Choice asserts the existence of a choice function for each indexed family of nonempty sets. Under this assumption the space of compatible residue tuples modulo $\ell^n$ is compact for its residue metric ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-integers-as-compatible-residue-classes), [math:def-p-adic-metric-on-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-metric-on-zp), [math:thm-zp-is-compact-hausdorff-totally-disconnected-and-complete](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-zp-is-compact-hausdorff-totally-disconnected-and-complete)).

[F1] The local projective units form a topological group in the scalar quotient topology. The map from quaternion units to their projective classes is open. The finite adelic projective group has the restricted-product topology, with open local factors at all primes and the prescribed integral subgroups outside finitely many primes ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units), Justification, steps 4.1 and 5.1). Invertible quaternions over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ have the topology of their four coefficients, and the absolute value defining $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is multiplicative and ultrametric ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). The quotient group law multiplies representatives. If the subgroup is normal, this law is well defined and gives a group ([math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group), [math:thm-quotient-group-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-group-laws)). A subgroup $N$ of a group $G$ is normal if $XNX^{-1}=N$ for every $X\in G$ ([math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup)).

[F2] Over a commutative ring a square matrix of positive size is invertible exactly when its determinant is a unit. Finite products of compact spaces and continuous images of compact spaces are compact. A closed subspace of a compact space is compact ([math:cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-square-matrix-invertible-iff-determinant-is-a-unit), [math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), [math:thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact), claim 1). The two terms of the determinant formula in size two give $\det X=x_{11}x_{22}-x_{12}x_{21}$ for $X=(x_{ij})$. [math:def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-determinant-of-a-square-matrix)

[F3] For a topological group $S$ and a complex Hilbert space $H$, a strongly continuous unitary representation is a homomorphism into the group of bijective complex-linear isometries of $H$ for which each orbit map $s\mapsto\pi(s)v$ is norm-continuous ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group), [math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space), [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)).

[F4] The full adelic group $G(\mathbb A)=G(\mathbb R)\times G(\mathbb A_f)$ has componentwise multiplication and the product topology, making it a topological group ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), Justification, step 3.1).

[F5] The canonical identification of the compatible residue tuples with the unit ball of $\mathbb Q_\ell$ takes agreement modulo $\ell^n$ to the distance condition $|a-b|_\ell\le\ell^{-n}$ on their images $a,b$. Agreement at level $n$ gives an inverse-limit neighborhood basis and corresponds under this identification to a subspace neighborhood basis on the unit ball ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles), Justification, step 1.3). The inverse-limit topology is the subspace topology from the product of the finite discrete residue groups. [math:def-inverse-limit-topology-for-finite-discrete-groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-inverse-limit-topology-for-finite-discrete-groups) The inverse-limit and residue-metric topologies on the compatible residue tuples coincide ([math:thm-inverse-limit-topology-equals-the-p-adic-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-inverse-limit-topology-equals-the-p-adic-metric-topology)).

[F6] An algebra over a commutative field has a unital central scalar map, and an algebra isomorphism preserves it and is linear over the field ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)). Every nonzero element of a field has a multiplicative inverse. [math:def-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-field) Multiplication in a commutative ring is commutative. [math:def-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-commutative-ring) A unital ring isomorphism is a bijection preserving addition, multiplication and the multiplicative identity. [math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism) A matrix over a commutative ring is a rectangular array of entries in the ring, and each column of a matrix with two rows can be written as a two-by-one matrix ([math:def-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-matrices-over-a-commutative-ring)). Matrix addition and scalar multiplication are entrywise. Matrix multiplication is row-by-column with identity $I_2$, and it is associative and distributive ([math:def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-matrix-product-identity-and-transpose), [math:thm-ring-matrix-arithmetic-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-ring-matrix-arithmetic-laws)). For a commutative ring $R$, $\mathrm{GL}_2(R)$ is the group of matrices with a two-sided inverse over $R$ ([math:def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-invertible-matrix-and-similarity-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F7] In any topology, the empty set, finite intersections of open sets, and arbitrary unions of open sets are open. [math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space) Finite product topologies have open boxes as a basis ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)). A subspace has the traces of ambient open sets as its open sets ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). A set in a quotient topology is open exactly when its preimage under the projection is open ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)).

[F8] Addition and multiplication in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ are continuous for its metric topology ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification, step 5.1).

[F9] A map is continuous exactly when preimages of open sets are open, or equivalently when preimages of closed sets are closed ([math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), conditions (a)–(c)). A homeomorphism is a continuous bijection with continuous inverse, and an embedding is an injection that is a homeomorphism onto its image with the subspace topology ([math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps)).

[F10] A compact subset is compact in its subspace topology ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). A subgroup contains the identity and is closed under multiplication and inversion ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F11] A group homomorphism preserves products, the identity and inverses. Composites of group homomorphisms are group homomorphisms. [math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism) A group isomorphism is a bijective group homomorphism. [math:def-group-isomorphism-and-automorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-isomorphism-and-automorphism)

[F12] For each prime $\ell$, the local integer ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and its unit group $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times=\{a\in\mathbb Q_\ell:|a|_\ell=1\}$ are open and closed in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification, step 2.1).

### Justification

**Goal:** Show that $J_\ell$ is compact open and that the test does not depend on the chosen splitting.

1.1 The isomorphism $\phi$ and its inverse are linear by [F6], so each output entry or Hamilton coefficient is a finite sum of scalar multiples of input coordinates. These sums are continuous by [F8], and the preimage of an open box is an intersection of four open coordinate preimages and is open by [F7]. Every open set is a union of basic boxes, so its preimage is a union of these open preimages and is open by [F7]. Thus $\phi$ and its inverse are continuous for the coefficient topologies and restrict to continuous maps on the subspaces of units. The set $N_\ell$ of nonzero scalar matrices contains $I_2$ and is closed under multiplication and inversion since $(aI_2)(bI_2)=(ab)I_2$ and $(aI_2)^{-1}=a^{-1}I_2$. It is a subgroup by [F10]. The scalar map in [F6] is central, so $X(aI_2)X^{-1}=aI_2$ for each invertible $X$. Thus $XN_\ell X^{-1}=N_\ell$, and the subgroup is normal by [F1]. By [F1] the cosets form a group under multiplication of representatives. In particular $q_M(XY)=q_M(X)q_M(Y)$ for the matrix scalar projection $q_M$. The isomorphism preserves units and takes $a1$ to $aI_2$, so it gives a bijection of scalar cosets, with inverse induced by $\phi^{-1}$. Because both coset laws multiply representatives, this bijection is a group isomorphism $\bar\phi$ by [F11]. For the quaternion scalar projection $q_B$, we have $q_M\circ\phi=\bar\phi\circ q_B$ on units. The left side is continuous, so the quotient open-set criterion for $q_B$ gives continuity of $\bar\phi$. Applying the same argument to $\phi^{-1}$ gives continuity of $\bar\phi^{-1}$. Thus $\bar\phi$ is a homeomorphism by [F9]. [F1, F6, F7, F8, F9, F10, F11]

2.1 Two splittings differ by a $\mathbb Q_\ell$-algebra automorphism of $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$. For such an automorphism let $A_{ij}$ be the image of the matrix unit $E_{ij}$, which has a single $1$ in row $i$, column $j$ and zeros elsewhere. Row-by-column multiplication gives $E_{ij}E_{rs}=\delta_{jr}E_{is}$, where $\delta_{jr}$ is $1$ when $j=r$ and $0$ otherwise. Multiplicativity and linearity give $A_{ij}A_{rs}=\delta_{jr}A_{is}$. Write column vectors as two-by-one matrices. Since $A_{11}\ne0$, choose a column containing a nonzero entry. For the vector $v$ with $1$ at that column's index and $0$ at the other index, $v_1=A_{11}v$ is that nonzero column. Set $v_2=A_{21}v_1$. Then $A_{11}v_1=v_1$, $A_{11}v_2=0$, and $A_{12}v_2=v_1$. If $\lambda v_1+\mu v_2=0$ for scalars $\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb Q_\ell$, applying $A_{11}$ gives $\lambda v_1=0$. Since $v_1\ne0$ this gives $\lambda=0$, and $A_{12}v_2=v_1\ne0$ gives $v_2\ne0$, so $\mu=0$. Let $T=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\ c&d\end{pmatrix}$ have columns $v_1,v_2$. If $\det T=ad-bc=0$ and $a\ne0$, then $d=bc/a$ and $v_2=(b/a)v_1$. If $\det T=0$ and $a=0$, then $c\ne0$ because $v_1\ne0$, so $b=0$ and $v_2=(d/c)v_1$. Either proportionality gives a zero relation with coefficient $1$ on $v_2$, which is impossible by the preceding calculation. The nonzero determinant is a unit in $\mathbb Q_\ell$, so $T$ is invertible by [F2]. The matrix-unit relations give $A_{ij}v_r=\delta_{jr}v_i$, so the $r$th columns of $A_{ij}T$ and $TE_{ij}$ agree for $r=1,2$. Thus $A_{ij}T=TE_{ij}$ and $A_{ij}=TE_{ij}T^{-1}$. By entrywise operations, every matrix $X=(x_{ij})$ equals $\sum_{i,j=1}^2 x_{ij}E_{ij}$, since entry $(i,j)$ of the sum is $x_{ij}$. The automorphism and conjugation by $T$ are linear, so their agreement on the four matrix units gives agreement on every $X$. Consequently the subgroups $J_\ell$ obtained from any two splittings are conjugate in the quaternion projective group. [F1, F2, F6, step 1.1]

2.2 The common inverse-limit neighborhood bases in [F5] make the canonical identification from the residue-metric space to the local unit ball continuous in both directions. It is a homeomorphism by [F9]. The domain is compact by [A1], so the continuous image rule in [F2] gives compactness of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ in its subspace topology. By [F12], both the integral ball $\mathbb Z_\ell$ and the unit set $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ are open and closed in $\mathbb Q_\ell$. The size-two determinant from [F2] is continuous by [F8] as a sum of products of entry projections and constants. By the determinant unit test, $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ is the set of matrices in $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ whose determinants have absolute value $1$. The four open entry conditions give an open box in $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$. The determinant condition is open by [F9], so its intersection with this box is open by [F7]. Its trace in $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ is $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. The determinant condition is also closed by [F9], and its trace in $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ is $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. The subspace topology on $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ is the product of the four unit-ball topologies because tracing an open box to the four integral entries traces each factor separately. The integral matrix space is compact by finite-product compactness, so the closed subspace $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ is compact. The inclusion of coefficient rings makes $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ a subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ because the identity, products and inverses over $\mathbb Z_\ell$ are the same over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. The pullback under $\phi$ and its image under $q_B$ contain the identity and are closed under products and inverses by the group homomorphism laws in [F11]. Thus both are subgroups by [F10]. The pullback is open by continuity of $\phi$ and compact by continuity of $\phi^{-1}$ on the unit subspaces, using step 1.1 and [F2]. The projective image $J_\ell$ is compact by continuity of $q_B$ and open because $q_B$ is open. [A1, F1, F2, F5, F6, F7, F8, F9, F10, F11, F12, step 1.1]

3.1 For a subgroup $J$ and a vector fixed by $J$, the vector $\pi_\ell(t)v$ is fixed by $tJt^{-1}$ because $\pi_\ell(tjt^{-1})\pi_\ell(t)v=\pi_\ell(t)\pi_\ell(j)v=\pi_\ell(t)v$. It is nonzero when $v$ is nonzero, and applying the same equality with $t^{-1}$ proves the converse. To embed $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ in the full adelic group, insert the local element at $\ell$ and use the identity at every other place, including the real place. This defines an injective homomorphism because the integral subgroups contain the identity and multiplication is componentwise. In the full product topology a basic open set is a real open factor times a finite adelic box. Its preimage is empty if some fixed coordinate is excluded, and is otherwise its open local factor at $\ell$. The inverse on the image is the local coordinate projection. For an open local set, its preimage under this inverse is the trace on the image of the cylinder imposing that open condition only at $\ell$. For each point of the cylinder, intersect the factor at $\ell$ of a basic neighborhood with the prescribed open set. This factor is open by [F7]. The resulting basic box is still allowed in the restricted product because only one factor was changed. It lies inside the cylinder, which is therefore a union of open boxes and is open by [F7]. Thus the injection is an embedding by [F7] and [F9]. For a full adelic representation $\pi$ on $H$, let $\iota_\ell$ be this embedding and set $\pi_\ell=\pi\circ\iota_\ell$. By [F11], this is a homomorphism into the same group of bijective complex-linear isometries of $H$. For each $v\in H$ write $o_v(h)=\pi(h)v$. For a norm-open set $W\subseteq H$, its preimage under the restricted orbit map $o_v\circ\iota_\ell$ is $\iota_\ell^{-1}(o_v^{-1}(W))$. The inner preimage is open by strong continuity of $\pi$, and the outer preimage is open by continuity of $\iota_\ell$, using [F9]. Thus $\pi_\ell$ is strongly continuous and unitary on the same complex Hilbert space. The conjugacy in step 2.1 and the fixed-vector argument therefore apply to this restriction as well. [F1, F3, F4, F7, F9, F11, step 2.1] ∎


## Compactness of the projective Hamilton quotient

[Open item](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-compact-projective-hamilton-quotient) · level 5


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra and $G=PB^\times$ its group of units modulo nonzero scalars. At a prime $\ell$, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the $\ell$-adic field and $\mathbb Z_\ell$ its ring of integers. The finite adele ring $\mathbb A_f$ consists of tuples in $\prod_\ell\mathbb Q_\ell$ with entries in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes, with coordinatewise operations. For the full adelic group $H=G(\mathbb R)\times G(\mathbb A_f)$ and diagonal subgroup $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)$, the coset space $X=\Gamma\backslash H$ with its quotient topology is compact. A coset is the set $\Gamma h=\{\gamma h:\gamma\in\Gamma\}$ and the quotient topology detects open sets by their preimages in $H$. Compactness means every open cover has a finite subcover.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice gives a choice function for a family of nonempty sets. Use it to select representatives of the projective double cosets, finite trace subcovers, and original cover members realizing the selected traces ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)).

[F1] The map $a\mapsto a1$ gives $B$ its central rational scalar structure with basis $1,i,j,k$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification 1.1). The Hamilton product is bilinear and its basis $1,i,j,k$ satisfies $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$ and $ij=k=-ji$, with the other products obtained by cyclic permutation. The reduced norm is $N(x)=x_0^2+x_1^2+x_2^2+x_3^2$, and $x\bar x=\bar x x=N(x)1$, where $\bar x$ negates the imaginary coordinates. An order $O$ is a unital subring with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. Its completion $O_\ell$ is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of that basis in $B_\ell$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). A division ring is a unital ring with $1\ne0$ in which every nonzero element has a two-sided inverse ([math:def-division-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-division-ring)).

[F2] The full adelic group $H$ is a topological group with the product topology, $G(\mathbb R)$ is compact, and the two group operations of multiplication and inversion act componentwise. The finite component of the diagonal embedding is the injective rational map into $G(\mathbb A_f)$ ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), Definition and Justification 3.1 and 4.1).

[F3] A finite scalar idele belongs to $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ at every prime and to $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many primes. These tuples form $\mathbb A_f^\times$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)). A rational number $a/b$ with $a,b\in\mathbb Z$ and $b\ne0$ belongs to $\mathbb Z_\ell$ whenever $\ell$ does not divide $b$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification 2.2). Each nonzero integer has finitely many prime divisors, and a union of two finite sets is finite ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification 2.2 and 1.1). The finite quaternion ideles $\widehat B^\times$ are defined by the local groups $B_\ell^\times$ and the integral unit groups $O_\ell^\times$, and $\widehat O^\times=\prod_\ell O_\ell^\times$ is compact open ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles)). The quotient $G(\mathbb A_f)=\widehat B^\times/\mathbb A_f^\times$ by scalar ideles identifies topologically with the restricted product of the local projective groups $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ using the open subgroups $K_\ell$ given by the images of $O_\ell^\times$. Its tuples lie in $K_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes, and a basic open box uses open local factors with $K_\ell$ at all but finitely many primes. The projective image $K_f=\prod_\ell K_\ell$ of $\widehat O^\times$ is compact open ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)).

[F4] The projective ideal dictionary identifies $G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A_f)/K_f$ with the locally principal right fractional ideal classes of that same order $O$. A locally principal right fractional ideal is a full integral lattice stable under right multiplication by $O$ whose completion at each $\ell$ is $x_\ell O_\ell$ for some invertible local quaternion $x_\ell$. Classes are taken up to left multiplication by $B^\times$ ([thm-adelic-right-ideal-dictionary](item-thm-adelic-right-ideal-dictionary)). A full integral lattice $I$ is the $\mathbb Z$-span of a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. Its completion $I_\ell$ is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of that basis in $B_\ell$ ([def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-quaternion-orders-and-lattice-completions)). Write $\Gamma_f$ for the image of $\Gamma$ under projection to $G(\mathbb A_f)$. By [F2], it is the embedded subgroup $G(\mathbb Q)$ in the dictionary. For $g\in G(\mathbb A_f)$, the double coset $\Gamma_f g K_f$ is the set of products $u g k$ with $u\in\Gamma_f$ and $k\in K_f$ ([math:def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups)).

[F5] A finite product of compact spaces is compact in its product topology, and a continuous map sends a compact subset to a compact subset of its target ([math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), [math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). A finite subfamily is empty or is the set of entries of a finite list, with repetitions allowed ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). The product topology on a binary product has a basis of open rectangles ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)).

[F6] Left translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion)). The coset projection $q(h)=\Gamma h$ satisfies $q(\gamma h)=q(h)$ for $\gamma\in\Gamma$ and is continuous for the quotient topology ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset), [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)). Subspace opens are intersections with ambient open sets ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)).

[F7] The class set of an order in a finite-dimensional division algebra over a global field is finite under the base-ring hypotheses in External input. For a number field, the ring of $S$-integers with $S$ equal to the set of all archimedean places is its ring of integers.

[F8] The ring of integers of a number field is the integral closure of $\mathbb Z$ in that field, namely the elements integral over $\mathbb Z$ ([math:def-ring-of-integers-of-a-number-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-of-integers-of-a-number-field), [math:def-integral-closure-and-integrally-closed-domain](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-closure-and-integrally-closed-domain)). A complex number is an algebraic integer exactly when it is integral over $\mathbb Z$, that is, a root of a monic polynomial with integer coefficients ([math:def-integral-element-and-algebraic-integer](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-element-and-algebraic-integer)). A rational number is an algebraic integer if and only if it is an integer ([math:cor-rational-algebraic-integers-are-integers](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-rational-algebraic-integers-are-integers)).

[F9] A finite extension of $\mathbb Q$ is a number field and a global field. The sole archimedean place of $\mathbb Q$ is its real place, induced by the usual absolute value.

[F10] A finite set is in bijection with some initial segment $\{0,\ldots,s-1\}$ of the natural numbers ([math:def-countable](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-countable)).

[F11] The open sets of a topology with a given basis are precisely the unions of its basic open sets ([math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)).

[F12] Partial sums of a finite list of natural numbers are natural numbers, with the empty sum zero and each next sum obtained by adding the next entry ([math:def-nat-finite-sum-and-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nat-finite-sum-and-product)).

[F13] The rationals form a totally ordered field ([math:thm-rat-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-rat-ordered-field)). In an ordered field, the sum of two positive elements is positive ([math:def-ordered-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ordered-field)). The square of each nonzero element of an ordered field is positive ([math:lem-of-square-positive](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-square-positive)).

[F14] Use the integer embedding $n\mapsto [(n,1)]$ into $\mathbb Q$ ([math:lem-int-embeds-rat](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-int-embeds-rat)). The rational embedding into $\mathbb R$ and the real embedding into $\mathbb C$ are unital field embeddings ([math:lem-rat-embeds-dense](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-rat-embeds-dense), [math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field)). Evaluation of a polynomial in a commutative target ring is the finite sum of its mapped coefficients times the corresponding powers of the argument ([math:def-polynomial-evaluation-and-root](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-polynomial-evaluation-and-root)).

[F15] For natural numbers, $a\le b$ means $a+j=b$ for some natural $j$, and $a<b$ means $a\le b$ with $a\ne b$ ([math:def-nat-order](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nat-order)). This is a linear order ([math:thm-nat-linear-order](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-nat-linear-order)). Addition has identity $0$ ([math:lem-nat-add-identity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-add-identity)). Addition is commutative ([math:lem-nat-add-commutative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-add-commutative)). Adding the same natural number on the right preserves and reflects both $\le$ and $<$ ([math:lem-nat-order-add-compatible](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nat-order-add-compatible)).

[F16] A property holding at $0$ and passing from each natural number to its successor holds for every natural number ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

### External input

Voight’s Corollary 27.6.20 gives finiteness of the locally principal right fractional ideal classes of an $R$-order in a finite-dimensional division algebra over a global field $F$, where $R$ is the ring of $S$-integers for a finite nonempty set $S$ containing all archimedean places. The equivalence is by left multiplication by $B^\times$ as in Lemma 27.6.8. For a number field $F$, if $S$ consists of all archimedean places, this $R$ is the ring of integers of $F$.

The opening of Voight’s §14.4 defines a finite extension of $\mathbb Q$ as a number field and a global field. His §14.4.8 identifies the real place induced by the usual absolute value as the only archimedean place of $\mathbb Q$.

### Proof

**Given:** The groups $H$ and $\Gamma$ in the statement, the quotient topology on $X$, and [A1].

**Goal:** Cover $X$ by finitely many compact subsets.

1.1 Put $O=\mathbb Z1+\mathbb Zi+\mathbb Zj+\mathbb Zk$. This span contains $1$ and is closed under subtraction. Each product of basis elements is a signed basis element, so bilinearity gives closure under multiplication. The generators form a $\mathbb Z$-basis of $O$ by their $\mathbb Q$-linear independence and form a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. Thus $O$ is a full order. At least one coordinate of a nonzero $x$ is nonzero by the basis in [F1]. By [F13], each nonzero coordinate has positive square and every zero coordinate contributes zero. Adding the positive terms gives $N(x)>0$ by closure under addition. The scalar $N(x)$ therefore has a rational inverse, and the norm identity in [F1] gives the two-sided inverse $\bar x/N(x)$ of $x$. The basis contains the identity $1$, so $1\ne0$ in $B$. Thus $B$ is a division ring by [F1]. Together with the central action $a\mapsto a1$ and the four-element rational basis from [F1], this makes $B$ a four-dimensional division algebra over $\mathbb Q$. [F1, F13]

2.1 Use this order for $K_f$ in [F3]. For any other full order $O'$, write $b_0,\ldots,b_3$ for the basis of $O$ and $c_0,\ldots,c_3$ for the basis of $O'$. For each $r=0,\ldots,3$, expand $b_r=\sum_{s=0}^3 a_{sr}c_s$ and $c_r=\sum_{s=0}^3 d_{sr}b_s$ with rational coefficients. Choose a nonzero integer denominator for each coefficient, using denominator $1$ for zero. Each denominator has a finite prime-divisor set by [F3]. Apply the two-set union rule from [F3] successively to the finite list of these sets to obtain a finite set of exceptional primes. Every coefficient belongs to $\mathbb Z_\ell$ outside that set by [F3]. At each prime outside that set, the expansion of every $b_r$ in the $c_s$ puts $b_r$ in $O'_\ell$, so $O_\ell\subseteq O'_\ell$. The expansions of the $c_r$ in the $b_s$ give $O'_\ell\subseteq O_\ell$ by the same span description in [F1]. Thus the completed orders, their unit groups and their projective images agree outside the finite set. The restricted products have the same tuples. Every basic box for either choice is a basic box for the other, since its local factors are open and the finite set of exceptions may include all primes where the integral factors differ. By [F11], both topologies consist of the unions of these same boxes, so the identity on tuples identifies the two topologies. The diagonal rational map is coordinatewise in both presentations, so this identification preserves $\Gamma$ in $H$. [F1, F2, F3, F11, step 1.1]

3.1 Take $F=\mathbb Q$, a degree-one extension of itself and hence a number field and a global field by [F9]. The set $S$ consisting of its real place is finite and nonempty and contains all archimedean places by [F9]. By [F7], $R$ is the ring of integers of $\mathbb Q$. By [F8], its elements are the rational numbers satisfying a monic polynomial over $\mathbb Z$. Let $\iota\colon\mathbb Q\to\mathbb C$ be the composite of the two field embeddings in [F14]. For each $f\in\mathbb Z[T]$ and $x\in\mathbb Q$, we have $\iota(f(x))=f(\iota(x))$: the unital map $\iota$ takes the image of each integer coefficient to the same integer multiple of $1$ in $\mathbb C$ and preserves the powers, products and finite sums in the evaluation formula in [F14]. By injectivity, $f(x)=0$ if and only if $f(\iota(x))=0$. With $f$ monic this identifies the integral rationals with the rational algebraic integers, which are precisely the integers by [F8]. Hence $R=\mathbb Z$. The full order and division algebra from step 1.1 then meet the hypotheses of [F7]. The ideal dictionary for that order then gives finitely many double cosets for the embedded subgroup $\Gamma_f=G(\mathbb Q)$ in [F4]. By [F10], index these double cosets by $0,\ldots,s-1$. Each double coset is nonempty by its product description in [F4], so use [A1] to choose a representative $g_i\in G(\mathbb A_f)$ for each index. By the membership rule in [F4], each finite adelic element belongs to some $\Gamma_f g_i K_f$ and so is $\gamma_f g_i k_f$ for some $\gamma_f\in\Gamma_f$ and $k_f\in K_f$. By the definition of $\Gamma_f$, some $\gamma\in\Gamma$ has finite component $\gamma_f$. [A1, F2, F4, F7, F8, F9, F10, F14, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 Put $K_0=G(\mathbb R)\times K_f$. Both factors are compact, so $K_0$ is compact for their product topology. An ambient basic rectangle $U\times V$ has trace $U\times(V\cap K_f)$ on $K_0$. By [F6], every basic open rectangle in $K_0$ is such a trace. By [F5] and [F11], every ambient open is a union of basic rectangles, so its trace is a union of the traced rectangles and therefore open in the product topology on $K_0$. Conversely every product open in $K_0$ is a union of traced rectangles, each of which is subspace open by [F6]. Thus the product and subspace topologies agree. For $h=(h_\infty,\gamma_f g_i k_f)$ as in step 3.1, take the lift $\gamma=(\gamma_\infty,\gamma_f)$. The componentwise laws in [F2] give $\gamma^{-1}h=(\gamma_\infty^{-1}h_\infty,\gamma_f^{-1}(\gamma_f g_i k_f))$. In the finite factor, associativity and the inverse law reduce $\gamma_f^{-1}(\gamma_f g_i k_f)$ to $g_i k_f$. Hence $\gamma^{-1}h=(1,g_i)(\gamma_\infty^{-1}h_\infty,k_f)\in(1,g_i)K_0$. Left translation by $(1,g_i)$ is continuous, so $(1,g_i)K_0$ is a compact subset of $H$. The continuous projection $q$ sends this translate to a compact subset $C_i$ of $X$. Since $q(\gamma^{-1}h)=q(h)$, the sets $C_i$ cover $X$. [F2, F3, F5, F6, F11, step 3.1]

5.1 If $\mathcal U$ is an open cover of $X$, the traces $U\cap C_i$ with $U\in\mathcal U$ are open in $C_i$ and cover $C_i$. By compactness and [F5], choose for each $i$ a finite list of traces covering $C_i$, using the empty list if $C_i$ is empty. For each listed trace choose a member of $\mathcal U$ giving that trace, so the chosen members form a list covering $C_i$. [A1, F5, F6, step 4.1]

6.1 For each $0\le i<s$, write the list from step 5.1 as $(U_{i,j})_{0\le j<m_i}$. Set $M_i=\sum_{r<i}m_r$ for $0\le i\le s$. By [F12], $M_0=0$ and $M_{i+1}=M_i+m_i$ for $i<s$. For $i<s$ let $I_i=\{k\in\mathbb N:M_i\le k<M_{i+1}\}$. The order definition in [F15] gives $M_i\le M_{i+1}$ since $M_{i+1}=M_i+m_i$. By linearity of this order, $\{k\in\mathbb N:k<M_{i+1}\}$ is the disjoint union of $\{k\in\mathbb N:k<M_i\}$ and $I_i$. The first initial segment, for $M_0=0$, is empty since $0+k=k$ gives $0\le k$ for every natural $k$. By finite induction using [F16], the intervals $I_i$ for $i<s$ partition $\{k\in\mathbb N:k<M_s\}$, allowing empty intervals. [F12, F15, F16, step 3.1, step 5.1]

7.1 Fix $i<s$. The map $j\mapsto M_i+j$ takes $\{j\in\mathbb N:j<m_i\}$ bijectively onto $I_i$. Indeed $M_i\le M_i+j$ by the definition of order, and $M_i+j<M_{i+1}=M_i+m_i$ is equivalent to $j<m_i$ by commutativity and order compatibility in [F15]. For every $k\in I_i$, the inequality $M_i\le k$ gives a natural $j$ with $M_i+j=k$, and then $j<m_i$ by the equivalence. The index $j$ is unique because strict order of two different natural numbers would give strict order of their translates. [F15, step 6.1]

8.1 Place $U_{i,j}$ at position $M_i+j$. By the partition and bijections this gives one list of length $M_s$ containing every chosen member. Each point of $X$ lies in some $C_i$ and hence in a cover member on its list. The set of entries of the concatenated list is therefore a finite subcover of $\mathcal U$ by [F5]. Thus $X$ is compact. [F5, step 4.1, step 5.1, step 6.1, step 7.1] ∎


## Fixed spaces of projective quaternion automorphic representations

[Open item](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space) · level 6


### Definition

Assume the Axiom of Choice. For the rational Hamilton algebra $B$, fix a full order $O\subset B$, a subring containing $1$ with a chosen $\mathbb Z$-basis that is also a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. Put $G=PB^\times$, $H=G(\mathbb A)=G(\mathbb R)\times G(\mathbb A_f)$, and $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)$ embedded diagonally in $H$. Use $O$ for the restricted-product construction of $G(\mathbb A_f)$. Give $H$ the product topology. Equip the space of cosets $X=\Gamma\backslash H$ with its quotient topology and choose a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on its Borel sigma-algebra. Write $\mathcal H=L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$ for the space of measurable complex functions of finite square integral, identifying two functions if they agree outside a Borel set of $\mu$-measure zero. Complex measurability means that the real and imaginary parts are both measurable for the Borel sigma-algebra of the real line. The inner product is $\langle f,g\rangle=\int_X f\overline g\,d\mu$.

The right regular representation on $\mathcal H$ is $R(h)[f]=[x\mapsto f(xh)]$. An irreducible automorphic representation here is a unitary representation equivalent to the restriction of $R$ to a nonzero invariant complex-linear subspace $M$ that is closed in the Hilbert norm and has no proper nonzero invariant complex-linear subspace closed in that norm. Invariance means $R(h)M=M$ for every $h\in H$. Unitary equivalence means a bijective complex-linear isometry that intertwines the two actions. At a split finite prime $\ell$, the unramified condition is the existence of a nonzero vector fixed by the projective image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ under the split identification. For a local representation use this subgroup of $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, and for a full adelic representation use its copy supported at $\ell$.

For a compact open subgroup $U\le G(\mathbb A_f)$ put $K=G(\mathbb R)\times U$ with the topology inherited from $H$. The fixed space at level $U$ is $\mathcal H^K=\{v:R(k)v=v\text{ for every }k\in K\}$. Let $D=G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A_f)/U$, the set of double cosets $\Gamma g_f U=\{\gamma_f g_f u:\gamma\in\Gamma,\ u\in U\}$, where $\gamma_f$ is the finite component. Give $D$ the discrete topology, in which every subset is open, and its Borel sigma-algebra is therefore the power set $\mathcal P(D)$. Define $q:X\to D$ by $q(\Gamma(g_\infty,g_f))=\Gamma g_f U$. The measure $\nu$ on $\mathcal P(D)$ is given by $\nu(S)=\mu(q^{-1}(S))$ for $S\subseteq D$. The isometric identification of $L^2(D,\nu;\mathbb C)$ with $\mathcal H^K$ sends a function $a$ to the class of $a\circ q$. When $D$ is finite, every complex function on $D$ is allowed and its inner product with $b$ is $\sum_{d\in D}\nu(\{d\})a(d)\overline{b(d)}$.

### Facts & Assumptions

**Given:** The full order and basis used for the finite adelic construction, the full adelic and rational projective unit groups, a nonzero invariant quotient measure $\mu$, a compact open subgroup $U$ of the finite adelic group, and the Axiom of Choice.

[A1] The Axiom of Choice gives a choice function for each indexed family of nonempty sets and implies Countable Choice, the corresponding principle for countable indexed families ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)). These assumptions supply compact Haar measure, the quoted density and completeness results, and the commuting integrations used below.

[F1] The integer span of $1,i,j,k$ is one full order. The Hamilton multiplication table sends products of basis elements to signed basis elements, so the integer span contains $1$ and is closed under subtraction and multiplication, with the Hamilton basis also a rational basis of $B$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). The full group $H$ is a locally compact Hausdorff topological group and $G(\mathbb R)$ is compact Hausdorff ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), Justification, steps 2.1–2.2 and 3.1). Local compactness gives a compact neighborhood of the identity ([math:def-locally-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space)). The finite group and its local factors have the projective and restricted product descriptions ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)). Use the same $O$ and its chosen basis for the finite restricted product, the construction of $H$, and the invariant-quotient lemma. The quotient $X$ is locally compact Hausdorff, the projection $H\to X$ is open and the right action is continuous. Nonzero right-invariant Radon measures on $X$ exist, and every such measure is positive on every nonempty open set ([lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient), Statement and Proof, step 1.2 for openness of the projection). Radon measures are finite on compact sets and have the regularity properties in [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space). Cosets are the sets of left translates by $\Gamma$ and double cosets also allow right multiplication by the indicated subgroup ([math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset), [math:def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-double-cosets-of-two-subgroups)).

[F2] Complex $L^2$ consists of square-integrable functions modulo equality almost everywhere, with measurability tested on the real and imaginary parts ([math:def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions)). Under Countable Choice it is a Hilbert space, meaning complete for the norm from its inner product ([math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space), [math:def-real-and-complex-inner-product-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-real-and-complex-inner-product-space)). The inner product is linear in the first argument and satisfies the Cauchy–Schwarz bound $|\langle f,g\rangle|\le\|f\|_2\|g\|_2$ ([math:lem-complex-lp-completeness-density-and-inner-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-lp-completeness-density-and-inner-product)). Under [A1], $C_c(X;\mathbb C)$ is dense in $\mathcal H$ for the locally compact Hausdorff space and Radon measure in [F1] ([math:lem-complex-haar-l1-and-l2-are-complete-and-cc-dense](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-haar-l1-and-l2-are-complete-and-cc-dense)). The notation $C_c$ denotes continuous functions whose support, the closure of the set where the function is nonzero, is compact ([math:def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space)). The closure of a set is its smallest closed superset ([math:def-interior-closure-boundary-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-interior-closure-boundary-top)).

[F3] A measure-preserving self-map leaves the integral of every nonnegative measurable function and every integrable complex function unchanged upon composition ([math:thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps)). Increasing nonnegative measurable approximations have integrals increasing to the integral of their limit ([math:thm-monotone-convergence-for-the-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-monotone-convergence-for-the-integral)).

[F4] Finite products and continuous images of compact spaces are compact ([math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), claim 1). A continuous real function on a nonempty compact space has a finite maximum ([math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), claim 2). Compact subsets of a Hausdorff space are closed ([math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed)). A closed subset of a compact space is compact ([math:thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact), claim 1). A compact Hausdorff group has a normalized Haar probability $dk$ under [A1], a Radon measure invariant under left and right multiplication and of total mass one ([math:cor-normalized-haar-probability-on-a-compact-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-normalized-haar-probability-on-a-compact-group)). A real-linear functional on real compactly supported continuous functions is positive when it takes nonnegative functions to nonnegative numbers ([math:def-positive-linear-functional-on-c-c](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-positive-linear-functional-on-c-c)). For a continuous compactly supported real kernel on a product of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, the two positive real-linear functionals on the separate spaces of compactly supported continuous real functions give continuous compactly supported partial integrals and commute. The same holds for complex kernels by complexification ([math:lem-compactly-supported-kernels-admit-commuting-radon-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compactly-supported-kernels-admit-commuting-radon-integrals)).

[F5] A linear map preserving the norm also preserves distances, and it is an isometric isomorphism onto its range ([math:def-linear-isometry-and-isometric-isomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-isometry-and-isometric-isomorphism)). A strongly continuous unitary representation is a group homomorphism into bijective complex-linear isometries with each vector's orbit map continuous in norm ([math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)). For such a representation, the orthogonal complement of a closed invariant subspace is closed and invariant ([math:lem-unitary-invariant-subspaces-have-invariant-orthogonal-complements](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-unitary-invariant-subspaces-have-invariant-orthogonal-complements)). Under Countable Choice a closed linear subspace $M$ of a Hilbert space $\mathcal H$ gives the orthogonal decomposition $\mathcal H=M\oplus M^\perp$ ([math:thm-orthogonal-decomposition-by-a-closed-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-orthogonal-decomposition-by-a-closed-subspace)). A split prime means $B_\ell\simeq M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, and the integral projective subgroup used in the unramified test is compact open and independent up to conjugacy of the matrix identification ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation), Definition and Justification, steps 2.1–2.2).

[F6] A measure space consists of a set, a sigma-algebra on it, and a measure on that sigma-algebra ([math:def-measure-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-space)). The quotient topology tests openness by the preimage under the quotient projection ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)). Compactness means that every open cover has a finite subcover ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). Every subset of a discrete space is open ([math:def-standard-topologies](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-standard-topologies)), and its Borel sigma-algebra, generated by the open sets, is the power set ([math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra)). A measure vanishes on the empty set and is countably additive on disjoint measurable sets ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure)). A nonnegative simple measurable function is a finite sum of nonnegative multiples of indicators of disjoint measurable sets ([math:def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function)). Its Lebesgue integral is the finite sum of each coefficient times the measure of its set ([math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function), [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral)). Every nonnegative measurable function is the pointwise limit of an increasing sequence of nonnegative simple measurable functions ([math:thm-increasing-simple-approximation-of-a-nonnegative-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-increasing-simple-approximation-of-a-nonnegative-measurable-function)).

[F7] A complex measurable function is integrable when the integral of its modulus is finite ([math:def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals)). The nonnegative integral is monotone ([math:prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-order-and-scalar-rules-for-the-nonnegative-integral)). The integral is complex linear on integrable functions ([math:thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-linearity-of-the-lebesgue-integral-on-l-one)), and the modulus of an integral is bounded by the integral of the modulus ([math:thm-integral-triangle-inequality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-integral-triangle-inequality)).

[F8] Arbitrary unions and finite intersections of open sets are open ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)). A map is continuous exactly when the preimage of each open set is open ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top)). In a Hausdorff space distinct points have disjoint open neighborhoods ([math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space)). Open rectangles form a basis for the product topology ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)), and the open sets of a subspace are the intersections of ambient open sets with that subspace ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)). Multiplication and inversion are continuous in a topological group ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)). A subgroup contains the ambient identity and is closed under multiplication and inversion ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F9] A map of measurable spaces is measurable exactly when the preimage of each measurable set is measurable ([math:def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces)). For a continuous map, the target subsets with Borel preimages form a sigma-algebra because inverse images commute with complements and countable unions ([math:def-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sigma-algebra), [math:def-algebra-of-subsets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-of-subsets)). It contains the target open sets by continuity, and therefore contains all target Borel sets ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra)). An almost-everywhere equality holds off a measurable set of measure zero ([math:def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere)). A measurable self-map $T$ is measure preserving if $\mu(T^{-1}(E))=\mu(E)$ for each measurable $E$ ([math:def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system)).

[F10] A closed subspace of a complete metric space is complete in the induced metric. Under Countable Choice, a complete subspace of a metric space is closed ([math:thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complete-subspace-iff-closed)). A complex-linear subspace contains zero and is closed under addition and multiplication by complex scalars ([math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)). The orthogonal complement $M^\perp$ consists of the vectors $w$ with $\langle w,m\rangle=0$ for every $m\in M$ ([math:def-orthogonality-and-orthogonal-complement](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-orthogonality-and-orthogonal-complement)).

[F11] The real line has its usual topology from the metric $d_{\mathbb R}(s,t)=|s-t|$ ([math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space)). For a metric $d$ and a radius $r>0$, the open ball centered at $a$ is $B_d(a,r)=\{b:d(a,b)<r\}$ ([math:def-metric-ball](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-ball)). Metric openness means that every point of the set has a ball centered there and contained in the set ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)). The complex plane uses the Euclidean metric $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)=|z-w|=\sqrt{(\operatorname{Re}(z-w))^2+(\operatorname{Im}(z-w))^2}$ ([math:def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity)). Complex conjugation is an involutive field automorphism with $z\overline z=|z|^2$, and modulus obeys $|zw|=|z||w|$ and $|z+w|\le |z|+|w|$ ([math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws)).

[F12] The $L^2$ norm from [F2] satisfies $\|v+w\|_2\le\|v\|_2+\|w\|_2$ and induces the metric $d_{\mathcal H}(v,w)=\|v-w\|_2$ ([math:def-normed-vector-space-over-an-absolutely-valued-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normed-vector-space-over-an-absolutely-valued-field)).

### Justification

**Given:** The full order and basis used for the finite adelic construction, the full adelic group $H$, its diagonal rational subgroup $\Gamma$, the nonzero right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$, the compact open subgroup $U$, and the Axiom of Choice.

**Goal:** Verify the unitary action and constituent conventions, and identify the $K$-fixed vectors with square-integrable functions on the discrete double-coset space.

1.1 The right translation $T_h(x)=xh$ is continuous and therefore Borel measurable. For every Borel set $E\subseteq X$, $T_h^{-1}(E)=Eh^{-1}$ and $\mu(Eh^{-1})=\mu(E)$, so $T_h$ is measure preserving. For a measurable representative $f$, each component of $f\circ T_h$ is the corresponding measurable real component of $f$ composed with the Borel map $T_h$. The preimage criterion in [F9] gives measurability of each composition, so $f\circ T_h$ is complex measurable by [F2]. The preimage of any Borel null exceptional set is null, so the action respects classes, and invariance of the nonnegative integral preserves square integrals. This gives a well-defined linear isometry $R(h)$ on the classes in $\mathcal H$, with inverse $R(h^{-1})$. The formula $R(h)R(h')f(x)=f((xh)h')=R(hh')f(x)$ gives the homomorphism property. Completeness and the pairing come from [F2] using the Countable Choice furnished by [A1]. [A1, F1, F2, F3, F5, F9]

1.2 The set $K=G(\mathbb R)\times U$ contains the identity and is closed under the componentwise multiplication and inversion of $H$, so it is a subgroup. It is open in $H$ as a product of open sets and compact as the product of two compact spaces. Its subspace topology is the product of the factor topologies, since restricting a basic rectangle in $H$ to $G(\mathbb R)\times U$ gives a basic rectangle there. The same rectangle argument identifies the product topology on $K\times K$ with its subspace topology from $H\times H$. The subgroup is Hausdorff by the inherited topology, and multiplication and inversion restrict continuously since open sets of $K$ are traces of those of $H$. Thus $K$ is a compact Hausdorff topological group, locally compact since $K$ is itself a compact neighborhood of each of its points. The map $q$ is well defined since left multiplication by a rational unit multiplies $g_f$ by its left finite component. It is surjective since the class of $(1,g_f)$ maps to the double coset of $g_f$. Its fibers are the right $K$-orbits. Indeed, if $g'_f=\gamma_f g_f u$, then $(g'_\infty,g'_f)$ differs from $\gamma(g_\infty,g_f)$ by right multiplication by $(g_\infty^{-1}\gamma_\infty^{-1}g'_\infty,u)\in K$. The converse follows by taking finite components. Left translation by $h$ is continuous, with continuous inverse left translation by $h^{-1}$, so $hK$ is open in $H$. The orbit of $\Gamma h$ is the image of the open set $hK$ under the projection $H\to X$, which is open by [F1], and is the image of the compact group $K$ under the continuous orbit map. For every $S\subseteq D$, the preimage $q^{-1}(S)$ is the union of the open fibers over $S$, hence is open and Borel in $X$. The open-preimage criterion gives continuity of $q$, and all preimages are Borel. Every fiber has positive finite measure by nonemptiness, openness and compactness. [given, F1, F4, F6, F8, F9]

2.1 To prove strong continuity at the identity, first take $f\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$ with compact support $C$. Choose a compact identity neighborhood $V$ in $H$ and an open identity neighborhood $W\subseteq V$. For $h\in V$ the difference $f(xh)-f(x)$ vanishes outside $E=C\cup CV^{-1}$. The set $CV^{-1}$ is compact as the image of $C\times V$ under the continuous map $(x,t)\mapsto xt^{-1}$. Finite subcovers of the two compact sets combine to give a finite subcover of $E$ for any open cover of $E$. Since $X$ is Hausdorff, $E$ is closed by [F4]. It contains the nonzero set of the difference and therefore the support, which is the smallest closed superset of that set by [F2]. The maps $(x,h)\mapsto f(xh)$ and $(x,h)\mapsto f(x)$ are continuous by the action and the product topology. For complex values $z,w,z_0,w_0$, the triangle inequality gives $|(z-w)-(z_0-w_0)|\le |z-z_0|+|w-w_0|$, so requiring each input to lie in the open ball of radius $\epsilon/2$ about its base value puts the difference in the open ball of radius $\epsilon$ about its base value. By [F8] and [F11] this proves joint continuity. Applying the triangle inequality to $z=(z-w)+w$ and interchanging the values gives $\big||z|-|w|\big|\le |z-w|$, which also proves continuity of the modulus. The difference vanishes at every $(x,e)$. Given $\epsilon>0$, continuity at each $(x,e)$ with $x\in E$ gives an open rectangle about $(x,e)$ on which the difference has modulus less than $\epsilon$. A finite cover in $x$ gives finitely many such identity neighborhoods. Intersect them with $W$ so that both the uniform bound on $E$ and vanishing outside $E$ hold. On this intersection the square integral of the difference is at most $\epsilon^2\mu(E)$, and $\mu(E)<\infty$. This gives norm continuity at the identity for $f$. For any $v\in\mathcal H$ approximate by $f\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$ and use the norm triangle inequality in [F12] to obtain $\|R(h)v-v\|_2\le 2\|v-f\|_2+\|R(h)f-f\|_2$. The homomorphism and isometry properties then give norm continuity at every $h$. [F1, F2, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8, F11, F12, step 1.1]

2.2 The rule for $\nu$ defines a measure on $\mathcal P(D)$ because all preimages are Borel and inverse images preserve the empty set and countable disjoint unions. Thus $(D,\mathcal P(D),\nu)$ is a measure space by [F6], as is $(X,\mathcal B(X),\mu)$ since $\mu$ is a measure on $\mathcal B(X)$. If $s=\sum_{j=1}^m c_j\mathbf 1_{E_j}$ is nonnegative simple with pairwise disjoint $E_j\subseteq D$, then $s\circ q$ has the same coefficients on the disjoint measurable sets $q^{-1}(E_j)$. By the simple integral formula both $\int_D s\,d\nu$ and $\int_X(s\circ q)\,d\mu$ equal $\sum_{j=1}^m c_j\nu(E_j)$. For any nonnegative measurable $b$ on $D$, take nonnegative simple functions $s_n\uparrow b$. Then $s_n\circ q\uparrow b\circ q$, and monotone convergence on these two measure spaces gives $\int_D b\,d\nu=\int_X(b\circ q)\,d\mu$. The real and imaginary parts of a pullback are the pullbacks of the respective components. They are measurable by the preimage criterion in [F9], so the pullback is complex measurable by [F2]. If $N$ is a $\nu$-null exceptional set, then $\mu(q^{-1}(N))=\nu(N)=0$. Applying the integral identity to squared moduli gives norm preservation, and pointwise linearity gives a linear isometry. The range is complete in the inherited metric because the domain $L^2(D,\nu;\mathbb C)$ is complete under Countable Choice from [A1] and pullback is an isometry. It is therefore closed by the Countable Choice direction of [F10]. Each pullback is pointwise constant on the $K$-orbits and hence represents a fixed class. If $a$ and $b$ agree outside a $\nu$-null set $N$ and $a(d)\ne b(d)$ at some $d$, then $d\in N$. Additivity on the disjoint sets $\{d\}$ and $N\setminus\{d\}$ gives $0=\nu(N)=\nu(\{d\})+\nu(N\setminus\{d\})\ge\nu(\{d\})>0$, a contradiction. Thus equality almost everywhere on $D$ is pointwise equality. [A1, F2, F3, F6, F9, F10, step 1.2]

3.1 For a closed invariant complex-linear subspace $M$, the restricted inner product makes $M$ a Hilbert space because the inherited norm is complete by [F2] and the closed-subspace direction of [F10]. The restriction of $R$ is a strongly continuous unitary representation since $R(h)$ and $R(h^{-1})$ preserve $M$ and the orbit maps are continuous in the inherited norm. Write $M^\perp$ for the orthogonal complement from [F10]. The Hilbert space property from [F2] and Countable Choice from [A1] give $\mathcal H=M\oplus M^\perp$ by [F5]. The same fact block supplies invariance of $M^\perp$, so $R$ acts on both summands separately. The irreducibility condition is exactly the absence of a proper nonzero invariant complex-linear subspace of $M$ closed for that norm. The simultaneous fixed space for any subgroup $K$ is linear because each $R(k)$ is linear. For a vector $v$ outside this space, choose $k\in K$ with $\delta=\|R(k)v-v\|_2>0$. Every simultaneous fixed vector $w$ satisfies $\delta=\|R(k)(v-w)-(v-w)\|_2\le 2\|v-w\|_2$ by isometry and the norm triangle inequality in [F12]. The open norm ball of radius $\delta/2$ about $v$ consists of the vectors $w$ with $\|v-w\|_2<\delta/2$ by the ball definition in [F11] and the metric formula in [F12], so it is contained in the complement of the fixed space. This proves that the complement is open in the norm topology and the fixed space is closed. For the local condition, the integral projective subgroups from different splittings are conjugate by [F5]. The corresponding conjugation carries the fixed vectors to those of the conjugate subgroup by the representation operator of the conjugating element. This is the split unramified criterion of [F5]. [A1, F2, F5, F8, F10, F11, F12, step 1.1, step 2.1]

3.2 For $f\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$ define $Af(x)=\int_K f(xk)\,dk$ using Haar probability on $K$. The kernel $(x,k)\mapsto f(xk)$ is continuous by continuity of the action and of $f$. Conjugation preserves distances: by [F11], $|\overline a|^2=\overline a\,a=|a|^2$, and conjugation takes differences to differences. For multiplication, $|zw-z_0w_0|\le |z||w-w_0|+|w_0||z-z_0|$, with $|z|\le|z_0|+1$ when $|z-z_0|<1$. Conjugation maps a ball of radius $\epsilon$ about any base value into the ball of radius $\epsilon$ about its conjugate. For multiplication, taking both input distances less than $\min(1,\epsilon/(|z_0|+1+|w_0|))$ puts the product in the ball of radius $\epsilon$ about $z_0w_0$. Thus both operations are continuous by [F8] and [F11]. Consequently $f(xk)\overline{w(x)}$ is continuous for each $w\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$. Both kernels vanish outside $(\operatorname{supp}f)K^{-1}\times K$. This set is compact by continuity of $(x,k)\mapsto xk^{-1}$ and product compactness. In $X\times K$, two distinct points have a differing coordinate. Disjoint open neighborhoods in that factor, multiplied by the whole other factor, separate the two points. Thus $X\times K$ is Hausdorff. The compact bounding set is therefore closed. It contains the nonzero set of each kernel, so it contains its closure by [F2]. Each kernel's support is thus a closed subset of the compact bounding set and is compact by [F4]. For a real or complex $C_c$ function with nonempty support, the modulus is continuous by the reverse triangle estimate in step 2.1 and has a finite maximum on the support by [F4]. A function with empty support is identically zero. For complex numbers $z,z_0$, the Euclidean formula in [F11] gives $|\operatorname{Re}(z-z_0)|\le |z-z_0|$ and $|\operatorname{Im}(z-z_0)|\le |z-z_0|$. The metric on the real line in [F11] makes each absolute coordinate difference its real distance. The same radius in the complex plane therefore controls each real component, so the components of any continuous complex function are continuous. They are Borel measurable by [F9], which gives complex measurability under [F2]. In particular the continuous complex kernels, their continuous sections, and the compactly supported continuous functions used here are measurable. The compactly supported functions are integrable by boundedness and finiteness of the measures on their supports. Integration against either Radon measure gives a real-linear functional on the corresponding real $C_c$ space by integral linearity. For a nonnegative function its value is at least the integral of zero by monotonicity, so both functionals are positive. Partial integration gives $Af\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$, so $Af$ is square-integrable by boundedness and finiteness of the measure on its support. For $k_0\in K$, left translation $k\mapsto k_0k$ is continuous and preserves $dk$ by left invariance of Haar probability. Integral invariance for this map gives $Af(xk_0)=\int_K f(x(k_0k))\,dk=Af(x)$. Thus $Af$ descends to a measurable function on $D$, whose squared modulus has finite integral by step 2.2. Commuting the integrals of $f(xk)\overline{w(x)}$ gives $\langle Af,w\rangle=\int_K\langle R(k)f,w\rangle\,dk$. [A1, F1, F2, F3, F4, F6, F7, F8, F9, F11, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2]

4.1 Let $v\in\mathcal H^K$. For $f,w\in C_c(X;\mathbb C)$, the scalar function $k\mapsto\langle R(k)(f-v),w\rangle$ is continuous by step 2.1 and Cauchy–Schwarz. Its real and imaginary parts are therefore Borel measurable on $K$ by the coordinate argument in step 3.2, so the scalar function is complex measurable by [F2]. By Cauchy–Schwarz and unitarity its modulus is bounded by $\|f-v\|_2\|w\|_2$, so it is integrable against Haar probability. Since $R(k)v=v$ and the integral of the constant $1$ is one, complex linearity and step 3.2 give $\langle Af-v,w\rangle=\int_K\langle R(k)(f-v),w\rangle\,dk$. The integral triangle inequality and the pointwise bound give $|\langle Af-v,w\rangle|\le\|f-v\|_2\|w\|_2$. By density and the inner product bound this holds for all $w\in\mathcal H$. Taking $w=Af-v$ gives $\|Af-v\|_2\le\|f-v\|_2$, with the inequality immediate if $Af=v$. Since $f$ can approximate $v$ arbitrarily closely and each $Af$ belongs to the closed pullback range, $v$ belongs to that range. This proves the isometric identification. When $D$ is finite, every function is square-integrable by finiteness of the singleton masses. For each $d\in D$, the simple integral formula gives $\int_D\mathbf 1_{\{d\}}\,d\nu=\nu(\{d\})<\infty$, so the singleton indicator is integrable. Apply complex linearity to their finite sum with coefficients $a(d)\overline{b(d)}$ to get the stated inner product. [F2, F4, F6, F7, F9, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 2.2, step 3.2] ∎


## Unimodularity and invariant measure for the compact Hamilton quotient

[Open item](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-hamilton-haar-normalization) · level 6


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Write $H=G(\mathbb A)$ for the full adelic group of rational Hamilton projective units, $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)$ for its diagonal rational subgroup, and $X=\Gamma\backslash H$ for the quotient by left multiplication by $\Gamma$. The group $H$ is unimodular, meaning its left Haar measure is also right invariant. The space $X$ has a nonzero finite right-invariant Radon measure, and any two nonzero right-invariant Radon measures on $X$ differ by a positive scalar. A Radon measure is a Borel measure finite on compact sets, outer regular on Borel sets and inner regular on open sets.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice gives a choice function for each indexed family of nonempty sets. It implies Dependent Choice, which permits successive choices along a relation with a successor at every point. These principles supply Haar measure, the quotient criterion and averaging, and uniqueness of Radon measures from continuous test integrals ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)).

[F1] The group $H$ is a locally compact Hausdorff topological group, and $\Gamma$ is closed and discrete. The coset space $X$ is locally compact Hausdorff, with a continuous right action of $H$ and a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure. Right invariance means that $\mu(Ea)=\mu(E)$ for every Borel set $E$ and every $a\in H$. The quotient $X$ is compact, so every Radon measure on it is finite ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), [lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient), [lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-compact-projective-hamilton-quotient](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-compact-projective-hamilton-quotient), [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space)).

[F2] Left Haar measure exists on a locally compact Hausdorff group under [A1]. A left Haar measure is a nonzero left-translation-invariant Radon measure on a group. Its modular function $\Delta_H$ is the continuous homomorphism to the positive reals such that right translation by $h$ scales the left Haar integral by $\Delta_H(h)^{-1}$ and pushes left Haar measure forward to $\Delta_H(h)^{-1}$ times itself. The subspace topology has open sets given by traces of ambient open sets. By [F1], every singleton of $\Gamma$ is open in this topology. Every subset is a union of these singletons, so $\Gamma$ has the discrete topology. In $\Gamma\times\Gamma$ each singleton is a rectangle of open singletons and hence is open by [F5]. Its subsets are likewise unions of open singletons, so $\Gamma\times\Gamma$ is discrete. Since preimages of open sets under inversion and multiplication are open in their respective discrete domains, [F5] makes both operations continuous. Thus $\Gamma$ is a topological group. Distinct points have disjoint open singleton neighborhoods, which gives Hausdorffness. In the subspace topology on a singleton, any open cover has a member containing its point, so that member alone is a finite subcover. Thus every point of $\Gamma$ has an open compact singleton neighborhood, which gives local compactness. Counting measure on the discrete group $\Gamma$ is both left and right Haar, so $\Delta_\Gamma=1$ ([math:cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures), [math:def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure), [math:def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group), [math:thm-the-modular-function-is-a-continuous-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-the-modular-function-is-a-continuous-homomorphism), [math:lem-counting-measure-on-a-discrete-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-counting-measure-on-a-discrete-group), [lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient), [math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space), [math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), [math:def-locally-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space), [math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top), [math:def-standard-topologies](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-standard-topologies)).

[F3] Since $\Gamma$ is closed, $Y=H/\Gamma$ with its quotient topology is locally compact Hausdorff. The invariant-measure criterion for its left action gives $\Delta_H|_\Gamma=\Delta_\Gamma$ when a nonzero invariant Radon measure exists. For such a measure $\eta$, the necessity argument in the criterion’s proof constructs a left Haar measure on $H$ represented on real test functions by the positive functional $f\mapsto\int_Y T_\Gamma f\,d\eta$. Here $T_\Gamma f(h\Gamma)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma} f(h\gamma)$ is quotient averaging with counting measure, onto $C_c(Y)$ from $C_c(H)$. The notation $C_c$ denotes continuous functions whose support, the closure of the nonzero set, is compact ([math:prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree), [Compact lifts and averaging onto $C_c(G/H)$](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-closed-subgroup-quotient-averaging-and-compact-lifts), [math:def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space)).

[F4] Left Haar measures are unique up to a positive scalar under [A1]. On a locally compact Hausdorff space, Radon measures agreeing on $C_c$ are equal under Dependent Choice. Continuous maps send compact sets to compact sets. Every continuous real-valued function on a nonempty compact space attains its maximum and minimum. Compact subsets of a Hausdorff space are closed. ([math:thm-uniqueness-of-left-haar-measure-up-to-scale](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-uniqueness-of-left-haar-measure-up-to-scale), [math:thm-rmk-uniqueness-among-radon-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-rmk-uniqueness-among-radon-measures), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps), [math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed)).

[F5] Let $q_X(h)=\Gamma h$ and $q_Y(h)=h\Gamma$ be the coset projections from $H$. A subset of either quotient is open exactly when its preimage under the corresponding projection is open in $H$. The right coset $\Gamma h$ consists of the elements $\gamma h$ with $\gamma\in\Gamma$, and the left coset $h\Gamma$ consists of the elements $h\gamma$ with $\gamma\in\Gamma$. Inversion is continuous in a topological group. A map is continuous exactly when the preimage of every open set is open. This is the equivalence of conditions (a) and (b) of the continuity criterion, taking all open sets in the codomain as the subbasis. A homeomorphism is a continuous bijection with continuous inverse. In a binary product, rectangles of open sets form a basis. An arbitrary union of open sets is open. ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology), [math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset), [math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group), [math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top), [math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps), [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology), [math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space), [math:def-topology-basis-subbasis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topology-basis-subbasis)).

[F6] A sigma-algebra on a set contains the whole set and is closed under complements relative to it and countable unions. These conditions also suffice because the complement of the whole set is empty and a finite union is a countable union after padding with empty sets. The Borel sigma-algebra of a topological space is generated by its open sets. It is contained in every sigma-algebra containing those open sets. A Borel measure takes values in $[0,+\infty]$, vanishes on the empty set and is countably additive on pairwise disjoint Borel sets. A nonnegative extended series is the supremum of its finite partial sums. ([math:def-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sigma-algebra), [math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra), [math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure), [math:def-nonnegative-extended-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-extended-series)).

[F7] A real-valued function on a topological space with its Borel sigma-algebra is measurable when its strict upper level sets are Borel for every real level.
