A nonnegative simple measurable function has finite range and can be written on pairwise disjoint measurable sets with nonnegative coefficients. The integral of a nonnegative simple function given on disjoint measurable sets is the sum of its values on those sets weighted by their measures, with $0\cdot(+\infty)=0$. The simple integral is independent of the chosen disjoint representation. The integral of a nonnegative measurable function is the supremum of the integrals of its nonnegative simple minorants. A real measurable function $f$ is integrable when both $f^+=\max(f,0)$ and $f^-=\max(-f,0)$ have finite integral. The integral of an integrable real function is the integral of its positive part minus the integral of its negative part. ([math:def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-real-valued-measurable-function), [math:def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function), [math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function), [math:lem-well-definedness-of-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-well-definedness-of-the-simple-integral), [math:def-nonnegative-lebesgue-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-lebesgue-integral), [math:def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integrable-real-and-complex-functions-and-their-integrals)).

### Proof

**Given:** The groups in the statement and [A1].

**Goal:** Establish unimodularity and uniqueness of the finite invariant measure up to scale.

1.1 If $h$ is replaced by $\gamma h$ in $q_X(h)$, its inverse is $h^{-1}\gamma^{-1}$, which has the same image under $q_Y$ as $h^{-1}$. If $h$ is replaced by $h\gamma$ in $q_Y(h)$, its inverse is $\gamma^{-1}h^{-1}$, which has the same image under $q_X$ as $h^{-1}$. Thus $I(\Gamma h)=h^{-1}\Gamma$ and $J(h\Gamma)=\Gamma h^{-1}$ are well defined and inverse to each other. For open $V\subseteq Y$, the $q_X$-preimage of $I^{-1}(V)$ is the preimage of $q_Y^{-1}(V)$ under inversion in $H$, hence open by [F1] and [F5]. Thus $I^{-1}(V)$ is open. For open $W\subseteq X$, the $q_Y$-preimage of $J^{-1}(W)$ is the preimage of $q_X^{-1}(W)$ under the same inversion, so $J^{-1}(W)$ is open too. This gives a homeomorphism. [F1, F5]

1.2 If $\rho$ is a Radon measure and $c>0$ is finite, define $(c\rho)(E)=c\rho(E)$ on Borel sets. Multiplication by $c$ is an order-preserving bijection of $[0,+\infty]$, so it preserves the suprema of partial sums in countable additivity and the infima and suprema in Radon regularity. The partial sums for $c\rho$ are $c$ times those for $\rho$ by distributivity, including $c(+\infty)=+\infty$. The rescaling also preserves zero mass of the empty set and finiteness on compact sets. Thus $c\rho$ is Radon. Use the same disjoint representation for each simple function under both measures. Its coefficient sum is independent of that representation by [F7]. Multiplying the measure by $c$ scales each summand by $c$, including the zero terms by the convention in [F7]. Taking the supremum over the same simple minorants gives $\int g\,d(c\rho)=c\int g\,d\rho$ for nonnegative measurable $g$. Subtracting the finite integrals of the negative parts from those of the positive parts gives the same identity for integrable real functions. [F1, F6, F7]

1.3 Let $f$ be a real compactly supported continuous function on $H$ or $Y$, and let $\rho$ be a Radon measure on its domain. For any real $a$, the set $\{f>a\}$ is open by continuity. For $a\ge0$ the sets $\{f^+>a\}$ and $\{f^->a\}$ are respectively $\{f>a\}$ and $\{f<-a\}$, which are open. For $a<0$ both sign-part level sets are the whole space. Thus $f$ and both sign parts are Borel measurable by [F6] and [F7]. Write $K$ for the compact support of $f$. A preimage of an open set under the restriction of $f$ to $K$ is the trace on $K$ of the corresponding open preimage under $f$, so this restriction is continuous by [F2] and [F5]. On $K$, the maximum and minimum of $f$ given by [F4] yield a finite bound $M>0$ for $|f|$ when $K$ is nonempty. If $K$ is empty, take $M=1$. Both sign parts vanish outside $K$ by [F3]. The set $K$ is closed by [F4] and therefore Borel. For a nonnegative simple minorant $s=\sum_j c_j\mathbf{1}_{E_j}$ of either sign part, each disjoint measurable $E_j$ with $c_j>0$ lies in $K$ and has $c_j\le M$ if $E_j$ is nonempty. Discard empty sets and terms with $c_j=0$. The remainder $K\setminus\bigcup_j E_j$ is the intersection of $K$ with the complement of a finite measurable union, so it is measurable by [F6] and disjoint from the retained sets. Apply countable additivity to the retained sets and the remainder, padded with empty sets. The partial sums are constant after the last of these terms, so [F6] gives $\rho(K)=\sum_j\rho(E_j)+\rho(K\setminus\bigcup_j E_j)$ and hence $\sum_j\rho(E_j)\le\rho(K)$. The coefficient sum for the retained disjoint representation gives the integral of $s$ by [F7], so $\int s\,d\rho\le M\rho(K)$. Taking suprema bounds each sign integral by $M\rho(K)<\infty$. Hence $f$ is integrable. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7]

2.1 The family of subsets $E\subseteq Y$ for which $I^{-1}(E)$ is Borel contains $Y$ because $I^{-1}(Y)=X$ is open in $X$. Preimages commute with relative complements and countable unions, and the Borel sets of $X$ are closed under both operations. By [F6], this family is a sigma-algebra on $Y$. This sigma-algebra contains all open sets by continuity of $I$, so it contains every Borel set by [F6]. Applying the same argument to $J$ gives a bijection between the Borel sets of $X$ and $Y$. For a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$, set $\eta(E)=\mu(I^{-1}(E))$ for Borel $E\subseteq Y$. Then $\eta(\varnothing)=0$. For pairwise disjoint Borel sets $E_n\subseteq Y$, the preimages $I^{-1}(E_n)$ are pairwise disjoint Borel sets, so $\eta(\bigcup_n E_n)=\mu(\bigcup_n I^{-1}(E_n))=\sum_n\mu(I^{-1}(E_n))=\sum_n\eta(E_n)$ by [F6]. Thus $\eta$ is a Borel measure. Both directions of the homeomorphism send compact sets to compact sets. Compact subsets of $X$ and $Y$ are closed by Hausdorffness and [F4], so they are Borel by [F6]. Consequently $\eta$ is finite on compact sets. Open supersets of a Borel set $E$ correspond to open supersets of $I^{-1}(E)$, and compact subsets of an open set correspond under $I^{-1}$ to compact subsets of its preimage. The same infima and suprema therefore give outer regularity on Borel sets and inner regularity on open sets. The Borel correspondence and nonzero $\mu$ give nonzero $\eta$. Transport along $J$ recovers $\mu$ because $J$ and $I$ are inverses. [F1, F3, F4, F6, step 1.1]

3.1 Fix $a\in H$. For the slice $x\mapsto(x,a)$, the preimage of a basic open rectangle $U\times V$ is $U$ if $a\in V$ and empty otherwise. Every open set in $X\times H$ is a union of those rectangles, so the slice is continuous by [F5]. The preimage of an open subset of $X$ under $x\mapsto xa$ is its preimage under the continuous action in [F1] pulled back along the continuous slice. It is therefore open, giving continuity of right translation by [F5]. Its inverse is $x\mapsto xa^{-1}$, which is continuous by the same reasoning, so right translation is a homeomorphism. The identity $I(xa)=a^{-1}I(x)$ gives $I^{-1}(aE)=I^{-1}(E)a^{-1}$. For Borel $E$, this preimage and hence $aE$ are Borel by the homeomorphism argument in step 2.1. It follows that $\eta(aE)=\eta(E)$ by right invariance of $\mu$. [F1, F5, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 Use the measure in [F1] in step 2.1 and apply the criterion in [F3] to the resulting left-invariant measure on $Y$. By [F2], $\Delta_H|_\Gamma=\Delta_\Gamma=1$. [A1, F1, F2, F3, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 For $\gamma\in\Gamma$, multiplicativity gives $\Delta_H(\gamma h)=\Delta_H(h)$. Hence $\bar\Delta(\Gamma h)=\Delta_H(h)$ is well defined. For each open $V\subseteq\mathbb R$, the preimage of $\bar\Delta^{-1}(V)$ under $q_X$ is the open set $\Delta_H^{-1}(V)$ in $H$. By [F5], $\bar\Delta$ is continuous. [F2, F5, step 4.1]

6.1 The compact space $X$ contains $\Gamma 1$, so the real-valued function $\bar\Delta$ attains its maximum $M=\bar\Delta(\Gamma u)=\Delta_H(u)>0$ for some $u\in H$. For every $h\in H$, $M\Delta_H(h)=\Delta_H(uh)\le M$. Thus $\Delta_H(h)\le1$. Applying the inequality to $h^{-1}$ gives $\Delta_H(h)^{-1}\le1$, so $\Delta_H(h)=1$. The scaling relation in [F2] now gives right invariance of left Haar measure, which is unimodularity. [F1, F2, F4, step 5.1]

7.1 For two nonzero right-invariant Radon measures $\mu_1,\mu_2$ on $X$, transport them by steps 2.1 and 3.1 to left-invariant Radon measures $\eta_1,\eta_2$ on $Y$. Write $\lambda_j$ for the left Haar measure on $H$ representing $f\mapsto\int_Y T_\Gamma f\,d\eta_j$ by [F3]. By [F4], $\lambda_2=c\lambda_1$ on Borel sets for some finite $c>0$. Steps 1.3 and 1.2 give $\int_H f\,d\lambda_2=c\int_H f\,d\lambda_1$ on $C_c(H)$. Surjectivity of $T_\Gamma$ gives $\int_Y \phi\,d\eta_2=c\int_Y \phi\,d\eta_1=\int_Y \phi\,d(c\eta_1)$ on $C_c(Y)$. The two measures $\eta_2$ and $c\eta_1$ are Radon by steps 2.1 and 1.2, and $Y$ is locally compact Hausdorff by [F3]. Radon uniqueness under the Dependent Choice furnished by [A1] gives $\eta_2=c\eta_1$. Transport back gives $\mu_2=c\mu_1$. Existence and finiteness follow from [F1]. [A1, F1, F3, F4, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 1.3, step 1.2] ∎


## Spherical lines for unitary split projective factors

[Open item](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-local-spherical-line) · level 6


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra and put $B_\ell=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell$. Let $\ell$ be a split prime, so $B_\ell$ admits a $\mathbb Q_\ell$-algebra isomorphism to the two-by-two matrices over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Choose such an isomorphism and use it to identify $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ with $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, the group of invertible matrices modulo nonzero scalar matrices. Let $K_\ell=\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ and let $J_\ell$ be its compact open projective image. Let $\rho$ be a strongly continuous unitary representation of the projective group on a complex Hilbert space $V$. Suppose the smooth vectors of its lift to $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ form an irreducible admissible representation. Smooth means fixed by some open subgroup. Irreducible here means nonzero with no proper nonzero invariant linear subspace. Admissibility means that fixed spaces for compact open subgroups are finite dimensional.

If $V^{J_\ell}\ne0$, the representation is called unramified and $V^{J_\ell}$ is a one-dimensional spherical line. The spherical Hecke algebra consists of compactly supported complex functions invariant under left and right multiplication by $J_\ell$, with convolution for a left Haar measure giving $J_\ell$ mass one. Such a measure exists. On the spherical line its operators act by a character, a unital complex-algebra homomorphism to $\mathbb C$.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice supplies choice functions for indexed families of nonempty sets and implies Countable Choice. Use the Axiom of Choice for the split compactness assertion and for existence of left Haar measure. Use Countable Choice for the matrix and quotient group result and for the degree-two theorem ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)).

[F1] The split identification is a topological isomorphism, and $J_\ell$ is its compact open subgroup corresponding to the image of $K_\ell$. The projection from the invertible matrix group to the projective group is continuous and onto. A strongly continuous unitary action has norm-continuous vector orbit maps and acts by bijective complex-linear isometries ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation), [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)).

[F2] A vector is smooth if it is fixed by some open subgroup. For a strongly continuous unitary representation of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ whose smooth vectors are irreducible and admissible, when the $K_\ell$-fixed space is nonzero it is one dimensional under Countable Choice ([thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character](item-thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character)).

[F3] For a compact open subgroup $J$ in a Hausdorff topological group, each translate $xJ$ is a compact open neighborhood of $x$. The unit in the spherical Hecke algebra is the indicator of $J$. Its action on fixed vectors is the unital algebra action $T_fv=\sum_{xJ} f(x)\rho(x)v$. Each $xJ=\{xj:j\in J\}$ is counted once, and only finitely many cosets have nonzero terms by compact support and openness. The algebra multiplication is left Haar convolution when the measure assigns $J$ mass one ([def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra), Definition and Justification steps 1.1, 2.2, 4.1, 5.1, 6.1 and 7.1).

[F4] Under Countable Choice, $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ and its quotient by the scalar matrices are Hausdorff topological groups in the matrix-entry and scalar quotient topologies, respectively. The subgroup $K_\ell$ and its projective image are compact and open ([lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets](item-lem-author-d45da46285be-triangular-cosets)).

[F5] Under the Axiom of Choice, a locally compact Hausdorff group admits a left Haar measure ([math:cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-existence-of-left-and-right-haar-measures)). This measure is positive on nonempty open sets and finite on compact sets ([math:lem-haar-measure-is-positive-on-nonempty-open-sets-and-finite-on-compact-sets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-haar-measure-is-positive-on-nonempty-open-sets-and-finite-on-compact-sets)). A left Haar measure is a nonzero left-invariant Borel measure that is finite on compact sets, outer regular on Borel sets and inner regular on open sets ([math:def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-left-haar-integral-and-left-haar-measure)).

[F6] A unital ring homomorphism preserves addition, multiplication and the multiplicative identity ([math:def-ring-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-ring-homomorphism)). A homomorphism of unital complex algebras is a unital ring homomorphism that intertwines the two scalar structure maps ([math:def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-over-a-commutative-ring)).

[F7] A finite-dimensional complex vector space has a finite basis, and dimension one means that such a basis has one element ([math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension)). A basis spans the space and is linearly independent ([math:def-linear-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-basis)).

[F8] A measure vanishes on the empty set and assigns a countable disjoint union the nonnegative extended sum of the individual measures ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure)). The finite partial sums start at zero and each successor adds the next term. A nonnegative extended sum is the supremum of its finite partial sums ([math:def-nonnegative-extended-series](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-extended-series)).

[F9] Outer regularity gives the measure of a Borel set as the infimum over open supersets. Inner regularity on an open set gives its measure as the supremum over compact subsets ([math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space)). For positive finite $c$, multiplication by $c$ on $[0,+\infty]$ uses ordinary real multiplication at finite values and sends $+\infty$ to $+\infty$ ([math:def-extended-reals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-extended-reals)).

[F10] A map of topological spaces is continuous exactly when preimages of open sets are open ([math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), conditions (a) and (b)).

[F11] A subset is compact when every open cover in its subspace topology has a finite subcover ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). A space is locally compact when every point has a compact neighborhood ([math:def-locally-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space)).

[F12] The Borel sigma-algebra is generated by the open sets, so it contains every open set ([math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra)). A sigma-algebra contains the empty set and is closed under countable unions ([math:def-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sigma-algebra)).

[F13] A property holds at every natural number if it holds at zero and passes from each number to its successor ([math:thm-induction-principle](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-induction-principle)).

### Proof

**Given:** The Axiom of Choice, the split group, and the strongly continuous unitary action $\rho$ with the specified hypotheses on its lift.

**Goal:** Normalize left Haar measure and show that a nonzero fixed space is a line on which the Hecke action is a character.

1.1 By [A1] and [F4] the matrix scalar quotient is Hausdorff, so by [F1] the split projective group $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ is Hausdorff. For each $x$ in this group the translate $xJ_\ell$ is a compact open neighborhood by [F3], so the group is locally compact by [F11]. Obtain a left Haar measure $\mu$ by [A1] and [F5]. The subgroup $J_\ell$ is open by [F1] and hence Borel by [F12]. It contains the identity and is compact by [F1], so $0<\mu(J_\ell)<\infty$ by [F5]. Put $c=1/\mu(J_\ell)$ and define $\nu(E)=c\mu(E)$ on the Borel sigma-algebra. Multiplication by $c$ on $[0,+\infty]$ preserves order and has the order-preserving inverse given by multiplication by $1/c$. It therefore sends upper and lower bounds to upper and lower bounds bijectively, preserving suprema and infima. For nonnegative extended numbers $a,b$, the identity $c(a+b)=ca+cb$ follows from real distributivity if both are finite, and both sides are $+\infty$ if either is infinite. For a pairwise disjoint sequence of Borel sets $E_k$, the union is Borel by [F12]. Both initial partial sums are zero. If the partial sum of the first $n$ values $\nu(E_k)$ is $c$ times the corresponding partial sum of $\mu(E_k)$, the same holds for $n+1$ by the recurrence in [F8] and $c(a+b)=ca+cb$. Thus the scaling identity holds at every index by [F13]. Taking their suprema and using countable additivity of $\mu$ gives countable additivity of $\nu$. The empty set is Borel by [F12], and $\nu(\varnothing)=c\mu(\varnothing)=0$. Hence $\nu$ is a Borel measure by [F8]. Multiplication by $c$ preserves the infimum over open supersets of each Borel set and the supremum over compact subsets of each open set, giving outer and inner regularity in the required domains. Left invariance and finiteness on compact sets follow from those of $\mu$. Finally, $\nu(J_\ell)=1$, so $\nu$ is nonzero and is the required left Haar measure. [A1, F1, F3, F4, F5, F8, F9, F11, F12, F13, given]

1.2 Suppose $V^{J_\ell}\ne 0$. Write $q:\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)\to G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ for the continuous projective quotient homomorphism from [F1]. The lift $\rho\circ q$ acts by the same complex-linear isometries as $\rho$. For $v\in V$, write $\omega_v(x)=\rho(x)v$. If $U$ is open in the norm topology of $V$, then $(\omega_v\circ q)^{-1}[U]=q^{-1}[\omega_v^{-1}[U]]$ is open by [F1] and [F10]. Thus $\rho\circ q$ is strongly continuous on the same complex Hilbert space $V$. A vector is fixed by $K_\ell$ for this lift exactly when it is fixed by $J_\ell$ for $\rho$, because $K_\ell$ maps onto $J_\ell$. Since $K_\ell$ is open by [F4], these vectors are smooth for the lift by the smooth-vector criterion in [F2]. The smooth vectors give an irreducible admissible representation by hypothesis, the $K_\ell$-fixed space is nonzero, and [A1] gives Countable Choice. Hence the fixed space is one dimensional by [F2]. [A1, F1, F2, F4, F10, given]

2.1 The volume-one measure from step 1.1 identifies Hecke multiplication with the specified convolution by [F3]. By the same fact the projective Hecke operators preserve this line and give a unital algebra action on it. By [F7] and step 1.2, take a basis $\{w\}$ of $V^{J_\ell}$. Since the basis spans the line and is linearly independent, each vector has a unique expression $aw$ with $a\in\mathbb C$. In particular, write $T_fw=\lambda(f)w$ with $\lambda(f)\in\mathbb C$ unique. By complex linearity, $T_f(aw)=aT_fw=\lambda(f)(aw)$ for every $a\in\mathbb C$. From the algebra action, $T_{f+h}w=(\lambda(f)+\lambda(h))w$, $T_{f*h}w=T_f(T_hw)=\lambda(f)\lambda(h)w$ and $T_{\mathbf 1_{J_\ell}}w=w$. Their unique coefficients show that $\lambda$ preserves addition, multiplication and the unit, so it is a unital ring homomorphism by [F6]. The scalar map of the Hecke algebra sends $c$ to $c\mathbf 1_{J_\ell}$, and $\lambda(c\mathbf 1_{J_\ell})=c$ by complex linearity and unitality of the action. Thus $\lambda$ respects the scalar structure maps and is a unital complex-algebra homomorphism by [F6]. [F3, F6, F7, step 1.1, step 1.2] ∎


## Passing automorphic representations through scalar quotients

[Open item](item-lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-projective-automorphic-passage) · level 6


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $L$ be either the full adelic unit group $B^\times(\mathbb A)$ of the rational Hamilton algebra $B$, or $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$. In the Hamilton case fix a full order $O$ in $B$, a subring containing $1$ with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is also a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. Give $L=B_{\mathbb R}^\times\times\widehat B^\times$ the product of the real unit topology and the restricted-product topology of the $B_\ell^\times$ relative to $O_\ell^\times$, where $B_\ell=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $O_\ell=O\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Z_\ell$. In the split case use the restricted product of the real and finite general linear groups relative to $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ at the finite places.

In either case let $Z$ consist of the scalar multiplications by ideles in $\mathbb A^\times=\mathbb R^\times\times\mathbb A_f^\times$. Give the group $P=L/Z$ the quotient topology and write $q:L\to P$ for its projection. Let $\widetilde\Gamma$ be the diagonal image of $B^\times$ or $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$, respectively, and put $\Gamma=q(\widetilde\Gamma)$. Give the two sets of cosets $X_L=\widetilde\Gamma Z\backslash L$ and $X_P=\Gamma\backslash P$ their quotient topologies. Then
$$\Phi:X_L\longrightarrow X_P,\qquad \widetilde\Gamma Zg\longmapsto\Gamma q(g)$$
is a homeomorphism of locally compact Hausdorff spaces and $\Phi(xg)=\Phi(x)q(g)$.

Equip the two spaces with their Borel sigma-algebras. For a Borel measure, right invariance means that translating any Borel set on the right by any acting group element leaves its measure unchanged. Given a right-invariant Borel measure $\nu$ on $X_P$, put $\mu(E)=\nu(\Phi(E))$ for every Borel $E\subseteq X_L$. Then $\mu$ is right invariant and its pushforward is $\nu$, meaning $\mu(\Phi^{-1}(A))=\nu(A)$ for every Borel $A\subseteq X_P$. Transport by $\Phi^{-1}$ gives the inverse correspondence.

Let $R_L(g)u(x)=u(xg)$ and $R_P(p)f(y)=f(yp)$ act on the respective complex $L^2$ spaces of square-integrable functions modulo equality almost everywhere. These actions are by bijective linear isometries. Pullback $Tf=f\circ\Phi$ is a bijective complex-linear isometry satisfying $R_L(g)T=TR_P(q(g))$. For corresponding Radon measures, both actions are strongly continuous unitary representations. For any corresponding right-invariant Borel measures, a closed invariant subspace on either side corresponds to a closed invariant subspace on the other, and the two restricted actions are strongly continuous together. A unitary constituent is the restricted unitary representation on such a nonzero subspace. The correspondence preserves irreducibility, complex linear dimension and the fixed vectors for every subgroup of $L$ and its image in $P$. Complex linear dimension is the cardinality of a complex linear basis, with the same convention in infinite dimension. On the $L$ side every scalar acts trivially.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] In the Hamilton case the real unit group and the finite restricted product are topological groups. The scalar quotient maps to the real and finite projective groups are continuous and open with kernels $\mathbb R^\times$ and $\mathbb A_f^\times$, respectively. Their targets have product $PB^\times(\mathbb A)$ in the product topology, and the two scalar quotient maps send diagonal rational units to diagonal projective units. [F3] In a quotient topology a set is open exactly when its inverse image is open. The cosets $Hg$ are the orbits for left multiplication by $H$. The quotient by a normal subgroup multiplies cosets by multiplying representatives. A homeomorphism is a continuous bijection with continuous inverse. An open map sends open sets to open sets. In a topological group multiplication and inversion are continuous. Open rectangles form a basis in a binary product. A surjective group homomorphism induces an isomorphism from the quotient by its kernel onto its target. Open sets are closed under arbitrary unions and finite intersections. A measurable self-map preserves the measure if each measurable set and its inverse image have equal measure. A sigma-algebra contains its ambient space and is closed under relative complements and countable unions. An equality holds almost everywhere exactly when its exceptional set lies in a measurable set of measure zero. [F8] For a continuous function its support is the closure of the set on which it is nonzero. Under Dependent Choice, real continuous compactly supported functions are dense in real $L^2$ for a Radon measure on a locally compact Hausdorff space. Finite products and continuous images of compact spaces are compact. Real continuous functions are bounded on nonempty compact spaces. An open cover in the ambient space of a compact subset has a finite subcover. Compact subsets of a Hausdorff space are closed. [math:def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-support-c-c-and-c-zero-on-an-lch-space) [math:thm-c-c-is-dense-in-l-p-for-radon-measures](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-c-c-is-dense-in-l-p-for-radon-measures) [math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces) [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps) [math:lem-compactness-of-a-subspace-is-ambient](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-compactness-of-a-subspace-is-ambient) [math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed)

[F9] A linear basis is a linearly independent spanning subset. Under the Axiom of Choice every vector space has a basis and every set can be well ordered. Finite-dimensional spaces have finite bases of unique size, and infinite-dimensional spaces have no finite basis. Under Choice an infinite set is equinumerous with its Cartesian square. Two sets that inject into one another have the same cardinality. Every nonempty subset of a well-ordered set has a least element. [math:def-linear-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-basis) [math:cor-every-vector-space-has-a-basis](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-every-vector-space-has-a-basis) [math:thm-well-ordering-theorem](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-well-ordering-theorem) [math:def-dimension](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dimension) [math:thm-any-two-finite-bases-have-the-same-size](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-any-two-finite-bases-have-the-same-size) [math:thm-tarski-square](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-tarski-square) [math:thm-schroder-bernstein](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-schroder-bernstein) [math:def-cardinal](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-cardinal) [math:def-well-order](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-well-order)

[F10] For a group action restricted to a subgroup, a vector is fixed exactly when every element of that subgroup acts as the identity on it. [math:def-fixed-point-sets-of-a-group-action](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-fixed-point-sets-of-a-group-action)

### Proof

**Given:** The two adelic group constructions, the Axiom of Choice and the right-invariant Borel measure on either quotient.

**Goal:** Identify the quotient spaces, the right actions on square-integrable functions and their unitary constituents.

1.1 In the Hamilton case the product of the two scalar quotient maps from [F1] is continuous, open by the rectangle basis, and surjective. Its kernel is $\mathbb R^\times\times\mathbb A_f^\times=Z$, viewed as central scalar multiplications. By the first isomorphism theorem the product map induces a group isomorphism from $P$ onto the full projective Hamilton group. It is continuous by the quotient topology on $P$ and open because the image of an open subset of $P$ is the image of its open inverse image in $L$ under the open product map. The rational image is the diagonal projective rational subgroup. In the split case the corresponding group projection and its kernel are supplied by [F2]. Hence in both cases $P$ is a topological group and $q$ is an open continuous quotient homomorphism. [F1, F2, F3]

1.2 To verify the dimension convention, take any two complex linear bases $U,V$ of a complex vector space. If one basis is finite, their sizes agree by [F9]. Otherwise both are infinite. Each vector of $V$ has a finite expansion in $U$ by spanning, and the expansion is unique by linear independence. These finite supports cover $U$. If a vector of $U$ were absent from all these supports, the span of $V$ would lie in the span of the remaining vectors of $U$, contrary to independence of $U$. Well order both bases, and for each vector of $U$ take the least vector of $V$ whose support contains it. The chosen vector of $V$ together with the position in its ordered finite support gives an injection $U\to V\times\mathbb N$. The latter injects into $V\times V$ because for each natural number $n$ we can take the element at position $n$ in the well-order of $V$, starting at position zero. Construct these elements by repeatedly taking the least member left after deleting the preceding finite list. Each remaining set is nonempty because $V$ is infinite, so the well-order gives its least member by [F9]. The square law gives an injection into $V$. Exchanging $U$ and $V$ gives the reverse injection, so the two bases have equal cardinality. This applies to the bases whose existence is supplied by [F9]. [F9, choose]

2.1 For $g,h\in L$, equality $\Gamma q(g)=\Gamma q(h)$ is equivalent to $q(h)=q(\gamma g)$ for some $\gamma\in\widetilde\Gamma$. Since $\ker q=Z$ and $Z$ is central, this holds exactly when $h=\gamma zg$ for some $\gamma\in\widetilde\Gamma$ and $z\in Z$. This proves that $\Phi$ is well defined and injective, and surjectivity follows from that of $q$. The successive quotient maps $L\to P\to X_P$ compose to a quotient map by the inverse-image criterion. They have the same inverse images through $\Phi$ as the projection $L\to X_L$, so $\Phi$ and its inverse are continuous. From $q(gh)=q(g)q(h)$, $\Phi$ respects the two right actions via $q$. Local compactness and Hausdorff separation pass through a homeomorphism because it carries compact sets and open neighborhoods to sets of the same type. Thus [F4] gives these properties on both sides. [F3, F4, F8, step 1.1]

3.1 To show that the projection $\pi:P\to X_P$ is open, take the inverse image of the image of an open set. It is the union of its left translates by $\Gamma$. Each translate is open because left multiplication and its inverse are continuous by [F3]. Their union is open by the topology axioms in [F3]. The quotient topology gives openness of the image. By the rectangle basis, $\pi\times\mathrm{id}_P$ is an open continuous surjection and hence a quotient map. The right action on $X_P$ is well defined on cosets and continuous, since its composite with this quotient map is $(p,a)\mapsto\pi(pa)$. Equivariance and continuity of $q$ and $\Phi$ give the joint continuity of the action on $X_L$. For any fixed group element, the right translation has the translation by the inverse element as continuous inverse. [F3, step 1.1, step 2.1]

4.1 These translations and $\Phi$ are Borel isomorphisms, meaning the bijections and their inverses are Borel measurable. For each of these maps, the family of sets with Borel inverse image contains the whole target, since its inverse image is the whole domain. It is closed under relative complements and countable unions because inverse images commute with both operations. Continuity puts every open set in this sigma-algebra. It therefore contains the Borel sigma-algebra generated by them as in [F5]. The bijection $\Phi$ preserves disjoint unions and the empty set, so $\mu(E)=\nu(\Phi(E))$ is a measure. Equivariance gives $\mu(Eg)=\nu(\Phi(E)q(g))=\mu(E)$ for every Borel $E$ and $g\in L$. Transport in the reverse direction uses $\Phi^{-1}$ and $q$ surjective to give right invariance of $\nu$ from that of $\mu$. The formulas are inverse to each other and give the claimed pushforward identity. Compact sets and open sets correspond under $\Phi$, so all three Radon conditions transfer in both directions. [F5, F8, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 For each Borel set $A\subseteq X_P$ the indicator of $A$ pulls back to the indicator of $\Phi^{-1}(A)$, whose integral is $\nu(A)$ by the pushforward identity, the simple integral formula and its agreement with the nonnegative integral. The weighted-fiber formula yields integral equality for nonnegative simple functions, since the measure of each fiber equals that of its inverse image. Composition with $\Phi$ bijects the nonnegative measurable simple minorants of any nonnegative Borel function $h$ with those of $h\circ\Phi$, since composition with $\Phi^{-1}$ reverses the correspondence. Taking the defining suprema gives $\int_{X_L}h\circ\Phi\,d\mu=\int_{X_P}h\,d\nu$. For $h=|f|^2$ this gives norm preservation. Borel measurability of both maps gives measurability of compositions. If two representatives agree outside a Borel $\nu$-null set $N$, the exceptional set for their pullbacks is contained in $\Phi^{-1}(N)$, which is Borel and $\mu$-null. The same argument applies to pullback through $\Phi^{-1}$ with the measures interchanged. Thus pullback is a well-defined complex-linear isometry on the classes with inverse given by pullback through $\Phi^{-1}$. Both spaces are Hilbert spaces by Countable Choice from [F7]. [F5, F6, F7, step 4.1]

6.1 On either quotient the inverse image of a Borel set $E$ under right translation by $a$ is $Ea^{-1}$. It has the same measure as $E$ by right invariance. Thus translation by $a$ is a measure-preserving measurable self-map. If representatives agree outside a Borel null set $N$, their translates agree outside $Na^{-1}$, which is Borel and null by measure preservation. This applies to both $a$ and $a^{-1}$. The measure-preserving self-map integral identity for the squared modulus makes each translation a linear isometry, with inverse the translation by the inverse group element. On either side, $R(a)R(b)f(x)=f((xa)b)=R(ab)f(x)$ gives the homomorphism property. Equivariance gives $R_L(g)Tf(x)=f(\Phi(xg))=f(\Phi(x)q(g))=TR_P(q(g))f(x)$ on representatives. [F5, F6, F7, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 5.1]

7.1 For a Radon measure $\nu$, take a continuous complex function $f$ on $X_P$ with compact support $C$. If $C$ is empty, the orbit is constant zero. Otherwise the components of $f$ are Borel measurable because the real and imaginary projections are continuous for $d_{\mathbb C}$. The modulus $|f|$ is continuous, since $\big||z|-|w|\big|\le |z-w|$ by the metric triangle inequality, and is bounded on $C$ by some $M\ge 0$. The support $C$ is closed. Since $|f|^2\le M^2\mathbf{1}_C$ and $\nu(C)<\infty$, the simple integral formula and monotonicity show that $f$ is square-integrable. The group $P$ is locally compact by [F4] and step 1.1, so choose a compact identity neighborhood $D$ in $P$. For $p\in D$ the difference $f(yp)-f(y)$ vanishes outside $K=\{ya^{-1}:y\in C,\ a\in D\}$. This is a compact set by continuity of the right action and inversion, and it contains $C$ since $1\in D$. The set $K$ is closed in the Hausdorff space $X_P$, hence Borel. Continuity of the action and of $f$, and the triangle inequality for the difference of two complex values, give joint continuity of the difference. It vanishes at each $(y,1)$ with $y\in K$. For any $\eta>0$, use the Axiom of Choice to select one open rectangle at each $(y,1)$ with $y\in K$ on which the modulus of the difference is less than $\eta$. Take a finite subcover of $K$ by the first factors. The intersection of the corresponding identity neighborhoods with an open identity neighborhood contained in $D$ is open by [F3]. For every $p$ in that intersection, the bound holds on all of $K$. Then $|f(yp)-f(y)|^2\le\eta^2\mathbf{1}_K(y)$ on all of $X_P$. Monotonicity and the simple integral formula give $\|R_P(p)f-f\|_2\le\eta\sqrt{\nu(K)}$. Since $D$ contains an open identity neighborhood and $\nu(K)<\infty$, this proves norm continuity at $1$. [given, F3, F4, F5, F6, F7, F8, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 3.1, step 6.1]

7.2 For any corresponding right-invariant Borel measures, the intertwining identity and surjectivity of $q$ identify the closed invariant subspaces by $T$. These are Hilbert spaces with their inherited inner products, because they are closed. For a vector on the projective side its orbit map, composed with $q$ and transported by $T$, is the corresponding orbit map on the full group. As $T$ is an isometric isomorphism and openness on $P$ is tested by inverse image under $q$, each orbit map is norm continuous exactly when its correspondent is. The bijection of invariant subspaces inside each constituent preserves irreducibility. Let $S$ be a subgroup of $L$ and use the fixed-vector condition in [F10]. If $v$ is fixed by $q(S)$, then $R_L(s)Tv=TR_P(q(s))v=Tv$ for every $s\in S$, so $Tv$ is fixed by $S$. If $Tv$ is fixed by $S$, each $p\in q(S)$ has the form $q(s)$ with $s\in S$, and $TR_P(p)v=R_L(s)Tv=Tv$ gives $R_P(p)v=v$ by injectivity. A scalar acts trivially because it maps to the identity under $q$. [F3, F7, F10, step 1.1, step 5.1, step 6.1]

8.1 The real density theorem applies by Dependent Choice from [F7] and the locally compact Hausdorff and Radon hypotheses. Write a measurable representative as $u+iw$. Its components are real measurable by the complex $L^2$ convention. Since $|u|^2,|w|^2\le |u+iw|^2$, monotonicity proves that both define real $L^2$ classes. The norm formulas agree for real-valued functions embedded in complex $L^2$, because $|r+i0|=|r|$ for real $r$. The same formula gives $|ir|=|r|$. Approximate $u$ and $w$ separately by real continuous compactly supported functions in the real $L^2$ norm. The support of the combined complex function is the union of the two supports, since the function is nonzero wherever either part is nonzero. The union is compact by taking a finite subcover of each support from any open cover of the union. The complex error in norm is at most the sum of the real errors by the triangle inequality. Thus the continuous compactly supported complex functions are dense. For $v$ in complex $L^2$ and an approximant $f$ from this dense space, the isometries give $\|R_P(p)v-v\|_2\le 2\|v-f\|_2+\|R_P(p)f-f\|_2$. First choose $f$ close to $v$, then use step 7.1 to control the last term near the identity. This gives norm continuity at the identity for every $v$. At $p_0$ it follows by applying the identity estimate to $p_0^{-1}p$ and using that $R_P(p_0)$ is an isometry. Continuity of $q$ and the intertwining identity give strong continuity of $R_L$. [F3, F5, F6, F7, F8, step 1.1, step 2.1, step 6.1, step 7.1]

9.1 The restriction of $T$ sends each basis bijectively onto its image. The image is linearly independent because a vanishing finite combination pulls back by $T^{-1}$ to a vanishing combination of the original basis vectors. It spans because every vector has a preimage that is a finite combination of those basis vectors. Thus the constituents have bases of equal cardinality, and step 1.2 gives equality of their complex linear dimensions. [F9, step 5.1, step 7.2, step 1.2] ∎


## Norm characters of projective quaternion units

[Open item](item-thm-projective-quaternion-norm-characters) · level 7


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice, that every family of nonempty sets has a choice function. Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra and $H=G(\mathbb A)=G(\mathbb R)\times G(\mathbb A_f)$ its full adelic projective group. Its elements are pairs of classes of real and finite quaternion units modulo nonzero real scalars and finite scalar ideles, respectively. A finite quaternion idele is a tuple of invertible local quaternions that are units in the local order at all but finitely many primes. Let $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)=B^\times/\mathbb Q^\times$ act by diagonal left multiplication and write $X=\Gamma\backslash H$ for the coset space with its quotient topology.

Fix a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$. Thus $\mu$ is a Borel measure finite on compact sets, inner regular on open sets and outer regular on Borel sets, and $\mu(Eh)=\mu(E)$ for every Borel set $E$ and $h\in H$. A one-dimensional automorphic representation here is the right action $R(h)[f]=[x\mapsto f(xh)]$ on a nonzero invariant complex line in $L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$, the space of square-integrable complex functions modulo equality almost everywhere. Such a line has the form $M=\{cv:c\in\mathbb C\}$ for a nonzero vector $v$, and invariance means $R(h)M=M$ for every $h\in H$.

Put $\mathbb A^\times=\mathbb R^\times\times\mathbb A_f^\times$, where $\mathbb A_f^\times$ is the group of tuples of nonzero local scalars that are integral units at all but finitely many primes. Give $\mathbb A_f^\times$ the restricted product topology, whose basic boxes use the groups of integral units at all but finitely many primes. Give $\mathbb R^\times$ the subspace topology from the usual metric $d_{\mathbb R}(s,t)=|s-t|$ on $\mathbb R$. Give $\mathbb A^\times$ the product of the usual topology on $\mathbb R^\times$ and this restricted product topology on $\mathbb A_f^\times$. The idele class group $\mathbb Q^\times\backslash\mathbb A^\times$ identifies ideles that differ by a diagonal rational scalar and carries the quotient group operations and quotient topology. A quadratic idele class character is a continuous homomorphism from this group to the multiplicative group $\{1,-1\}$, allowing the trivial character. The sign group has the discrete topology, so every subset is open.

Give $\mathbb C$ the topology of the metric $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)=|z-w|$. For each one-dimensional automorphic representation there is a quadratic idele class character $\eta$ such that the line consists of the classes of the continuous functions $\Gamma h\mapsto c\,\eta([N(\widetilde h)])$ for $c\in\mathbb C$. Here $\widetilde h$ is any real and finite quaternion idele lift of $h$, $N(\widetilde h)$ is the full scalar idele of coordinatewise reduced norms, and brackets on that norm denote its idele class. The reduced norm at each place is the sum of four Hamilton coordinate squares. Changing the quaternion idele lift multiplies the norm by the square of a scalar idele.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice supplies a choice function for each family of nonempty sets ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice)), as assumed in the invariant measure and right translation results used below.

[F1] The full adelic group is the product of the real and finite projective groups with each rational projective unit embedded by its class at every place ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group)). The real projective quotient is by $\mathbb R^\times$ and the finite one is by $\mathbb A_f^\times$. The finite projective group is the restricted product of $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ with respect to the images $K_\ell$ of $O_\ell^\times$, where $B_\ell$ has Hamilton coordinates in $\mathbb Q_\ell$, the $\ell$-adic completion of $\mathbb Q$. Write $\mathbb Z_\ell$ for its ring of elements of absolute value at most $1$. The local order $O_\ell$ is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of the basis of a fixed order $O$, a subring containing $1$ with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). Basic open boxes have open local factors and have factor $K_\ell$ outside a finite set of primes. The subgroup $K_f=\prod_\ell K_\ell$ is compact open ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units), Justification 4.1–5.1). In the finite quaternion idele group the allowed integral factors are $O_\ell^\times$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles)). In the finite scalar idele group the allowed integral factors are $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$. In both cases the basic boxes leave those factors unchanged at all but finitely many primes ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)).

[F2] The Hamilton norm is multiplicative over $\mathbb R$, $\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $\mathbb Q$, and $N(ax)=a^2N(x)$ for each scalar $a$ by the coordinate formula ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Definition and Justification 3.2). Projective classes with square reduced norm map to the identity under each homomorphism to a commutative group ([lem-author-eebeda62e446-projective-norm-one-commutator](item-lem-author-eebeda62e446-projective-norm-one-commutator)). Every positive rational is a rational Hamilton norm, every nonzero local scalar is a local Hamilton norm, and $N(O_\ell^\times)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ for all but finitely many $\ell$. The open identity neighborhood $1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$ consists of local squares ([lem-author-eebeda62e446-hamilton-norm-values](item-lem-author-eebeda62e446-hamilton-norm-values)).

[F3] Every nonempty set of reals bounded above has a least upper bound ([math:thm-reals-cauchy-complete](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-reals-cauchy-complete), steps 8.1–9.1). The real field therefore satisfies the least-upper-bound hypothesis that gives a nonzero square root for each positive real ([math:thm-of-square-roots](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-of-square-roots)).

[F4] On $X$ the right action is continuous and every nonzero invariant Radon measure is positive on every nonempty open subset under [A1] ([lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient)). Radon regularity means approximation from within by compact subsets for open sets and from above by open sets for Borel sets ([math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space)).

[F5] The right action on $L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$ is by linear surjective isometries and each orbit map is continuous in the $L^2$ norm, which is strong continuity ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space), [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)). Under [A1], for any compact open $U\le G(\mathbb A_f)$, a vector fixed by $G(\mathbb R)\times U$ is represented by a pullback from the discrete space $D=G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A_f)/U$. The quotient map $X\to D$ is continuous and sends $\Gamma(h_\infty,h_f)$ to the double coset $\Gamma h_f U=\{\gamma_f h_f u:\gamma\in\Gamma,\ u\in U\}$ ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space)).

[F6] A subgroup is normal if conjugation by every group element preserves it ([math:def-normal-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normal-subgroup)). The cosets of a normal subgroup form a group with product given by multiplying representatives ([math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group), [math:thm-quotient-group-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quotient-group-laws)). A map on a quotient is continuous if its composition with the quotient projection is continuous, since openness in the quotient is defined by preimages ([math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)).

[F7] Products of open sets in two spaces form a basis for their product topology ([math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology)).

[F8] A closed subspace of a compact space is compact ([math:thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-closed-subspace-of-a-compact-space-is-compact), claim 1).

[F9] The complex metric is $d_{\mathbb C}(z,w)=|z-w|$, with its restriction on the unit circle ([math:def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-metric-convergence-and-continuity)). In the metric topology, balls form a neighborhood basis ([math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)). Every metric ball of positive radius is open ([math:thm-metric-open-set-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-open-set-algebra), claim 1).

[F10] A map is continuous at a point exactly when every open neighborhood of its image contains the image of an open neighborhood of that point ([math:def-continuous-map-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-continuous-map-top)). Continuity at every point is equivalent to having open preimages of all open sets ([math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top), (a)–(b)). Every subset of a discrete space is open ([math:def-standard-topologies](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-standard-topologies)). A map into the two-element discrete sign group is continuous if both singleton preimages are open, since every subset is a union of singletons.

[F11] The groups $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ and $\mathbb A_f^\times$ are topological groups in the local subspace and restricted product topologies, respectively ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification 1.2 and 3.1). In a topological group, left translation is a homeomorphism ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion)). It sends open sets to open sets because the image under the translation is the preimage under its continuous inverse. The diagonal insertion of nonzero rationals into the finite scalar ideles is a homomorphism ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification 2.2).

[F12] For $r>0$, the ball of radius $r$ about $t$ in the usual real metric is $(t-r,t+r)$ ([math:lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-real-line-is-a-metric-space), claim 2). Its intersection with $\mathbb R^\times$ is open by [F9] and the definition of the subspace topology ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)).

[F13] In an ordered field, the product of two positive elements or two negative elements is positive, and a product of opposite signs is negative ([math:lem-of-sign-rules](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-sign-rules), claims 1–3, with commutativity for the negative-positive order).

[F14] For complex numbers $z,w$, the modulus satisfies $|zw|=|z||w|$ and $|z+w|\le |z|+|w|$ ([math:lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-conjugation-and-modulus-laws)).

[F15] The nonnegative $L^2$ norm in [F5] is a complex norm. Thus $\|cv\|_2=|c|\|v\|_2$ for every complex scalar $c$, and $\|v\|_2=0$ only if $v=0$ ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space), [math:def-complex-norm-and-complex-normed-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-norm-and-complex-normed-space)).

[F16] The complex numbers form a field, and in any field a product is zero only if at least one factor is zero ([math:thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-complex-numbers-form-a-field), [math:lem-of-no-zero-divisors](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-of-no-zero-divisors)).

[F17] A group homomorphism preserves products ([math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism)), and its kernel consists of the elements it sends to the target identity ([math:def-kernel-and-image-of-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-kernel-and-image-of-group-homomorphism)).


[F18] A compact subset is compact in its subspace topology, meaning every open cover of that subspace has a finite subcover ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space)). A subset is a subgroup if it contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F19] Scalar multiplication distributes over scalar addition in a complex vector space ([math:def-vector-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-vector-space), axiom (V3)).

[F20] In a topological space, the empty set, arbitrary unions of open sets and intersections of two open sets are open. A subset is closed exactly when its complement is open ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space), axioms (T1)–(T3)). In a subspace, the open subsets are the traces of the ambient open sets ([math:def-subspace-topology-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subspace-topology-top)).

[F21] The Borel sigma-algebra is generated by the open sets, so every open set is Borel ([math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra)). A Borel measure is a measure on this sigma-algebra and takes nonnegative values ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure)). A property holds almost everywhere if it holds outside a measurable null set ([math:def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere)). For two Borel sets with one contained in the other, the smaller set has measure at most that of the larger ([math:prop-measure-monotonicity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-measure-monotonicity)).

[F22] A finite list in a monoid is multiplied recursively by appending one factor at a time, starting from the identity ([math:def-monoid-finite-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-monoid-finite-product)). In a commutative monoid this defines a product over a finite index set, independent of enumeration, with empty product equal to the identity ([math:def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-finite-sum-in-a-commutative-monoid), using multiplicative notation). Such products split over disjoint unions and can be evaluated in either iterated order over two finite index sets ([math:lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-finite-sum-reindexing-and-fubini), claims 2–3, using multiplicative notation).

### Proof

**Given:** The group, measure and one-dimensional invariant line in the statement.

**Goal:** Exhibit a quadratic idele class character giving both the action and the stated continuous representatives.

1.1 A lift $\widetilde h=(x_\infty,(x_\ell)_\ell)$ has nonzero norms at every place by multiplicativity applied to the invertible coordinates and their inverses. Its finite norms are integral units at almost all primes by [F1] and the order-unit norm assertion of [F2], so $N(\widetilde h)\in\mathbb A^\times$. Two lifts differ by a scalar idele $a$ by [F1]. Their norms differ by $a^2$ by [F2], so a character with values in $\{1,-1\}$ will give the same value for either lift. [F1, F2, F6]

1.2 Take a nonzero generator $v$ of $M$. By invariance, $R(h)v=cv$ for some $c\in\mathbb C$. If $cv=c^{\prime}v$, then $(c-c^{\prime})v=0$ by [F19] and the additive group laws. By [F15] this gives $|c-c^{\prime}|\|v\|_2=0$, so $c=c^{\prime}$ because $\|v\|_2>0$. This defines $\lambda(h)$ uniquely by $R(h)v=\lambda(h)v$. The unit circle is an abelian group under complex multiplication. Products have modulus $1$ by [F14], and for $|z|=1$ the equality $1=|zz^{-1}|=|z||z^{-1}|$ gives $|z^{-1}|=1$. The action law and linearity in [F5] give $R(hh')v=R(h)R(h')v=\lambda(h)\lambda(h')v$. Since $v\ne0$, this gives the product law for $\lambda$. Isometry and [F15] give $\|v\|_2=\|R(h)v\|_2=|\lambda(h)|\|v\|_2$, and $\|v\|_2>0$ since $v\ne0$. Hence $|\lambda(h)|=1$, so $\lambda$ is a homomorphism to the unit circle $\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|=1\}$ by [F17]. Equip the unit circle with the restricted metric in [F9]. The identity $\|R(h)v-R(h')v\|_2=|\lambda(h)-\lambda(h')|\|v\|_2$ from [F15] and $\|v\|_2>0$ show that, for each $\epsilon>0$, strong continuity makes $|\lambda(h)-\lambda(h')|<\epsilon$ throughout an open neighborhood of $h'$. Since open balls form a neighborhood basis, [F10] shows that $\lambda$ is continuous. A real projective unit has a lift of positive norm, which is a square by [F3]. The square-norm commutator result therefore gives $\lambda|_{G(\mathbb R)}=1$. [F1, F2, F3, F5, F9, F10, F14, F15, F17, F19]

2.1 Insert $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ into $G(\mathbb A_f)$ by taking the identity at every other prime. The preimage of each basic open box is its open $\ell$-factor when all other factors contain the identity and is empty otherwise. The real component of this insertion into $H$ is constant at the identity. Preimages of open sets are therefore open by the product basis and the union law in [F20]. Thus the insertion is a continuous homomorphism by [F1], [F7] and [F10], and we denote the restriction of $\lambda$ along it by $\lambda_\ell$. For $a\in\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, choose $x\in B_\ell^\times$ with $N(x)=a$ and define $\theta_\ell(a)=\lambda_\ell([x])$. If $N(y)=a$, then $N(xy^{-1})=1$, so [F2] gives $\lambda_\ell([x])=\lambda_\ell([y])$. Products of lifts give the homomorphism law. The value on any local square is $1$ by [F2], so $\theta_\ell(a)^2=\theta_\ell(a^2)=1$. With $z=\theta_\ell(a)$, factoring gives $(z-1)(z+1)=z^2-1=0$, so [F16] gives $z=1$ or $z=-1$. For any group homomorphism $\psi$ to the signs, $\psi(e)=\psi(e)^2$ gives $\psi(e)=1$, and applying the product law to $uu^{-1}=e$ gives $\psi(u^{-1})=\psi(u)^{-1}$. The kernel is therefore a subgroup by [F18], since it contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses. If $\psi(b)=\delta$, then $\psi(y)=\delta$ exactly when $\psi(b^{-1}y)=1$, so the fiber of $\delta$ is $b\ker\psi$. For $\psi=\theta_\ell$, the kernel contains the open neighborhood $1+\ell^3\mathbb Z_\ell$ of $1$ by [F2]. Each kernel element translates this neighborhood to an open neighborhood of itself still in the kernel by [F11]. The kernel is the union of these translates, so it is open by [F20]. Each nonempty singleton fiber is a translate of the open kernel, so both singleton preimages are open and $\theta_\ell$ is continuous into the discrete sign group by [F10]. [F1, F2, F7, F10, F11, F16, F17, F18, F20, step 1.2]

3.1 The open ball of radius $1$ about $1$ in the unit-circle metric of [F9] excludes $-1$. By [F7] and [F10], continuity at the identity gives a basic open box about the finite projective identity that $\lambda$ maps into that ball with the real component fixed at the identity. Outside the finite exceptional set of this box its factors are $K_\ell$ by [F1]. For each such prime, the single-place copy of $K_\ell$ lies in the box because every other factor contains the identity, so $\lambda_\ell|_{K_\ell}=1$ there by step 2.1. At the primes where $N(O_\ell^\times)=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ as well, this implies $\theta_\ell|_{\mathbb Z_\ell^\times}=1$. For an input $a_f$, only finitely many factors $\theta_\ell(a_\ell)$ differ from $1$. Define $\theta(a_f)=\prod_{\ell\in S}\theta_\ell(a_\ell)$ on any finite set $S$ containing these primes, with empty product $1$. By [F22], the product is independent of the enumeration of $S$. Enlarging $S$ leaves the value unchanged by disjoint splitting and the recursion for the product of identity factors. Two possible sets give the same value by passing to their union, which can be listed by appending the elements of the second set missing from the first. For $a_f,b_f$, take a common finite set $S$ containing the primes with nonidentity factors for either input. It also works for $a_fb_f$ by the local product laws. Applying finite Fubini from [F22] to the sign factors indexed by $S\times\{0,1\}$, with $0$ for $a_f$ and $1$ for $b_f$, gives $\prod_{\ell\in S}(\theta_\ell(a_\ell)\theta_\ell(b_\ell))=(\prod_{\ell\in S}\theta_\ell(a_\ell))(\prod_{\ell\in S}\theta_\ell(b_\ell))$. Together with the empty product at the identity, this proves the homomorphism law for $\theta$. Its kernel contains an open box using the open local kernels at the finitely many exceptional primes and the groups of integral units elsewhere. Each nonempty singleton fiber is a coset of the kernel by step 2.1. Translating the box by any element of a singleton fiber gives an open neighborhood in that fiber by [F11]. Each fiber is the union of these open neighborhoods, with an empty fiber open as well by [F20]. Thus $\theta$ is continuous into the discrete sign group by [F10]. [F1, F2, F7, F9, F10, F11, F20, F22, step 1.2, step 2.1]

4.1 Fix $h=(h_\infty,h_f)$ with a lift whose finite coordinates are $x_\ell$, and let $\varepsilon>0$. By continuity of $\lambda$ and the product topology, choose a basic box about $h_f$ such that $|\lambda(h_\infty,g_f)-\lambda(h)|<\varepsilon$ for every $g_f$ in that box. Let $S$ be its finite set of exceptional primes, enlarged by every prime where $\theta_\ell(N(x_\ell))\ne1$. There are finitely many such primes by steps 1.1 and 3.1. The tuple $g_f$ equal to $h_f$ on $S$ and the identity elsewhere is in the box, since the other factors of the box are $K_\ell$. In any enumeration of $S$, the product of the corresponding single-place elements is $g_f$, since each coordinate receives its prescribed value and all other contributions are identities. Iterating the homomorphism law along the finite-list recursion in [F22] and using the trivial real factor gives $\lambda(h_\infty,g_f)=\prod_{\ell\in S}\lambda_\ell([x_\ell])$. Each factor is $\theta_\ell(N(x_\ell))$ by step 2.1. The enlarged $S$ contains all primes with nonidentity factors, so this product is $\theta((N(x_\ell))_\ell)$ by step 3.1. The distance from $\lambda(h)$ to this same value is less than $\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon>0$, hence $\lambda(h)=\theta((N(x_\ell))_\ell)$. [F1, F7, F9, F10, F22, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 The equality in step 4.1 shows that $\lambda$ takes values in $\{1,-1\}$. The open balls of radius $1$ about $1$ and $-1$ in the unit-circle metric of [F9] have preimages under $\lambda|_{G(\mathbb A_f)}$ equal to the kernel and its complement, respectively. These preimages are open by continuity and [F10]. The kernel is a subgroup by step 2.1. The intersection $U=K_f\cap\ker(\lambda|_{G(\mathbb A_f)})$ contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses because both factors have those properties, so $U$ is a subgroup by [F18]. It is open as the intersection of two open sets by [F20]. Its complement in $K_f$ is the trace of the open complement of the kernel, so it is open in $K_f$ by [F20]. Thus $U$ is closed in $K_f$. Since $U$ is closed in the compact space $K_f$, it is compact by [F8]. The vector $v$ is fixed by $G(\mathbb R)\times U$. By [F5], it is represented by a function $f$ pulled back from $D$. Every function on the discrete space $D$ is continuous by [F10] because every preimage is open there. The quotient map in [F5] is continuous, so the pullback is continuous by taking successive preimages. [F1, F5, F8, F9, F10, F18, F20, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 4.1]

6.1 For fixed $h$, put $b(x)=f(xh)-\lambda(h)f(x)$. By [F14] and $|\lambda(h)|=1$, $|b(x)-b(y)|\le |f(xh)-f(yh)|+|f(x)-f(y)|$. Given $y$ and $\varepsilon>0$, each term is less than $\varepsilon/2$ on some open neighborhood of $y$ by continuity of the right action and of $f$. The intersection is open by [F20], so [F10] gives continuity of $b$. For any $x$ with $b(x)\ne0$, the open ball of radius $|b(x)|/2$ about $b(x)$ omits zero by [F9]. Its preimage under $b$ is an open neighborhood of $x$ on which $b$ is nonzero by [F10]. Hence $E=\{x:b(x)\ne0\}$ is open by [F20] and therefore Borel by [F21]. The relation $R(h)v=\lambda(h)v$ means that $b$ vanishes outside a Borel null set $Z$ by [F21]. Thus $E\subseteq Z$ and $0\le\mu(E)\le\mu(Z)=0$ by [F21]. By [F4], every nonempty open subset has positive measure. Hence $E=\varnothing$. Therefore $f(\Gamma h)=\lambda(h)f(\Gamma)$ for every $h$. Since $v\ne0$, this gives $f(\Gamma)\ne0$. For $\gamma\in\Gamma$, the equality $\Gamma\gamma=\Gamma$ now gives $\lambda(\gamma)=1$. [F1, F4, F9, F10, F14, F20, F21, step 1.2, step 5.1]

7.1 Given $r\in\mathbb Q_{>0}$, take a rational lift of norm $r$ by [F2] and evaluate step 4.1 on its diagonal projective class. By step 6.1 this gives $\theta(r_f)=1$, where $r_f$ is the constant finite idele with value $r$. On $\mathbb A^\times$ put $\widetilde\eta(a_\infty,a_f)=\theta((\operatorname{sgn} a_\infty)_f a_f)$, where $\operatorname{sgn} t$ is $1$ for $t>0$ and $-1$ for $t<0$, with its subscript $f$ indicating diagonal insertion. The three product sign cases in [F13] give $\operatorname{sgn}(st)=\operatorname{sgn}(s)\operatorname{sgn}(t)$ for nonzero reals $s,t$. Diagonal insertion preserves multiplication, so this identity and the homomorphism law for $\theta$ give the homomorphism law for $\widetilde\eta$. For $\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}$ let $I_\epsilon$ be the half-line on which the sign is $\epsilon$. For each $t\in I_\epsilon$, the interval $(t-|t|/2,t+|t|/2)$ has both endpoints of sign $\epsilon$, so it is an open neighborhood of $t$ in $\mathbb R^\times$ contained in $I_\epsilon$ by [F12]. The union of these neighborhoods is $I_\epsilon$, so $I_\epsilon$ is open by [F20]. For each value $\delta\in\{1,-1\}$, its preimage on $I_\epsilon\times\mathbb A_f^\times$ is the rectangle $I_\epsilon\times\theta^{-1}(\{\theta(\epsilon_f)^{-1}\delta\})$. The second factor is open by continuity of $\theta$ from step 3.1. Each singleton preimage is the union of two rectangles, so it is open by [F7] and [F20]. Thus $\widetilde\eta$ is continuous into the discrete sign group by [F10]. [F2, F7, F10, F11, F12, F13, F20, step 3.1, step 4.1, step 6.1]

8.1 The real and finite scalar embeddings preserve the identity, products and inverses, so the diagonal rationals form a subgroup of $\mathbb A^\times$ by [F18]. Multiplication in $\mathbb A^\times$ is commutative coordinatewise, so conjugation fixes each rational scalar and the subgroup is normal by [F6]. Thus [F6] supplies a well-defined group product on idele classes. On a diagonal rational $q$ the value of $\widetilde\eta$ is $\theta(|q|_f)=1$. For a scalar idele $a$, the product law gives $\widetilde\eta(qa)=\widetilde\eta(q)\widetilde\eta(a)=\widetilde\eta(a)$. Thus $\eta([a])=\widetilde\eta(a)$ is well defined on idele classes. The quotient product in [F6] gives $\eta([a][b])=\widetilde\eta(ab)=\eta([a])\eta([b])$. Its composite with the quotient projection equals the continuous $\widetilde\eta$, so $\eta$ is continuous by [F6]. Since the real reduced norm of a lift is positive, step 4.1 gives $\lambda(h)=\eta([N(\widetilde h)])$. The pointwise formula in step 6.1 yields the claimed continuous representatives on the whole line, with lift independence from step 1.1. [F1, F2, F6, F11, F17, F18, step 1.1, step 4.1, step 6.1, step 7.1] ∎

### Source notes

Ellenberg, Michel and Venkatesh state the norm-character formula in their finite-level argument in §8.4, pp. 39–40. The proof here derives the formula on the full adelic quotient. [Linnik’s ergodic method and the distribution of integer points on spheres](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1001.0897#page=40)


## Definite quaternion transfer to cuspidal weight two

[Open item](item-thm-definite-quaternion-jacquet-langlands-weight-two) · level 7


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton quaternion algebra with basis $1,i,j,k$ and the relations $i^2=j^2=-1$ and $ij=k=-ji$. Its scalar extension $B_{\mathbb R}=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb R$ is the real Hamilton division algebra. Let $G=PB^\times$ be its group of units modulo scalars. Let $\pi$ be a non-one-dimensional irreducible automorphic representation of $G(\mathbb A)$, meaning an irreducible unitary constituent of right translation on $L^2(G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A))$ for a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure on the quotient. Here a Radon measure is a Borel measure finite on compact sets, outer regular on Borel sets and inner regular on open sets. Assume the real component $\pi_\infty$ is trivial, meaning the real projective units act as the identity.

Its global Jacquet–Langlands transfer is an irreducible cuspidal representation $\Pi$ of $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb A)$. Cuspidality means occurrence under right translation in the square-integral closure of the continuous square-integrable functions on the split automorphic quotient whose upper unipotent constant term vanishes at every translate. The constant term averages along the matrices with ones on the diagonal, zero below it and an upper right entry ranging over $\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A$ with Haar probability measure. The split quotient uses a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure. The real component of $\Pi$ has weight-two discrete-series type, with the holomorphic and antiholomorphic weight-two models on restriction to determinant-one real matrices.

At every split finite prime $\ell$, meaning $B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell\simeq M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, the local factors $\pi_\ell$ and $\Pi_\ell$ are unitarily equivalent under the resulting identification with $\mathrm{PGL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$. Here local factors are the single-place representations on the Hilbert spaces in the restricted tensor product of the full-unit pullbacks. Choose a unit reference vector $e_v$ in each Hilbert space, fixed by the subgroup defining the restricted product of the adelic group at almost every finite place. The restricted Hilbert tensor product is the completion of the span of elementary tensors whose entries are $e_v$ outside a finite set. Finite sets of places give ordinary finite tensor products, identified by inserting the reference vectors at the added places. The pairing of elementary tensors $\bigotimes_v x_v$ and $\bigotimes_v y_v$ is the finite product of the local pairings $\langle x_v,y_v\rangle$ over the places where either entry differs from $e_v$. The action applies each local operator to its own entry and extends by continuity. The local factors are strongly continuous unitary representations. At split finite places their spaces of smooth vectors are irreducible and admissible. A split local factor is unramified when it has nonzero fixed vectors under the image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. The two factors are unramified at the same split primes and there their spherical Hecke eigencharacters agree. Such a character gives the scalar on the fixed line of each compactly supported function invariant on both sides under that subgroup, with one term per coset in its action. Every odd prime is split. At each odd prime use a splitting carrying the completed Hurwitz order onto $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ for both the local comparison and the unramified test. It identifies the projective image of the Hurwitz order's units with the image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$.

The representation $\Pi$, viewed on $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb A)$ by composition with the scalar quotient, is generated by the unitary adelic lift of a normalized holomorphic weight-two cuspidal newform $f$ on $\Gamma_0(N)$ for some positive integer $N$. Here $\Gamma_0(N)$ consists of determinant-one integral matrices with lower left entry divisible by $N$. The form is new in the sense of orthogonality to the lower-level forms pulled to level $N$, is an eigenform for the classical weight-two Hecke operator $T_m$ at every positive integer $m$ and has first coefficient one in its Fourier expansion in $q=\exp(2\pi i z)$. Its Dirichlet character is trivial, meaning it is one on the invertible classes modulo $N$.

For each finite prime $q$, let $a_q$ be the least integer $n\ge0$ for which the full matrix local factor has a nonzero vector fixed by $K_{1,q}(n)$, the subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_q)$ with bottom row congruent to $(0,1)$ modulo $q^n\mathbb Z_q$. At $n=0$ this is the full integral group. The exact level is the finite conductor $N=\prod_q q^{a_q}$, with only finitely many $a_q>0$. At each split unramified prime $\ell$ one has $\ell\nmid N$.

For the lift, write $g=\gamma g_\infty k$, where the rational factor $\gamma$ is an element of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q)$ embedded diagonally by using the same matrix at every place. Here $g_\infty\in\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)$ has positive determinant and is supported at the real place, and $k\in K_0(N)$ is supported at the finite places. $K_0(N)$ is the product over primes $q$ of the subgroups of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_q)$ with lower left entry in $q^{v_q(N)}\mathbb Z_q$, where $v_q$ is the normalized valuation on $\mathbb Q_q$ and $v_q(N)$ is the exponent of $q$ in $N$. The lift is the well-defined function $\phi_f(g)=(f|_2g_\infty)(i)$, with $i$ the imaginary unit and $(f|_2 A)(z)=\det(A)(cz+d)^{-2}f((az+b)/(cz+d))$ for a positive-determinant matrix $A$ with rows $(a,b)$ and $(c,d)$. Generation means taking the closed linear span of the right translates of $\phi_f$ in the cuspidal space.

### Facts & Assumptions

[F1] The full quaternion adelic unit group is $B_{\mathbb R}^\times\times\widehat B^\times$, with the finite restricted product taken relative to the units of the completed Hurwitz orders. For the rational Hamilton units, the scalar-quotient identifications of adelic groups and rational orbits give the same irreducible constituents and fixed vectors on passing between projective units and full units with trivial scalar action. The same passage applies to split matrices. [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles) [def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group) [def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space) [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups) [lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-projective-automorphic-passage](item-lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-projective-automorphic-passage)

[F2] The classical degree-two global transfer sends a non-one-dimensional irreducible quaternion automorphic constituent to an irreducible cuspidal constituent with the same central character. It agrees with the quaternion factors at the split places and uses classical local transfer at the nonsplit places. With unitary central character these discrete constituents are restricted Hilbert tensor products of irreducible unitary local representations, with integral-subgroup-fixed reference vectors at almost all finite places. The split matrix factors have irreducible admissible smooth representations at the finite places. A smooth vector is fixed by some open subgroup, and admissibility means that the fixed space of each compact open subgroup is finite dimensional. The smooth cuspidal realization of the transferred constituent supplies a nonzero smooth cusp vector square-integrable modulo the center. Its upper unipotent constant term vanishes identically with Haar probability on the unipotent quotient. See the global transfer, Hilbert factorization, classical newform and smooth cuspidal realization inputs.

[F3] Scalar extension to $\mathbb R$ keeps the Hamilton basis and multiplication table. The resulting algebra is a division algebra by the inverse formula for nonzero real quaternions. [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions) [math:thm-quaternions-form-a-division-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-quaternions-form-a-division-ring) For the real Hamilton algebra the local transfer takes the representation with positive-scalar character $\omega_+$ and norm-one factor $\operatorname{Sym}^n(\mathbb C^2)$ to the split discrete series with positive-scalar character $\omega_+$ and lowest absolute rotation weight $n+2$. The parity of $n$ is determined by the central character. The split partner restricts to determinant-one real matrices as the direct sum of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic models of that weight. See the real local transfer input and [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-real-weight-two-discrete-series](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-real-weight-two-discrete-series).

[F4] At a split prime let $J_\ell$ be the image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ under the projective identification. At every odd prime the chosen splitting carries the completed Hurwitz order onto $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$, so its units map onto $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. Any two split algebra identifications differ by conjugation by an invertible matrix. The Axiom of Choice implies countable choice, as required by the spherical fixed-line theorem. An irreducible admissible smooth local factor of a unitary representation has a one-dimensional spherical fixed space if it is unramified. The normalization assigns volume one to the integral subgroup, equivalently one summand per coset. An intertwiner identifies the Hecke actions with this convention. [def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation) [thm-hurwitz-integral-splitting-at-odd-primes](item-thm-hurwitz-integral-splitting-at-odd-primes) [thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character](item-thm-normalized-degree-two-spherical-character) [lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-spherical-action-under-equivalence](item-lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-spherical-action-under-equivalence) [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice) [def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)
