[F5] The rational case of the classical newform input constructs a holomorphic weight-two cusp form of trivial character at the conductor level, with the lift in the given cuspidal representation. It has normalized ideal coefficient at $(1)$ and is an eigenform for the prime operators away from the level. For classical newness apply the inverse lift in [F7] to this same vector. The classical old subspace is the span of $g(tz)$ for $g$ a cusp form on $\Gamma_0(M)$, with $M$ a proper positive divisor of $N$ and $t$ a positive divisor of $N/M$. Newness is orthogonality to this span for the Petersson pairing. The adelic decomposition and slash-action formula in the Statement give the lift. The classical newform input also gives the lower-level degeneracy translates and the normalized Petersson pairing. Use the classical Hecke operators of the normalized-newform definition with the character extended by zero on nonunits. See [def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-weight-two-cusp-form), [def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform](item-def-author-e2fc0f4318ab-normalized-weight-two-newform) and [math:def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-dirichlet-character-modulo-q).

[F6] Every finite smooth local factor of a cuspidal matrix representation is irreducible, admissible and generic. Here genericity is the existence of a nonzero Whittaker functional, a linear functional transforming on the upper unipotent subgroup by a nontrivial additive character of the local field. The generic factor is isomorphic to an irreducible induced representation of Langlands type. The finite-place newvector theorem for such an induced representation gives the least congruence index with nonzero $K_{1,q}(n)$-fixed space and identifies the local conductor ideal as $q^{a_q}\mathbb Z_q$. See the finite newvector input.

[F7] The rational classical level is the product of the local conductor prime powers. The local test in Corbett–Saha requires the upper left entry congruent to one and the lower left entry congruent to zero at positive index. At index zero its literal upper-left condition requires a unit, while the global group $K_1(N)$ in their §3.1 uses the full integral group at primes not dividing $N$. In the smooth automorphic model the vectors fixed by this global group and transforming by $e^{ik\theta}$ under the rotations in (21) form a one-dimensional space. The inverse lift in (22) recovers a classical newform at that level, of weight $k$ and with the Dirichlet character attached to the central character. Use unique prime factorization for divisibility of the product. See the conductor product input and [math:thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-fundamental-theorem-of-arithmetic).

[F8] The full adelic groups are locally compact Hausdorff, and the two orbit spaces modulo rational points and central ideles are Hausdorff. Their identity cosets are closed, so their inverse images are closed subgroups of the full groups. The orbit homeomorphisms preserve Borel sets, open sets and compact sets, hence preserve Radon regularity in the stated convention. [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space) Nonzero invariant Radon measures on these quotients are unique up to positive scale, by inversion and the quotient measure input. Multiplication of the measure by a positive constant leaves the square-integrable classes and closed subspaces unchanged and rescales the norm uniformly. [lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-projective-automorphic-passage](item-lem-author-34e2e6a0519f-projective-automorphic-passage) [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-cuspidal-spectrum](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-cuspidal-spectrum)

[F9] An irreducible unitary admissible smooth representation of a split finite matrix group has only one invariant Hermitian inner product up to positive scale. See the local inner product input.

[F10] For complex Hilbert spaces under countable choice, the product pairing on the algebraic tensor product is an inner product and gives the elementary tensor norm as the product of the factor norms. A normed space has a Banach completion, and the completion of an inner-product space is Hilbert with the extended pairing. [math:lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction) [math:thm-metric-completion-carries-a-unique-banach-space-structure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-metric-completion-carries-a-unique-banach-space-structure) [math:thm-completion-of-an-inner-product-space-is-hilbert](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-completion-of-an-inner-product-space-is-hilbert)

[F11] Fix a positive integer $m$. Use the set $X_m$ of finite adelic matrices consisting of the tuples $x=(x_p)_p$ in the finite adelic matrix group with integral entries everywhere and with $v_p(\det x_p)=v_p(m)$ at every prime $p$. At each $p\mid N$ require the upper left entry to be a unit and the lower left entry to lie in $N\mathbb Z_p$. The corresponding full adelic set is $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)^+\times X_m$, where the real determinant is positive. The real group $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb R)^+$ is also the real part of the subgroup acting on the left, so taking cosets there leaves just the finite cosets $K_0(N)x$, each represented with real component the identity. The valuation is additive on products, agrees with the rational valuation on $\mathbb Q^\times$ and vanishes exactly on $\mathbb Z_p^\times$. [def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation](item-def-author-24fda87362bc-local-adic-valuation) [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups)

[F12] The ring $\mathbb Z_p$ is the unit ball of $\mathbb Q_p$, whose absolute value on nonzero rational $r$ is $|r|_p=p^{-v_p(r)}$. A rational number is integral if it belongs to every $\mathbb Z_p$, since a prime in the reduced denominator would give absolute value greater than one. Bézout's identity gives an integral linear combination equal to the positive greatest common divisor of two integers that are not both zero. Division by a positive integer has a unique remainder from zero up to one less than the divisor. [math:thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-p-adic-completion-agrees-with-the-fraction-field-of-zp) [math:def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-p-adic-absolute-value-on-the-rationals) [math:thm-bezout-identity](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-bezout-identity) [math:thm-division-algorithm-in-z](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-division-algorithm-in-z)

[F13] Under countable choice a bounded linear map from a dense normed subspace to a Banach space extends uniquely to a bounded linear map on the ambient normed space. A Hilbert space is Banach in its induced norm. [Extension from a dense subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-extension-of-a-bounded-map-from-a-dense-subspace) [Hilbert space completeness](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space)

[F14] The integral subgroup $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ in the split finite matrix group is compact open in its entrywise topology, as shown in the construction of the split cuspidal quotient. The ring $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is open in $\mathbb Q_\ell$, and field multiplication by a nonzero constant and addition by a constant are homeomorphisms. Give the local scalar quotient the quotient topology. [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-cuspidal-spectrum](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-cuspidal-spectrum) [def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups](item-def-author-34e2e6a0519f-split-adelic-matrix-groups)

[F15] For matrices of the same size over a commutative ring the determinant is multiplicative. For an invertible matrix over a field its adjugate is the determinant times the inverse. In size two the adjugate sends $(a,b,c,d)$ in row order to $(d,-b,-c,a)$, so applying it twice gives the original matrix. [math:thm-determinant-multiplicative](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-determinant-multiplicative) [math:cor-inverse-matrix-by-adjugate](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-inverse-matrix-by-adjugate)

### Proof

**Given:** The projective Hamilton automorphic constituent $\pi$ and its trivial real action.

**Goal:** Obtain its cuspidal weight-two transfer, compare the split local actions and identify the corresponding classical newform.

1.1 Pull back $\pi$ along the quotient from the full quaternion adelic unit group in [F1]. The orbit identification places the pullback in the square-integrable full-unit space modulo rational units and scalar ideles. The transported measure is Radon under the orbit homeomorphism and differs from the invariant measure in the automorphic realization by a positive factor by [F8]. The pullback is irreducible and still has dimension greater than one, since invariant subspaces and the representation space are preserved. Its central character is trivial, and its real action is trivial because that action factors through the projective real action. [given, F1, F8]

1.2 For each family of local Hilbert spaces supplied by [F2], use the Axiom of Choice to select unit reference vectors with the stated tail invariance. Take the places in order, beginning with the real place and then the primes in increasing order. Form the finite Hilbert tensor products by iterating [F10] with the countable choice in [F4]. Passing to the next stage by tensoring with the next unit vector is an isometric linear embedding by the product pairing. Thus the compatible union has the product inner product, which gives the same pairing regardless of the stage where two elementary tensors are compared. Finite sums of elementary tensors are dense at each stage, and [F10] gives their span a Hilbert completion. For an adelic element, [F1] and the split matrix restricted product ensure that its entries fix the reference vectors outside a finite set. The coordinatewise action is defined on each finite tensor span by taking a stage containing that set and all the varying entries. Multilinearity defines the corresponding map on finite tensors, and the product pairing shows that it is an isometry. The inverse element gives its inverse and the local group laws give the action law, which extends by density to the completion. For an elementary tensor, near the identity the tail is fixed on a basic open box and continuity on the finitely many remaining entries follows from the local orbit maps and the product norm. Indeed, the difference of two finite elementary tensors expands into a sum replacing one factor at a time. Linearity, density and unitarity then give continuity on the completion. Inserting a vector at a single place and taking the reference vectors elsewhere gives an isometric embedding that intertwines the group supported at that place. [given, F1, F2, F4, F10]

2.1 Apply [F2] to the full-unit constituent of step 1.1 with the trivial unitary central character. This gives the full cuspidal matrix representation $\widetilde\Pi$ with trivial central character. Take a nonzero smooth cusp vector $u$ in its square-integrable realization from [F2]. It is invariant under left rational matrices and scalar ideles, so it defines a continuous function on the full-unit orbit space by the quotient topology. The upper unipotent quotient in [F2] identifies with $\mathbb Q\backslash\mathbb A$ via the upper right entry, with the same Haar probability as in the Statement. For every right translate $R(h)u$, its constant term at $g$ equals the constant term of $u$ at $gh$, and thus vanishes. These translates remain continuous by continuity of right translation and square-integrable by measure invariance. Their closed linear span is nonzero and invariant, hence equals $\widetilde\Pi$ by irreducibility. The quotient homeomorphism in [F1] preserves each unipotent average under pullback and identifies the two square-integral closures. It therefore realizes the projective constituent $\Pi$ in the specified cuspidal space, using the stated split quotient measure by [F8]. The real scalar extension in [F3] identifies the nonsplit real factor to which local transfer applies. The single-place embedding in step 1.2 identifies triviality of the global action of real quaternion units with triviality on the real factor. The trivial Hamilton representation has trivial positive-scalar character and norm-one part $\operatorname{Sym}^0(\mathbb C^2)$. Setting $n=0$ in [F3] gives both weight-two models on restriction to the determinant-one group. [F1, F2, F3, F8, step 1.1, step 1.2]

3.1 At a split finite prime $\ell$, [F2] identifies the smooth representations of the full local factors. If the split identification changes by conjugation by $C$ as in [F4], compose the intertwiner on the matrix side with the action of $C$. This intertwines the actions for the new identification. The cuspidal matrix factor has a nonzero smooth space by [F6], and the split smooth equivalence makes the quaternion smooth space nonzero as well. On either side the smooth vectors form a linear subspace because two open fixing subgroups have an open intersection. Translation by a group element carries vectors fixed by an open subgroup to vectors fixed by its conjugate, which is open. The closure of the smooth subspace is therefore nonzero and invariant, so irreducibility of the local unitary factor in [F2] makes that closure the whole Hilbert space. Pulling the target inner product back along the smooth intertwiner gives another invariant inner product on the source smooth space, a positive scalar multiple of its original inner product by [F9]. Rescale the smooth intertwiner to be isometric. Its inverse is also isometric on the other smooth space since the smooth intertwiner is bijective. The two smooth domains are dense by the preceding argument, and both target Hilbert spaces are Banach by [F13]. With countable choice from [F4], extend the rescaled map and its inverse using [F13]. Their compositions agree with the identities on dense subspaces, hence are the identities by continuity. Norm preservation and equivariance pass from the dense smooth space to the whole space by continuity. This gives the split unitary intertwiner. [F2, F4, F6, F9, F13, step 2.1]

3.2 The cuspidality from step 2.1 gives finite local genericity by [F6], so each smooth local factor is of Langlands type and the finite-place newvector theorem applies. The congruence groups $K_{1,q}(n)$ are open, since the entry conditions use the open sets $q^n\mathbb Z_q$ and $1+q^n\mathbb Z_q$ inside the open integral matrix group. Their Hilbert fixed vectors are therefore smooth, so the smooth test gives the stated $a_q$. For almost all primes the reference vectors in [F2] are fixed by the whole integral group $K_{1,q}(0)$, so $a_q=0$ there. For positive $n$, conjugation by $W=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-q^n&0\end{pmatrix}$ sends the entries $(a,b,c,d)$ from the convention in [F7] to $(d,-c/q^n,-q^nb,a)$ in row order. These entries are integral, the determinant is still a unit, and the bottom row is congruent to $(0,1)$. Since $W^2=-q^n I$, conjugation is its own inverse and carries the bottom-row subgroup back to the upper-left subgroup by the same entry computation. The action of $W$ thus identifies the two fixed spaces. At zero let $S_q$ be the set of integral invertible matrices with unit upper left entry, the literal local test in [F7]. If $g$ in the full integral group has nonunit upper left entry, its lower left entry is a unit because its first column is nonzero modulo $q$. With $E=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$, both $E$ and $Eg$ belong to $S_q$, since adding a unit to a nonunit gives a unit in $\mathbb Z_q$. Hence a vector fixed by every element of $S_q$ is fixed by $E^{-1}(Eg)=g$ as well. The two zero-index fixed spaces coincide, and the positive-index comparison then gives the same exponent at every prime. [F2, F6, F7, F11, F14, step 2.1]

4.1 At a split prime $\ell$, a local scalar embeds as a central idele supported at $\ell$. By step 1.2 its global action intertwines its local action under the corresponding isometric embedding. It acts trivially on both global representations, so injectivity of the embeddings forces trivial action on both local factors. Thus the equivalence descends to the projective group. The local projection is open because the preimage of the image of an open set is a union of its scalar translates, which are open. The orbit maps of the descended actions are continuous because their pullbacks are continuous. Since the projection is surjective, invariant subspaces are exactly the same for the two actions. A fixing subgroup that is open pulls back to an open fixing subgroup, and an open fixing subgroup upstairs projects to an open fixing subgroup downstairs, so the smooth spaces agree. For a compact open subgroup $J$ of the projective group, intersect its open preimage with $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. The intersection is an open subgroup of the full matrix group and is closed in the compact integral group because its other cosets there are open. It is therefore compact open, and its fixed space contains the $J$-fixed space. Admissibility in [F2] bounds that space by a finite dimension. Via the split equivalence this proves smooth irreducibility and admissibility for both projective factors. The full integral subgroup and its projective image have the same fixed vectors. The full integral subgroup is open, so its fixed vectors belong to the smooth local factors. Under [F4] the nonzero fixed spaces are lines and the split intertwiner identifies all normalized spherical Hecke scalars on them. At odd primes the same integral splitting from [F4] identifies the projective images of the Hurwitz units and the integral matrix units. [F1, F2, F4, F14, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 By step 2.1 the full matrix representation is cuspidal of real weight-two type with trivial central character. By [F7] and step 3.2 its conductor level is $N=\prod_q q^{a_q}$. Use [F5] to obtain the holomorphic weight-two cusp form $f$ and its lift $\phi_f$ in this constituent. The ideal coefficient at $(1)$ is the coefficient of $q^1$, since the rational narrow class representative can be taken to be $1$. The lift is fixed by $K_0(N)$, hence by the product of the upper-left congruence subgroups in [F7]. For the rotation $k_\theta=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$, one has $k_\theta i=i$ and the slash factor at $i$ is $(\cos\theta-i\sin\theta)^{-2}=e^{2i\theta}$. Applying the lift formula gives $\phi_f(gk_\theta)=e^{2i\theta}\phi_f(g)$. The restriction of the lift formula to the real group is inverse to (22), so [F7] identifies this same $f$ as a classical newform. For each proper divisor $M$ of $N$ and each $t\mid N/M$, rational left invariance turns the finite right translate by $\operatorname{diag}(t^{-1},1)$ of a lower-level lift into the real slash by $\operatorname{diag}(t,1)$. In the degeneracy formula of [F5] the multiplier $t^{-1}$ cancels the weight-two slash factor $t$, giving $g(z)\mapsto g(tz)$. Thus the proper-level degeneracy images give precisely the old space in [F5]. At weight two the Petersson integrand uses $y^2\,dx\,dy/y^2=dx\,dy$, so the positive volume normalization in that formula leaves orthogonality unchanged. Consequently $f$ is orthogonal to the old subspace in the stated convention. The lift belongs to the one-dimensional space in [F7]. The closed span of the right translates of $\phi_f$ is the whole constituent by irreducibility. At a split unramified prime $\ell$, step 4.1 gives a nonzero full integral fixed space, so $a_\ell=0$. Unique prime factorization gives $\ell\nmid N$, including when the product of the positive-exponent factors is empty and $N=1$. [F5, F7, step 2.1, step 3.2, step 4.1]

6.1 Fix $m\ge1$. For the finite cosets in [F11], take $x\in X_m$ and apply the adelic decomposition in [F5] to the adele with finite part $x^{-1}$ and real part the identity. The rational factor $\gamma$ has the same matrix at every place and satisfies $\gamma g_\infty=I$ at the real place, so $A=\gamma^{-1}$ has positive determinant. Inverting the finite equation gives $x=k^{-1}A_f$, with $A_f$ the finite tuple consisting of that same rational matrix $A$ at every prime. Multiplication by $K_0(N)$ on either side preserves $X_m$. The entries stay integral. By determinant multiplicativity in [F15] the determinant is multiplied at each prime by a local unit of valuation zero, so additivity preserves the determinant ideal. At a level prime the lower left entry stays in $N\mathbb Z_p$ and the upper left entry is multiplied modulo $p$ by a unit. The upper left entry of an element of $K_0(N)$ is a unit there by its unit determinant and its zero lower left entry modulo $p$. Both matrices are upper triangular modulo $N\mathbb Z_p$ there. By [F12] the entries of $A$ are therefore integers, its lower left entry is divisible by $N$ by applying the same test after dividing by $N$, and its upper left entry is coprime to $N$. The normalized local valuations on the rational determinant of $A$ agree with its rational prime exponents. These exponents are those of $m$, so its positive determinant is $m$ by prime factorization. Two such rational matrices give the same coset exactly when they differ by left multiplication by $\Gamma_0(N)$. Indeed their rational left multiplier then belongs at every finite prime to the local level group, so its entries are integral, its lower left entry is divisible by $N$ and its positive determinant is a unit at every prime, hence is one. Conversely every such $\Gamma_0(N)$ multiplier has its finite part in $K_0(N)$. [F5, F7, F11, F12, F15, step 5.1]

7.1 For a rational representative in step 6.1 let $(u,w)$ be its integral first column and $a=\gcd(u,w)>0$. The conditions on this column give $(a,N)=1$. By [F12] choose integers $s,t$ with $su+tw=a$. The matrix with rows $(s,t)$ and $(-w/a,u/a)$ has determinant one and belongs to $\Gamma_0(N)$, as follows. Bézout gives integers $r,v$ with $ra+vN=1$ from $(a,N)=1$. Multiplying by $w/a$ gives $w/a=rw+vN(w/a)$, so $N\mid w/a$. Left multiplication sends the first column to $(a,0)$, so the lower right entry is $d=m/a>0$. Adding an integral multiple of the second row to the first gives the unique upper right entry $b$ in $0\le b<d$. The representatives are thus the finite components of $A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\0&d\end{pmatrix}$ with $ad=m$, $a,d>0$, $(a,N)=1$ and $0\le b<d$. Each satisfies the local conditions. A determinant-one integral row operation preserves the first-column greatest common divisor, so equivalent representatives have the same $a$ and hence the same $d$. For two such representatives their left multiplier is upper unipotent with upper right entry $(b'-b)/d$. This is integral only when the two residues coincide. [F7, F12, step 6.1]

8.1 For the now finite set of cosets $K_0(N)x$ define $H_m\phi_f(g)$ to be the sum of $\phi_f(g(1,x^\iota))$ over one representative $x$ of each coset, where $\iota$ is the componentwise adjugate. The adjoined real component in $(1,x^\iota)$ is the identity. By [F15] the adjugate reverses products of invertible matrices. For $k\in K_0(N)$, its adjugate $k^\iota$ also belongs to $K_0(N)$, by the two-by-two formula and the unit determinant. Replacing $x$ by $kx$ leaves the summand unchanged since $(kx)^\iota=x^\iota k^\iota$ and $\phi_f$ is fixed on the right by $K_0(N)$. For $h\in K_0(N)$, evaluating at $g(1,h)$ replaces $x^\iota$ by $h x^\iota=(x h^\iota)^\iota$. Right multiplication by $h^\iota$ permutes the finite left-level cosets by step 6.1. Thus $H_m\phi_f$ is fixed by $K_0(N)$. Real rotations commute with the finite translations and preserve the same rotation character in the sum. The sum belongs to the smooth automorphic model of the constituent because it is a finite sum of right translates of $\phi_f$. It is fixed by the global $K_1(N)$ and has the rotation character in [F7], so it is a scalar multiple of $\phi_f$ by uniqueness, with zero allowed. [F5, F7, F11, F15, step 5.1, step 6.1, step 7.1]

9.1 For $z=x+iy$ with $y>0$, evaluate $H_m$ at the adele supported at the real place with component $h_z=\begin{pmatrix}y&x\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$. For each representative from step 7.1, its adjugate $A^\iota=mA^{-1}$ from [F15] contributes $\phi_f(h_z(A^\iota)_f)$. The argument equals the rational matrix $A^\iota$ embedded at every place times the adele supported at the real place with component $(A^\iota)^{-1}h_z$. Formula [F5] and the weight-two slash composition rule give $((f|_2(A^\iota)^{-1})|_2h_z)(i)=y(f|_2 A)(z)$, since $(A^\iota)^{-1}=A/m$ and the numerator determinant and the squared denominator in the slash action both scale by the square of the scalar. Divide the eigenfunction equation by $y$ using $\phi_f(h_z)=yf(z)$. Each summand on the classical side is $(f|_2 A)(z)=md^{-2}f((az+b)/d)$. By step 7.1 the sum precisely matches the classical $T_m$ in [F5], including its restriction to $(a,N)=1$ from the Dirichlet character extended by zero on nonunits. This applies also when $m$ has a prime divisor of $N$. Thus $f$ satisfies the classical eigenform condition at every positive index and is a normalized weight-two cuspidal newform. [F5, F15, step 5.1, step 7.1, step 8.1] ∎

### External input

The global transfer input gives the forward correspondence for quaternion automorphic constituents of dimension greater than one at fixed central character, including the split and nonsplit comparisons.

The Hilbert factorization input gives the restricted Hilbert tensor product for both full-unit discrete automorphic constituents.

The real local transfer input relates the Hamilton symmetric-power index to the split lowest absolute rotation weight and supplies the two models on the determinant-one group.

The classical newform input constructs the cusp form and its lift at the conductor level, with the stated character, Fourier normalization and prime eigenform properties away from the level. It also supplies the irreducible admissible split local factors.

The finite newvector input gives local genericity, the implication to Langlands-type induction and the least congruence index giving the local conductor ideal.

The conductor product input identifies the rational newform level with the product of local conductor prime powers. Its subgroup and rotation conditions specify the one-dimensional adelic newform line, and its inverse lift recovers the classical newform.

The quotient measure input gives uniqueness up to positive scale for invariant Radon measures on the full-unit orbit spaces.

The local inner product input gives uniqueness up to positive scale of the invariant Hermitian inner product on each smooth split local factor.

The smooth cuspidal realization input supplies a nonzero smooth square-integrable cusp vector with identically vanishing upper unipotent constant term.


## The trivial central-character model of the projective quaternion quotient

[Open item](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-quaternion-trivial-central-character-model) · level 7


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $B$ be the rational Hamilton algebra and fix a full order $O\subset B$, a unital subring with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$. At each prime $\ell$ put $O_\ell=O\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Z_\ell\subset B_\ell=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell$. Let $\widehat B^\times$ be the restricted product of $B_\ell^\times$ with respect to $O_\ell^\times$, with its restricted product topology. Give $B_{\mathbb R}^\times$ the subspace topology of Euclidean $\mathbb R^4$ in the Hamilton coordinates and give the full quaternion idele group $\widetilde H=B_{\mathbb R}^\times\times\widehat B^\times$ the product topology. Use the same order in the finite projective construction of $H=G(\mathbb A)$, where $G=PB^\times$, and let $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)$ be its diagonal rational subgroup. Embed $B^\times$ in $\widetilde H$ diagonally by extending coefficients at each place.

The center of $\widetilde H$ is the scalar idele group $Z=\mathbb R^\times\times\mathbb A_f^\times$, embedded by multiplying the quaternion identity by each scalar. The real and finite scalar projections give a surjective homomorphism $p:\widetilde H\to H$ with kernel $Z$. On the spaces of right cosets with their quotient topologies it induces an equivariant homeomorphism
$$\Phi:Y=B^\times Z\backslash\widetilde H\longrightarrow X=\Gamma\backslash H,\qquad B^\times Zx\longmapsto\Gamma p(x).$$
Equivariance means that right translation by $a\in\widetilde H$ corresponds to right translation by $p(a)$. For a right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$, the measure $\nu(E)=\mu(\Phi(E))$ on Borel sets of $Y$ is right-invariant Radon. Pullback gives a surjective linear isometry from $L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$ to $L^2(Y,\nu;\mathbb C)$. It intertwines the projective right regular action lifted along $p$ with the action of $\widetilde H$ by right translation on $Y$. The latter has trivial central character, meaning that every scalar idele acts as the identity. The projective and lifted actions on $L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$ have the same closed invariant subspaces. These are complex-linear subspaces closed in the $L^2$ norm topology.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] Assume the Axiom of Choice. It implies Countable Choice, which is used for the Hilbert space conclusion for complex $L^2$ ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)).

[F1] At each place the Hamilton basis satisfies $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$, $ij=k=-ji$ and the cyclic relations. The local order $O_\ell$ contains $1$ and has the free $\mathbb Z_\ell$-basis obtained from the chosen order basis. The finite quaternion ideles lie in $O_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many primes. The finite scalar ideles have nonzero local scalars in $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many primes ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles), [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles)).

[F2] A topological group has continuous multiplication and inversion ([math:def-topological-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-group)). The real and finite factors of $\widetilde H$ are topological groups in the specified topologies. Their projective groups use the scalar quotient topologies and together form the topological group $H$ with its product topology. Quotient group multiplication is induced by multiplication of representatives. The rational diagonal embedding into the finite ideles extends coefficients, and the image of the full rational diagonal under the scalar projections is $\Gamma$ ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), [def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles), [def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units), [math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group)). The full idele group $\widetilde H$ is the external direct product of its real and finite factors. The product is a group with componentwise multiplication and inversion ([math:def-external-direct-product-of-groups](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-external-direct-product-of-groups), [math:thm-external-direct-product-is-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-external-direct-product-is-a-group)).

[F3] Left and right translations in a topological group are homeomorphisms. Binary product topologies have bases of products of two opens. A set in a quotient topology is open exactly when its preimage under the projection is open. For a subgroup $L$ and an element $x$ in a group, the right coset is $Lx=\{lx:l\in L\}$ ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion), [math:def-product-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-product-topology), [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology), [math:def-coset](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-coset)). Open sets are closed under binary intersections and arbitrary unions ([math:def-topological-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-topological-space)). For a subgroup $L$ and elements $x,y$ of the ambient group, $Lx=Ly$ exactly when $yx^{-1}\in L$ ([math:lem-coset-membership-and-equality](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-coset-membership-and-equality)).

[F4] The projective quotient $X$ is locally compact Hausdorff under [A1]. A homeomorphism is a continuous bijection with continuous inverse. Local compactness means that each point has a compact neighborhood, and Hausdorffness means distinct points have disjoint open neighborhoods. Continuous maps send compact subsets to compact subsets. Right invariance means $\mu(Dh)=\mu(D)$ for every Borel $D\subset X$ and every $h\in H$ ([lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient), [math:def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-homeomorphism-and-open-maps), [math:def-locally-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-locally-compact-space), [math:def-hausdorff-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hausdorff-space), [math:thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compactness-under-continuous-maps)). A compact subset is compact in its subspace topology, and every compact subset of a Hausdorff space is closed ([math:def-compact-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-compact-space), [math:thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-compact-subset-of-a-hausdorff-space-is-closed)). Right multiplication on $X$ is the action $(\Gamma h)a=\Gamma(ha)$, and each right translation is a homeomorphism ([lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient)).

[F5] The Borel sigma-algebra is generated by the open sets. A Radon measure on a locally compact Hausdorff space is a Borel measure finite on compact subsets, outer regular on Borel subsets and inner regular on open subsets ([math:def-borel-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-borel-sigma-algebra), [math:def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radon-measure-on-an-lch-space)). An algebra of subsets contains the empty set and is closed under complements relative to the ambient set and under binary unions ([math:def-algebra-of-subsets](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-algebra-of-subsets)). A sigma-algebra is an algebra of subsets closed under countable unions ([math:def-sigma-algebra](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-sigma-algebra)). A measure space is a triple $(W,\mathcal A,\lambda)$ with $\mathcal A$ a sigma-algebra on $W$ and $\lambda$ a measure on it ([math:def-measure-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-space)).

[F6] Complex $L^2$ consists of complex measurable functions with finite square integral modulo equality almost everywhere. Under Countable Choice, complex $L^2$ on a measure space is complete for the norm induced by its complex inner product, and this norm is the square root of the square integral ([math:def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-complex-lp-and-euclidean-test-function-conventions), [math:lem-complex-lp-completeness-density-and-inner-product](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-complex-lp-completeness-density-and-inner-product)). A complex Hilbert space is a complex inner-product space complete for its induced norm ([math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space)). The product $K_f=\prod_\ell K_\ell$ of the images of $O_\ell^\times$ is a compact open subgroup of $G(\mathbb A_f)$ ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units)). For a nonzero right-invariant Radon measure on $X$, the projective right regular representation $R(h)[f]=[x\mapsto f(xh)]$ is strongly continuous by Justification step 2.1 of the automorphic fixed-space definition, applied with $U=K_f$ ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space)).

[F7] A nonnegative simple measurable function is a finite sum of nonnegative multiples of indicators of pairwise disjoint measurable sets ([math:def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-nonnegative-simple-measurable-function)). The integral of a nonnegative simple function equals the sum of its coefficients times the measures of their disjoint sets, with zero coefficients contributing zero even on sets of infinite measure ([math:def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-integral-of-a-nonnegative-simple-function), [math:prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-the-nonnegative-integral-agrees-with-the-simple-integral)).

[F8] A nonnegative measurable function is the pointwise increasing limit of nonnegative measurable simple functions. Monotone convergence identifies its integral with the increasing limit of the integrals of these approximations, allowing infinity ([math:thm-increasing-simple-approximation-of-a-nonnegative-measurable-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-increasing-simple-approximation-of-a-nonnegative-measurable-function), [math:thm-monotone-convergence-for-the-integral](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-monotone-convergence-for-the-integral)).

[F9] The center of a group consists of elements commuting with every group element. A group homomorphism preserves products and its kernel is the preimage of the identity. A subgroup contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses ([math:def-center-of-a-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-center-of-a-group), [math:def-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-group-homomorphism), [math:def-kernel-and-image-of-group-homomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-kernel-and-image-of-group-homomorphism), [math:def-subgroup](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-subgroup)).

[F10] A measure has value zero on the empty set and is countably additive on pairwise disjoint measurable sets. A measurable map pulls back measurable sets to measurable sets. Functions agree almost everywhere if they agree outside a measurable set of measure zero ([math:def-measure](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure), [math:def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measurable-function-between-measurable-spaces), [math:def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-null-set-and-almost-everywhere)).

[F11] A linear isometry is a linear map preserving norms. For a group representation $\rho$, a closed linear subspace $M$ is invariant if $\rho(g)M=M$ for every group element $g$. An intertwiner $T$ between actions $\rho$ and $\sigma$ is a bounded linear map with $T\rho(g)=\sigma(g)T$ for every $g$ ([math:def-linear-isometry-and-isometric-isomorphism](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-isometry-and-isometric-isomorphism), [math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)). A complex-linear subspace contains zero and is closed under addition and multiplication by complex scalars ([math:def-linear-subspace](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-subspace)). Closedness in $L^2$ means that the complement is open for the metric $d(u,v)=\|u-v\|_2$ ([math:def-normed-vector-space-over-an-absolutely-valued-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-normed-vector-space-over-an-absolutely-valued-field), [math:def-metric-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-metric-topology)). A complex-linear map preserves sums and multiplication by complex scalars ([math:def-linear-map](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-linear-map)). A linear map $T$ between normed spaces is bounded if $\|Tv\|\le C\|v\|$ for some $C\ge0$ and every $v$ ([math:def-bounded-linear-operator](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-bounded-linear-operator)). A strongly continuous unitary representation on a complex Hilbert space is a group homomorphism into its bijective complex-linear isometries with norm-continuous orbit maps ([math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)).

[F12] A measurable self-map $\tau$ of a measure space with measure $\lambda$ preserves the measure if $\lambda(\tau^{-1}D)=\lambda(D)$ for every measurable set $D$. For such a map and any nonnegative measurable $g$, $\int g\circ\tau\,d\lambda=\int g\,d\lambda$, allowing infinity ([math:def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-measure-preserving-transformation-and-system), [math:thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-integrals-are-invariant-under-measure-preserving-maps)).

[F13] A rational number lies in $\mathbb Z_\ell$ whenever $\ell$ does not divide its denominator. See [the definition of finite scalar adeles and ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-scalar-adeles-and-ideles), Justification step 2.2.

[F14] A map of topological spaces is continuous exactly when every open set has open preimage. It suffices to test the open sets of a basis for the target topology (clauses (b) and (d) of [math:thm-continuity-characterisations-top](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-continuity-characterisations-top)).

### Proof

**Given:** The order and groups in the statement, a right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$ on $X$, and [A1].

**Goal:** Identify the full quaternion quotient and its invariant measure with the projective model, and show that pullback intertwines the actions and that scalar ideles act trivially.

1.1 By the center criterion in [F9], a central idele commutes with the ideles that equal $i$ or $j$ at a single place and equal $1$ elsewhere. The Hamilton relations in characteristic zero imply that commutation with $i$ eliminates the $j,k$ coefficients and commutation with $j$ then eliminates the $i$ coefficient. Each component is therefore a nonzero scalar. If $t1\in O_\ell$ with $t\in\mathbb Q_\ell$, multiplication by $t1$ preserves $O_\ell$ and acts by the scalar matrix $tI$ in the free $\mathbb Z_\ell$-basis of $O_\ell$. Thus $t\in\mathbb Z_\ell$. Conversely $t1\in O_\ell$ for every $t\in\mathbb Z_\ell$ since $1\in O_\ell$. Applying this intersection equality to $t$ and $t^{-1}$ gives $O_\ell^\times\cap\mathbb Q_\ell^\times 1=\mathbb Z_\ell^\times 1$. The restricted product conditions now identify the central ideles with $Z$, whose scalar components commute with every idele. [F1, F9]

2.1 The product group law and inverses in [F2] give the componentwise operations on $\widetilde H$. By the product topology, the projections to the factor domains of multiplication and inversion are continuous. The preimage of a basic rectangle under either operation is the intersection of the two open preimages obtained from the continuous factor operations and these projections. By the basis test in [F14], both operations are continuous, so $\widetilde H$ is a topological group by [F2]. Multiplication of representatives in [F2] makes the product of the scalar projections a homomorphism $p$. Each factor is surjective by construction and continuous by the quotient topology and [F14]. Hence $p$ is surjective, and preimages of basic open rectangles are open rectangles by [F3], so $p$ is continuous by the basis test in [F14]. Its kernel is the preimage of the identity. A pair maps to the identity exactly when each factor is in its respective scalar subgroup, so $\ker p=Z$. For each component quotient map $q$ and each open $V$ in its domain, $q^{-1}(q(V))$ is the union of the left translates of $V$ by the scalar subgroup. Each translate is open by [F3], and their union is open by the topology axioms in [F3], so the quotient topology makes $q(V)$ open. The image under $p$ of any open set is the union of the images of its basic open rectangles. Each rectangle maps to the product of its two open images, so [F3] gives openness of $p$. The projection $\pi_X:H\to X$ is also open, since $\pi_X^{-1}(\pi_X(V))=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma V$ for open $V\subset H$. [F2, F3, F9, F14, step 1.1]

3.1 For $b\in B^\times$, apply [F13] to the finitely many coefficients of $b$ and $b^{-1}$ in the order basis. Outside the primes dividing a common denominator for these coefficients, both tuples have components in $O_\ell$ by [F1]. Coefficient extension preserves multiplication, so the components of the two tuples are inverse at every prime and belong to $O_\ell^\times$ outside those denominator primes. Thus both tuples are finite quaternion ideles. The real component gives injectivity. By the diagonal descriptions in [F2], $p(b)$ is the diagonal projective class of $b$ and every element of $\Gamma$ is such a class, so $p(B^\times)=\Gamma$. Since $p(1)=p(1)^2$, one has $p(1)=1$. Then $p(uu^{-1})=p(u)p(u^{-1})=1$ gives $p(u^{-1})=p(u)^{-1}$. As $\Gamma$ contains the identity and is closed under products and inverses, these identities and multiplicativity give the same properties for $L=p^{-1}(\Gamma)$, so $L$ is a subgroup. If $x\in L$, choose $b\in B^\times$ with $p(b)=p(x)$. Then $p(b^{-1}x)=1$, so $b^{-1}x\in Z$. Conversely $p(bz)=p(b)$ for $b\in B^\times$ and $z\in Z$. Thus $L=B^\times Z$. [F1, F2, F9, F13, step 2.1]

4.1 By the right-coset criterion in [F3], for $x,y\in\widetilde H$ the equality $Lx=Ly$ holds exactly when $yx^{-1}\in L$, or equivalently $p(y)p(x)^{-1}\in\Gamma$. This last condition is $\Gamma p(x)=\Gamma p(y)$, so the coset map is well defined and injective. It is surjective because $p$ is surjective. For the quotient projection $\pi_Y:\widetilde H\to Y$ and every open $D\subset X$, the set $\pi_Y^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}(D))=p^{-1}(\pi_X^{-1}(D))$ is open by [F3], [F14] and step 2.1. The quotient topology and [F14] then give continuity of $\Phi$. For open $W\subset Y$, its image $\Phi(W)=(\pi_X\circ p)(\pi_Y^{-1}(W))$ is open by step 2.1. By [F14], this proves continuity of $\Phi^{-1}$. Right multiplication by $a$ preserves the representative rule since $(ya)(xa)^{-1}=yx^{-1}$. The same rule holds in $H$, and $p(xa)=p(x)p(a)$ gives equivariance. [F2, F3, F4, F14, step 2.1, step 3.1]

5.1 Under [A1], the projective quotient is locally compact Hausdorff by [F4]. Pulling back compact neighborhoods and disjoint open neighborhoods under $\Phi$ shows that $Y$ is locally compact Hausdorff as well. The homeomorphism and its inverse carry opens to opens and compact sets to compact sets. For either map, the subsets of its target with Borel preimages include the empty set and the whole target, and are closed under complements and under binary and countable unions, because inverse images preserve these operations and Borel sets are closed under them. The family is therefore a sigma-algebra by [F5]. They also include all open sets by continuity and [F14]. By the generation property in [F5], every Borel set therefore has Borel preimage in either direction. The formula for $\nu$ gives $\nu(\varnothing)=\mu(\varnothing)=0$. The bijection $\Phi$ sends a countable disjoint union to the disjoint union of the images, so countable additivity of $\mu$ gives countable additivity of $\nu$. Thus $\nu$ is a Borel measure. By [F5], $(X,\mathcal B(X),\mu)$ and $(Y,\mathcal B(Y),\nu)$ are measure spaces, where $\mathcal B$ denotes the Borel sigma-algebra. By [F4], compact subsets of $X$ and $Y$ are closed. Their complements are open, so the compact sets are Borel by [F5]. The correspondences of open and compact sets transfer finiteness on compact sets, outer regularity on Borel sets and inner regularity on open sets from $\mu$ to $\nu$. Right translations on $X$ are homeomorphisms by [F4]. Equivariance transports these homeomorphisms to $Y$. It gives $\nu(Ea)=\mu(\Phi(E)p(a))=\mu(\Phi(E))$ for every Borel $E\subset Y$, using right invariance of $\mu$. [A1, F4, F5, F10, F14, step 4.1]

6.1 For a Borel $D\subset X$, one has $\mathbf1_D\circ\Phi=\mathbf1_{\Phi^{-1}(D)}$ and $\nu(\Phi^{-1}(D))=\mu(D)$. Pulling back each set in a disjoint simple representation from [F7] gives a disjoint simple representation on $Y$ with the same coefficients. The disjoint-set formula in [F7] extends the integral identity from indicators to every nonnegative simple measurable function. For nonnegative measurable $g$ on $X$, take $s_n\uparrow g$ from [F8]. Both $s_n\circ\Phi$ and $g\circ\Phi$ are measurable because inverse images of measurable sets under each composition are successive inverse images under measurable maps. The approximation still increases pointwise after composition. Apply monotone convergence on both measure spaces from step 5.1 to deduce $\int_Y g\circ\Phi\,d\nu=\int_X g\,d\mu$ from the equal simple-function integrals. [F5, F7, F8, F10, step 5.1]

6.2 By step 5.1 every right translation $\tau$ on either coset space is a homeomorphism, so $\tau$ and its inverse are Borel measurable by the sigma-algebra argument of that step. The inverse image under $\tau$ of a Borel set is its translate by the inverse group element, so right invariance gives $\lambda(\tau^{-1}D)=\lambda(D)$ for the measure $\lambda$ of the corresponding measure space from step 5.1. Thus $\tau$ preserves $\lambda$ by [F12]. Therefore translation preserves the square integral by [F12] with $g=|f|^2$ and takes agreement outside a Borel null set to agreement outside its null preimage. This defines $R(h)[f]=[x\mapsto f(xh)]$ on $X$ and $S(a)[u]=[y\mapsto u(ya)]$ on $Y$ for all measures in the statement, including the zero measure. For either translation $\tau$, one has $(cf+dg)\circ\tau=c(f\circ\tau)+d(g\circ\tau)$ for complex scalars $c,d$. Hence each translation operator is complex-linear by [F11]. The operator for the inverse group element is its inverse. The identity acts as the identity and $f((xh)k)=f(x(hk))$ gives $R(h)R(k)=R(hk)$. On $Y$ the equality $u((ya)b)=u(y(ab))$ likewise gives $S(a)S(b)=S(ab)$. [F6, F10, F11, F12, step 4.1, step 5.1]

7.1 Taking $g=|f|^2$ gives norm preservation for pullback. If two representatives agree outside a Borel $\mu$-null set $N$, their pullbacks agree outside $\Phi^{-1}(N)$, which is $\nu$-null. Thus $T[f]=[f\circ\Phi]$ is well defined on the classes. The identity $(cf+dg)\circ\Phi=c(f\circ\Phi)+d(g\circ\Phi)$ for complex scalars $c,d$ gives linearity by [F11], so $T$ is a linear isometry. For a square-integrable representative $u$ on $Y$, the measurable function $u\circ\Phi^{-1}$ has finite square integral by step 6.1 applied to $|u\circ\Phi^{-1}|^2$. Its pullback is $u$, giving surjectivity. On the two measure spaces from step 5.1, [F6] and Countable Choice from [A1] give complex inner products inducing the $L^2$ norms and completeness for those norms. Thus both spaces are complex Hilbert spaces by [F6]. [A1, F6, F10, F11, step 5.1, step 6.1]

8.1 The map $T$ is bounded with bound $1$ by [F11], since $\|Tv\|_2=\|v\|_2$ for all $v$. For $a\in\widetilde H$, equivariance gives $f(\Phi(ya))=f(\Phi(y)p(a))$ for every $y\in Y$. On classes this is $S(a)T=TR(p(a))$, so the surjective isometry $T$ intertwines the two actions. For $a\in Z$, one has $p(a)=1$, hence $S(a)$ is the identity by surjectivity of $T$. The projective and lifted actions on $L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$ have the same operator family because $p$ is surjective. Thus the conditions $R(h)M=M$ for every $h\in H$ and $R(p(a))M=M$ for every $a\in\widetilde H$ are equivalent for each complex-linear subspace $M$ closed in the $L^2$ norm topology, by the invariance criterion in [F11]. If $\mu\ne 0$, [F6] gives strong continuity of $R$ with the same formula. If $\mu=0$, every class is zero, so every orbit is constant. The linear isometry $T$ satisfies $\|Tv-Tw\|_2=\|T(v-w)\|_2=\|v-w\|_2$ for all $v,w$. Surjectivity implies that $T^{-1}$ also preserves norm distances, so both maps are continuous for the norm topologies in [F11]. The lifted orbit map $a\mapsto R(p(a))v$ is continuous by continuity of $p$ and of $h\mapsto R(h)v$. For each $u$ on $Y$, the orbit is $a\mapsto T R(p(a))T^{-1}u$ by the intertwining identity, hence is continuous as well. The operator and continuity properties just proved on the Hilbert spaces from step 7.1 give strongly continuous unitary representations by [F11]. [F6, F11, step 2.1, step 4.1, step 6.2, step 7.1] ∎


## Discrete automorphic spectrum for definite projective quaternion units

[Open item](item-thm-projective-quaternion-discrete-spectrum) · level 8


### Statement

Assume the Axiom of Choice. Let $G=PB^\times$ be the group of units modulo nonzero scalars of the definite rational Hamilton division algebra $B$. Put $H=G(\mathbb A)=G(\mathbb R)\times G(\mathbb A_f)$ and $\Gamma=G(\mathbb Q)$ embedded diagonally. The quotient $X=\Gamma\backslash H$ is compact and has a nonzero finite right-invariant Radon measure $\mu$, a regular Borel measure finite on compact sets. On $\mathcal H=L^2(X,\mu;\mathbb C)$, the Hilbert space of complex square-integrable functions modulo equality almost everywhere, right translation acts by $R(h)f(x)=f(xh)$.

The representation $R$ is a Hilbert direct sum of irreducible unitary automorphic representations. Its summands are pairwise orthogonal closed invariant subspaces $M_i$ with closed linear span $\mathcal H$, and irreducibility means that each $M_i$ has no proper nonzero closed invariant subspace. Each equivalence class occurs with finite multiplicity. For a compact open subgroup $U\le G(\mathbb A_f)$ and $K=G(\mathbb R)\times U$, the fixed space $\mathcal H^K=\{v:R(k)v=v\text{ for all }k\in K\}$ is the orthogonal sum of the spaces $M_i^K$, with only finitely many nonzero terms. A summand contributing to this space has trivial real action.

For a prime $\ell$, let $\mathbb Q_\ell$ be the $\ell$-adic completion of $\mathbb Q$ and $\mathbb Z_\ell=\{a\in\mathbb Q_\ell:|a|_\ell\le1\}$ its ring of integers. Write $B_\ell=B\otimes_{\mathbb Q}\mathbb Q_\ell$ for $B$ with coefficients extended to $\mathbb Q_\ell$. Use the scalar quotient $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$ for the finite local group. Call $\ell$ split if $B_\ell$ is isomorphic as a $\mathbb Q_\ell$-algebra to $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$, the algebra of two-by-two matrices over $\mathbb Q_\ell$. At a split prime choose such an isomorphism and let $J_\ell$ be the projective image of the invertible matrices over $\mathbb Z_\ell$. The copy of $J_\ell$ supported at $\ell$ in the finite adelic restricted product consists of the tuples with $\ell$-coordinate in $J_\ell$ and identity at every other prime. A local component is the factor on which the local group acts in the restricted Hilbert tensor product of a constituent. This tensor product is the completion of the span of elementary tensors equal to specified unit vectors at all but finitely many places, with the product inner product. A local component is unramified when its $J_\ell$-fixed space is nonzero. In that case the fixed space is a line, and the spherical Hecke algebra acts on it by a character. This algebra consists of compactly supported complex functions invariant on both sides by $J_\ell$. Its convolution uses Haar measure with $J_\ell$ of mass one, and a character is a unital complex-algebra map to $\mathbb C$. When $U$ contains the copy of $J_\ell$ supported at $\ell$, the same local Hecke operators act on every nonzero $M_i^K$ by that constituent’s local character.

### Facts & Assumptions

[A1] The Axiom of Choice provides choice functions for indexed families of nonempty sets. It implies Countable Choice, the corresponding principle for sequences. Use these hypotheses in the quotient and Haar constructions, Hilbert direct sums and tensor products, and the spherical-line result ([math:def-axiom-of-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-axiom-of-choice), [math:thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-choice-implies-dependent-implies-countable-choice)).

[F1] The full projective group $H$ has the product topology and componentwise multiplication and is locally compact Hausdorff, $G(\mathbb R)$ is compact, and $\Gamma$ is closed and discrete. The quotient is compact. The group $H$ is unimodular, meaning left Haar measure is also right invariant, and the invariant Radon measure on $X$ is finite, nonzero and unique up to positive scale ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-full-adelic-hamilton-group), [lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient](item-lem-author-5b8600b4f4af-hamilton-invariant-quotient), [lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-compact-projective-hamilton-quotient](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-compact-projective-hamilton-quotient), [lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-hamilton-haar-normalization](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-hamilton-haar-normalization)).

[F2] The quotient representation is strongly continuous and unitary ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space)). A unitary representation is a homomorphism to the group of bijective linear isometries. Strong continuity means that the orbit map of every vector is continuous in the Hilbert norm ([math:def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-strongly-continuous-unitary-representation)). With $K=G(\mathbb R)\times U$, its fixed space identifies isometrically with $L^2(D)$ for $D=G(\mathbb Q)\backslash G(\mathbb A_f)/U$ and the measure assigning each double coset the measure of its preimage. The map $X\to D$ is a continuous surjection to a discrete space, with fibers the right $K$-orbits. A double coset of $g_f$ consists of the elements $\gamma_f g_f u$ with $\gamma\in\Gamma$ and $u\in U$ ([def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space](item-def-definite-quaternion-automorphic-fixed-space)). In a Hilbert direct sum the group acts in each coordinate separately, and vectors are families with finite sum of squared norms, defined as the supremum of the finite subsums ([math:def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-direct-sum-of-unitary-representations)).

[F3] On the full quaternion ideles $\widetilde H=B_{\mathbb R}^\times\times\widehat B^\times$, the quotient by the center of scalar ideles $Z$ identifies with $H$ as a topological group. Lifting along this quotient identifies $R$ with the trivial central-character right translation action on $B^\times Z\backslash\widetilde H$, for the transported measure. The lift preserves closed invariant subspaces ([lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-quaternion-trivial-central-character-model](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-quaternion-trivial-central-character-model)).

[F4] A complex Hilbert space is complete in the norm of its inner product ([math:def-hilbert-space](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-hilbert-space)). The completion of the algebraic tensor product of two complex Hilbert spaces uses the product pairing on elementary tensors, and their finite sums are dense. If a subgroup acts on only one Hilbert tensor factor, the fixed space is the completed tensor product of that factor’s fixed space with the other factor. The same scalar action of any finite operator sum on the fixed space passes to this tensor product ([math:lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-nrqm-composites-hilbert-tensor-construction), [lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-fixed-space-of-tensor-factor](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-fixed-space-of-tensor-factor)).

[F5] A split identification gives a continuous surjection $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)\to G(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ modulo scalars and a compact open subgroup $J_\ell$ given by the image of $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. If the lifted local representation has irreducible admissible smooth vectors, its nonzero $J_\ell$-fixed space is a line with a Hecke character. The spherical action is the finite sum $T_fv=\sum_{xJ_\ell} f(x)\rho(x)v$, with one term per contributing coset $xJ_\ell=\{xj:j\in J_\ell\}$ ([def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation](item-def-author-5b8600b4f4af-split-unramified-quaternion-representation), [lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-local-spherical-line](item-lem-author-c3a9b9243c74-projective-local-spherical-line), [def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra](item-def-author-d45da46285be-spherical-hecke-algebra)).

[F6] Use the compact-quotient, factorization, smooth-vector and reductivity results and the source measure convention stated below.

[F7] Write $O$ for the full order in the central-character model. Its completion $O_\ell$ is the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-span of a full order basis in $B_\ell$, and $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is the unit ball for the nonarchimedean absolute value of $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). The finite idele group uses basic open boxes equal to $O_\ell^\times$ outside a finite set ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles)). The Hurwitz order $O_{\mathrm H}$ is a full order with a $\mathbb Z$-basis that is also a $\mathbb Q$-basis of $B$ ([thm-hurwitz-order-integral-structure](item-thm-hurwitz-order-integral-structure)). At each odd prime an integral splitting carries the completed Hurwitz order onto $M_2(\mathbb Z_\ell)$ ([thm-hurwitz-integral-splitting-at-odd-primes](item-thm-hurwitz-integral-splitting-at-odd-primes)).

[F8] The $F$-points of the general linear algebraic group of degree two over a field $F$ are the invertible two-by-two matrices over $F$ ([math:lem-general-linear-group-scheme-and-its-coordinate-ring](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-general-linear-group-scheme-and-its-coordinate-ring)). The local-points topology on $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)$ is the topology from the embedding $A\mapsto(A,\det(A)^{-1})$ into $M_2(\mathbb Q_\ell)\times\mathbb Q_\ell$. For a smooth connected affine algebraic group, reductivity means that the unipotent radical after base change to an algebraic closure is trivial ([math:def-radical-and-unipotent-radical-of-an-algebraic-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-radical-and-unipotent-radical-of-an-algebraic-group)). With Countable Choice the complete nonarchimedean field $\mathbb Q_\ell$ is locally compact and its ring of integers is compact open ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), [math:cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/cor-p-adic-field-is-locally-compact-and-totally-disconnected)).

[F9] The rational Hamilton basis $1,i,j,k$ satisfies $i^2=j^2=k^2=-1$, $ij=k=-ji$, $jk=i=-kj$, and $ki=j=-ik$. Rational scalars are central ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions)). Every nonzero $x\in B$ has the two-sided inverse $\bar x/N(x)$, where $\bar x$ negates the three imaginary coefficients and $N(x)$ is the sum of all four coefficient squares ([lem-rational-hamilton-norm-and-inverse](item-lem-rational-hamilton-norm-and-inverse)).

[F10] An element of $\mathbb Z_\ell$ is a unit exactly when its reduction modulo $\ell$ is nonzero ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles), Justification, step 1.2). The kernel of scalar reduction modulo $\ell^n$ is $\ell^n\mathbb Z_\ell$ for $n\ge1$. Its translates by $b$ are the balls $\{a:|a-b|_\ell\le\ell^{-n}\}$ and give a neighborhood basis at $b$ in $\mathbb Q_\ell$ ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-finite-quaternion-ideles), Justification, step 1.3).

[F11] The finite adelic scalar quotient is the restricted product of the groups $G(\mathbb Q_\ell)=B_\ell^\times/\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$, each with its local quotient topology, with respect to the projective images of $O_\ell^\times$. The identification sends a finite idele class to its tuple of local classes ([def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units](item-def-finite-quaternion-adeles-and-projective-units), Justification, steps 4.1 and 5.1).

[F12] A finite product of compact spaces is compact in the product topology ([math:thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-finite-products-of-compact-spaces)).

[F13] A number field is a finite extension of $\mathbb Q$ ([math:def-number-field](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-number-field)). A global field of characteristic zero is a finite extension of $\mathbb Q$. A place of $\mathbb Q$ is an equivalence class of nontrivial absolute values ([math:def-place-of-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-place-of-the-rationals)). Ostrowski’s theorem identifies these places with the classes of the usual absolute value and of the $\ell$-adic absolute values, one for each prime $\ell$ ([math:thm-ostrowskis-theorem-for-the-rationals](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-ostrowskis-theorem-for-the-rationals)). The corresponding completions are $\mathbb R$ and $\mathbb Q_\ell$, respectively ([math:def-rational-local-fields](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-rational-local-fields)).

[F14] With Countable Choice, a bounded linear map from a normed space to a Banach space extends uniquely to a bounded linear map on the completion of its domain ([math:thm-completion-universal-property-for-bounded-linear-maps](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-completion-universal-property-for-bounded-linear-maps)).

[F15] Addition and multiplication on $\mathbb Q_\ell$ are continuous in the $\ell$-adic metric, as is inversion on $\mathbb Q_\ell^\times$. The estimates in the local order construction also give continuity of polynomial coordinate functions and finite tuples of them ([def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions](item-def-author-6f52147f3ab3-hamilton-order-completions), Justification, steps 5.1 and 6.1).

[F16] The modular function records the scaling of left Haar integrals by right translations ([math:def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-modular-function-of-a-locally-compact-group)). When the modular functions of a locally compact Hausdorff group and its closed subgroup agree on the subgroup, the Weil measure for the constant weight one is a nonzero left-invariant Radon measure. It is the unique Radon measure giving the quotient integration identity for the chosen left Haar measures, with the inner integral averaging over right multiplication by the subgroup ([math:prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/prop-invariant-measure-on-g-mod-h-iff-modular-functions-agree), [math:thm-weil-quotient-integration-formula-with-rho-function](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/thm-weil-quotient-integration-formula-with-rho-function)). Counting measure is left and right Haar on a discrete group, and its integrals are sums ([math:lem-counting-measure-on-a-discrete-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-counting-measure-on-a-discrete-group)). In a topological group inversion is a homeomorphism, and in a unimodular group it preserves Haar measure ([math:lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-topological-group-translations-and-inversion), [math:lem-haar-change-of-variables-under-inversion](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/lem-haar-change-of-variables-under-inversion)).

[F17] For a normal subgroup $N$, its quotient group has multiplication $(aN)(bN)=abN$. A subset of a quotient space is open exactly when its preimage under the quotient map is open ([math:def-quotient-group](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-group), [math:def-quotient-topology](https://alphabetamath.cc/item/def-quotient-topology)).

### External input

Arthur’s compact-quotient result gives the orthogonal Hilbert decomposition, with finite multiplicities, for right translation on a compact quotient by a discrete subgroup of a locally compact unimodular group.

Badulescu and Renard’s factorization statement applies to each irreducible closed invariant summand in the trivial central-character square-integrable action of the quaternion ideles. It supplies irreducible unitary representations of the individual local unit groups and an equivalence with their restricted Hilbert tensor product. Their adelic group is the restricted product defined by the integral matrix unit subgroups in the chosen local matrix-algebra identifications. At all but finitely many finite places the local factors have unit vectors fixed by those subgroups. For their square integral they first take the quotient of adelic Haar measure by the Haar measure on the adelic center. The resulting measure on the projective group defines the measure on the quotient by the discrete rational image.

Getz and Hahn’s smooth-vector proposition and the Harish-Chandra–Bernstein admissibility theorem give irreducibility and admissibility of the smooth vectors of an irreducible unitary representation of the group of local points of a reductive group over a nonarchimedean local field. Smooth vectors in their Hilbert-space convention are the union of the spaces fixed by compact open subgroups. Admissibility means finite dimensionality of each compact-open fixed space.

Milne’s example gives reductivity of the general linear algebraic group over a field.

Getz and Hahn define global fields as finite extensions of $\mathbb Q$ or of $\mathbb F_p(t)$ for some prime $p$.

Getz and Hahn give $\mathrm{GL}_n(F_v)$ the subspace topology from the embedding $A\mapsto(A,\det(A)^{-1})$ into $M_n(F_v)\times F_v$, where $F_v$ is the completion at the place $v$.

### Proof

**Given:** $H,\Gamma,X,\mu$ as in the statement, $U$ compact open in $G(\mathbb A_f)$, and [A1].

**Goal:** Obtain the discrete decomposition, identify its finite-level fixed spaces, and compute the form of the split unramified action.

1.1 The compactness, nonzero finite invariant measure, locally compact Hausdorff group and discrete subgroup are supplied by [F1]. Its unimodularity meets Arthur’s remaining group hypothesis. Apply the first input in [F6] to get $\mathcal H=\widehat\bigoplus_i M_i$ with each $M_i$ irreducible and with finite multiplicities. Any choice of $\mu$ gives the same unitary equivalence class because scaling the invariant measure by a positive constant rescales the square norm by that constant and a constant multiplier implements the isometry. [A1, F1, F2, F6]

1.2 With the Hamilton rules of [F9], $x=a+bi+cj+dk$ satisfies $xi-ix=2dj-2ck$ and $xj-jx=2bk-2di$. If $x$ commutes with both $i$ and $j$, then the independence of the basis over $\mathbb Q$ implies $b=c=d=0$. Since rational scalars commute with all of $B$, its center is $\mathbb Q1$. The inverse formula in [F9] makes $B$ a division algebra. $\mathbb Q$ has characteristic zero and is a number field of degree one since $\{1\}$ is a basis of $\mathbb Q$ over $\mathbb Q$. Thus $\mathbb Q$ is a global field of characteristic zero by [F13]. By [F13] the base-field completions in the source are $\mathbb R$ at the single archimedean place and $\mathbb Q_\ell$ at the place of each prime $\ell$. Thus its local unit groups are $B_{\mathbb R}^\times$ at infinity and $B_\ell^\times$ at the finite places, obtained by extending the Hamilton coefficients. Apply the adelic convention in [F6] with base field $\mathbb Q$, central division algebra $B$, and matrix size one over $B$. By [A1] and [F7] choose their odd-prime identifications to carry the completed Hurwitz orders onto the integral matrix rings. The identifications are homeomorphisms on local units because the linear maps and their inverses use finite linear coordinate functions, continuous by [F15] in the two subspace topologies. The source’s odd-prime defining subgroups then pull back to $(O_{\mathrm H})_\ell^\times$. Take a positive integer $m$ clearing the denominators of the rational basis changes between $O$ and $O_{\mathrm H}$ in both directions. For $\ell\nmid m$ the two change-of-basis matrices are integral over $\mathbb Z_\ell$, so each completed order lies in the other and their unit groups agree. Outside the primes dividing $2m$, the source’s defining subgroups thus agree with those in [F7]. The restricted products have the same tuples, and each basic open box in either system is basic in the other after allowing the primes dividing $2m$ as exceptional coordinates. Including the real factor gives a topological group identification. It carries the diagonal rational units to the diagonal rational units by the same coefficient extensions in the local identifications, and carries the center onto the center. [A1, F3, F6, F7, F9, F13, F15]

2.1 Under step 1.2 and [F3], the two successive quotients in [F6] are by $Z$ and by $\Gamma$. The source provides Haar measures on the locally compact Hausdorff adelic group $\widetilde H$ and on $Z$. The latter is closed as the kernel of the continuous projection to the Hausdorff group $H$. For $z\in Z$, right translation by $z$ on $\widetilde H$ equals left translation by $z$, so the modular function of $\widetilde H$ is one there. The abelian group $Z$ is unimodular for the same reason. The first Haar quotient is a left Haar measure $dh$ on $H$ by [F16]: it is a nonzero left-invariant Radon measure, and the left and right coset spaces for $Z$ agree because $Z$ is central. Now $\Gamma$ is closed and discrete by [F1]. Its counting Haar measure and $dh$ have modular functions equal to one. Apply [F16] to $H/\Gamma$ to obtain the nonzero left-invariant Radon measure with the Weil identity for $dh$ and counting measure. Push it to $X$ by $h\Gamma\mapsto\Gamma h^{-1}$, which is a homeomorphism by inversion and the quotient topologies in [F17]. Denote the pushforward by $\nu$. The homeomorphism preserves the Borel, open and compact sets used in Radon regularity, so $\nu$ is nonzero Radon. It is right invariant because the homeomorphism changes left translation by $a$ to right translation by $a^{-1}$. Applying the Weil identity to $h\mapsto f(h^{-1})$ and reindexing the counting sum by $\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{-1}$ gives $\int_H f(h)\,dh=\int_X\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma} f(\gamma h)\,d\nu(\Gamma h)$ for every compactly supported continuous $f$. The left side uses inversion invariance of $dh$ from [F16]. The reverse substitution gives uniqueness for this right-coset Haar quotient. Thus the second quotient measure of [F6] is $\nu$ with counting normalization. Using a different Haar multiple of counting measure only rescales $\nu$ by the inverse multiple. In either normalization the source measure is nonzero, right invariant and Radon, and it is finite by compactness of $X$. [A1, F1, F3, F6, F16, F17, step 1.2]

2.2 Using the coordinate action in [F2], the equality $R(k)v=v$ for every $k\in K$ holds exactly when each component of $v$ belongs to $M_i^K$. The discrete space $D$ in [F2] is compact by continuity and surjectivity from the compact $X$: an open cover of $D$ pulls back to an open cover of $X$, whose finite subcover maps onto $D$. Its singleton cover shows $D$ is finite. The fixed space is therefore finite dimensional by its identification with $L^2(D)$. Any finite family of nonzero distinct fixed summands supplies the same number of linearly independent vectors by orthogonality, so only finitely many summands are nonzero. For $h=(a,b)\in H$ and $g\in G(\mathbb R)$, the product law gives $h^{-1}(g,1)h=(a^{-1}ga,1)$. If $v\in M_i^{G(\mathbb R)}$, the representation law gives $R((g,1))R(h)v=R(h)R((a^{-1}ga,1))v=R(h)v$. Thus $M_i^{G(\mathbb R)}$ is invariant under the full group. This space is closed as an intersection of kernels of continuous operators $R(g)-I$. If $M_i^K\ne0$ then $M_i^{G(\mathbb R)}\ne0$, and irreducibility forces it to equal $M_i$. [F1, F2, step 1.1]

3.1 By [F3] and step 1.2, the lift of each $M_i$ is irreducible for the trivial central-character action with the transported measure. On the identified projective quotient, the transported measure of [F3] and the source Haar quotient measure of step 2.1 are nonzero right-invariant Radon measures. They differ by a positive scalar by [F1]. Constant rescaling as in step 1.1 therefore identifies this lift with an irreducible subrepresentation of the source’s square-integrable right translation action. Apply the factorization in [F6]. A scalar supported at a single place $v$ belongs to $Z$ and acts only on the factor at $v$. It fixes every elementary tensor. Since tensoring with fixed unit reference vectors at all other places preserves the norm of the vector at $v$, the scalar acts as the identity on the local factor at $v$. The lifted action is strongly continuous by [F2] because the lift composes the quotient action with the continuous projection in [F3]. The insertion of a local unit with identity at all other places is continuous by the full idele product topology at infinity and the basic open boxes at finite places. For an arbitrary vector $w$ in the factor at $v$, tensoring with the other reference vectors is an isometry and carries the orbit of $w$ to the orbit of the corresponding elementary tensor under the inserted local group. The orbit map of each local vector is therefore norm-continuous, which gives strong continuity by [F2]. For the local unit group at $v$, write $Z_v$ for its scalar subgroup and $\pi_v$ for its action before passage to the quotient. Define $\rho_v(tZ_v)=\pi_v(t)$. Replacing $t$ by $tz$ with $z\in Z_v$ leaves $\pi_v(t)$ unchanged because $\pi_v(z)=I$. The product formula in [F17] gives $\rho_v((tZ_v)(sZ_v))=\pi_v(ts)=\rho_v(tZ_v)\rho_v(sZ_v)$, and the identity coset acts by the identity. Since the two actions have the same operators, they have the same closed invariant subspaces, so $\rho_v$ is irreducible. Each descended vector orbit map pulls back to the continuous local orbit map. For an open subset of the factor Hilbert space, the preimage under the descended orbit map is open by [F17] because its preimage under the quotient projection is open upstairs. The real and finite quotient topologies here are those in [F1] and [F11], so [F2] gives strong continuity. [F1, F2, F3, F4, F6, F7, F11, F17, step 1.1, step 1.2, step 2.1]

4.1 The quotient map $\widetilde H\to H$ sends the real component to its projective class and each finite component to its local scalar class by [F3] and [F11]. Use unit reference vectors fixed by the source’s defining subgroups at all but finitely many finite places as in [F6]. By step 1.2, these are fixed by $O_\ell^\times$ at all but finitely many finite places and therefore by its projective image in the descended action at those places. For $h\in H$, choose a lift in $\widetilde H$ by [F3]. On elementary tensors its action is the tensor of the local actions of the lift, which agrees coordinate by coordinate with the tensor of the descended actions at the components of $h$. These actions agree on the completion by density and unitarity. [F3, F4, F6, F7, F11, step 1.2, step 3.1]
